I told her plainly I believed him much too good for her, and with equal plainness that she was but a vain coquette. She laughed, shook curls from her eyes, and danced away as if I had praised her.

Ginevra’s studies were little better than nominal. There were three things only she practised in earnest—music, singing, dancing; also the embroidering of fine cambric handkerchiefs she could not afford to buy ready worked. History, geography, grammar, arithmetic—those she left undone or got others to do. Much of her time was spent in visiting. Madame, aware that her stay was limited and would not be extended for progress, allowed her great licence. Mrs. Cholmondeley—her chaperon—a gay fashionable lady, invited her whenever she entertained, and sometimes took her to evening parties. Ginevra approved this life; it had but one inconvenience: she must be well dressed, and she had not money for variety. All her thoughts ran upon this want. It was strange to see how quickened her otherwise indolent mind became on the matter—how necessity, like a loud street-organ under one’s window, forces attention and drives the same tune through the brain till you cannot help but hum it.

She begged boldly of Mrs. Cholmondeley—boldly, not with reluctant shame, but in this strain:

“My darling Mrs. C., I have nothing in the world fit to wear for your party next week; you must give me a book-muslin dress, and then a ceinture bleu céleste: do—there’s an angel! will you?”

The “darling Mrs. C.” yielded at first; but as requests multiplied with indulgence, she was soon obliged—like all Miss Fanshawe’s friends—to resist encroachment. After a while I heard no more of her gifts; yet the visits went on, and the absolutely necessary dresses still appeared; also many expensive etceteras—gloves, bouquets, even trinkets. These things, contrary to her custom and even nature—for she was not secretive—were kept sedulously out of sight for a time. But one evening, when she was going to a large party where particular care was demanded, she could not resist coming to my chamber to display herself.

Beautiful she looked: so young, so fresh, with that delicacy of skin and flexibility of shape which is English, and not common among continental charms. Her dress was new, costly, perfect—complete in those finishing details that cost so much and make the whole effect tastefully finished, like a dish carried out with its final glaze and garnish.

I viewed her from head to foot. She turned airily round, that I might survey her on all sides. Her small blue eyes sparkled; she was in her best humour, conscious of her power. She was going to bestow on me a kiss—her schoolgirl fashion of sharing triumph—but I held her off.

“Steady!” I said. “Let us be steady, and know what we are about, and find out the meaning of our magnificence.”

“Shall I do?” was her question.

“Do? There are different ways of doing; and, upon my word, I don’t understand yours.”

“But how do I look?”

“You look well dressed.”

She found the praise too cool, and began to point out each ornament—each ribbon, each fold, each sparkle. “Look at this parure,” said she. “The brooch, the ear-rings, the bracelets: no one in the school has such a set—not Madame herself.”

“I see them all.” (Pause.) “Did M. de Bassompierre give you those jewels?”

“My uncle knows nothing about them.”

“Were they presents from Mrs. Cholmondeley?”

“Not they indeed. Mrs. Cholmondeley is a mean, stingy creature; she never gives me anything now.”

I did not choose to ask further; I turned abruptly away, as if a sudden harsh sound had jarred through the room and set my teeth on edge.

“Now, old Crusty—old Diogenes,”—these were her familiar names for me when we disagreed—“what is the matter now?”

“Take yourself away. I have no pleasure in looking at you or your parure.”

For an instant she looked surprised.

“What now, Mother Wisdom? I have not got into debt for it—that is, not for the jewels, nor the gloves, nor the bouquet. My dress is certainly not paid for, but uncle de Bassompierre will pay it in the bill: he never notices items, but just looks at the total; and he is so rich, one need not care about a few guineas more or less.”

“Will you go? I want to shut the door…. Ginevra, people may tell you you are very handsome in that ball-attire; but in my eyes you will never look so pretty as you did in the gingham gown and plain straw bonnet you wore when I first saw you.”

“Other people have not your puritanical tastes,” she retorted, angry. “And besides, I see no right you have to sermonize me.”

“Certainly! I have little right; and you, perhaps, have still less to come flourishing and fluttering into my chamber—a mere jay in borrowed plumes. I have not the least respect for your feathers, Miss Fanshawe; and especially for the peacock’s eyes you call a parure: pretty enough, if you had bought them with money that was your own, and which you could spare; not pretty at all under present circumstances.”

“On est là pour Mademoiselle Fanshawe!” called the portress; and away she tripped.

The semi-mystery of the parure was not solved till two or three days later, when she chose to confess.

“You need not be sulky with me,” she began, “in the idea that I am running somebody—papa or M. de Bassompierre—deeply into debt. I assure you nothing remains unpaid for but the few dresses I have lately had: all the rest is settled.”

There lay the riddle; for I knew her means were a few shillings, and I knew how carefully she hoarded even those.

“Écoutez!” she went on, drawing close, coaxing, for my disapproval inconvenienced her; she liked me to listen, even if I listened only to blame. “Écoutez, chère grogneuse! I will tell you all how and about it; and you will then see not only how right the whole thing is, but how cleverly managed. In the first place, I must go out. Papa himself said he wished me to see something of the world; he remarked to Mrs. Cholmondeley that though I was sweet enough, I had rather a bread-and-butter-eating, schoolgirl air, which he desired me to get rid of by an introduction to society here, before my regular début in England. Well then, if I go out, I must dress. Mrs. Cholmondeley is turned shabby and will give nothing more; it would be too hard upon uncle to make him pay for all the things I need: that you can’t deny—that agrees with your own preachments. Well, but somebody who heard me—quite by chance, I assure you—complaining to Mrs. Cholmondeley of my distressed circumstances, and what straits I was put to for an ornament or two—somebody, far from grudging a present, was quite delighted at the idea of being permitted to offer some trifle. You should have seen what a blanc-bec he looked when he first spoke of it: how he hesitated and blushed, and positively trembled for fear of a repulse.”

“That will do, Miss Fanshawe. I suppose I am to understand that M. Isidore is the benefactor: that it is from him you have accepted that costly parure; that he supplies your bouquets and your gloves?”

“You express yourself so disagreeably,” she complained, “one hardly knows how to answer. What I mean to say is, that I occasionally allow Isidore the pleasure and honour of expressing his homage by the offer of a trifle.”

“It comes to the same thing…. Now, Ginevra, to speak the plain truth, I don’t very well understand these matters; but I believe you are doing very wrong—seriously wrong. Perhaps, however, you now feel certain you will be able to marry M. Isidore; your parents and uncle have consented, and, for your part, you love him entirely?”

“Mais pas du tout!”—she always fled into French when about to be most heartless. “Je suis sa reine, mais il n’est pas mon roi.”

“Excuse me: I must believe this is nonsense and coquetry. There is nothing great about you; yet you are above profiting by the good nature and purse of a man to whom you feel indifference. You love M. Isidore far more than you think, or will avow.”

“No. I danced with a young officer the other night, whom I love a thousand times more than he. I often wonder why I feel so very cold to Isidore, for everybody says he is handsome, and other ladies admire him; but, somehow, he bores me: let me see now how it is….”

She tried, for once, to think; and I encouraged her, for if she could but taste her own motives clearly—name the flavour of them—she might perhaps loathe them.

“Yes,” I said, “try to get a clear idea of the state of your mind. To me it seems in a great mess—chaotic as a rag-bag.”

“It is something in this fashion,” she cried ere long: “the man is too romantic and devoted, and he expects something more of me than I find it convenient to be. He thinks I am perfect—furnished with sterling qualities and solid virtues such as I never had, nor intend to have. Now, one can’t help, in his presence, rather trying to justify his good opinion; and it does so tire one to be goody, and to talk sense—for he really thinks I am sensible. I am far more at my ease with you, old lady—you dear crosspatch—who take me at my lowest, and know me coquettish, and ignorant, and flirting, and fickle, and silly, and selfish, and all the other sweet things you and I have agreed to be part of my character.”

“This is all very well,” I said, striving to keep severity steady, though her whimsical candour shook it, “but it does not alter that wretched business of the presents. Pack them up, Ginevra, like a good honest girl, and send them back.”

“Indeed I won’t,” she answered stoutly.

“Then you are deceiving M. Isidore. It stands to reason that by accepting his presents you make him understand he will one day receive an equivalent—in your regard…”

“But he won’t,” she broke in. “He has his equivalent now, in the pleasure of seeing me wear them—quite enough for him: he is only bourgeois.”

That phrase—so arrogant, so empty—shut the last little gate of indulgence in me. She rattled on, untroubled; and while her voice ran, light and loud as an ill-played air, I heard behind it the sharper things the house was full of: the constant creak of doors opening and closing without kindness; the tap of shoes that came and went as silently as spies; the murmurs of borrowed phrases; the rustle of confessions made habitual till words lost their weight. In such a place even one’s own mother-tongue seemed sometimes to shrink and grow hoarse, as if it could not keep its true meaning alive amid so much practised gloss.

Madame Beck never startled you by inconsistency. She could be forbearing with everybody, and tender to nobody; and in her the two habits went hand in hand, as naturally as a well-swept floor goes with a locked pantry. Her own little ones did not soften her. She was vigilant for their health, provident for what she called their future, careful of their clothes, their lessons, their portion of air and exercise; but she seemed not to know the impulse which makes a woman draw a child to her bosom as one draws bread to the mouth when hunger bites.

I have seen her seated in the garden, where the gravel made a discreet, dry sound underfoot and the clipped leaves kept their own stillness, while, far down an alley, her “little bees”—the children—went and came under Trinette’s eye. In Madame’s posture there was always prudence—an attitude of mind more than of body. If the youngest, a puny, delicate darling, chanced to spy her, and, breaking away, came tottering along the path, laughing and panting with that breathy eagerness which is half joy, half distress, and would have flung itself at her knee, Madame only extended a hand—an even, intercepting hand—to avert what she would have called an inconvenient concussion.

“Prends garde, mon enfant!” she would say, unmoved.

She let it stand near her a moment, as one lets a warm dish cool before covering it; then, without smile, kiss, or fond word, she rose, took the small fingers, and led it back to Trinette.

To the eldest girl, Désirée, she bore herself in another fashion; yet the same trait—calm without candour—ruled it. That child was a plague. In kitchen and class-room alike one heard: “Quelle peste que cette Désirée! Quel poison que cet enfant-là!” and the epithet was justified. Désirée had the genius of provocation; she would creep to the servants’ garrets, lift lids and turn keys, tear their best caps with a quick, spiteful hand, soil shawls as neatly as if malice were a needle and she were skilled in hemming it. She watched for her chance at the dining-room buffet, where a glass would fall and shiver, or porcelain crack with a sharp little cry that made the ear wince; she haunted the storeroom cupboard, where jars stood ranked—preserves glowing in their own sugar, sweet wine sleeping in its bottle—and there she plundered and broke, and—most finished of all—she contrived that suspicion should drip, drop by drop, upon the cook and kitchen-maid.

When Madame saw, or when report reached her, her sole observation, uttered in matchless serenity, was, “Désirée a besoin d’une surveillance toute particulière.”

So she kept that promising olive-branch much at her side. Never once, I think, did she sit the child down and tell her plainly what she was—thief, liar, spoiler; never did she speak of the evil of the habit, the after-taste of such indulgence, the bitterness it leaves when youth is done. Surveillance was to do the whole work, as if watching were seasoning enough to change the nature of a dish.

It failed, of course. Shut out from the servants, Désirée pillaged her mother. Whatever lay on Madame’s work-table, whatever glittered at her toilet, the child’s fingers sought and conveyed. A brooch vanished; scissors went; a ring was missing. Madame saw—and pretended not to see. She had not the rectitude to confront vice in its small face and name it aloud. When the value of what was gone made restitution necessary, she would affect to suppose Désirée had taken it “in play,” and would beg her to restore it.

Désirée was too practised to be caught in so mild a net. She brought falsehood promptly to the aid of theft, and denied she had touched brooch, ring, or scissors. Then Madame, maintaining her hollow system with admirable composure, assumed belief—calm, bland belief—after which she watched and dogged the child with untiring patience, tracked her to a hiding-place—some hole in the garden-wall, some cranny in garret or out-house. When she had found the cache, she sent Désirée out walking with Trinette, and, profiting by the absence, robbed the robber.

Désirée, true daughter of an astute parent, never let cheek or eye betray mortification on discovering her loss. She would come back with the same composed face, as if she had mislaid nothing, as if it were all part of the game.

The second child, Fifine, they said resembled her dead father. Madame had given her healthy frame, blue eye, ruddy cheek; but not from Madame came the moral creature. Fifine was honest, gleeful, warm-tempered; she bustled like a little cook about a hot hearth—apt to knock things over, apt to burn her fingers, but meaning no harm. She had, too, a frank gourmandise: the most direct road to her heart lay through her palate.

One day that bustling instinct led her into calamity. She fell—top to bottom—down a steep flight of stone steps; the sound was of flesh and bone against hard edges, and it rang through the house in a way that made every ear flinch. Madame, who always heard every noise—heard them as some people hear a false note in music—issued from the dining-room, lifted the child, and said quietly, like one naming a fact on an inventory:

“Cet enfant a un os de cassé.”

At first we hoped she was wrong; we were not. One small, plump arm hung powerless, a helpless thing, as if it belonged to another body. Madame turned to me.

“Let Meess take her,” she said, in the children’s English. “Et qu’on aille tout de suite chercher un fiacre.”

In a fiacre she departed at once, cool, prompt, self-possessed, to fetch a surgeon. She did not find the family-doctor—Dr. Pillule—at home; but that did not embarrass her. She sought until she laid her hand on a substitute to her mind, and brought him back as decisively as one brings home a needed ingredient from a distant market when the pot is already on the fire.

Meanwhile I had cut the child’s sleeve from the injured arm—cloth yielding with that quick, tearing sound scissors make when they work under haste—and I undressed her and put her to bed. The little chamber was small and heated; it smelt of warm linen, of human breath and fear, of camphor from some old drawer, and—faintly—of the kitchen below where supper had been begun and abandoned. We were gathered there—the bonne, the cook, the portress, and I—yet none of us, I suppose, looked very closely at the new doctor when he entered. I, at least, had my whole soul taken up with Fifine’s cries. She had good lungs; her wail filled the room, broke against the ceiling, beat back upon us, and made the very air seem sharp.

Those cries redoubled when the stranger approached the bed. When he lifted her, she fought him with all the passionate indignation of pain.

“Let alone!” she screamed, in her broken English. “I will not you: I will Dr. Pillule!”

“And Dr. Pillule is my very good friend,” came the answer—in perfect English, clear and untroubled—“but he is busy at a place three leagues off, and I am come in his stead. So now, when we get a little calmer, we must commence business; and we will soon have that unlucky little arm bandaged and in right order.”

He called for a glass of eau sucrée. The spoon chimed softly against the glass; the sugar-water smelt thin and comforting, like childhood’s simplest bribe. He fed her a few teaspoonfuls, speaking with that easy mastery which quiets by taking for granted that quiet will come. Fifine, true little gourmand, swallowed between sobs; her rage softened, not through reason but through sweetness.

“More when it is over,” he promised; and then he went to work.

He needed help. He demanded it of the cook, a robust woman with strong arms—arms fit to knead heavy dough, to lift iron pots. But at the thought of touching that tortured little limb, courage deserted the stoutest. The cook fled; the portress fled; Trinette, too, gave way and vanished, leaving behind only the rustle of skirts and the shameful silence of retreat.

I did not like to touch the broken arm. I had that shrinking which is not cowardice but a sort of reverence for pain; yet there was no alternative. My hand was already extended—trembling—to do what was requisite, when I was anticipated. Madame Beck put out her own hand. Hers was steady while mine shook.

“Ça vaudra mieux,” said the doctor, turning from me to her.

He chose wisely. My firmness would have been forced; hers was neither forced nor feigned. She stood there, composed as a woman tasting a bitter draught without a change of face.

The doctor’s fingers were quick, skilful, decisive; he set, adjusted, bound. The bandage, when finished, lay neat and sure, as if his hands had a memory for order. When it was done, he looked up, satisfied.

“Merci, Madame; très bien, fort bien!” he said. “Voilà un sang-froid bien opportun, et qui vaut mille élans de sensibilité déplacée.”

He was pleased with her firmness; she was pleased with his praise. And indeed his whole appearance—voice, mien, manner—was such as to work in his favour with women accustomed to measure men by their bearing. When a lamp was brought in—for it was evening and the room had begun to darken—you saw him better. He was young. In that small chamber, among those Dutch-made women, he looked imposingly tall. His profile was clear, fine, expressive. His eye glanced from face to face a little too vividly, too quickly, too often; yet the character of it was pleasant, and so was his mouth. His chin was full, cleft, Grecian, perfect.

As to his smile, one could not at once decide what epithet suited it. It pleased—and yet it carried with it a power of summoning up, like a sudden sound in a quiet wood, all one’s weak points, all one’s little ridiculousness, all that might lay one open to a laugh. Still, Fifine liked that dubious smile and called its owner genial. He had hurt her; nevertheless, when he prepared to go, she stretched out her hand to bid him good-night, as if sweetness and skilful mastery had made him friend.

He patted her hand kindly. Then he and Madame went down-stairs together—she talking with an unwonted tide of spirits and volubility, he listening with good-natured amenity dashed with an unconscious roguish archness hard to define. I noticed he spoke French well, yet English better; he had, too, an English complexion, eyes, and form.

And I noticed more.

As he passed me in leaving the room, turning his face in my direction for a moment—not to address me, but to speak to Madame, yet so standing that I could not but look up—a recollection which had struggled in my mind since the first sound of his voice started up perfected. This was the very gentleman I had spoken to at the bureau; who had helped in the matter of my trunk; who had guided me through the dark wet park. When he went down the long vestibule and out into the street, I recognised even his tread—that firm, equal stride I had followed under dripping trees, while the night’s water pattered on leaves with a cold, incessant whisper.

It seemed likely that this young surgeon-physician’s first visit to the Rue Fossette would be his last. Dr. Pillule, respectable and established, was expected home next day; why should a substitute return? But Fate—if one must call by a large name the small concatenations by which lives are bent—had written otherwise.

Dr. Pillule was summoned to attend a rich old hypochondriac in the antique university town of Bouquin-Moisi. He prescribed change of air and travel, and, having prescribed, was retained to accompany the timid patient on a tour of some weeks. Thus it remained for the new doctor to continue his attendance in the Rue Fossette.

I often saw him when he came; for Madame would not trust the little invalid wholly to Trinette, and required me to spend much time in the nursery. He was, I think, skilful. Fifine recovered rapidly under his care; yet even convalescence did not hasten his dismissal. Destiny and Madame Beck seemed in league, and both had ruled that he should make deliberate acquaintance with the vestibule, the private staircase, the upper chambers.

No sooner did Fifine emerge from his hands than Désirée declared herself ill.

That possessed child had a genius for simulation, and, captivated by the indulgences of a sick-room—pillows like little thrones, sheets to build tents, broths and sweet draughts as tributes—she concluded an illness would suit her perfectly and took to bed. She acted well, and her mother better; for though the whole case was transparent to Madame Beck as day, she treated it with astonishing gravity and good faith. What surprised me was that Dr. John—for so the young Englishman had taught Fifine to call him, and we all, taking the name from her, fell into the habit until it became established, and he was known by no other in the Rue Fossette—what surprised me was that Dr. John consented, tacitly, to adopt Madame’s tactics and fall in with her manoeuvres.

At first he betrayed a moment of comic doubt; he cast one or two rapid glances from the child to the mother, paused as if consulting himself; then, with a good grace, resigned himself to play his part in the farce.

Désirée ate like a raven, and her appetite was a kind of insolence. She gambolled day and night in bed, pitched tents with sheets and blankets, lounged like a Turk among pillows and bolsters; she amused herself by throwing shoes at her bonne and grimacing at her sisters. In short, she overflowed with unmerited health and evil spirits; she only languished when mamma and the physician paid their daily visit.

Madame Beck, I knew, was glad—at any price—to have her daughter in bed and out of mischief; but I wondered that Dr. John did not tire. Every day, on that mere pretext, he came punctually. Madame received him always with the same empressement, the same sunshine for himself, the same admirably counterfeited concern for her child. Dr. John wrote harmless prescriptions for the patient—tisanes that smelled of steeped herbs, innocuous powders, a regimen as empty of danger as it was full of ceremony—and he viewed the mother with a shrewdly sparkling eye.

Madame caught his rallying looks without resenting them; she had too much good sense for that. Supple as the young doctor seemed, one could not despise him. This pliancy was not adopted to curry favour: while he liked his office at the pensionnat and lingered strangely about the Rue Fossette, he carried himself there with independence—almost carelessness; and yet he was often thoughtful and preoccupied, as if the house held for him not merely patients and politenesses, but some question his mind kept chewing like a hard crust.

It was not my business to observe the mystery of his bearing, or to search out its origin or aim; but placed as I was, I could hardly help it. He laid himself open to my observation, because in my presence he accorded me precisely that degree of notice a person of my exterior habitually expects: about what is given to unobtrusive articles of furniture—chairs of common joiner’s work, carpets of no striking pattern. Often, while waiting for Madame, he would muse, smile, watch, or listen like a man who thinks himself alone. The house, at such times, had its own sound-world: the far-off clink of a saucepan neglected on the kitchen range; the faint scrape of a chair-leg in the schoolroom; the hush and flutter of a child’s restless turning among sheets; in the garden below, the thin, tired chirp of a sparrow persevering against the city’s muffled roar. I, meantime, was free to puzzle over his face and movements, to wonder what peculiar interest and attachment—mixed with doubt, strangeness, and ruled by some presiding spell—wedded him to this demi-convent buried in the built-up core of a capital, where voices were trained into one dominant tongue and small native tones were daily rubbed away like names from an old spoon.

He, I believe, never remembered that I had eyes in my head, much less a brain behind them.

Nor would he ever have found it out, but that one day, while he sat in the sunshine and I was observing the colouring of his hair, whiskers, complexion—the whole being of such a tone as a strong light brings out with perilous force (I recollect comparing his beamy head, in thought, to that “golden image” which Nebuchadnezzar set up)—an idea, new, sudden, startling, riveted me with over-mastering strength. It was not admiration that fixed me; it was recognition sharpened into certainty, and something beyond recognition—some sense of what names do, what they hide, what they proclaim. Here, in this house where we called him “Dr. John” because a child’s tongue had christened him so, was a man whose very appellation seemed a translation, a dominance—English set upon French soil; and I felt, with a quick inward recoil, how easy it is for the strong language to take the place of the weak, for one sound to drown another, as carriage-wheels drown birdsong in a lane.

I know not to this day how I looked at him. Surprise and conviction made me forget myself. I only recovered my usual consciousness when I saw his notice arrested—when I perceived that he had caught my movement in a clear little oval mirror fixed in the side of the window recess, by aid of which reflector Madame often secretly spied persons walking in the garden below.

Though of so gay and sanguine a temperament, he was not without a nervous sensitiveness that made him ill at ease under a direct, inquiring gaze. On surprising me thus, he turned and spoke in a tone courteous, yet with just so much dryness as marked annoyance and gave what he said the character of rebuke.

“Mademoiselle does not spare me,” he observed. “I am not vain enough to fancy that it is my merits which attract her attention; it must then be some defect. Dare I ask—what?”

I was confounded; yet not beyond recovery. I knew that neither incautious admiration nor unjustifiable inquisitiveness had drawn my look. I might have cleared myself at once, but I would not. I did not speak. I was not in the habit of speaking to him. So I let him think what he pleased and accuse me as he would. I resumed some work I had dropped, and kept my head bent over it during the remainder of his stay, while his voice, and Madame’s, moved on above me—two currents in the same room, one dominant, one yielding, both careful, both practised.

There is a perverse mood of mind which is rather soothed than irritated by misconstruction; and in quarters where we can never be rightly known, we take, I think, a strange pleasure in being consummately ignored. What honest man, casually taken for a housebreaker, does not feel rather tickled than vexed at the mistake?

Summer settled over the house like a hot lid, holding in every breath and every sound until even the stair-boards seemed to creak with thirst. In the kitchen below, the great pots sang dully, lids rattling with a weak impatience; on the landing above, the nursery smelt of warmed linen, vinegar water, and that particular sour-sweetness of fevered skin which no rose-water will wholly conquer. The town outside was full of carriage-grind and hammer-stroke and the thin whistle of commerce; but within these walls the day had its own acoustic weather—an unceasing ripple of girls’ voices, the struck glass of a triangle in music-class, the perpetual practising of scales like knives being sharpened, and, under all, the faint fretful piping of a little child who could not be comforted.

Georgette, the youngest, took a fever—one of those sudden, sly fevers that come with heat and crowded rooms and too many breaths shared. Désirée, who had lately complained with all her might and then, as if by caprice, recovered in an hour, was instantly whisked away with Fifine to Bonne-Maman in the country, “par précaution,” Madame said; and there, no doubt, they would drink cool milk and eat thick bread still warm from the oven, while here we measured out spoonfuls of barley-water and listened for the change in a child’s breathing.

With Georgette ill, the need of medical aid became real and immediate. Dr. Pillule had returned—he had been home a week, available and eager for his post as if nothing had interrupted it—yet Madame Beck, who could ignore what she did not choose to see, summoned his English rival to keep coming. Dr. John, with his step that always sounded brisk without being noisy, his voice clear as poured water, was called again and again.

A couple of the pensionnaires began to complain of headache; a few more grew pale, pressed fingers to temples, made languid little moans which, in a cooler season, might have been mere imitation but now seemed the first faint participation in Georgette’s ailment. I thought: Now, at last, she must recall Pillule. Prudence will forbid her to expose her pupils to a young man’s attendance; in this country of convent walls and confessionals, a handsome doctor crossing daily into the school-division is a spark near powder.

Madame was prudent; but Madame could also be intrepidly venturous. She brought Dr. John not only to her own children but among the older girls; she installed him, with an air of decision, beside Blanche de Melcy—proud, beautiful, conscious of both—and Angélique, her friend, vain and flirting as a ribbon in a breeze. Dr. John seemed gratified by this confidence; and if discretion of bearing could have made the step safe, his would have done it: he moved with a tact that appeared almost modest.

Yet a “pensionnat de demoiselles” is a place where a whisper travels faster than a bell. The school gossiped, the kitchen whispered, and the town—always hungry—caught the rumour as a dog catches scent. Letters came from parents; visits too, stiff with remonstrance and scented gloves. Rival educational houses waited, eyes bright, ready to turn Madame’s false step—if false it was—into her ruin.

Had she been weak, she would have fallen. She was not weak. I watched her meet those offended mothers and fathers with a good-humoured ease, a round frankness of “bonne femme,” so natural—or so perfectly assumed—that it disarmed where solemn reasoning would have sharpened resistance. She would clasp her fat little white hands, chuckling as if amused at the world’s anxieties.

“Ce pauvre Docteur Jean! ce cher jeune homme! le meilleur créature du monde!”

Then she would explain—always explaining as if it were the simplest domestic matter—how she employed him for her own children; how they were so fond of him they would scream themselves into fits at the thought of another doctor; how, where she trusted him for her own, it was only natural to trust him for others. And besides, she insisted, it was temporary, a mere expedient. Blanche and Angélique had the migraine: Dr. John had written a prescription—voilà tout.

The parents’ mouths closed, as if she had pressed her palm lightly over them. Blanche and Angélique saved her the rest by loudly chanting duets in their physician’s praise; the other pupils echoed, declaring with one voice that if they were ill they would have Dr. John and nobody else. Madame laughed; and the parents, indulgent as this nation is of its offspring—making the children’s will a law—laughed too. Madame even gained credit for motherly partiality. She came off with flying colours; she was liked better than ever.

To this day, the reason she risked her interest for Dr. John remained, to me, not wholly explained. The house, from pupil to servant, decided upon an explanation with the certainty of a verdict: she was going to marry him. They settled it as if it were an item on the timetable. Difference of age—some fourteen years—seemed to them no obstacle. It was to be so.

Appearances did not wholly discountenance the idea. Madame seemed bent on keeping his services, oblivious of Pillule. She made a point of receiving Dr. John herself, and never failed to be cheerful, blithe, benignant with him. About this time she gave marked attention to dress; the morning dishabille, the nightcap and shawl vanished. His early visits found her with auburn braids neatly arranged, silk dress trimly fitted, laced brodequins in place of slippers: toilette complete, fresh as a flower set out before guests. I doubted her intention went further than to show a very handsome man she was not quite plain; and plain she was not. Without beauty of feature or elegance of form, she pleased; without youth’s graces, she cheered. Her unfaded hair, her tempered blue eye, her cheek with its wholesome fruit-like bloom—these pleased in moderation, but with constancy. She was never colourless.

Had she, indeed, some floating vision of adopting Dr. John as husband—taking him to her well-furnished home, endowing him with savings said to amount to a moderate competency, and making him comfortable for the rest of his life? And did he suspect such visions? More than once I met him coming out from her presence with a half-mischievous smile and the look, in his eyes, of a vanity tickled. With all his good nature and good looks, he was not perfect; it would have been very imperfect to roguishly encourage aims he never meant to fulfil. Yet did he never mean them? People said he had no money; that he depended wholly on his profession. Madame—though older—was the kind of woman who would never wholly wither, never break down. They were certainly on good terms. He perhaps was not in love; but how many marry for love in this world? We waited the end.

What he waited for, what he watched, I could not tell. There was in his manner a peculiarity—an expectant vigilance, an absorbed eager look—that never wore off; it intensified, as if he were listening for some particular note amidst all the daily noise. He had never been quite within my compass, and he seemed to range farther beyond it.

One morning Georgette had been more feverish and therefore more peevish. Her cry had turned thin and persistent, a sound like a small bird beating itself against glass.

“Minnie, Minnie, me very poorly!”

A draught had been ordered—some mixture bitter with drops I did not know; I suspected it disagreed with her. I longed to consult the doctor before giving more. The bell rang; he was admitted. I heard his voice addressing the portress—clear, English, making a small island of another tongue in the French tide. It was his custom to mount straight to the nursery, taking three steps at once, arriving like a cheerful surprise. Five minutes passed—ten—and still he did not come.

Georgette’s cry went on; it worked upon the nerves like a repeated tap. I went down to ascertain why he lingered. The corridor was empty. Whither had he vanished?

Madame could not be with him in the salle-à-manger; I had left her moments before, dressing in her chamber. I paused and listened, but in that house listening was like trying to catch a single voice in a market. Three pupils were practising at three proximate instruments—the dining-room and the greater and lesser drawing-rooms; between these salons and the corridor lay the portress’s cabinet, a little room communicating with them, once meant for a boudoir. Farther off, at a fourth instrument in the oratory, a whole class were having a singing lesson; they were joining in what they called a “barcarole,” its syllables rocking—“fraîchë,” “brisë,” “Venisë”—as if trying to make Venice with their mouths.

Under such a canopy of sound, what could I hear? Yet—yes—through it, near and distinct, a giddy treble laugh came from the little cabinet, close by whose half-unclosed door I stood. A man’s voice answered, soft and deep, pleading. I caught only the adjuration, strained and sudden:

“For God’s sake!”

Then a pause, as brief as the space between spoon and lips; and Dr. John came out.

His eyes shone, but not with joy or triumph; his fair English cheek was high-coloured. Something had seized him and shaken him—some baffled vexation, anxious and tender at once. His brow bore not wounded pride so much as wounded affection—cruelly wounded, it seemed. The half-open door screened me; had I stood full in his way, I believe he would have passed without seeing. He moved as if the house had narrowed to a single thought.

Who had power to torture him thus within these walls? Madame I believed to be in her chamber; the cabinet belonged to the portress alone—Rosine Matou, pretty, unprincipled, a little French grisette airy and fickle, dressy, vain, and mercenary. It could not be—surely not—her hand that had wrung such a look from him.

While I pondered, Rosine’s voice, clear and a little sharp, broke out in a lightsome French song, trilling through the still-ajar door. I glanced in, doubting my senses. She sat at the table in a smart dress of jaconas rose, trimming a tiny blond cap—picking up lace and thread as calmly as if she had been basting a cuff for a child, not pricking a heart. In the room there was nothing alive but herself, except some goldfish in a glass globe and a few flowers in pots, and a broad July sunbeam lying across the boards like spilled wheat.

Here was a problem; but Georgette’s medicine would not wait upon my confusion. I went up.

Dr. John sat in a chair at the child’s bedside; Madame stood before him. The little patient had been examined and soothed; now she lay composed in her crib, her damp curls clinging to her forehead, her small mouth at last slack with relief. The nursery air was thick with camomile and warmed milk, and the faint metallic tang of a spoon rinsed in haste.

As I entered, Madame Beck was discussing the physician’s own health. She remarked on some change—real or fancied—in his looks; she charged him with over-work and recommended rest and change of air, as if prescribing for him were a privilege of her household. He listened good-naturedly, with laughing indifference; he told her she was “trop bonne” and that he felt perfectly well.

Then she appealed to me; and Dr. John followed her movement with a slow glance, languidly surprised that reference should be made to a quarter he deemed insignificant.

“What do you think, Miss Lucie? Is he not paler and thinner?”

It was rare I uttered more than monosyllables in his presence; he was the kind of person with whom I was likely always to remain the neutral, passive thing he thought me. Yet now I took licence to answer in a phrase—and I made it deliberately significant.

“He looks ill at this moment; but perhaps it is owing to some temporary cause: Dr. John may have been vexed or harassed.”

I did not look up to read his face. Georgette, in her broken English, asked me if she might have a glass of eau sucrée. I answered her in English; and for the first time, I think, he noticed that I spoke his language. Hitherto he had always taken me for a foreigner, addressing me as “Mademoiselle” and giving his directions in French. He seemed on the point of remarking upon it, but checked himself and held his tongue—as if words, in any tongue, were suddenly too blunt a tool.

Madame recommenced advising him. He shook his head, laughing; rose; bade her good-morning with courtesy, yet with the careless air of one surfeited by too much unsolicited attention, as if kindness had become cloying, like sugared water given when the stomach wants plain broth.

When he was gone, Madame sank into the chair he had just left and rested her chin on her hand. All animation, all amiability vanished. Her face turned stony, stern—almost mortified and morose. She sighed once: a single deep sigh, as if something within had dropped its weight.

A loud bell rang for morning-school, its clang cutting cleanly through the softer sounds, calling bodies and thoughts back into order. Madame rose. Passing a dressing-table with a glass, she looked at her reflection. One single white hair streaked her nut-brown tresses; she plucked it out with a shudder. In the full summer light, though her face still held colour, it had lost the texture of youth; and youth’s contours—where were they? She turned darkly away from the glass.

Never had I pitied Madame before; yet then my heart softened. A calamity had come upon her. Disappointment—that hag who greets with a grisly “All-hail”—stood at her shoulder, and her soul recoiled from intimacy even as it felt the cold touch.

But Rosine.

My bewilderment at Rosine surpassed description. That day I embraced five opportunities of passing the portress’s cabinet, with a view to contemplating her charms and finding out the secret of their influence. Pretty—yes; young—yes; well-made dress—certainly: the jaconas rose sat upon her like a confectioner’s icing, smooth and shining. Her room always carried a faint odour of cheap perfume mingled with starch and the warm dusty sweetness of ribbon-boxes. All good points; amply sufficient, perhaps, in any philosophic mind, to account for agony and distraction in a young man like Dr. John.

Still, I could not help forming half a wish that the doctor were my brother—or at least that he had a sister or a mother who would kindly sermonize him, who would speak to him in the old homely language that holds a man fast, the dialect of hearth and family, not the polished phrases that float and flatter. I say half a wish; I broke it and flung it away before it grew whole, discovering in time its exquisite folly.

“Somebody,” I argued, “might as well sermonize Madame about her young physician: and what good would that do?”

Perhaps Madame sermonized herself. She did not behave weakly; she did not make herself ridiculous. True, she had neither strong feelings to overcome nor tender feelings by which to be miserably pained—so, at least, one might have said before that deep sigh. True also, she had an important avocation, a real business, to fill her time, divert her thoughts, divide her interest; the running of the house went on with its measured noises—bells, footsteps, lessons, the clink of plates below where meals must still be served though hearts were hungry in other ways. And especially she possessed that genuine good sense not given to all women nor all men. By dint of these combined advantages she behaved wisely—she behaved well.

Brava! once more, Madame Beck. I saw you matched against an Apollyon of predilection; you fought a good fight, and you overcame.

Behind the house in the Rue Fossette there was a garden—no countryside’s sweep, no honest horizon of fields, but still a breadth of green startling in the city’s stone heart. Memory, which sweetens what it cannot restore, insists it was always pleasant; yet even then, with blank walls all round and hot pavement close by, a single shrub seemed a mercy, and an enclosed plot of earth—planted, watered, kept—felt like a living room for the soul.

There was a story fastened to it, as ivy fastens to grey masonry. People said Madame Beck’s house had once been a convent; and that in the centuries when this quarter was not yet overbuilt—when it was more avenue than alley, more tilled ground than flagstone—something had happened here that left fear as an inheritance. A black-and-white nun, so ran the vague tradition, showed herself at certain seasons, in certain nights, in some part of the neighbourhood. The city had long since walled the ghost in with houses, yet convent-relics remained: enormous fruit-trees, old as a superstition, their roots lifting the soil as if remembering a different world.

At the foot of one—a Methuselah pear-tree, dead in most of its limbs yet faithful in a few that still each spring burst into perfumed snow, and each autumn hung honeyed pendants—there was, if you scraped away the mossy earth between half-bared roots, a glimpse of slab: smooth, hard, black as sealed ink. People whispered it was a vault-door; that below, where grass now grew and flowers bloomed, a girl had been buried alive by a monkish conclave for some sin against her vow. Her bones were dust, but her shadow, they said, had outlasted generations; and moonlight, and wind in leaves, mocked timid eyes with a black robe and a white veil fluctuating through the thicket.

Romantic rubbish, perhaps; yet the garden had honest charms besides fear. On summer mornings I rose early to enjoy them alone; on summer evenings I lingered solitary, keeping tryst with the rising moon, taking one kiss of the cooling breeze, tasting—more in fancy than in fact—the freshness of dew descending. The turf lay vivid; the gravel walks were white as sifted flour. Nasturtiums, sun-bright and pepper-scented, clustered about the roots of doddered orchard giants; and when you brushed their leaves, the air gave back a green, sharp note, as if the plant itself had a tongue.

There was a large berceau shaded by an acacia; when its blossoms came, they shed sweetness like warmed honey, and bees made a soft industry beneath. There was a smaller bower, more hidden, nestled in the vines that ran along a high grey wall. Their tendrils knotted together, their clusters hung in loving profusion where jasmine and ivy met and married them—jasmine breathing out its slow, thick perfume, ivy smelling of shade and stone and persistence.

At high noon, when Madame Beck’s school turned out rampant—externes and pensionnaires abroad together, vying with the neighbouring boys’ college in brazen exercise of lungs and limbs—the garden was trodden and trite enough. The very air shook with it: shrill laughter, stamping feet, the scrape of shoes on gravel, the harsh clap of hands, noise that rose and fell like a market. But at sunset, or at the hour of salut, when the externes were gone home and the boarders were quiet at their studies, then the garden drew its breath again. Then you could hear, not the girls, but the bells of St. Jean Baptiste—sweet, soft, exalted—sending their sound through the streets like a benediction that asked nothing back.

One evening I walked there thus, detained farther within twilight than usual by the still-deepening calm. The day’s heat had retreated; now the flowers, which no sunshine could coax into such confession, answered the dew’s persuasion. Their breathing came up at me from the beds: earth-damp, bruised leaf, a last warm spice from the nasturtiums, the faint milky odour of vine sap. In the oratory window a light shone; I knew the Catholic household were gathered for evening prayer—a rite from which, as a Protestant, I now and then exempted myself. Solitude and the summer moon whispered, “One moment longer: stay with us. All is truly quiet now; for another quarter of an hour your presence will not be missed.”

One side of the garden was skirted by the rear of a long line of premises belonging to the neighbouring college—blank stone, high wall, with only a few attic loopholes and one lower casement said to mark a master’s chamber or study. Along that wall ran a narrow alley forbidden to the pupils: “l’allée défendue.” Any girl setting foot there would have earned as severe a penalty as Madame Beck’s mild rules permitted. Teachers might go there; but the shrubs grew thick and close, weaving overhead a roof of branch and leaf that let the sun through only in rare chequers. In daylight it was seldom entered; after dusk it was shunned as if it had teeth.

From the first, the very gloom tempted me. For a long time fear of seeming singular kept me away; but by degrees, as people grew accustomed to my habits and to such quiet shades of peculiarity as were engrained in my nature—born in and with me, no more to be parted with than my identity—I became a frequenter of that strait path.

I made myself gardener there. Between the closely ranked shrubs some tintless flowers struggled—pale things, as if they had forgotten their own names. I cleared away the relics of past autumns that choked a rustic seat at the far end. Borrowing from Goton, the cuisinière, a pail of water and a scrubbing-brush, I set to work. The water smelled of iron from the pump; the brush, wet and rough, rasped the wood till the mould yielded, and the damp rot’s sourness rose into the leaf-shadowed air. Madame saw me and smiled approbation—whether sincere or not I cannot tell; but she seemed sincere.

“Voyez-vous,” cried she, “comme elle est propre, cette demoiselle Lucie? Vous aimez donc cette allée, meess?”

“Yes,” I said; “it is quiet and shady.”

“C’est juste,” returned she with an air of bonté; and she recommended me to confine myself to it as much as I chose, adding that as I was not charged with surveillance I need not trouble myself to walk with the pupils—only I might permit her children to come there to talk English with me.

On the night in question I sat on the hidden seat I had reclaimed from fungi and mould, listening to what seemed the far-off sounds of the city. Far off they were not; the school stood in the centre. Five minutes’ walk would have brought you to the park; scarce ten to palatial buildings. Quite near, wide streets blazed with gaslight and life; carriages rolled there to balls or the opera. The same hour that tolled curfew for our convent—extinguishing each lamp, dropping the curtain round each couch—rang for the gay city the summons to festal enjoyment.

I did not think much of that contrast. Gay instincts my nature had few. Ball or opera I had never seen; when I wished to see them, it was not as one who hopes to partake, but as one who would look on a new thing from outside, tasting it with the eyes, not the blood.

A crescent moon hung in the sky—not full, but young. Through a space in the boughs overhead I saw her, and a few stars beside. They were no strangers where all else was strange: my childhood knew them. I had seen that golden sign, the dark globe in its curve, leaning back on azure beside an old thorn at the top of an English field in long past days, just as it now leaned back beside a stately spire in this continental capital.

When I thought of those days, passive as I lived, little as I spoke, cold as I looked, I could feel. About the present it was better to be stoical; about the future—such a future as mine—to be dead. In catalepsy and a dead trance I held the quick of my nature down, as one holds down the lid of a pot that would boil over.

Whatever could excite—certain accidents of the weather, for instance—I almost dreaded, because they woke the being I was always lulling, and stirred up a craving cry I could not satisfy. Once a thunder-storm broke; a hurricane shook us in our beds. The Catholics rose in panic and prayed loud to their saints. For me, the tempest took hold with tyranny. I dressed and crept out upon the casement ledge near my bed, feet set on the roof of a lower adjoining building. It was wet; the slates were slick under my shoes; the night was pitch-dark. Within the dormitory they huddled round the night-lamp, prayers running together in fright. I could not go in. Too resistless was the delight of staying with the wild hour, black and full of thunder, pealing out an ode language never delivered to man—too terribly glorious the clouds split and pierced by blinding bolts, and then the sudden thick hush in which you heard rain begin, like a thousand soft fingers on leaf and glass and stone.

After that storm I longed, achingly, for something to fetch me out of my present existence and lead me upwards and onwards. Such longings, and all of a similar kind, it was necessary to knock on the head; which I did figuratively, after the manner of Jael to Sisera, driving a nail through their temples. Unlike Sisera they did not die; they were but stunned, and at intervals would turn on the nail with a rebellious wrench; then did the temples bleed and the brain thrill to its core.

To-night I was not so mutinous. My Sisera lay quiet in the tent, slumbering; and if his pain ached through those slumbers, something like an angel—the ideal—knelt near, dropping balm on soothed temples, holding before sealed eyes a magic glass of sweet solemn visions repeated in dreams. Jael, the stern woman, sat apart, relenting somewhat over her captive, yet more prone to dwell on the faithful expectation of Heber coming home. By which I mean: the cool peace and dewy sweetness of the night filled me with a mood of hope—not hope on any definite point, but a general sense of encouragement and heart-ease.

Should not such a mood, so tranquil and unwonted, have been the harbinger of good? Alas, no good came of it.

Presently the rude Real burst in—coarse, repellent as she too often is. In the intense stillness, with the high wall and the thick shrubs hemming my narrow walk, I heard a sound: a casement creaked. Before I could look up and mark in which story, or by whose hand, a tree overhead shook, as if struck by a missile; something dropped prone at my feet.

Nine was striking by St. Jean Baptiste’s clock. Day was fading, but it was not dark. The crescent moon gave little light; yet the last gilding where the sun had beamed, and the crystalline clearness of the sky, sustained the summer twilight. Even in that dark walk I could, by stepping into an opening, have read small print.

I stooped. It was a box—a small casket of white and coloured ivory, cool under my fingers, polished smooth as a well-used spoon-handle. Its loose lid opened in my hand. Violets lay within—violets so fresh their scent sprang up at once, that deep, bruised sweetness which is half flower, half earth, half memory. They smothered a closely folded bit of pink paper, a note superscribed, “Pour la robe grise.”

I wore indeed a dress of French grey.

Good. Was this a billet-doux? A thing I had heard of, but never handled. Was it this sort of commodity I held between finger and thumb? Scarcely: I did not dream it for a moment. Suitor or admirer my very thoughts had not conceived. Other teachers had their romances; pupils over fourteen spoke with certainty of future bridegrooms as if of a harvest already sown. Two or three were affianced by their parents and had been so since childhood. Into that realm my speculations had never once had warrant to intrude.

If the other teachers went into town, or took a walk on the boulevards, or only attended mass, they were very sure to meet some “individual of the opposite sex” whose rapt gaze assured them of their power to strike and attract. I cannot say my experience tallied. I went to church; I took walks; I am convinced nobody minded me. The whole Rue Fossette could testify to having received an admiring beam from our young doctor’s blue eyes at one time or other; I am obliged, however humbling, to except myself. As far as I was concerned those blue eyes were guiltless, calm as the sky whose tint they shared.

So I heard the others, wondered at their gaiety and security, but did not trouble myself to look up and gaze along the path they were so certain of treading.

This then was no billet-doux; and in settled conviction to the contrary I quietly opened it. It ran thus—I translate:

“Angel of my dreams! A thousand, thousand thanks for the promise kept: scarcely did I venture to hope its fulfilment. I believed you indeed to be half in jest; and then you seemed to think the enterprise beset with such danger—the hour so untimely, the alley so strictly secluded—often, you said, haunted by that dragon, the English teacher—une véritable bégueule Britannique à ce que vous dites—espèce de monstre, brusque et rude comme un vieux caporal de grenadiers, et revêche comme une religieuse—”

(The reader will excuse my modesty in letting this flattering sketch of my amiable self retain the slight veil of the original tongue.)

“You are aware,” it went on, “that little Gustave, on account of his illness, has been removed to a master’s chamber—that favoured chamber whose lattice overlooks your prison-ground. There, I, the best uncle in the world, am admitted to visit him. How tremblingly I approached the window and glanced into your Eden—an Eden for me, though a desert for you!—how I feared to behold vacancy, or the dragon aforesaid! How my heart palpitated with delight when, through apertures in the envious boughs, I at once caught the gleam of your graceful straw-hat, and the waving of your grey dress—dress that I should recognise amongst a thousand. But why, my angel, will you not look up? Cruel, to deny me one ray of those adorable eyes!—how a single glance would have revived me! I write this in fiery haste; while the physician examines Gustave, I snatch an opportunity to enclose it in a small casket, together with a bouquet of flowers, the sweetest that blow—yet less sweet than thee, my Peri—my all-charming! ever thine—thou well knowest whom!”

“I wish I did know whom,” was my inward comment; and the wish bore even closer reference to the person addressed than to the writer. Perhaps it was meant for one of the engaged pupils; perhaps for some pretty pensionnaire with a vow of secrecy and a brother or cousin in the college next door. In that case there was no great harm intended—only an irregularity.

Yet “la robe grise, le chapeau de paille”—here was a clue, and a confusing one. The straw hat was a common garden screen worn by a score besides myself. Grey dresses were then in vogue; Madame Beck herself wore grey just now; another teacher and three of the pensionnaires had grey of the same shade and fabric as mine.

While I pondered, I knew I must go in. Lights moving in the dormitory announced that prayers were over and the pupils going to bed. Another half-hour and all doors would be locked, all lamps put out. The front door yet stood open, inviting the coolness of the summer night into the heated house. From the portress’s cabinet shone a lamp, showing the long vestibule and the street-door closing the vista.

All at once the bell rang—quick, not loud: a cautious tinkle, a warning metal whisper. Rosine darted from her cabinet to open. The person she admitted stood two minutes in parley; there was demur, delay. Rosine came to the garden door, lamp in hand; she stood on the steps lifting her light and looking round vaguely.

“Quel conte!” she cried, with a coquettish laugh. “Personne n’y a été.”

“Let me pass,” pleaded a voice I knew; “I ask but five minutes;” and a familiar shape, tall and grand—as we of the Rue Fossette all thought it—issued from the house and strode down among beds and walks.

It was sacrilege: a man in that place at that hour. But he knew himself privileged, and perhaps trusted to the friendly night. He wandered the alleys, looking right and left, trampling flowers, breaking branches in his haste. The sound of his steps—however careful—changed the whole acoustic of the garden: the quiet that had held the bell-notes tenderly now flinched; leaves shook; a snapped twig cried out; somewhere a bird stirred and made a sleepy protest. He penetrated at last the forbidden walk.

There I met him.

“Dr. John! it is found.”

He did not ask by whom; his quick eye saw the casket and the note in my hand. “Do not betray her,” he said, looking at me as if I were indeed the dragon described.

“Were I ever so disposed to treachery, I cannot betray what I do not know,” I answered. “Read the note, and you will see how little it reveals.”

Perhaps you have read it already, I thought; and yet I could not believe he wrote it. That style could hardly be his. Besides, I was fool enough to feel there would be hardship in his calling me such names. His look vindicated him: he grew hot, and coloured as he read.

“This is indeed too much: this is cruel, this is humiliating,” fell from him.

I thought it cruel too, seeing his countenance so moved. No matter whether he was to blame or not, somebody must be more to blame; and in my heart there stirred, not triumph, but a reluctant pity, like the way one pities a singer who has been made ridiculous by a cheap refrain.

“What shall you do about it?” he asked me. “Shall you tell Madame Beck what you have found, and cause a stir—an esclandre?”

I thought I ought to tell, and said so; adding that I did not believe there would be stir or esclandre. Madame was far too prudent to make a noise over an affair of that sort connected with her establishment.

He stood looking down, meditating. He was too proud and too honourable to entreat my silence where duty evidently commanded speech. I wished to do right, yet loathed to grieve or injure him.

Just then Rosine glanced out through the open door. She could not see us, but between the trees I could plainly see her; her dress was grey, like mine.

That circumstance, taken with certain previous transactions, suggested to me that perhaps the case—however deplorable—was one in which I was under no obligation to involve myself.

Accordingly I said, “If you can assure me that none of Madame Beck’s pupils are implicated in this business, I shall be very happy to stand aloof from all interference. Take the casket, the bouquet, and the billet; for my part, I gladly forget the whole affair.”

“Look there!” he whispered suddenly, as his hand closed on what I offered; and at the same time he pointed through the boughs.

I looked.

Behold Madame—shawl, wrapping-gown, slippers—softly descending the steps and stealing, cat-like, round the garden. In two minutes she would have been upon Dr. John.

If she moved like a cat, he, quite as much, resembled a leopard: nothing could be lighter than his tread when he chose. He watched; and as she turned a corner, he took the garden in two noiseless bounds. A leaf scarcely stirred; yet the air felt altered, as if the night itself had held its breath. She reappeared—and he was gone.

Rosine helped him, instantly interposing the door between him and his huntress.

I too might have got away; but I preferred to meet Madame openly. Though it was my frequent custom to spend twilight in the garden, never till now had I remained so late. I was sure she had missed me, had come in search of me, meant to pounce on the defaulter unawares. I expected a reprimand.

No.

Madame was all goodness. She offered not even a remonstrance; she testified no surprise. With that consummate tact—never surpassed, I think, by any living thing—she even professed merely to have issued forth to taste “la brise du soir.”

“Quelle belle nuit!” cried she, looking up at the stars—the moon was now gone down behind the broad tower of Jean Baptiste. “Qu’il fait bon! que l’air est frais!”

And instead of sending me in, she detained me to take a few turns with her down the principal alley. The gravel sounded softly under our steps; a distant carriage rolled somewhere beyond the walls with a dull, rich rumble; nearer, the leaves whispered and then were still again, as if listening too. At last we re-entered. She leaned affably on my shoulder by way of support in mounting the front-door steps; at parting her cheek was presented to my lips, and “Bon soir, my bonne amie; dormez bien!” was her kindly adieu.

I caught myself smiling as I lay awake and thoughtful on my couch—smiling at Madame. The unction, the suavity of her behaviour offered, for one who knew her, a sure token that suspicion of some kind was busy in her brain. From some aperture of observation—through parted bough, open window—she had doubtless caught a glimpse, remote or near, deceptive or instructive, of the night’s transactions.

Finely accomplished as she was in the art of surveillance, it was next to impossible that a casket could be thrown into her garden, or an interloper cross her walks to seek it, without her catching intimation—by shaken branch, passing shade, unwonted footfall, or stilly murmur. And though Dr. John had spoken low, the hum of his man’s voice seemed, to my sense, to pervade the whole conventual ground, altering the night’s soundscape as surely as a bell alters air.

What things were transpiring, she might not yet know; but a delicious little ravelled plot lay tempting her to disentanglement; and in the midst, folded round and round in cobwebs, had she not secured “Meess Lucie” clumsily involved—like the foolish fly she was?

Within four-and-twenty hours of that little garden business, Madame made me smile again—nay, laugh, and afterwards taste a bitterness as sharp as vinegar on the tongue.

Villette can turn in a night, as a kitchen turns when a draught catches the flame. After a mild sunset came a night of loud wind; the next day kept the same temper—dry storm, no rain, a sky like lead, and the streets blurred with sand and powdered grit whirled off the boulevards. Even had the weather been soft as warmed milk, it would not have tempted me back to my usual evening nook amongst the shrubs. My alley, and all the walks, had taken on a new interest—not pleasant, but watchful. That casement which had rained billets had vulgarised the once dear quiet it overlooked; the flowers seemed to have learned eyes; the knots in the tree-boles had grown ears.

Some plants Dr. John had trodden down in his hurried search. I could not bear to leave them so. At dawn, before common eyes were abroad, I propped a bruised stalk, settled loosened earth with my fingers, and gave water as carefully as if I had been basting a delicate joint—drop by drop, not to drown, only to revive. I smoothed away his footmarks too; the strong wind had begun to worry the borders, yet there was a moment’s leisure to efface what I wished forgotten.

So, with a pensive content, I sat at my desk and my German while the pupils composed themselves to the evening lessons, and the other teachers drew out their needlework. Our “étude du soir” was always held in the refectory—a smaller room than the classes; only boarders entered there, and they were about a score. Two lamps hung low over the tables. At dusk Rosine kindled them, and that lighting was like the striking of a bell in a convent kitchen: books shut, chairs drew in, a grave air put on, and silence laid like a lid.

Then began “la lecture pieuse.”

I had soon discovered its aim: not to feed the mind, but to fast it; to take the reason by the nape and press it down; to set before common sense a mess she must swallow and digest as she could. The book—never changed, only recommenced when finished—was a venerable old volume, grey as the Hôtel de Ville, old as the hills. I would have paid two francs merely to hold it, to turn the yellow, thick leaves, to read for myself what my ears were forced to drink. For drink it one did—like a hot draught poured down when you are not thirsty.

It was legends of saints. God forgive me, I say it reverently, but what legends! If those saints first boasted the feats, they must have been gasconading; if they did not, then monkish fingers invented them. Some extravagances one could laugh at inwardly; but with them were priestly matters—priestcraft—worse than the monkery: tales of moral martyrdom inflicted by Rome; confessor’s boasts, wickedly abusing an office to trample high-born women into the dust, turning countess and princess into tormented slaves. Again and again came stories like Conrad and Elizabeth of Hungary, with their black impiety, their tyranny, their nightmare privations. My temples would burn as if a tight band were drawn round them; my heart beat against my ribs; my wrist throbbed. More than once I broke the points of my scissors by driving them, without knowing, into the worm-eaten board.

For some nights I bore it as quietly as I could. At last I found that if I stayed, my sleep afterwards was broken with feverish excitement, like a room where a machine has been left running and you cannot get the humming out of your head. Prudence counselled flight. No Mause Headrigg ever felt a stronger call to testify against Sergeant Bothwell than I to speak my mind against this popish “lecture pieuse.” Yet I curbed myself. When Rosine came to light the lamps, in that little stir before the enforced hush, I rose and vanished—quickly, but with no noise—while the boarders put away their books.

When I vanished, it was into darkness. Candles were not carried about. The teacher who left the refectory had only the unlit hall, the empty schoolrooms, or the dormitory for refuge. In winter I paced the long classes to keep warm, grateful if the moon gave a little silver; and if there were only starlight, I learned to see by it; and if there was nothing, I endured the blank as best I could. In summer it was never wholly dark. Then I would climb to my quarter of the long dormitory—lit by five tall casements like great doors—open my own, and lean out upon the city beyond the garden. From the park or palace-square band-music would drift, thin and sweet, then smothered as a gust pushed it away—music always fighting the town’s harsher sounds: wheels over stone, distant voices, the thin whip-crack of modern hurry. Those coarse noises, multiplying year by year as if the streets bred them, did something to one’s spirit; they scraped at thought the way grit scrapes a pan if you scour it too hard. Yet between blasts I could catch a clarinet phrase or a horn-call, and it was as if some older, gentler Villette tried to speak through the new clangour. I would listen and think my own thoughts, living my own life in that still, shadow-world where words—whether French or English—never quite named what the heart knew.

This evening, fugitive as usual before the Pope and his works, I mounted the stairs, reached the dormitory, and opened the door, which, like every door in that house, revolved noiselessly on well-oiled hinges.

Before I saw, I felt life in the great room usually void—not by any stir or breath, but by the absence of true solitude. All the white beds—the “lits d’ange,” poetically called—lay in a row and were empty; no sleeper was there. Yet a sound touched the ear: a drawer, slid out with caution.

I stepped aside. The curtains did not hinder my sight now. I commanded my own bed and my own toilet: the work-box on the bed, the drawers beneath—locked. Or so they ought to be.

A dumpy, motherly little figure stood at my table, in a decent shawl and the cleanest nightcap imaginable, labouring at my “meuble” as if doing me a kindness. The lid of my work-box stood open. The top drawer gaped. Each drawer in turn was opened; each paper glanced over; each small box unlidded; each folded thing lifted, unfolded, refolded. Beautiful was the adroitness; exemplary the care. Madame wrought like a star—unhasting, yet unresting.

I watched her with secret glee. Had I been a gentleman, I think I should have liked Madame the better for the neat, compact way she did everything. Some people vex you with loose awkwardness; she satisfied you by trim thoroughness. I stood fascinated—yet knew I must break the spell and retreat. If she turned and caught me, there would be a scene; conventionalities would go down at one blow, disguises swept away, and we should look into each other’s eyes and know we could not work together more. What was the use of tempting catastrophe?

I was not angry. I had no wish to leave her. Another employer might lay on a heavier yoke; hers, for all its craft, sat light. Besides, her system did me no harm. Loverless, and expecting no love, I was safe from spies in my heart-poverty as a beggar is safe from thieves in his empty purse.

So I turned and fled—down the stairs swiftly and soundlessly, like a spider running the bannister.

When I reached the schoolroom, I laughed—laughed outright. Now I understood: she had seen Dr. John in the garden; she had fed her suspicions and set them to work in my drawers. The spectacle of a suspicious nature misled by its own inventions tickled me.

But when the laughter died, wrath smote; then came bitterness, as if one had struck a rock and drawn out water—Meribah’s waters, not sweet. For an hour my heart was shared between soreness and mirth, fire and grief. I wept hot tears—not because Madame mistrusted me (I cared not twopence for her mistrust), but for other reasons, tangled and disquieting. Thoughts broke up my inward repose, as wind breaks the smooth skin of broth in a pot, showing the turmoil beneath.

By next day the tempest had sunk: I was Lucy Snowe again.

Revisiting my drawers, I found them all locked. The closest examination could not discover a change. My few dresses were folded as I had left them. Between the folds of my best dress lay a little bunch of white violets—once silently presented by a stranger (a stranger to me, for we had never exchanged words), dried and kept for its faint, persistent perfume. They lay unstirred. My black silk scarf, my lace chemisette and collars, were unrumpled. Had she creased one solitary article I should have found forgiveness harder; but finding all straight and orderly, I said, Let bygones be bygones. I am unharmed: why bear malice?

Yet a riddle troubled me as obstinately as hunger troubles the body. How had Dr. John—if he were not accessory to the dropping of that casket—known it was dropped, and appeared so promptly to seek it? The wish to clear this point grew daring. I began to think: Why may I not, if I ever have the opportunity, ask Dr. John himself?

So long as Dr. John stayed away, I believed I had courage.

Little Georgette was convalescent. Her physician’s visits grew rare; he would have ceased them altogether, had not Madame insisted on an occasional call till the child was quite well. One evening Madame entered the nursery just after I had listened to Georgette’s lisped prayer and put her to bed. Taking the little hand, she said, “Cette enfant a toujours un peu de fièvre.” Then, with a quicker glance than usual, “Le Docteur John l’a-t-il vue dernièrement? Non, n’est-ce pas?”

Of course she knew better than any one.

“Well,” she pursued, “I am going out, pour faire quelques courses en fiacre. I shall call on Dr. John, and send him to the child. I will that he sees her this evening; her cheeks are flushed, her pulse is quick; you will receive him—for my part, I shall be from home.”

The child was well enough; only warmed with July. It was scarcely more needful to send for a doctor than for a priest with extreme unction. Besides, Madame rarely made “courses” in the evening; and this was the first time she chose to be absent on the occasion of Dr. John’s visit. The whole arrangement smelled of plan—like a dish too artfully seasoned, where you detect the cook’s intent. I saw it plainly, and felt not the least anxiety.

“Ha! ha! Madame,” laughed Light-heart the Beggar within me, “your crafty wits are on the wrong tack.”

She departed, dressed smartly, in a costly shawl and a chapeau vert tendre—hazardous tint for any complexion less fresh than her own, but to her not unbecoming. I wondered what she intended: whether she truly would send Dr. John, whether he would come, whether he might be engaged.

Madame had charged me not to let Georgette sleep till he came. So I occupied the time with nursery tales, and with that soft palaver of baby-language which makes words into food a child can swallow. I was fond of Georgette. To hold her warm little body on my lap, to carry her in my arms, was a treat—like a draught of clean water where the house offered little feeling. Tonight she begged me to lay my head on the pillow of her crib; she even put her arms round my neck. The clasp, the nestling press of her cheek to mine, made me almost cry with a tender pain. It was too sweet, that pure little drop from a pure source; it sank deep, subdued the heart, and sent a gush to the eyes.

Half an hour passed, perhaps an hour. Georgette murmured she was growing sleepy.

“And you shall sleep,” I thought, “malgré maman and médecin, if they are not here in ten minutes.”

Hark—there was the bell; and then a tread on the stairs, quick as if the steps were startled by the speed. Rosine introduced Dr. John; and with a freedom not peculiar to her alone, but common amongst Villette domestics, she stayed to hear what he had to say. Madame’s presence would have awed her away to vestibule and cabinet; mine, or any teacher’s, she cared not a jot for.

Smart, trim, pert, she stood with a hand in each pocket of her gay grisette apron, eyeing Dr. John as if he were a picture and she had paid to look at it.

“Le marmot n’a rien n’est ce pas?” she said, jerking her chin towards Georgette.

“Pas beaucoup,” he answered, scribbling a harmless prescription with his pencil.

“Eh bien!” Rosine pursued, stepping quite near while he put up the pencil. “And the box—did you get it? Monsieur went off like a coup-de-vent the other night; I had not time to ask him.”

“I found it: yes.”

“And who threw it, then?” continued Rosine—speaking with all the ease the very question I had longed to ask and had not the address to bring out. How short some people make a road that for others is a mountain.

“That may be my secret,” said Dr. John briefly, with no hauteur. He seemed to understand Rosine’s kind.

“Mais enfin,” she persisted, unabashed. “Monsieur knew it was thrown, since he came to seek it—how did he know?”

“I was attending a little patient in the college near,” he replied, “and saw it dropped out of his chamber window, and so came to pick it up.”

So simple. The note had spoken of a physician examining “Gustave.” My riddle unlocked itself with a click, and all the fine suspicions fell apart like pastry cut too thin.

“Ah ça!” Rosine went on; “il n’y a donc rien là-dessous: pas de mystère, pas d’amourette, par exemple?”

“Pas plus que sur ma main,” said he, showing his palm.

“Quel dommage!” she returned. “Et moi—à qui tout cela commençait à donner des idées.”

“Vraiment! vous en êtes pour vos frais,” was his cool rejoinder.

She pouted. He laughed at her “moue,” and when he laughed there was something so genial and good-natured in his look that one heard it, almost, like a kinder note in a harsh soundscape. I saw his hand incline to his pocket.
