“How many times have you opened the door for me within this last month?” he asked.

“Monsieur ought to have kept count of that,” said Rosine readily.

“As if I had not something better to do!” he retorted; but he gave her a piece of gold. She took it without scruple, and danced off to answer the bell again, which rang every five minutes as servants came for the half-boarders.

Do not think too hardly of Rosine. On the whole she was not bad; she simply had no notion there could be disgrace in grasping what she could get, or effrontery in chattering like a pie to the best gentleman in Christendom.

From that scene I learned something besides the explanation of the ivory box: not on the robe de jaconas—pink or grey—nor on the frilled, pocketed apron, lay the blame of breaking Dr. John’s heart. Those items of array were as guiltless as Georgette’s little blue tunic. So much the better. But who, then, was the culprit? What was the origin, the perfect explanation of the whole business? Some points cleared, but how many remained obscure as night.

“However,” I told myself, “it is no affair of yours.” And turning from Dr. John’s face—on which I had been unconsciously dwelling with a questioning gaze—I looked out of the window upon the garden.

He stood by the bedside, slowly drawing on his gloves, watching his little patient as her eyes closed and her rosy lips parted in sleep. I waited for him to go, as usual, with a quick bow and scarce-articulate “good-night.”

Just as he took his hat, my eyes, fixed on the tall houses bounding the garden, saw the one lattice, already commemorated, cautiously open. A hand projected—white—and a white handkerchief. Both waved. I know not whether some viewless quarter of our own dwelling answered the signal; but immediately after, from that lattice fluttered a falling object, white and light: billet the second.

“There!” I ejaculated involuntarily.

“Where?” Dr. John demanded, with sudden energy, making straight for the window. “What is it?”

“They have gone and done it again,” I replied. “A handkerchief waved and something fell.” I pointed to the lattice; it was now closed, blank, hypocritical.

“Go at once; pick it up and bring it here,” he said promptly; adding, “Nobody will take notice of you: I should be seen.”

I went. After a little search, I found the folded paper lodged on a low shrub-branch, caught there like a scrap of white cloth on a thorn. I seized it and brought it directly to Dr. John. This time, I think not even Rosine saw me.

He tore the billet into small pieces without reading.

“It is not in the least her fault, you must remember,” he said, looking at me.

“Whose fault?” I asked. “Who is it?”

“You don’t yet know, then?”

“Not in the least.”

“Have you no guess?”

“None.”

“If I knew you better,” he continued, “I might be tempted to risk some confidence, and thus secure you as guardian over a most innocent and excellent, but somewhat inexperienced being.”

“As a duenna?” I said.

“Yes,” he replied absently. “What snares are round her!” he added, musing.

Then, for the first time, he examined my face, as if to see whether any kindly expression there might warrant him in placing his “ethereal creature” under my care—some angel, it seemed, for whom powers of darkness plotted.

I felt no vocation to watch ethereal creatures. Yet the memory of Madame at my drawers rose before me, and with it a sense that I owed him a good turn. If I could help, I would; it was not mine to decide how.

So I intimated, with as little reluctance as might be, that I was willing to do what I could towards taking care of any person in whom he might be interested.

“I am no farther interested than as a spectator,” he said, with a modesty admirable to witness. “I happen to be acquainted with the rather worthless character of the person who, from the house opposite, has now twice invaded the sanctity of this place. I have also met in society the object at whom these vulgar attempts are aimed. Her exquisite superiority and innate refinement ought, one would think, to scare impertinence from her very idea. It is not so, however; and innocent, unsuspicious as she is, I would guard her from evil if I could. In person, however, I can do nothing; I cannot come near her—” He paused.

“Well, I am willing to help you,” I said; “only tell me how.”

And in my mind I ran over our inmates, hunting this paragon, this pearl of great price. It must be Madame, I concluded. She alone amongst us had the art even to seem superior; but as to being unsuspicious and inexperienced, Dr. John need not vex himself. However, it was his whim; he should be humoured. His angel should be an angel.

“Just notify the quarter to which my care is to be directed,” I continued gravely, chuckling inwardly at the thought of being set to chaperon Madame Beck or one of her pupils.

Dr. John had fine nerves. He felt, by instinct, what a coarser mind would not have detected: that I was a little amused at him. Colour rose to his cheek; with half a smile he turned and took his hat—he was going.

My heart smote me. I sprang to mend what I had hurt.

“I will—I will help you,” I said eagerly. “I will do what you wish. I will watch over your angel; I will take care of her, only tell me who she is.”

“But you must know,” he urged, earnest now, speaking very low. “So spotless, so good, so unspeakably beautiful! impossible that one house should contain two like her. I allude, of course—”

At that instant the latch of Madame Beck’s chamber-door—which opened into the nursery—gave a sudden click, as if the hand holding it had been slightly convulsed. There followed the suppressed explosion of an irrepressible sneeze.

These little accidents will happen to the best of us.

Madame—excellent woman!—was on duty. She had come home quietly, stolen upstairs on tiptoe, and stood in her chamber. Had she not sneezed, she would have heard all, and so should I. But that unlucky sternutation routed Dr. John. He stood aghast.

Madame came forward alert, composed, in the best and yet most tranquil spirits. No novice to her habits would have believed that her ear had been glued to the keyhole for ten minutes. She affected to sneeze again, declared she was “enrhumée,” and proceeded volubly to recount her “courses en fiacre,” every detail laid out as neatly as a well-ordered supper service.

The prayer-bell rang, and I left her with the doctor.

Georgette, once her cheeks had taken back their little roses and her cough was gone, was sent away into the country. Madame called it prudence; I felt it as loss. The child had been a warm hand slipped into mine in a house where, though there was always movement, there was little tenderness. When she left, the corridors seemed longer, the stair-steps more hollow to the foot, as if the building itself had exhaled and emptied.

I might have had companions, too. Each of the teachers, in turn, made her trial of me—an invitation, a smile held out like a spoon to see whether I would taste and approve. I did taste. One was honest enough, but she handled ideas as a farmwife handles bruised apples—sound where you bite, rotten where you don’t look; her feelings were coarse-grained, her talk salted always with herself. The second was Parisian, glazed over with a varnish of manners that shone prettily till you scraped it; beneath lay a damp, unwholesome slough. The third was a pale, characterless creature, yet she had one passion as sharp as vinegar: money. Not money to buy bread, or pay rent, or put a little meat into the pot, but money for the very look and clink of it, as if coin were spice and she lived on aroma.

Once, by way of favour, she drew me up-stairs, unlatched a secret panel, and showed me her hoard—a heap of coarse five-franc pieces, perhaps fifteen guineas’ worth, spread like dull, heavy lozenges on a board. The sight of it made her eyes take a greenish glisten, like a cat’s in the dark. She hovered over it with dotage, speaking of it with a perseverance as strange as it was unlovely in one not yet twenty-five; she loved it as a hen loves her eggs, not because they will hatch, but because they are there—warm under her, hers.

The Parisienne, Mademoiselle St. Pierre, was the opposite: prodigal in desire, though how far she ventured in deed I never knew. Once only I saw a snake-head of profligacy peep from her habits—cautious, curious, tasting the air. It rustled in the leaves of a bad novel; and when, in haste and unwisdom, I struck at it with anger, it recoiled, vanished, and hissed from hiding. From that hour she hated me with the cold, clean hatred that never troubles itself to flame.

She was always in debt. Her salary was anticipated, not merely in dress, but in perfumes that clouded a room with heavy sweetness, in cosmetics that gave her sallow skin a false bloom, in confectionery, and in condiments—little costly jars and packets that sharpened a palate already deadened. She had in her something like an epicure’s habits without an epicure’s joy: she sought sensation as one might seek a strong sauce when the meat is tainted. Thin, regular, yellowed, with perfect teeth and lips drawn fine as a thread, she looked at once craving and ungrateful. Work she hated; what she called pleasure she loved—a brainless dissipation, an insipid chewing of time.

Madame Beck knew her through and through, and spoke of her with an odd mixture of shrewdness, indifference, and dislike. When I asked why she kept such a woman, she answered, simply, that it suited her interest. For St. Pierre possessed, in almost a singular degree, the power of keeping order among a turbulent troop. She held them without noise, without passion, as a still frost can hold a stream. Knowledge she scarcely communicated; vigilance she carried like a rod that never needed to strike. “Je sais bien qu’elle n’a pas de principes,” Madame admitted, with a candour as calm as calculation; “son maintien est toujours convenable: c’est tout ce qu’il faut.” Parents looked no farther; the pupils looked no farther; therefore she looked no farther either.

It was a strange, frolicsome, noisy little world, this school, where chains were hidden under flowers. Romanism pervaded the place like incense—sweetening, stupefying, penetrating every chink. There was jealous spiritual restraint, but beside it an ample licence of the body, as if food, play, warmth, and bright shows could be used to bribe the senses into silence. I could not but mark how carefully the days were padded with comfort and diversion, lest a mind should fall still enough to hear its own questions. The house was always full of sound—the scrape and patter of feet, the chirp of girls, the sharp call of a mistress, the bell’s metallic command. Even the garden had its appointed clamour, laughter like flung pebbles, a racket of games. I learned to think of happiness there as something manufactured, not grown: a kind of music-box gaiety, wound up daily, and as mindless.

Yet at this season—summer in its ripest glow—even that manufactured gaiety took on a charm. The broad folding-doors and the two-leaved casements stood open from morning till evening. Sunshine seemed naturalized in the air; clouds, those dear grey drifts I had known at home, appeared banished to far seas. The house lived out of doors. Classes were held in the grand berceau; meals were taken there too, and I remember especially the odour of bread just broken, the warm, faint sourness of butter, the bruised sweetness of strawberries, and the green, cooling breath of cucumbers sliced thin and salted, sweating on their plates. Bees worked in the borders as steadily as any diligent pupil; the acacias, when stirred, dropped a faint almond scent; and under all the domestic bustle ran the soft acoustic fabric of summer—leaf-rustle, insect-hum, distant cart-wheels, the occasional clack of a gate.

There was, moreover, a note of preparation—holiday edging toward licence. Though the long autumn vacation was still two months away, another important day approached first: Madame’s fête.

The management of it fell chiefly on Mademoiselle St. Pierre. Madame, of course, maintained the air of disinterested ignorance; she never knew, never suspected, that a subscription was annually levied on pupils and teachers to purchase her a handsome present. One must, out of politeness, forget a brief, secret consultation on this point held in Madame’s own chamber.

“What will you have this year?” St. Pierre asked, chin already set.

“Oh, no matter—let it alone. Let the poor children keep their francs,” Madame replied, with a benign modesty worn like a lace cap.

St. Pierre’s chin protruded more. She called Madame’s displays of bonté “grimaces,” and never even pretended to respect them. “Vite!” she said. “Name the article. Jewellery or porcelain? Haberdashery or silver?”

“Eh bien! Deux ou trois cuillers, et autant de fourchettes en argent.”

And so it became a handsome case of plate worth three hundred francs—forks and spoons sleeping in velvet, to be awakened and flashed at table.

The programme of fête-day ran thus: the presentation, a collation in the garden, a dramatic performance with pupils and teachers as actors, then a dance and supper. To my eyes it looked gorgeous—flowers and white dresses, ribbons, and bright voices floating among the greenery. St. Pierre understood such arrangements; she managed them with the neatness of one who can set out a table so that every dish shines, and yet can taste nothing.

The play was the main labour. It demanded a month’s drilling: choosing actors, teaching them to speak, to hold themselves, to move; then rehearsal on rehearsal till their little parts were worn into them like a pattern into cloth. For this St. Pierre did not suffice. Another power was needed, and it came in the person of M. Paul Emanuel, professor of literature.

I was never present at his histrionic lessons; but I saw him often crossing the carré—quick, intent, as if always on his way to rebuke some error. In warm evenings, when doors stood open, I heard his voice ring through the place. His name, with anecdotes of him, went round the house like a refrain. Miss Ginevra Fanshawe, chosen for a prominent part, seasoned all her talk to me with him—as if she sprinkled a dish with pepper, not because she loved it, but because it gave her an agreeable thrill to sneeze. She called him hideously plain, and professed herself frightened into hysterics at the sound of his step; yet she watched him with a curious attention, like one who cannot resist listening to thunder.

He was a dark little man; pungent and austere. His close-shorn black head, broad sallow brow, thin cheek, wide quivering nostril, and thorough glance gave him, even at a distance, the look of a keen instrument. Irritable he was; one heard that, as he apostrophized his awkward squad. Sometimes impatience broke from him like a snapped cord. “Ecoutez!” he would cry, and his voice—clear, vehement—rang through the premises like a trumpet. Then would come the thin pipe of a Ginevra, a Mathilde, a Blanche, trying to echo him; and one understood the groan of scorn, the fierce hiss of rage, that answered their tame imitation.

“Vous n’êtes donc que des poupées!” I heard him thunder once. “Vous n’avez pas de passions. Vous ne sentez donc rien? Votre chair est de neige, votre sang de glace! Moi, je veux que tout cela s’allume, qu’il ait une vie, une âme!”

Vain resolve. When at last he found it vain, he broke the whole business down. Hitherto he had been drilling them in grand tragedy; he tore the tragedy to morsels, and came next day with a compact comic trifle—light enough for their smooth pates to carry without strain. To this they took more kindly; he knocked it into them with brisk blows of instruction.

Mademoiselle St. Pierre always presided at his lessons. I was told her polished manner, her tact and grace, pleased him greatly. She could please whom she would—for an hour. Then the feeling dried, vanished like dew.

The day before the fête was holiday too, given up to clearing, cleaning, arranging, decorating the schoolrooms. Indoors there was such bustle that a quiet person could not plant a foot without being jostled. I escaped to the garden. All day I wandered or sat there, taking warmth from the sun, shade from the trees, and a kind of company from my own thoughts. I spoke but two sentences to any living being, and did not feel lonely; I was glad to be still. Once or twice I passed through the rooms as a looker-on, saw the changes: a green-room contrived, a dressing-room planned, a little stage erected with scenery; and there, directing all with St. Pierre, was M. Paul Emanuel, while a merry band of pupils—Ginevra among them—worked under his control.

The great day arrived. The sun rose hot and unclouded, and burned so till evening. Doors and windows were flung wide, and that gave a sense of summer freedom—freedom, indeed, was the day’s proclaimed order. Teachers and pupils came down to breakfast in dressing-gowns and curl-papers, indulging forenoon slovenliness with delight, as if fasting aldermen permitted themselves a last plain crust before the feast.

About nine o’clock, the coiffeur arrived—an important functionary. Sacrilegious as it seems to confess, he fixed his head-quarters in the oratory; and there, in presence of bénitier, candle, and crucifix, he solemnised the mysteries of his art. Each girl in turn passed through his hands and emerged with hair smoothed like a shell, intersected by faultless white lines, and wreathed in Grecian plaits that shone as if lacquered. When my turn came, I hardly believed the glass. The garlandry of woven brown hair amazed me; I feared it was not all my own, and tugged at it to make sure. The artist had made the most of indifferent materials—like a cook who, with patient beating and careful seasoning, can coax a lightness out of stubborn dough.

Then the oratory closed, and the dormitory became a steaming scene of ablutions, arrayings, and bedizenings. To me it was always an enigma how so much time could be spent in doing so little. The process was intricate and prolonged; the result simple: clear white muslin, a blue sash—the Virgin’s colours—white or straw-coloured kid gloves. Three mortal hours were devoted to that uniform. Yet the effect was perfect in fit and freshness; every head dressed with compact taste—suiting the firm, full comeliness of Labassecourien outlines, though too stiff for any more flowing style.

Amid that diaphanous snowy mass, I felt myself a shadowy spot on a field of light. I had not courage to put on transparent white. Something thin I must wear—the weather and rooms were too hot for substantial fabrics—so I hunted through a dozen shops till I found a crape-like material of purple-grey, the colour of dun mist lying on a moor in bloom. My tailleuse made it as well as she could; as she wisely observed, since it was “si triste—si peu voyant,” care in fashion was more imperative. I had no flower, no jewel to relieve it; and, worse, no natural rose of complexion.

Daily routine lets us forget such deficiencies; but bright occasions force them on us, as a feast forces one to notice who has no appetite.

Still, in that gown of shadow I felt at home—an ease I should not have had in anything brilliant. Madame Beck herself kept me in countenance; her dress was almost as quiet as mine, except for a bracelet and a brooch bright with gold and fine stones. We met on the stairs; she nodded and smiled approval. Not that she thought me handsome—beauty seldom engaged her—but she deemed me convenable, décente; and Convenance and Décence were the two calm deities she worshipped. She even paused, laid her gloved hand on my shoulder—holding an embroidered, perfumed handkerchief—and whispered a sarcasm on the other teachers, whom she had just complimented aloud. “Nothing so absurd,” she said, “as for des femmes mûres to dress themselves like girls of fifteen—quant à la St. Pierre, elle a l’air d’une vieille coquette qui fait l’ingénue.”

Dressed a couple of hours before the rest, I did not go to the garden, where servants were propping long tables, placing seats, spreading cloths for the collation. The thought of all that food being arranged—the cold meats glazed, the cakes stacked, fruit washed and piled, bottles set in rows—might have tempted some; but the clamour of preparation repelled me. I went instead to the schoolrooms, now empty, cool, and clean: walls fresh stained, floors newly scoured and still scarcely dry; flowers fresh gathered in pots; draperies newly hung to soften the great windows.

I withdrew to the first classe, smaller and neater than the others, and took from the glazed bookcase—of which I kept the key—a volume whose title promised interest. The glass-door of the room opened into the large berceau. Acacia boughs caressed its panes as they stretched across to meet a rose-bush blooming by the opposite lintel; in that rose-bush bees murmured, busy and content. I began to read. The stilly hum, the embowering shade, the warm lonely calm, started to steal meaning from the page and lure me into reverie—as if the garden’s natural music were washing the harder noises of the house from my mind.

Then the street-door bell rang—sharper than any peal that much-tried instrument had yet produced—and snapped the thread of my dream.

The bell had rung all morning: workmen, servants, coiffeurs, tailleuses, coming and going. It would ring all afternoon too, as a hundred externes arrived in carriages and fiacres; and in the evening, parents and friends would gather for the play. A ring—even a sharp one—should have been nothing. Yet this peal had an accent of its own. It startled my book from my knee.

As I stooped to pick it up, I heard a step—firm, fast, straight—cutting through the house: through vestibule, along corridor, across carré, past the divisions, into the grand salle. The closed door of my little classe offered no obstacle; it burst open. A paletôt and a bonnet grec filled the space; and two eyes, first vague, then hungry, dived into me.

“C’est cela!” said a voice. “Je la connais: c’est l’Anglaise. Tant pis. Toute Anglaise, et, par conséquent, toute bégueule qu’elle soit—elle fera mon affaire, ou je saurai pourquoi.”

Then, with a stern politeness—as if he thought I had not caught his mutterings—and in a jargon of English so mangled it seemed chewed rather than spoken: “Meess—play you must: I am planted there.”

“What can I do for you, M. Paul Emanuel?” I asked; for it was he, and he was in a state of no little excitement.

“Play you must. I will not have you shrink, or frown, or make the prude. I read your skull that night you came; I see your moyens: play you can; play you must.”

“But how, M. Paul? What do you mean?”

“No time to lose,” he said now in French, brisk as a knife. “Let us thrust to the wall reluctance, excuses, minauderies. You must take a part.”

“In the vaudeville?”

“In the vaudeville. You have said it.”

I drew breath as if I had been struck. What did the little man mean to do with me—me, who would rather have been set to scrub the boards than stand in public mimicry?

“Listen,” he commanded. “The case shall be stated, and you shall answer Yes or No; and according to your answer shall I ever after estimate you.”

His cheek was lit with the glow of a nature always near ignition. His glances shot sharp as skewers; and I saw how quickly the injudicious, the hesitating, the sullen, the affected—above all, the unyielding—might make him violent and implacable. Silence was safest; I listened.

“The whole matter is going to fail,” he began. “Louise Vanderkelkov has fallen ill—at least so her ridiculous mother asserts. For my part, she might play if she would: it is only good-will that lacks. She had a rôle—whether you know it or not is equal—without that rôle the play stops. There are but a few hours to learn it. Not a girl here will accept it. It is not an interesting part, not an amiable part; their vile amour-propre—that base quality of which women have so much—revolts. Englishwomen are either the best or the worst of their sex. Dieu sait que je les déteste comme la peste, ordinairement—” this he bit out between teeth that seemed to grind the words—“but I apply to an Englishwoman to rescue me. What is her answer—Yes, or No?”

A thousand objections crowded me: the foreign tongue, the scant time, the public exposure. Inclination recoiled; ability faltered; self-respect trembled. “Non, non, non,” cried everything within. Yet when I looked up and met his vexed, fiery searching eye, I saw behind its menace something like appeal—almost need. My lips dropped the word “Oui.”

For a moment his rigid countenance relaxed, a quick quiver of content passing over it; then it bent again to business.

“Vite à l’ouvrage! Here is the book; here is your rôle: read.”

I read. He did not praise; at some passages he scowled and stamped. He gave a lesson; I imitated as diligently as I could. The part was disagreeable—a man’s part, an empty-headed fop’s. One could put into it neither heart nor soul: it was like being asked to cook with no fire, to season with no salt. I hated it. The play itself was a trifle: two rivals striving for the hand of a fair coquette. One lover was called the Ours—good, gallant, unpolished, a rough diamond; the other was a butterfly—a talker and traitor. I was to be that butterfly.

I did my best—which was bad. It provoked M. Paul; he fumed. I strained to do better than my best. Perhaps he gave me credit for good intention, for he grew partially content. “Ça ira!” he cried at last. Then, as voices began sounding from the garden and white dresses fluttered among the trees, he added, “You must withdraw. You must be alone to learn this. Come with me.”

Without time to deliberate, I was swept along as in a whirlwind—up-stairs, up two flights, indeed up three; that fiery little man moved as if the house were mapped in his blood. He bore me to a solitary lofty attic, put me in, locked me in. The key turned; he took it and vanished.

The attic was no pleasant place. In summer it was hot as Africa; in winter, they said, cold as Greenland. Boxes and lumber filled it; old dresses hung against the bare wall; cobwebs netted the ceiling. It was known to be tenanted by rats, black beetles, and cockroaches; rumour even affirmed that the ghostly Nun of the garden had once been seen there. At one end the light fell away into partial darkness, and across that gloom an old russet curtain was drawn, screening a sombre row of winter cloaks hanging each from its pin like malefactors from gibbets. From behind that curtain, they said, the Nun would issue.

I did not believe in the Nun; I believed, immediately and against my will, in the vermin. A large dark rat with a long tail glided from that squalid alcove, smooth and quick as a shadow spilled. My eye fell on black beetles dotting the floor like burnt currants scattered from a careless hand. Those sights discomposed me more than I care to confess. The dust, the heat, the stale smell of stored cloth and old wood—everything in that place oppressed the senses.

The heat would soon have been intolerable had I not opened and propped the skylight, letting in a slice of air. Under it I pushed a large empty chest, set a smaller box upon it, wiped the dust from both, gathered my dress fastidiously around me—my best, and therefore a legitimate object of care—climbed up to this makeshift throne, and sat down to learn my rôle. And while I repeated the lines—French words tasting foreign and dry on the tongue—I kept a sharp watch on beetles and cockroaches, of which, even more than of rats, I sat in mortal dread.

After Madame Beck’s fête—with its three weeks of permitted ease, its one bright day of laughter like a cork drawn from a bottle, and the slack morrow when even the chairs seemed too weary to stand—there came, as comes the souring after sweetness, two months of close work. These last weeks of the school-year were the true harvest-time of the pensionnat; what had been delayed and spared earlier was now thrust upon masters, mistresses, and pupils together. There were examinations to face and prizes to win; and for public show—always for public show—lessons must be kneaded, beaten, glazed, and baked into something that looked well on a table set for strangers’ eyes.

I did not pause to watch the others at their craft; I had my own furnace to tend. My duty was to season near ninety young brains with what they called a dark and intricate science—the English tongue—and to train near ninety tongues to its troublesome hiss and lisp, to those island dentals that to them felt like chewing dry crust with a sore mouth. It was not enough that they should know the words; they must sound them; and the effort—repeated, corrected, repeated again—was like making a novice whisk an egg-white to stiffness without letting her hand rest.

The day of examination arrived—stern day. It was prepared for anxiously and dressed for with no flutter. No gauze, no childish blue streamers; only the close, compact gravity of a working dress, as one ties up sleeves before plunging hands into dough. It seemed to me, too, that the strain of this day was appointed chiefly for my shoulders. The other female teachers were not expected to stand forth and question in their own branches; the professor of literature, M. Paul Emanuel, would do that for them. He gathered the reins of the whole establishment into his single hand, and held them hard. Help he rejected; colleague he would not admit. Even Madame Beck—who could have examined geography with real skill, for she loved that study as some women love a garden—was obliged to yield to the will of her imperious kinsman.

Only English he could not manage. That one dish he was forced to leave to the English teacher, and he gave it up with an ill grace—his jealousy flashing out, naïve as a child’s, sharp as salt on a raw lip.

To run a constant crusade against the amour-propre of every creature but himself was his chosen discipline. He relished representation—so long as he was the figure displayed; he abhorred it in others. When he could, he kept down; when he could not, he stormed and fretted like a kettle stopped too tightly.

On the evening before the examination, I walked in the garden as the boarders walked, and the teachers, and the dull leaves whispered in the alleys. M. Emanuel joined me in the allée défendue. A cigar lay at his lips; his paletôt—dark, shapeless, and somehow menacing—hung about him like a cloak thrown over a weapon; the tassel of his bonnet grec shaded his left temple; his whiskers curled, feline in their anger; his blue eye glittered under a cloud.

“Ainsi,” he began, stepping before me and stopping me short, “vous allez trôner comme une reine; demain—trôner à mes côtés? Sans doute vous savourez d’avance les délices de l’autorité. Je crois voir en je ne sais quoi de rayonnante, petite ambitieuse!”

He was wrong—quite wrong. I could not value the applause of to-morrow’s audience at the price he set upon it. Had those faces been mine by acquaintance, had friends sat there for me as for him, perhaps the warmth would have reached me; but as the matter stood, school triumphs shed on me only a cold shine, like pale sun on a stone floor. Yet I wondered—as I had wondered before—how such things could glow to him like hearth-fire.

Still, I had my own tastes. I liked to see him jealous. It woke him. It threw odd lights and shadows over his dun face, and made his violet-azure eyes—he called his black hair and blue eyes “une de ses beautés”—flash with a life that was almost youth. There was a relish in his anger, like mustard—sharp, unreasonable, but honest. I offered no denial of the complacency he imputed to me. I merely asked, quietly, when the English examination would come—at the opening or the close?

“I hesitate,” said he, “whether at the very beginning, before many persons are come, when your aspiring nature will not be gratified by a large audience; or quite at the close, when everybody is tired, and only a jaded, worn-out attention will be at your service.”

“Que vous êtes dur, Monsieur!” I said, with an affectation of dejection, as one tastes bitterness on purpose.

“One ought to be dur with you. You are one of those beings who must be kept down. I know you! I know you! Other people in this house see you pass, and think that a colourless shadow has gone by. As for me, I scrutinized your face once, and it sufficed.”

“You are satisfied that you understand me?”

He did not answer directly. He went on, as if pursuing some private proof in his own mind: “Were you not gratified when you succeeded in that vaudeville? I watched you and saw a passionate ardour for triumph in your physiognomy. What fire shot into the glance! Not mere light, but flame: je me tiens pour averti.”

“What feeling I had on that occasion, Monsieur—and pardon me if I say you immensely exaggerate both its quality and quantity—was quite abstract. I did not care for the vaudeville. I hated the part you assigned me. I had not the slightest sympathy with the audience below the stage. They are good people, doubtless; but do I know them? Are they anything to me? Can I care for being brought before their view again to-morrow? Will the examination be anything but a task to me—a task I wish well over?”

“Shall I take it out of your hands?”

“With all my heart; if you do not fear failure.”

“But I should fail. I only know three phrases of English, and a few words: par exemple, de sonn, de mone, de stare—est-ce bien dit? My opinion is that it would be better to give up the thing altogether: to have no English examination, eh?”

“If Madame consents, I consent.”

“Heartily?”

“Very heartily.”

He smoked a moment in silence—his cigar making a small dry crackle now and then, like twigs snapping in a grate. Then he turned quickly.

“Donnez-moi la main,” said he; and the spite and jealousy melted out of his face, and a generous kindliness rose there instead, warm as a sudden flame.

“Come, we will not be rivals, we will be friends,” he pursued. “The examination shall take place, and I will choose a good moment; and instead of vexing and hindering, as I felt half inclined ten minutes ago—for I have my malevolent moods: I always had from childhood—I will aid you sincerely. After all, you are solitary and a stranger, and have your way to make and your bread to earn; it may be well that you should become known. We will be friends: do you agree?”

“Out of my heart, Monsieur. I am glad of a friend. I like that better than a triumph.”

“Pauvrette!” said he, and, with that one word—half pity, half rough tenderness—he turned away and left the alley.

The examination passed well. M. Paul was as good as his promise; he chose my hour, steadied my place, and did what he could to make my part easy. Next day came the distribution of prizes. That, too, went by; the great machine of the school loosened, slackened, and stopped; the pupils went home, the professors scattered, and then began the long vacation.

That vacation—shall I ever forget it? I think not.

The very first day Madame Beck departed to the sea-side to join her children. The three teachers had parents or friends to shelter them, and took refuge without looking back. Every professor quitted the city: some went to Paris, some to Boue-Marine; M. Paul set forth on a pilgrimage to Rome. The house was left empty—empty, save for me, one servant, and a poor, deformed, imbecile pupil, a sort of crétin, whose stepmother in a distant province refused to receive her.

My heart seemed to shrink within me as if a hand tightened round it. The September days were long—so long that their hours tasted stale, like bread kept too many days in a tin; and yet they brought no nourishment. The premises, once noisy as a kitchen at dinner-time, seemed now vast, void, and echoing. The garden, forsaken, had turned grey with town-dust; leaves, when they fell, made no soft country sound but a dry, faint scrape, as though even nature here had learned to be cautious and muted under walls.

At the beginning of those eight weeks I could hardly see how I should live through to the end. My spirits had been sinking for months; now, the prop of labour withdrawn, they went down rapidly, like a fire when no one feeds it. To look forward was not hope; the dumb future offered no promise, no inducement to endure. A sorrowful indifference pressed on me; a resignation that was not peace but weariness—an inclination to reach, sooner rather than later, the end of earthly things.

When leisure forced me to look at life—life as it must appear to such as me—it showed itself a desert, tawny and trackless. The hopes dear to youth, that carry it on like a song hummed while work goes forward, I did not know, and did not dare to know. When they knocked sometimes at my heart, an inward bolt was drawn. I feared the sin and weakness of presumption more than I feared hunger.

Reader—religious, moral, stoic, cynical, epicurean—each will have his rebuke ready; each will sermonize or sneer. Take it as you will. Perhaps you are right; perhaps, placed where I stood, you would have been wrong with me. The first month was long, black, heavy.

The crétin did not seem unhappy. If I fed her and kept her warm, she asked no more. Food and sunshine contented her; and, when sunshine lacked, fire. Her faculties approved of inaction; her brain, her eyes, her ears, her heart slept. Lethargy was her paradise.

I did my best to feed her well. It is strange how, when one’s own mind is fainting, the small domestic acts still go on, like hands obeying a habit after thought has gone away. I measured out soup; I cut bread; I poured milk; I watched her swallow with a kind of dull vigilance, as one watches a creature that cannot tell you if it is choking. Yet my own appetite would fail. There were days when attendance on her turned the very thought of eating sour; I would go, faint, into the court to the well or fountain and drink water because it asked no effort of the stomach. But this duty—menial and distasteful—did not wring my heart as my solitude did. It did not brim my eyes, nor scald my cheek with tears that seemed molten.

She was, moreover, at times a heavy charge. I could not take her beyond the garden; I could not leave her alone. Her mind, like her body, was warped. A vague bent to mischief, an aimless malevolence, made constant watchfulness necessary. She seldom spoke. She would sit for hours moping and mowing, twisting her features into grimaces indescribable, so that it was like being shut up with some strange untamed animal rather than with a human being. There were personal attentions, too, that called for the nerve of a hospital nurse. They should not have fallen to me; a servant—now absent—had performed them before, and in the hurried flight of holiday no substitute had been found. I bore them; my resolution held, then fell ill, then rose again. Still, even that trial was less wasting than the gnawing, soundless pain of the mind.

It was a relief, when an aunt of the poor creature—a kind old woman—came one day and took her away.

Then I was free to walk out.

At first I had not the courage to venture far from the Rue Fossette. Yet by degrees I sought the city gates, passed them, and went wandering away—along chaussées, through fields, beyond cemeteries Catholic and Protestant, beyond farmsteads, to lanes and little woods, and I know not where. A goad seemed always at my side. A fever forbade rest. In my soul there was the craving of a famine—not for food, but for companionship. I walked through burning noon and arid afternoon, through dusk, and came back by moonrise.

I began to listen—not as one listens to music bought and displayed, but as one listens for life. The city had its own incessant hum: wheels over stones, hammers too early in the morning, voices thrown sharp between walls, laughter that did not belong to me. Outside the gates the sound changed. Earth took the noise into itself; the air broadened; the small notes of living things—wind in grass, a bird’s brief call, water in a ditch—came like a language older than French or English. It should have soothed; sometimes it did. Sometimes it only made more plain what I lacked: that my own tongue, my native accent, my familiar names for ordinary comfort, had no answering voice here. In this place I spoke with borrowed words; and the borrowed words, though correct enough, could not carry the taste of home. There are feelings one cannot translate; they perish in the passage, like delicate herbs crushed in transit, their perfume lost.

In those long solitary rambles I would picture where others were now. Madame Beck, at a cheerful watering-place with her children, her mother, and a troop of friends who had chosen the same shore. Zélie St. Pierre in Paris with relatives. The other teachers safe at home. Ginevra Fanshawe travelling south with connections—Ginevra, who seemed to me the happiest. I imagined her on roads of beautiful scenery, under September suns that shone for her on fertile plains where harvest and vintage mellowed. I saw the gold and crystal moons rise for her over blue horizons waved in mounted lines.

Yet all that was nothing. The same sun shone on me; the same moon rose for me; and sometimes I wished to be covered with earth and turf, deep out of their influence. I could not live in their light as in companionship. I could not give them affection and have it returned. But Ginevra—so I fancied—had a spirit with her, empowered to give constant strength and comfort, to gladden daylight and embalm darkness. By True Love was Ginevra followed; she could never be alone.

Was she insensible to that presence? I could not believe it. I imagined her grateful in secret, loving with reserve, purposing one day to show the depth of her love. I pictured her hero half conscious of her coy fondness, comforted by it; I conceived an electric chord of sympathy between them, a fine chain of mutual understanding sustaining union across a hundred leagues, carrying communication across mound and hollow by prayer and wish. In my mind Ginevra gradually became a heroine; and one day, startled by my own illusion, I said: “I really believe my nerves are getting overstretched. My mind has suffered somewhat too much; a malady is growing upon it—what shall I do? how shall I keep well?”

There was, indeed, no way to keep well under such circumstances. At last, after a day and night of peculiar depression, physical illness followed. I was forced to my bed.

The Indian summer closed. Equinoctial storms began. For nine dark wet days—hours rushing on turbulent, deaf, dishevelled, bewildered with sounding hurricane—I lay in a fever of nerves and blood. Sleep went quite away. In the night I would rise and look round for it—sleep—beseeching it in my weakness as one begs for water. The windows rattled; the blast cried; that was all. No soft return, no kind hand over the eyes.

I err: it came once, but in anger. Impatient of my importunity, it brought an avenging dream.

By the clock of St. Jean Baptiste the dream lasted scarcely fifteen minutes—brief, and yet long enough to wring my whole frame with unknown anguish; long enough to confer an experience nameless, with the hue, mien, terror, very tone of a visitation from eternity. Between twelve and one that night a cup was forced to my lips—black, strong, strange; drawn from no well, but filled seething from a boundless sea. Suffering brewed in mortal measure, mixed for mortal lips, tastes not as that suffering tasted. When I drank and woke, I thought all was over; the end had come and passed.

Trembling, as consciousness returned—ready to cry out for help, and yet knowing no fellow-creature was near enough to catch any summons—Goton, in her far attic, could not hear—I rose on my knees in bed. Some fearful hours went over me. Indescribably I was torn and oppressed in mind. And in that dream’s horror, the worst lay here: methought the well-loved dead—those who had loved me well in life—met me elsewhere alienated. A despair of the future galled my inmost spirit. There was no motive why I should recover or wish to live; and yet unendurable was the pitiless, haughty voice in which Death challenged me to engage his unknown terrors. When I tried to pray I could only say, “From my youth up Thy terrors have I suffered with a troubled mind.”

Most true it was.

In the morning Goton brought tea and urged me to call a doctor. I would not. I thought no doctor could cure what ailed me.

One evening—and I was not delirious; my mind was sane—I rose, dressed myself, weak and shaking. The solitude and stillness of the long dormitory could no longer be borne. The ghastly white beds were turning into spectres. The round tops of their frames became death’s-heads, huge and sun-bleached; dead dreams of an elder world and mightier race lay frozen in their wide gaping eyeholes. That evening the conviction fastened itself on my soul that Fate was of stone and Hope a false idol—blind, bloodless, with a granite core. I felt, too, that the trial appointed me had reached its climax, and must now be turned—by my own hands, hot, feeble, trembling.

Rain still fell; wind still blew, but with what seemed clemency compared to the day’s rage. Twilight dropped like a veil. From the lattice I saw night-clouds trailing low, like banners drooping. It seemed at that hour as though Heaven held affection and sorrow for all pain suffered on earth; and the weight of that dreadful dream became a little alleviated—the insufferable thought of being no more loved, no more owned, half yielded to a hope of the contrary. I thought that hope would shine clearer if I got out from under this roof, crushing as a tomb-slab, and went beyond the city to a certain quiet hill far off among fields.

I covered myself with a cloak—sense and recollection remained, for I knew to put on warm clothing—and set forth.

As I passed, the bells of a church arrested me. Their sound, made solemn by rain and stone streets, seemed to call me in to the salut. I went. Any rite, any spectacle of sincere worship, any opening for appeal to God was then as welcome to me as bread to one in extremity. I knelt with others on the stone pavement. It was an old church, its gloom not gilded but purpled by stained light, as if colour itself had been steeped long years in prayer.

Few worshippers were there; when the salut ended, half departed. I soon perceived those who stayed remained to confess. I did not stir. The doors were shut carefully, one after another. A holy quiet sank down; a solemn shade gathered round.

After a space, breathless and worn with prayer, a penitent approached the confessional. She whispered her avowal; shrift was whispered back; she returned consoled. Another went, and another. A pale lady kneeling near me said low and kindly, “Go you now; I am not quite prepared.”

Mechanically obedient, I rose and went. I knew what I did; my mind had run over the intent with lightning speed. This step could not make me more wretched than I was; it might soothe.

The priest within the confessional did not at first turn his eyes on me; he only inclined his ear, as if this duty had become a form gone through with the phlegm of custom. I hesitated. I knew no formula. Instead of beginning as penitents begin, I said simply, “Mon père, je suis Protestante.”

He turned then. He was not a native priest; the native cast of physiognomy is often grovelling, but this profile and brow showed a Frenchman. Grey and advanced in years, he did not, I thought, lack intelligence or feeling. Not unkindly he asked why, being a Protestant, I came to him.

I told him I was perishing for one word of advice, one accent of comfort. I had lived for weeks quite alone; I had been ill; there was an affliction pressing on my mind under which it could hardly endure.

“Was it a sin, a crime?” he asked, startled.

I reassured him; and, as well as I could, I showed him the mere outline of my experience. I did not dress it up in eloquence; I gave him what I had—plain pain, long pent up.

He looked thoughtful, surprised, puzzled. “You take me unawares,” said he. “I have not had such a case as yours before. Ordinarily we know our routine and are prepared; but this makes a great break in the common course of confession. I am hardly furnished with counsel fitting the circumstances.”

I had not expected he would be. Yet the mere relief of speaking—of letting some portion of accumulated anguish flow into a human ear that was sentient and yet consecrated, into a vessel from which it could not be poured back again among the world—did me good. Even while he spoke thus, I felt already solaced.

“Must I go, father?” I asked, for he sat silent.

“My daughter,” he said kindly—and I am sure he was kind; his eye had compassion—“for the present you had better go. But I assure you, your words have struck me. Confession, like other things, becomes formal with habit. You have come and poured your heart out—a thing seldom done. I would fain think your case over, and take it with me to my oratory. Were you of our faith I should know what to say: a mind so tossed can find repose but in retreat, and in punctual practice of piety. The world, it is well known, has no satisfaction for that class of natures. Holy men have bidden penitents like you to hasten their path upward by penance, self-denial, and difficult good works. Tears are given them here for meat and drink—bread of affliction and waters of affliction; their recompense comes hereafter. It is my conviction that these impressions under which you are smarting are messengers from God to bring you back to the true Church. You were made for our faith: depend upon it, our faith alone could heal and help you—Protestantism is too dry, cold, prosaic for you. The further I look, the more plainly I see it is out of the common order. On no account would I lose sight of you. Go now, my daughter; but return again.”

I rose and thanked him. I was withdrawing when he signed to me to return.

“You must not come to this church,” he said. “I see you are ill, and this church is too cold. You must come to my house: I live—” and he gave me his address. “Be there to-morrow morning at ten.”

I only bowed. Then I pulled down my veil, gathered my cloak close, and glided away into the wet twilight, the bell-sound fading behind me like a language half understood, yet—somehow—answered at last.

Where my soul went in that swoon I cannot name. If she wandered, she kept her travel locked away as closely as a recipe whispered once and lost—no crumb left for Memory, no steam for fancy to follow. Perhaps she rose—light as sifted flour—and, for one trembling moment, scented the air of some eternal kitchen where no hunger gnaws and no pot ever boils dry. Perhaps, at the very threshold, an unseen hand—stern as a matron’s, gentle as a nurse’s—turned her back, tied her again, shivering and unwilling, to the cold clay of my body.

I know only how she returned: not with gladness, but with resistance, like dough that will not take the last water. Spirit and substance met again in a struggle, not an embrace. Sight came first—red and swimming, as if my eyes opened through blood. Hearing followed—violent, a crash of sound, like thunder striking close over the roof. Consciousness woke in fright; I sat up, appalled, staring as one dragged from deep water into a strange room.

At first nothing was itself. A wall did not look like a wall, a lamp not like a lamp; all things wore that queer, half-transparent air which might make a ghost as intelligible as a chair. Yet, as a hearth draws a room back into order, my faculties, one by one, settled to their places; the mechanism of life resumed its old, stubborn rhythm.

Still I did not know where I was. I had been lifted from the church-step where I fell—night and tempest were shut out by ceiling, glass, and plaster. Some house had received me. What house?

Half-dreaming, I tried to fit the place to the pensionnat in the Rue Fossette: a dormitory, perhaps, or one of the little rooms. But the glimpses I caught did not agree—no ranks of white beds, no long bare windows. “It is not Madame Beck’s own chamber?” I thought, and even as I questioned, my eye found an easy-chair covered in blue damask.

More followed: seats cushioned to match; then a whole parlour came slowly into focus—the steady, domestic fact of it. A wood fire lived upon a clear hearth; the carpet showed arabesques of bright blue against shaded fawn; the walls were pale, and over them ran a delicate, endless garland of azure forget-me-nots, lost and found again among gold leaves and tendrils. Between two windows hung a gilded mirror; the windows were curtained amply with blue damask. In the mirror I saw myself—not in a bed, but laid upon a sofa. I looked like something unseasonably kept: hollow-eyed, hair darker by contrast with the thin ash of my cheeks. This room, by the place of its doors and windows, was unknown; this house, unknown.

And yet—my brain, still unsteady, began to play its tricks with recognition. That blue arm-chair grew familiar. So did a certain scroll-couch, and the round centre-table with its blue cover bordered in autumnal foliage; most of all, two little footstools with worked covers, and a small ebony-framed chair whose seat and back were embroidered with brilliant flowers on a dark ground.

My heart gave a hard little leap. I searched with my eyes, and—strange miracle of the commonplace—old acquaintance sat everywhere, as if Time had folded back on itself and laid yesterday on top of ten years ago. “Auld lang syne” looked out from every corner: two oval miniatures above the mantelpiece—the pearl-edged, powdered heads, the velvet collars circling white throats, the swell of muslin kerchiefs, the lace ruffles; upon the shelf, two china vases and remnants of a tiny tea-service thin as eggshell, smooth as enamel; under glass, a white centre ornament, a classic alabaster group. And there—most piercing of all—handscreens with elaborate pencil-drawings finished like engravings: I felt my eyes ache at the sight, remembering the long hours when these same fingers, now so spare, had held the school-girl pencil and crept line by line over a tedious grace.

Where was I? Not only what place, but what year? These things belonged to other days, another country. Ten years ago I had bade them farewell; since my fourteenth year they and I had not met. The question broke from me, audible, helpless: “Where am I?”

A shape I had not noticed stirred, rose, and came forward—inharmonious with the room as a coarse apron would be beside a damask gown. It was a native bonne, in common cap and print dress. She spoke neither French nor English. Her phrases were in dialect—close as knots to my ear—and yielded me no sense. But she cooled my forehead and temples with water scented faintly—something clean and herbal in it, like bruised leaves after rain. Then she propped my cushion, signed that I must not speak, and returned to her place at the foot of the sofa.

She knit busily, eyes down; thus I could watch her without interruption, and wonder how she came to be set like a plain domestic utensil among these relics of my childhood. More—what had those scenes and those years to do with me now?

Too weak to untangle the riddle, I tried to settle it by dismissing it: mistake, dream, fever. Yet I knew there was no mistake; I was not asleep; and—confused as I was—I believed myself sane. I wished the room had been dimmer, that the familiar details would not show so clearly: the worked chair, the screens, the ornaments, the blue damask—precisely the same, to the least flaw and shade, as I had known in the drawing-room at my godmother’s house at Bretton. Only the room itself, in proportion and measure, seemed altered.

The tale of Bedreddin Hassan rose to my mind—carried in sleep from Cairo to the gates of Damascus. Had some Genius stooped through the storm that overcame me, lifted me from the church-step, and borne me high over land and sea to lay me before an English hearth? But no; I knew that hearth’s old fire burned no more. Its Lares had long since been carried away.

The bonne looked up, saw my eyes wide, and perhaps read in them an agitation she did not understand. She set down her knitting, poured water, measured drops from a vial into a glass, and came towards me. What dark draught was this—what distillation of foreign mercy?

There was no time to question; I swallowed it passively. At once a quietude, warm and heavy as broth taken after long fasting, flowed through my brain. Thought softened; fear subsided. The pain of weakness slid from my limbs; muscles slept. I could not move—yet, with the desire gone, the loss felt like relief. The bonne placed a screen between me and the lamp; I saw her rise to do it, but I do not remember seeing her sit again. Between the motion and its end, I fell asleep.

When I woke, all was changed.

High day lay over me—not summer’s gold, but the leaden gloom of raw, blustering autumn. Rain beat the casement; wind worried the trees with that low, long “wuther” which tells of a garden outside. Chill, whiteness, solitude surrounded me. Whiteness—because dimity curtains dropped before a French bed bounded my view.

I lifted them and looked out. My eye, ready for the long, whitewashed dormitory of the pensionnat, blinked and failed before the smallness of a cabinet: sea-green walls, one high lattice shaded with muslin festoons; a toilette-table dressed—like a lady for a ball—in white over pink; a polished glass above it, a lace-frilled pincushion; a small, low chintz arm-chair green and white; a washstand with grey marble top and pale-green ware. Nothing more was needed to furnish the tiny space.

And yet fear seized me. Not because the room was ugly—it was pretty enough—but because it was impossible. These were not merely chairs and glass and china: they were the very ghosts—if ghosts could be solid—of things I knew too well.

I knew that chair—its chintz pattern, its snugness. I knew the black carved frame of the glass, shining and foliated. I knew the milky-green china on the stand. I knew the stand itself, and the marble splintered at one corner. I was forced to recognize them, as I had recognized the rosewood and porcelain of the parlour the night before.

Bretton. Bretton. Ten years ago looked back at me from that mirror. Why did that past haunt me with such exactness, and yet return incomplete? Why give me furniture as faithfully as an inventory, and deny me the room, the street, the very air of the place?

On the toilette lay a crimson satin pincushion, ornamented with gold beads and frilled with thread-lace. I had made it myself. I sprang up, seized it, and examined it closely. There was the cipher, “L. L. B.,” wrought in gold beads, within an oval wreath embroidered in white silk—the initials of my godmother’s name: Louisa Lucy Bretton.

“Am I in England? Am I at Bretton?” I muttered. I pulled up the blind and looked out, half-ready to meet the calm old buildings and clean grey pavement of St. Ann’s Street, and at the end the minster towers; or, failing that, at least some town view—a rue in Villette, some street in some known city.

Instead, I looked through a frame of leafage clustering about the lattice, and out upon a grassy, mead-like level—a lawn-terrace and trees rising from lower ground beyond: high forest trees such as I had not seen for many a day. They groaned under the October gale; between their trunks lay the line of an avenue where yellow leaves drifted in heaps, or were whipped, one by one, before the sweeping west wind. Whatever landscape lay further seemed flat; the tall beeches shut it out. The place was secluded—and strange to me.

Once more I lay down. My bed stood in a little alcove. Turning my face to the wall, I hoped to exclude the bewildering room. But no—between the looped curtains hung a broad gilded frame holding a portrait.

It was a water-colour sketch: a boy’s head, well drawn—fresh, life-like, animated. He seemed sixteen: fair, sanguine, long hair with a sunny sheen, penetrating eyes, an arch mouth, a gay smile. A face parents might be proud of, sisters tender over; a romantic school-girl might almost have loved it in its frame. Those eyes looked as though, when older, they would flash quick responses to love; whether they would keep the steady beam of faith, I could not tell. On his lips lay a beautiful menace—caprice and light esteem ready to rise at any sentiment offered him too easily.

I forced myself to accept each new revelation quietly, and whispered, as if speaking a remembered list of household treasures:

“That portrait used to hang in the breakfast-room, over the mantelpiece—too high, I always thought. I remember mounting a music-stool to unhook it, holding it close, searching those bonny wells of eyes—hazel under lashes like a pencilled laugh—studying the cheek’s colour, the mouth’s expression.” I did not think fancy could better the curve of that mouth or the line of the chin; even my ignorance knew them for beautiful, and still I had pondered, perplexed, how what charmed could also pain so keenly.

Once, to test something I could not name, I had taken little Missy Home, lifted her up, and bade her look. “Do you like it, Polly?” I had asked. She never answered; she gazed long, and at last a shadow trembled through her sensitive eye as she said, “Put me down.” And I had put her down, thinking, “The child feels it too.”

Now I thought on it again, adding, against my will, a tenderness: he had faults; yet scarce ever was a finer nature—liberal, suave, impressible. The reflection closed on a word spoken aloud, as if it had been waiting in my mouth all night:

“Graham!”

“Graham!” echoed a sudden voice at my bedside. “Do you want Graham?”

I turned. The wonder did not thin; it thickened. If the pictured face on the wall had startled me, the living woman before me startled me more: tall, well-attired, in widow’s silk; a cap suited to her matronly braids; a face marked now beyond beauty, but not beyond character—good, firm, and sensible. She was little changed: somewhat sterner, more robust. She was my godmother—the distinct presence of Mrs. Bretton.

I kept quiet; inwardly, I shook. My pulse fluttered; the blood left my cheek, and cold lay there.

“Madam, where am I?” I asked.

“In a very safe asylum; well protected for the present. Make your mind quite easy till you are a little better. You look ill this morning.”

“I am so bewildered I scarcely know whether to trust my senses, or whether they mislead me in everything. But you speak English, do you not, madam?”

“I should think you might hear that. It would puzzle me to hold a long discourse in French.”

“You do not come from England?”

“I am lately arrived thence. Have you been long in this country? You seem to know my son?”

“Do I, madam? Perhaps I do. Your son—the picture there?”

“That is his portrait as a youth. While looking at it, you pronounced his name.”

“Graham Bretton?”

She nodded.

“I speak to Mrs. Bretton, formerly of Bretton, ——shire?”

“Quite right; and you, I am told, are an English teacher in a foreign school here: my son recognised you as such.”

“How was I found, madam, and by whom?”

“My son shall tell you that by-and-by,” she replied. “At present you are too confused and weak for conversation. Try to eat some breakfast, and then sleep.”

In spite of all I had undergone—fatigue, agitation, wet and cold—the true fever that had pressed on my frame was abating. For nine days I had taken no solid food; thirst had been my constant companion. Yet now, when breakfast was offered, hunger rose—sharp and faint at once—as if something within me, long starved, had begun to remember how to live. I drank the tea she gave—English tea, strong enough to carry warmth down into the chest—and ate the small allowance of dry toast. It was only a morsel, but it steadied me. Two or three hours later the bonne brought a little cup of broth and a biscuit: the broth smelled of patient simmering, of bones giving up their strength slowly, of salt measured and not squandered. I drank it as if it were medicine and kindness together.

When evening began to fall, the wind still harried the trees, and the rain streamed on like a continual pouring from some inexhaustible pail. I grew weary of my bed. The room, though pretty, felt too small—its very neatness confining. The chill and the gathering gloom pressed on my spirits; I wanted firelight—wanted not only to see it but to hear it: that domestic crackle and hush which gives courage, that little acoustic shelter against the world’s roar. Outside, the storm made a brutal music—raw, insistent, wearing; inside, I longed for the older, kinder sounds of a home: a kettle’s murmur, a cup set down on a saucer, the soft rustle of a newspaper turned.

And always I thought of the son of that tall matron. When should I see him? Not, surely, while I remained shut away.

At last the bonne came to make the bed for the night. She would have wrapped me in a blanket and set me in the little chintz chair; but I declined and dressed myself. It cost effort; my limbs still held that weak, watery uncertainty. Yet I finished, and sat down to take breath.

Then Mrs. Bretton appeared again.

“Dressed!” she exclaimed, smiling the smile I remembered so well—pleasant, not soft. “You are quite better then? Quite strong—eh?”

She spoke to me as she used to—half-kindly, half-authoritative. There was the same patronage in her voice and manner that, as a girl, I had always known from her—a sheltering, not insulting; the protection of the strong, as a tree shelters an herb. I made my request without ceremony.

“Do let me go down-stairs, madam. I am so cold and dull here.”

“I desire nothing better, if you are strong enough to bear the change,” she answered. “Come then; here is an arm.”

I took it, and we went down one flight of carpeted steps to a landing. A tall door stood open, admitting us into the blue-damask room.

The sight was like warmth itself. Domestic comfort sat there in order and glow. Amber lamplight filled the corners; the fire threw vermilion flush on the walls. And to complete the old picture, tea stood ready on the table—an English tea, shining and familiar: the solid silver urn of antique pattern, the massive pot to match, the thin porcelain cups dark with purple and gilding. I knew the very seed-cake, baked in its peculiar mould, which at Bretton always had its place. Graham liked it. There it was again, set before Graham’s plate, with silver knife and fork beside it. So Graham was expected. Graham might be in the house. In a few minutes—perhaps—I should see him.

“Sit down—sit down,” said Mrs. Bretton, as my step faltered a little in crossing to the hearth. She placed me on the sofa. I soon moved behind it, pleading the fire was too hot; in the shadow I found a seat that suited me better. She did not argue. She was never one to fuss: she let others have their way when it cost nothing essential.

She made the tea and took up the newspaper. I watched her with a kind of hungry attention. All her movements were young. She must have been above fifty, yet neither sinew nor spirit had rusted. Though portly, she was alert; though serene, capable of sudden impulse. Good health and good temper kept her green.

As she read, I perceived she listened—listened for her son. She would not confess uneasiness; yet the storm did not lull, and if Graham were out in that hoarse wind—still roaring, still unsatisfied—I knew well her heart would be out with him.

“Ten minutes behind his time,” she said, looking at her watch.

A minute later she lifted her eyes from the page and inclined her head slightly towards the door. Her brow cleared; she had heard something. Then even my less-practised ear caught it: the iron clash of a gate swung to; steps on gravel; the door-bell.

He was come.

Mrs. Bretton filled the teapot from the urn. She drew nearer the hearth the stuffed blue chair—hers by right, yet prepared as if for an habitual usurper. And when that one came up the stairs—soon, after some necessary attention to the disorder of a wild wet night—and strode in—

“Is it you, Graham?” she asked, hiding a glad smile under curt speech.

“Who else should it be, mamma?” demanded the unpunctual, taking possession irreverently of the abdicated throne.

“Don’t you deserve cold tea, for being late?”

“I shall not get my deserts, for the urn sings cheerily.”

“Wheel yourself to the table, lazy boy. No seat will serve you but mine. If you had one spark of a sense of propriety, you would always leave that chair for the Old Lady.”

“So I should—only the dear Old Lady persists in leaving it for me.” He looked towards me. “How is your patient, mamma?”

“Will she come forward and speak for herself?” said Mrs. Bretton, turning to my corner.

At the invitation I came forward. Graham rose courteously. He stood tall by the hearth, the very figure his mother’s pride would naturally fasten upon and feed. He looked at me with concern not unkindly worn, as if his mind had been with me since the night before.

“So you are come down,” he said. “You must be better then—much better. I scarcely expected we should meet thus, or here. I was alarmed last night; and if I had not been forced to hurry away to a dying patient, I certainly would not have left you. But my mother herself is something of a doctress, and Martha an excellent nurse. I saw the case was a fainting-fit, not necessarily dangerous. What brought it on I have yet to learn, and all particulars. Meantime, I trust you really do feel better?”

“Much better,” I answered calmly. “Much better, I thank you, Dr. John.”

Struggles with one’s own nature look, to the bystander, like fruitless wrestling in a locked room: the door is shut, the noise muffled, the victory uncertain. Yet they do their quiet work. They tilt the smallest action, as a cook’s thumb turns a knife-blade, so that what is cut falls clean instead of ragged; they give conduct that turn which Reason approves, and Feeling—faithful, vehement Feeling—so often resists. They smooth the outer face of life; they keep the surface water from breaking into perpetual chop, so that the common eye, which never dives, may call the whole sea calm. What lies under—leave it with God. Your equal, frail as you, is no fit judge of what he cannot sound. Carry the secret to the Maker who gave it; show Him the spirit’s bruises; ask how to endure pains appointed; kneel and pray for light where the lamp will not burn, for strength where muscle fails, for patience when need has scraped you to the bone.

For sometimes—at some hour, not perhaps your hour; in some shape, not perhaps the beloved shape you dared to dream—help comes down, and the waters stir. Long are Heaven’s times. An angel’s going and returning may seem to mortal sight a circle wide enough to enring ages; the cycle may clasp unnumbered generations; and meanwhile dust kindles into brief suffering life and, through pain, drops again to dust—perishing out of memory, and then again perishing. To how many maimed and mourning millions is the first and sole messenger the one the East names Azrael?

In the morning I tried to rise; I attempted to dress. The mere act of lifting my arms and fastening a hook cost me as if I were tying knots in wet rope with numb fingers. Between each effort I drank from the carafe on the washstand—cold water, clean and sharp as a blade; I swallowed it for courage, as one takes a mouthful of brine to keep from fainting in a boat. While I stood so, obstinate and shaking, Mrs. Bretton came in upon me.

“Here is an absurdity!” was her greeting; and before I could shape a remonstrance she had me by the shoulders—brisk, prompt, strong in her own decided way, that way once applied to her son and vigorously resisted by him—and in two minutes she had consigned me prisoner to the French bed.

“There you lie till afternoon,” said she. “My boy left orders before he went out that such should be the case; and I can assure you my son is master, and must be obeyed. Presently you shall have breakfast.”

Presently she brought it—brought it herself, not leaving me to any servant. She sat on the bed while I ate, and the room filled with her wholesome presence as a kitchen fills with the warmth of an open oven. There are friends whose eye is a draught in sickness, whose nearness frets; but to me Mrs. Bretton’s look was like a clear window, her voice like a steady clock in the night. What she placed in my hand—tea, bread, a little delicate food—seemed to have, by the very passage through her care, a better savour and a kinder virtue. Her touch communicated reassurance as surely as if it were a spice.

Her lively black eye, her clear brunette cheek, her warm ready hand, her self-reliant mood, her decided bearing, acted on me as the air of some salubrious climate acts on lungs that have been breathing foul rooms. Her son called her “the old lady;” it always surprised me, with pleasure, to note how the alacrity and force of five-and-twenty still breathed from her and around her.

“I would bring my work here,” she said, taking the emptied cup, “and sit with you all day, if that overbearing John Graham had not put his veto on such a proceeding. ‘Now, mamma,’ he said when he went out, ‘take notice, you are not to knock up your god-daughter with gossip,’ and he particularly desired me to keep close to my own quarters and spare you my fine company. He says, Lucy, he thinks you have had a nervous fever, judging from your look—is that so?”

I answered that I hardly knew what to name my ailment, but that I had suffered much, especially in mind. Beyond that I would not go. The details belonged to a part of my existence into which I never expected my godmother to enter. Into what strange region would such a confidence have led that hale, serene nature! The difference between her and me might be figured by that between a stately ship cruising safe on smooth seas, full-manned, full-found, with a captain gay and brave, venturous and provident; and the life-boat that lies most days dry and solitary in an old dark boat-house, only putting to sea when billows run high, when cloud grapples with water, when danger and death divide between them the rule of the deep. Louisa Bretton had never been out of harbour on such a night and in such a scene. Her crew could not conceive it. So the half-drowned life-boat man keeps his own counsel and spins no yarns.

She left me. I lay content. It was good of Graham to remember me before he went out. My day was lonely, but the thought of evening shortened it as surely as a fire shortens a winter twilight. Weakness made rest welcome; and after the morning hours went by—those hours that bring even to the necessarily unoccupied a sense of tasks waiting, of something that ought to be done—when that stirring time was past, and the silent descent of afternoon hushed the housemaid’s steps on stair and landing, I slipped into a dreamy mood, not unpleasant.

My little room seemed like a cave in the sea. Its whiteness and pale green suggested foam and deep water; shell-shaped ornaments blanched on the cornice; the ceiling-angles wore white mouldings like dolphins. Even the one touch of colour, a red satin pin-cushion, looked akin to coral; even the dark shining glass might have reflected a mermaid combing out weed-green hair. When I closed my eyes I heard a gale subside—wind pressing on the house-front like a swell settling on a rock-base; heard it drawn and withdrawn far off like a tide retiring from some upper-world shore, so high above that the rush of its largest waves could sound down in this submarine home only as murmurs and a lullaby.

Evening came. Martha brought a light; with her aid I dressed quickly, stronger now than in the morning, and made my way down to the blue saloon unassisted.

Dr. John had finished his round earlier than usual. As I entered the parlour, his form was the first thing I saw: he stood in the window-recess opposite the door, reading the close type of a newspaper by the dull remainder of day. The fire shone clear, but the lamp on the table was unlit and tea not yet brought up. Mrs. Bretton—my active godmother—lay half-reclined in her deep-cushioned chair, actually lost in a nap; afterwards I learned she had been out in the open air all day, as if the weather were her cordial.

Her son, seeing me, came forward. He trod carefully, that he might not wake the sleeper; and he spoke low—his mellow voice, never sharp, modulated now to soothe rather than startle.

“This is a quiet little château,” he observed, after drawing me to a seat near the casement. “Perhaps you have noticed it in your walks; from the chaussée it is not visible. A mile beyond the Porte de Crécy you turn down a lane, soon an avenue, and that leads you through meadow and shade to the very door. It is not modern—built rather in the old style of the Basse-Ville. A manoir, more than a château. They call it ‘La Terrasse,’ because its front rises from a broad turfed walk, whence steps lead down a grassy slope to the avenue. See—yonder! The moon rises: she looks well through the tree-boles.”

Where, indeed, does the moon not look well? What scene—confined or vast—does her orb not hallow? Rosy, almost fiery, she mounted above a not distant bank; and even while we watched her flushed ascent she cleared into gold, then in a brief space floated up stainless into a now calm sky.

Did moonlight soften or sadden Dr. Bretton? Did it touch him with romance? I think it did. He was not of a sighing mood, yet as he watched, he sighed quietly to himself. I did not need to trace the course of that breath. I knew it was wakened by beauty; I knew it pursued Ginevra.

And because I knew, the thought pressed upon me that it was in some sort my duty to speak the name he meditated. He was ready; his countenance held a teeming plenitude of comment, question, interest, a pressure of language and sentiment checked only—so I thought—by an embarrassment how to begin. To spare him that embarrassment was my best, perhaps my sole use. I had but to utter the idol’s name and love’s tender litany would flow out.

I had found my phrase—“You know that Miss Fanshawe is gone on a tour with the Cholmondeleys”—and was opening my lips, when he scattered my plans by turning to another theme.

“The first thing this morning,” said he—putting sentiment into his pocket, turning from the moon, and sitting down—“I went to the Rue Fossette and told the cuisinière you were safe and in good hands. Do you know I actually found she had not yet discovered your absence from the house? She thought you safe in the great dormitory. With what care you must have been waited on!”

“Oh! all that is very conceivable,” I answered. “Goton could do nothing for me but bring me a little tisane and a crust of bread; and I had rejected both so often during the past week that the good woman grew tired of useless journeys from the dwelling-house kitchen to the school-dormitory, and only came once a day at noon to make my bed. I believe, however, she is good-natured, and would have been delighted to cook me côtelettes de mouton, if I could have eaten them.”

“What did Madame Beck mean by leaving you alone?”

“Madame Beck could not foresee that I should fall ill.”

“Your nervous system bore a good share of the suffering?”

“I am not quite sure what my nervous system is,” said I; “but I was dreadfully low-spirited.”

“Which disables me from helping you by pill or potion. Medicine can give nobody good spirits. My art halts at the threshold of hypochondria; she just looks in and sees a chamber of torture, but can neither say nor do much.
