Cheerful society would be of use; you should be as little alone as possible; you should take plenty of exercise.”

I assented. The remarks sounded right; they bore the safe sanction of custom and the well-worn stamp of use.

Then he recommenced—my health, nervous system included, being to my relief dismissed. “Miss Snowe, is it permitted me to ask what your religion is? Are you a Catholic?”

I looked up, startled. “A Catholic? No. Why suggest such an idea?”

“The manner in which you were consigned to me last night made me doubt.”

“I consigned to you? But indeed I forget. It yet remains for me to learn how I fell into your hands.”

“Under circumstances that puzzled me. I had been in attendance all day yesterday on a case singularly interesting and critical—the disease rare, and its treatment doubtful. I saw a similar and still finer case in a hospital in Paris; but that will not interest you. At last a mitigation of the patient’s most urgent symptoms—acute pain is one of its accompaniments—liberated me, and I set out homeward. My shortest way lay through the Basse-Ville, and as the night was excessively dark, wild, and wet, I took it. In riding past an old church belonging to a community of Béguines, I saw, by a lamp burning over the porch or deep arch of the entrance, a priest lifting some object in his arms. The lamp was bright enough to show the priest’s features clearly, and I recognised him; he is a man I have often met by sick beds of rich and poor, chiefly the latter. He is, I think, a good old man—better than most of his class here—superior in every way, better informed as well as more devoted. Our eyes met; he called on me to stop: what he supported was a woman, fainting or dying. I alighted.

“‘This person is one of your countrywomen,’ he said: ‘save her, if she is not dead.’

“My countrywoman, on examination, turned out to be the English teacher at Madame Beck’s pensionnat. You were perfectly unconscious, perfectly bloodless, nearly cold.

“‘What does it all mean?’ I asked.

“He communicated a curious account: you had been to him that evening at confessional; your exhausted and suffering appearance, coupled with some things you had said—”

“Things I had said? I wonder what things!”

“Awful crimes, no doubt; but he did not tell me what: the seal of the confessional checked his garrulity and my curiosity. Your confidences, however, had not made an enemy of the good father. He was so struck, so sorry you should be out on such a night alone, that he esteemed it a Christian duty to watch you when you quitted the church, and so to manage as not to lose sight of you till you should have reached home. Perhaps the worthy man might, half unconsciously, have blended in this proceeding a little of the subtlety of his class; it might have been his resolve to learn the locality of your home—did you impart that in your confession?”

“I did not,” I said. “On the contrary, I carefully avoided the shadow of any indication. And as to my confession, Dr. John, you will think me mad for taking such a step, but I could not help it. I suppose it was all the fault of what you call my nervous system. I cannot put the case into words. My days and nights were grown intolerable. A cruel sense of desolation pained my mind: a feeling that would make its way—rush out—or kill me; like”—and here I chose a phrase he would understand—“the current which passes through the heart, and which, if aneurism or any morbid cause obstruct its natural channels, seeks abnormal outlet. I wanted companionship; I wanted friendship; I wanted counsel. I could find none of these in closet or chamber, so I went and sought them in church and confessional. As to what I said, it was no confidence, no narrative. I have done nothing wrong: my life has not been active enough for any dark deed, either of romance or reality. All I poured out was a dreary, desperate complaint.”

“Lucy, you ought to travel for about six months: why, your calm nature is growing quite excitable! Confound Madame Beck! Has the little buxom widow no bowels, to condemn her best teacher to solitary confinement?”

“It was not Madame Beck’s fault,” I replied. “It is no living being’s fault, and I won’t hear any one blamed.”

“Who is in the wrong, then, Lucy?”

“Me—Dr. John—me; and a great abstraction on whose wide shoulders I like to lay the mountains of blame they were sculptured to bear: me and Fate.”

“‘Me’ must take better care in future,” said Dr. John, smiling, I suppose, at my grammar. “Change of air—change of scene; those are my prescriptions. But to return to our muttons. As yet Père Silas, with all his tact—they say he is a Jesuit—is no wiser than you choose him to be; for, instead of returning to the Rue Fossette, your fevered wanderings—there must have been high fever—”

“No, Dr. John: the fever took its turn that night. Now, don’t make out that I was delirious; I know differently.”

“Good! You were as collected as myself at this moment, no doubt. Your wanderings had taken an opposite direction to the pensionnat. Near the Béguinage, amidst the stress of flood and gust, and in the perplexity of darkness, you had swooned and fallen. The priest came to your succour; the physician, as we have seen, supervened. Between us we procured a fiacre and brought you here. Père Silas, old as he is, would have carried you upstairs and laid you on that couch himself. He would certainly have remained with you till suspended animation was restored; and so should I, but at that juncture a hurried messenger arrived from the dying patient I had scarcely left—the last duties were called for: the physician’s last visit and the priest’s last rite; extreme unction could not be deferred. Père Silas and I departed together. My mother was spending the evening abroad. We gave you in charge to Martha, leaving directions, which it seems she followed successfully. Now—are you a Catholic?”

“Not yet,” said I, with a smile. “And never let Père Silas know where I live, or he will try to convert me. But give him my best and truest thanks when you see him; and if ever I get rich I will send him money for his charities. See, Dr. John—your mother wakes; you ought to ring for tea.”

He rang; and as Mrs. Bretton sat up—astonished and indignant at herself for the indulgence to which she had succumbed, fully prepared to deny that she had slept at all—her son came gaily to the attack.

“Hushaby, mamma! Sleep again. You look the picture of innocence in your slumbers.”

“My slumbers, John Graham! What are you talking about? You know I never do sleep by day: it was the slightest doze possible.”

“Exactly! A seraph’s gentle lapse—a fairy’s dream. Mamma, under such circumstances you always remind me of Titania.”

“That is because you yourself are so like Bottom.”

“Miss Snowe—did you ever hear anything like mamma’s wit? She is a most sprightly woman of her size and age.”

“Keep your compliments to yourself, sir, and do not neglect your own size, which seems to me a good deal on the increase. Lucy, has he not rather the air of an incipient John Bull? He used to be slender as an eel, and now I fancy in him a sort of heavy dragoon bent—a beef-eater tendency. Graham, take notice! If you grow fat I disown you.”

“As if you could sooner disown your own personality! I am indispensable to the old lady’s happiness, Lucy. She would pine away in green and yellow melancholy if she had not my six feet of iniquity to scold. It keeps her lively; it maintains the wholesome ferment of her spirits.”

They stood opposite each other, one on each side of the fireplace. Their words were not very tender; their looks atoned. The best treasure of Mrs. Bretton’s life was casketed in her son’s bosom; her dearest pulse throbbed in his heart. As to him—of course another love shared his feelings with filial love; and no doubt, as the new passion was the latest born, he allotted it in his emotions Benjamin’s portion.

Ginevra. Ginevra. Did Mrs. Bretton yet know at whose feet her own young idol had laid his homage? Would she approve? I could not tell; but I could guess: if she knew Miss Fanshawe’s conduct towards Graham—her alternations between coldness and coaxing, repulse and allurement; if she could suspect the pain with which she had tried him; if she could have seen, as I had seen, his fine spirits harassed, his inferior preferred, his subordinate made the instrument of humiliation—then Mrs. Bretton would have pronounced Ginevra imbecile, or perverted, or both. Well—I thought so too.

That second evening passed as sweetly as the first—more sweetly indeed. We enjoyed a smoother interchange of thought; old troubles were not reverted to; acquaintance was better cemented. I felt easier, happier, more at home. And that night, instead of crying myself asleep, I went down to dreamland by a path bordered with pleasant thoughts.

During the first days of my sojourn at the Terrace, Graham never drew his chair into the little province where I sat with my work. He traversed the room in long, steady measures, as if keeping time to some inward march; he spoke to his mother with his usual ease, laughed when she smiled, and yet—under all that—there was a restraint, fine as the film that skins cooling broth, telling you the pot has been watched and worried over.

I watched him too. I listened—not only with the ear, but with that sharper sense which catches what is going to be said before it is spoken. I expected one name to break from him as naturally as steam from a kettle: Miss Fanshawe’s. I kept myself ready—sympathy set out like cups and saucers upon a tray, patience standing by like a maid in an orderly kitchen, waiting the bell.

At last, after a struggle I saw and could not but respect, he ventured on the subject as if it were a dish to be offered with some ceremony, not flung down.

“Your friend is travelling, I hear?”

Friend. The word, in his mouth, had the softening of butter on hot bread. In mine, it stuck like an unrisen crust; yet I let it pass. It was not my business to contradict him in his chosen seasoning of things.

“Friend?” I asked, as if I did not know whom he meant.

He had sat down at my work-table. His fingers, restless as if they must be doing something, took up a reel of thread and began to unwind it with a heedless extravagance—drawing out yards as one might spill flour upon a clean board. It was a small waste, but it betrayed him: he could not be still.

“Ginevra—Miss Fanshawe—has accompanied the Cholmondeleys, they say, on a tour in the south of France?”

“She has.”

“Do you correspond?”

“It will astonish you to hear that I never once thought of applying for that privilege.”

“You have seen her letters?”

“Yes; several to her uncle.”

“They will not be deficient in wit and naïveté,” he said, as if tasting the phrase. “There is so much sparkle, and so little art in her soul.”

“She writes comprehensively enough when she writes to M. de Bassompierre: he who runs may read.”

He did not hear—would not hear—the plainness under my words; he was intent on the pleasing part, as if choosing only the candied fruit out of a cake and leaving the currants of truth behind.

“And her handwriting? It must be pretty—light—ladylike?”

It was. I said so.

“I verily believe,” he went on, “that all she does is well done.”

I did not echo him quickly enough, so he added, almost eagerly, “You, who know her—could you name a point in which she is deficient?”

“She does several things very well.” In thought, I subjoined: flirtation among the rest; the art of making a man’s peace depend upon her smile, and his hunger upon her caprice.

“When do you suppose she will return to town?” he asked next, as if his question could summon her like a bell calls to the supper-table.

“Pardon me, Dr. John, I must explain. You honour me too much in ascribing to me an intimacy with Miss Fanshawe I have not the felicity to enjoy. I have never been the depositary of her plans and secrets. You will find her particular friends in another sphere than mine: amongst the Cholmondeleys, for instance.”

He understood me—wrongly. He thought I spoke as one slighted speaks: that I had been put aside, and that pride had taken the tone of indifference.

“Excuse her,” he said, in that tone of generous excuse with which he would cover any fault in her, as one lays a white cloth over a stain. “Judge her indulgently. The glitter of fashion misleads her; but she will soon find these people hollow, and return to you with augmented attachment and confirmed trust. I know something of the Cholmondeleys—superficial, showy, selfish people. Depend on it, at heart Ginevra values you beyond a score of such.”

“You are very kind,” I answered briefly.

A denial burned on my tongue, but I quenched it. I submitted—reader, I submitted—to be taken for the discarded confidante of Miss Fanshawe, humbled, cast off, and pining. It was a bitter posture; the very air in that room seemed to taste of it.

“Yet,” he pursued, “while I comfort you, I cannot take the same consolation to myself. I cannot hope she will do me justice. De Hamal is most worthless, yet I fear he pleases her. Wretched delusion!”

Then it was as if some hand, long and steadily pressing down on a lid, slipped—only for a second—and the steam of all my pent endurance rushed out. Illness and weakness had made me brittle; the least tap broke me.

“Dr. Bretton,” I cried, “there is no delusion like your own. On all points but one you are a man—frank, healthful, right-thinking, clear-sighted: on this exceptional point you are but a slave. I declare, where Miss Fanshawe is concerned, you merit no respect; nor have you mine.”

I rose and left the room, heated and ashamed even while I was hot.

That little scene occurred in the morning. In the evening I met him again, and at once knew mischief had been done. He was not made of common clay. His nature had breadth and strength in its main lines; but in the finishing there was workmanship almost feminine in delicacy—such fineness as you do not suspect in a man until you have brushed, too sharply, against the hidden nerve and felt him shrink. He was not quick at reading another’s feeling by instinct; but he could feel his own with an exquisite exactness. The sympathetic faculty—divining another’s pain before it is spoken—was not his predominant gift; yet he was kind, generous, open-handed: show him need, and his palm offered itself; put grief into words, and he listened. Only do not demand miracles of intuition.

That night, when he entered and the lamplight met his face, I saw the whole mechanism of him as plainly as if a cover had been lifted. To be called “slave,” and told that, on a point, he merited no respect, had struck him as a sharp knife strikes under the rib. He did not deny it—indeed, I think his candid mind turned it over and over, as if looking for the part in him that had earned the name. His gravity—perhaps his coldness—showed itself to me and to his mother; but on his countenance there was neither malice nor rancour nor littleness. Even depressed, it had the best beauty of a man: not the beauty of smiling, but of being able to bear.

When I placed his chair at the table—hastening to do it myself, before the servant could—when I gave him his tea with trembling care, he said, “Thank you, Lucy,” in as kind a tone as ever my ear had owned for comfort.

For me, there was only one course. I must expiate my vehemence, or I should not sleep. I could not endure a household where the air between two people is cut into by a quarrel: it is like trying to eat in a kitchen where a knife keeps striking the board—tap, tap—till appetite turns to nausea. School solitude, conventual silence, any stagnation seemed preferable to living at odds with Dr. John.

As for Ginevra—let her take her silver wings, let her mount where she pleased, let her set up her little throne among the stars of his imagination; never again, if I could help it, would I dispute the constellation he chose to worship.

Long I tried to catch his eye. Again and again it met mine, and then withdrew—no defiance in it, only that quiet withdrawal of one who waits to be approached, and will not force himself. After tea he sat reading, sad and still. I wanted to go and sit near him, as one draws close to the hearth when the room is chilly; yet I feared, absurdly, that he would show hostility if I ventured.

His mother left the room. The absence of her presence made the silence louder, as the hush after a door shuts makes you hear the ticking of a clock. At last regret grew insupportable, and I murmured, “Dr. Bretton.”

He looked up from his book. His eyes were not cold; his mouth had no curl of scorn. He was ready to hear me. His spirit was too mellow—of too generous a vintage—to sour in one thunder-clap.

“Dr. Bretton, forgive my hasty words: do—do forgive them.”

He smiled the moment I spoke.

“Perhaps I deserved them, Lucy. If you don’t respect me, I am sure it is because I am not respectable. I fear I am an awkward fool: I must manage badly in some way; for where I wish to please, it seems I don’t please.”

“Of that you cannot be sure; and even if such be the case, is it the fault of your character, or of another’s perceptions? But now—let me unsay what I said in anger. In one thing, and in all things, I deeply respect you. If you think scarcely enough of yourself, and too much of others, what is that but an excellence?”

“Can I think too much of Ginevra?”

“I believe you may; you believe you can’t. Let us agree to differ. Let me be pardoned: that is what I ask.”

“Do you think I cherish ill-will for one warm word?”

“I see you do not and cannot; but just say, ‘Lucy, I forgive you!’ Say that, to ease me of the heart-ache.”

“Put away your heart-ache, as I will put away mine; for you wounded me a little, Lucy. Now, when the pain is gone, I more than forgive: I feel grateful, as to a sincere well-wisher.”

“I am your sincere well-wisher: you are right.”

So our quarrel ended.

If, as you read on, you should find my opinion of Dr. John shifting, pardon the seeming inconsistency. I can only give the feeling as I felt it then, and show the character as it appeared when discovered. He proved his fineness by being kinder to me after that misunderstanding than before. The very incident which, by my fears, should have estranged us, changed our relation otherwise than I had anticipated.

An invisible coldness—very slight, very transparent, but very chill—had long glazed the medium through which we spoke. It was like a thin screen of ice over water: you could see through it, yet it kept you from touching. Those few warm words—warm only with anger—breathed on the frostwork. About that time it gave its little crackling note of dissolution.

From that day, so long as we continued friends, he no longer held to ceremony in discourse with me. He seemed to know that if he would talk of himself—above all, of what most absorbed him—my expectation would always be answered, my will—strangely—always ready to yield.

It follows, of course, that I continued to hear much of “Ginevra.”

“Ginevra!” He thought her so fair, so good; he spoke so lovingly of her charms, her sweetness, her innocence, that, in spite of my plain, prose knowledge of the reality, a reflected glow began to settle on her image even for me—as lamplight makes common china look like porcelain. Still, I confess, he often talked nonsense; but I strove to be unfailingly patient. I had learnt my lesson. I had learnt how severe the pain was to me of crossing him, grieving him, disappointing him. In a strange, new sense, I grew selfish—selfish for the pleasure of indulging his mood, of being pliant to his will, as one gives way in kneading dough: press it too roughly and it resists; work with it, and it becomes smooth beneath your hands.

He seemed to me most absurd when he obstinately doubted, and desponded, about his power to win at last Miss Fanshawe’s preference. The fancy rooted itself in my mind more stubbornly than ever that she was only coquetting to goad him, and that, at heart, she coveted his looks and words as greedily as she coveted anything that glittered.

Sometimes, in spite of my resolution to bear and hear, he harassed me. There was a gall-and-honey pleasure in the bearing; yet now and then he struck so on the flint of what firmness I owned that it emitted fire.

One day, to still his impatience, I asserted with decision that I felt positive Miss Fanshawe must eventually intend to accept him.

“Positive!” he echoed. “It was easy to say so; but had I any grounds for such assurance?”

“The best grounds.”

“Now, Lucy—do tell me what!”

“You know them as well as I; and, knowing them, Dr. John, it amazes me you should not repose the frankest confidence in her fidelity. To doubt, under the circumstances, is almost to insult.”

“Now you are beginning to speak fast and breathe short,” he said; and there was eagerness under his banter. “Speak a little faster and breathe a little shorter, till you have given an explanation—a full explanation. I must have it.”

“You shall, Dr. John. In some cases you are a lavish, generous man: you are a worshipper ever ready with the votive offering. Should Père Silas ever convert you, you will give him abundance of alms for his poor; you will supply his altar with tapers; the shrine of your favourite saint you will do your best to enrich. Ginevra, Dr. John—”

“Hush!” he interposed quickly. “Don’t go on.”

“Hush, I will not: and go on I will. Ginevra has had her hands filled from your hands more times than I can count. You have sought for her the costliest flowers; you have busied your brain devising gifts the most delicate—such as one would have thought only a woman could have imagined; and, besides, Miss Fanshawe owns a set of ornaments to purchase which your generosity must have verged on extravagance.”

A flush—sudden, deep—ran over the face of her admirer; a modesty she herself had never shown in the matter rose in him for her, as if he would veil what she had paraded. He snipped at a skein of silk with my scissors, cutting too sharply, as though the blades could shear off my words.

“Nonsense!” he said. “I offered them to please myself. I felt she did me a favour in accepting them.”

“She did more than a favour, Dr. John: she pledged her honour that she would make some return; and if she cannot pay you in affection, she ought to hand out a business-like equivalent—in the shape of some rouleaux of gold pieces.”

“But you don’t understand her,” he persisted, with the obstinacy of a man defending what he loves against the very evidence of his senses. “She is far too disinterested to care for my gifts, and too simple-minded to know their value.”

I laughed out—could not help it. I had heard her price each jewel as neatly as a tradesman counts coins; I knew her embarrassments, her schemes, her perpetual measuring of worth, her efforts to realise supplies—money, its want and its pursuit, had been, young as she was, the most frequent and favourite stimulus of her thoughts for years.

He went on, as if my laugh had not sounded.

“You should have seen her whenever I have laid on her lap some trifle—so cool, so unmoved. No eagerness to take, not even pleasure in contemplating. Just from amiable reluctance to grieve me, she would permit the bouquet to lie beside her, and perhaps consent to bear it away. Or, if I achieved the fastening of a bracelet on her ivory arm—however pretty the trinket might be (and I always carefully chose what seemed to me pretty, and what of course was not valueless)—the glitter never dazzled her bright eyes; she would hardly cast one look on my gift.”

“Then, of course, not valuing it, she would unloose and return it to you?”

“No; for such a repulse she was too good-natured. She would consent to seem to forget what I had done, and retain the offering with ladylike quiet and easy oblivion. Under such circumstances, how can a man build on acceptance of his presents as a favourable symptom? For my part, were I to offer her all I have, and she to take it—such is her incapacity to be swayed by sordid considerations—I should not venture to believe the transaction advanced me one step.”

“Dr. John,” I began, “love is blind—”

But at that instant a blue, subtle ray shot sideways from his eye—quick as the glint on a knife lifted from the board. It reminded me of old days; it reminded me of his picture. It half led me to think that part, at least, of his professed belief in Miss Fanshawe’s naïveté was assumed; it made me doubtfully conjecture that, in spite of his passion for her beauty, his appreciation of her foibles might possibly be less mistaken—more clear-sighted—than his general language implied.

After all, it might have been only a chance look—at best the token of a momentary impression. Chance or intention, real or imagined, it closed the conversation.

My stay at La Terrasse ran two weeks beyond the end of the holidays, as if time itself had been coaxed into a gentler simmer. It was Mrs. Bretton who managed it—quietly, deftly, like a careful housekeeper who knows exactly how long a broth must stand off the fire if it is to clear. Her son, after one look at me, delivered his verdict with that decisive kindness of his: I was not yet fit to be sent back to the Rue Fossette, to the close air and constant vigilance of the pensionnat. Mrs. Bretton drove into town, spoke with the directress, and returned having won me the indulgence on the plea of rest, of sun, of recovery made complete only by change.

With that mercy, however, came an attention I could have spared: a ceremonious visit from Madame Beck.

She arrived one bright day in a hired carriage, and I knew—before I saw her—that she had come to examine, as one inspects a kitchen one has never trusted, the place where Dr. John lived. The château’s pleasant rise, the clipped order of the garden, the neatness within, all exceeded whatever she had imagined, and she praised everything with a brisk lavishness, as if compliments were coin she paid out to purchase knowledge. The blue salon became, on her tongue, “une pièce magnifique.” She congratulated me—profusely—on my friends, “tellement dignes, aimables, et respectables,” and even tossed me a small, well-turned compliment as one throws a sugared almond to a child to keep it sweet-tempered.

When Dr. John came in she ran to him with a spring, and in a moment had lit a whole Catherine-wheel of rapid French: felicitation upon his “château,” upon “madame sa mère, la digne châtelaine,” upon his looks—freshened, as they always were, by that amused good-nature with which he listened to her florid speech. She shone; she dazzled; she seemed all sparkle and good will.

Half for mischief and half to ask a question about school business, I followed her out and looked into the fiacre after she had taken her seat and the door was shut. In that mere breath of time, the change was complete. The gay wheel had stopped. She sat rigid—stern as a judge, grave as a sage—her mouth a straight line, her eyes sharpened into calculation. Strange little woman: froth on the surface, iron below.

When I returned, I teased Dr. John on Madame Beck’s devotion. He laughed—laughed as if it were excellent sport. His eyes flashed with that lively humour which made his company so light and warm when he could forget Miss Fanshawe; and he repeated Madame’s speeches, mimicking her voluble delivery with such accuracy that even her trills and quick emphases seemed to flutter about the room like ribbons.

Those days at La Terrasse were sunlight in the plainest sense: light on the floor, light on the walls, light in the mind. I had often thought how, when little Georgette Beck was convalescent, I would carry her in my arms and pace the garden for hours beneath a certain wall where vines hung heavy. The southern sun ripened those grapes slowly, working sweetness into them as patient hands work seasoning into a stew; and it did something similar for the child—warmed her thin frame, tinted her cheeks, coaxed her breath into steadiness.

There are tempers like that sun—bland, genial, nourishing; in their presence the poor in spirit recover a kind of appetite for living. Both Mrs. Bretton and her son had this gift, and they bestowed it without fuss, as naturally as a table is spread when one expects a guest. Each day, some little plan would be proposed that ended in wholesome enjoyment—a drive, a walk, some modest visit that turned, by their tact, into pleasure.

Dr. John’s time was full—his engagements lay about him like a day’s work laid out upon a board—yet by system he sliced it into order and always contrived an hour of liberty. I saw him work hard, but rarely over-driven; never fretful, never confused. What he did seemed done with the ease of ample strength, and with a cheerfulness that was like good air. Under his guidance, in that one happy fortnight, I learned more of Villette and its environs than in the whole eight months before—streets, quarters, people, names of places I had never heard, doors I had never imagined opened on anything worth seeing. He spoke, too, not in cold generalities, not in borrowed book-phrases, but in exact particulars; his talk had the relish of something freshly prepared, not reheated. He observed character as a practiced palate distinguishes flavours—subtly, patiently, with recognition of what others missed. Before my eyes his disposition seemed to unfold into a new day.

His mother was benevolent; he, even more so. When I accompanied him into the Basse-Ville—the crowded, poor quarter—I saw that his errands there were not only those of a physician. He moved among the wretched as if it were natural to him to carry relief. The lower orders liked him. In hospitals, his patients welcomed him with something like enthusiasm.

And yet—here I must stop myself. It is easy to slip from faithful narrator into partial eulogist. Dr. John was not perfect—no more than I am. His humanity showed itself in every hour. A god could not have his cruel vanity, nor his occasional levity; no immortal could share that temporary oblivion of all but the present, that passing passion for it—less in material indulgence than in a selfish extraction of whatever it could yield to feed his masculine self-love. He delighted in nourishing that hungry sentiment, careless of cost, indifferent to the price others paid for its provender.

If the reader finds contradiction between the public and private Graham Bretton—out of doors and by the fireside—let it be noted both portraits are true. In public, modest, earnest, almost unconscious of himself; at home, aware of what he was and had, pleased by homage, reckless at times in exciting it, vain in receiving it.

It was hardly possible to oblige him quietly. If you thought you had made some trifle for his use unseen, and that—like other men—he would take it as a matter of course, he would prove by a smile and a word that his eye had followed the work from beginning to end. It pleased him to be served, and he let the pleasure show—brightly, frankly.

And he liked to discharge what he called debts. If his mother worked for him, he repaid her with an overflow of spirits—teasing, loving, gay. If he discovered my hand in any small service, he planned, by way of recompense, some recreation.

It astonished me how completely he possessed Villette: not merely its open streets, but its galleries, salles, cabinets; every door shutting in something worth seeing seemed to yield to him like “Open, Sesame.” Science never held my head; but art drew me with a blind, fond instinct, and I loved picture-galleries—especially to be left there alone. In company, a wretched idiosyncrasy prevented me from seeing much or feeling anything; talk pressed on me like a tight bodice, and in half an hour I would be knocked up with fatigue and mental incapacity. Dr. Bretton, cicerone after my own heart, took me early before the rooms filled, left me for two or three hours, and returned when his business allowed. Then I was happy—not always in admiring, but in examining, questioning, forming conclusions.

At first there was a struggle between Will and Power. Will demanded I praise what was orthodox to admire; Power groaned its inability to pay the tax. I sneered at myself, spurred my taste like a dull horse, tried to refine, to whet my zest; but the more I bullied my senses, the more stubbornly they refused false homage. Conscientious admiration produced only a wonderful fatigue. In time I concluded I might dispense with the labour, and sank into a calm luxury before ninety-nine out of a hundred frames.

It seemed to me good pictures were as scarce as good books. Standing before certain celebrated chef-d’œuvres I no longer trembled to say, “This is not like nature.” Nature’s daylight never wore those colours; skies were not laid out in turbid indigo with weeds plastered on for trees. Several complacent fat women, admirably finished, struck me as no goddesses at all. There were scores of little Flemish pieces—marvellously wrought—like fashion-plates for costumes; industry whimsically misapplied. And yet here and there a fragment of truth satisfied the conscience; a gleam of real light cheered the eye. Nature broke through in a mountain snow-storm; her glory shone in a southern day; a portrait held a look that proved clear insight; a face in an historical painting startled one by its living likeness, as if genius had breathed and the paint remembered. These exceptions I loved; they grew dear as friends.

One day, at a quiet early hour, I found myself almost alone in a gallery where a single picture, of portentous size, sat enthroned in the best light. A cordon guarded it; a cushioned bench was placed before it for worshipping connoisseurs who, having gazed themselves off their feet, might finish sitting. The canvas seemed to consider itself queen of the collection.

It represented a woman larger than life. I calculated, with a coolness almost professional, that if this lady were put upon a scale meant for bulk commodities, she would turn fourteen to sixteen stone. She was very well fed; much butcher’s meat—bread, vegetables, liquids besides—must have gone to build that breadth, that wealth of muscle, that affluence of flesh. She lay half-reclined on a couch in broad noon-day, as if indolence were her office. Yet she looked strong enough to do the work of two plain cooks; no weak spine pleaded for the sofa. She ought to have been standing, or sitting bolt upright, and—moreover—decently dressed. Out of an abundance of drapery—seven-and-twenty yards, I should think—she had contrived an inefficiency of garment. Around her lay untidiness without excuse: pots and pans—vases and goblets, if you will—rolled on the foreground; a rubbish of flowers, and an absurd disorder of heavy curtains smothering couch and floor. The catalogue named it “Cleopatra.”

I sat and wondered, taking advantage of the bench more for rest than devotion, and thinking that while roses, gold cups, jewels were prettily painted, the whole was enormous claptrap. The room began to fill, though I scarcely noticed; it mattered little. I soon tired of the huge, dark gipsy-queen and turned, for refreshment, to some exquisite little still-lifes hung modestly beneath her coarse splendour: wild-flowers, wild-fruit, mossy wood-nests; eggs casketed like pearls seen through clear green sea-water. They seemed to carry their own hush with them, the quiet one hears in hedgerows—small sounds, true sounds: a leaf brushing another, a bird’s foot in grass.

Suddenly a light tap touched my shoulder. I started and met a face bent close to mine—frowning, almost shocked.

“Que faites-vous ici?” said a voice.

“Mais, Monsieur, je m’amuse.”

“Vous vous amusez! et à quoi, s’il vous plait? Mais d’abord, faites-moi le plaisir de vous lever; prenez mon bras, et allons de l’autre côté.”

I did precisely as I was bid. It was M. Paul Emanuel, returned from Rome and now—if possible—less tolerant of insubordination than before. His presence altered the air; even the gallery’s murmur seemed to shift, as if his small steps had changed the rhythm of sound in the room.

“Permit me to conduct you to your party,” he said, as we crossed.

“I have no party.”

“You are not alone?”

“Yes, Monsieur.”

“Did you come here unaccompanied?”

“No, Monsieur. Dr. Bretton brought me here.”

“Dr. Bretton and Madame his mother, of course?”

“No; only Dr. Bretton.”

“And he told you to look at that picture?”

“By no means; I found it out for myself.”

His hair was shorn close as raven-down; had it been longer I think it would have bristled. I began to understand his drift and felt a certain pleasure in staying cool and letting him heat.

“Astounding insular audacity!” he cried. “Singulières femmes que ces Anglaises!”

“What is the matter, Monsieur?”

“Matter! How dare you, a young person, sit down with the self-possession of a garçon and look at that picture?”

“It is a very ugly picture; but I cannot see why I should not look at it.”

“Bon! bon! Speak no more. But you ought not to be here alone.”

“If I have no society—no party, as you say? And what does it signify whether I am alone or accompanied? Nobody meddles with me.”

“Taisez-vous, et asseyez-vous là—là!” He set a chair with emphasis in a dull corner before a series of especially dreary frames. “Mais, Mademoiselle, asseyez-vous, et ne bougez pas—entendez-vous?—jusqu’à ce qu’on vienne vous chercher, ou que je vous donne la permission.”

“Quel triste coin!” I cried. “Et quels laids tableaux!”

Laids indeed: four pictures called in the catalogue “La vie d’une femme.” They were flat, dead, pale, formal as boiled starch. The first, “Jeune Fille,” came out of a church-door with a missal in hand; prim dress, eyes cast down, mouth pursed—the image of a precocious little hypocrite. The second, “Mariée,” knelt at a prie-dieu with a long white veil and hands plastered finger to finger, showing the whites of her eyes in a way that exasperated. The third, “Jeune Mère,” drooped over a clayey, puffy baby with a face like an unwholesome full moon. The fourth, “Veuve,” a black woman holding a black little girl by the hand, both studiously contemplating an elegant French monument in some Père la Chaise corner. These four “Anges” were grim and grey as burglars, cold and vapid as ghosts. What women to live with—insincere, ill-humoured, bloodless, brainless nonentities! As bad in their way as the indolent gipsy-giantess Cleopatra in hers.

My eyes would not stay long chained to such vapour. By degrees I veered round and surveyed the gallery.

By then a crowd had gathered round the Lioness from whose vicinage I had been banished; nearly half were ladies. M. Paul afterwards told me these were “des dames,” and therefore it was proper for them to contemplate what no “demoiselle” ought to glance at. I told him plainly I could not agree; the doctrine had no sense. With his usual absolutism, he requested my silence and, in the same breath, denounced my rashness and ignorance. A more despotic little man never filled a professor’s chair.

I noticed, too, that he looked at Cleopatra himself at his ease, and for a long while. Yet he did not neglect to glance toward me from time to time—making sure, I suppose, that I remained obedient in my dull corner and did not break bounds.

By-and-by he accosted me again.

Had I not been ill, he wished to know. He understood I had.

“Yes; but now I am quite well.”

Where had I spent the vacation?

“Chiefly in the Rue Fossette; partly with Madame Bretton.”

He had heard I was left alone there—was it so?

“Not quite alone: Marie Broc was with me.”

He shrugged; expressions crossed his face in quick succession, contradictory as gusts of wind. Marie Broc was well known to him; she never failed to stir in him a conflict between taste and justice. Her appearance, manners, and often unmanageable temper irritated him and roused antipathy—he was too apt to feel that when his will was thwarted. Yet her misfortunes appealed strongly to his compassion, a claim his nature could not deny. Thus, almost daily, impatience and disgust fought pity and fairness; and, to his credit, the better feelings usually prevailed. But when the former did prevail, M. Paul showed a terrible phase: his passions ran deep; his aversions and attachments were vivid; and though he held them in check with force, their vehemence was not hidden from an observer. Ordinary minds were prone to fear him; yet to fear him was an error. Nothing drove him nearer frenzy than the tremor of distrust; nothing soothed him like confidence tempered with gentleness. But to offer that required understanding, and his nature was rarely comprehended.

“How did you get on with Marie Broc?” he asked, after a silence.

“Monsieur, I did my best; but it was terrible to be alone with her.”

“You have then a weak heart. You lack courage; and perhaps charity. Yours are not the qualities that might constitute a Sister of Mercy.”

He was religious, in his way: the self-denying part of his faith commanded his homage.

“I don’t know. I took as good care of her as I could; but when her aunt came to fetch her away, it was a great relief.”

“Ah! you are an egotist. There are women who have nursed hospitals-full of similar unfortunates. You could not do that?”

“Could Monsieur do it himself?”

“Women worthy the name ought infinitely to surpass our coarse, fallible, self-indulgent sex in such duties.”

“I washed her, kept her clean, fed her, tried to amuse her; but she made mouths at me instead of speaking.”

“You think you did great things?”

“No; but as great as I could do.”

“Then limited are your powers; for in tending one idiot you fell sick.”

“Not with that, Monsieur. I had a nervous fever: my mind was ill.”

“Vraiment! Vous valez peu de chose. You are not cast in an heroic mould; your courage will not sustain you in solitude; it merely gives you the temerity to gaze with sang-froid at pictures of Cleopatra.”

It would have been easy to show anger at his teasing hostility; but I had never yet been angry with him, and I would not begin then.

“Cleopatra!” I repeated quietly. “Monsieur, too, has been looking at Cleopatra; what does he think of her?”

“Cela ne vaut rien,” he replied. “Une femme superbe—une taille d’impératrice, des formes de Junon; mais une personne dont je ne voudrais ni pour femme, ni pour fille, ni pour sœur. Aussi vous ne jetterez plus un seul coup d’œil de sa côté.”

“But I have looked at her a great many times while Monsieur has been talking: I can see her quite well from this corner.”

“Turn to the wall and study your four pictures of a woman’s life.”

“Excuse me, M. Paul; they are too hideous. But if you admire them, allow me to vacate my seat and leave you to their contemplation.”

“Mademoiselle,” he said, and a half-smile—or what he meant for a smile—twitched across his face, grim and hurried. “You nurslings of Protestantism astonish me. You unguarded Englishwomen walk calmly amid red-hot ploughshares and escape burning. I believe if some of you were thrown into Nebuchadnezzar’s hottest furnace you would issue forth untouched by the smell of fire.”

“Will Monsieur have the goodness to move an inch to one side?”

“How! At what are you gazing now? You are not recognising an acquaintance amongst that group of jeunes gens?”

“I think so—yes. I see there a person I know.”

One morning my godmother came into my room with the air she wore when she meant to be obeyed before one had even guessed there was to be an order.

“Open your drawers, Lucy; let me see your dresses.”

I drew them out and laid them before her—my scant, sober little store—each gown smelling faintly of lavender and the shut-wardrobe darkness in which it had lived. She turned them over with quick, decisive fingers, as if she were weighing flour by touch.

“That will do,” she said at last. “You must have a new one.”

She went, and almost before the air had settled back into quiet she returned with a dressmaker. I stood like a patient board while tape measured my shoulders, my waist, my length; the woman’s pins flashed and bit, and the chalk made pale, dusty kisses on the cloth.

“I mean,” said Mrs. Bretton, “to follow my own taste, and to have my own way in this little matter.”

Two days afterwards the box came home, and when the lid lifted there lay—pink.

“A pink dress!” I cried, the words breaking from me as if they were an alarm. “That is not for me.” I felt I might almost as soon have been asked to go abroad in some dazzling, ceremonial costume, stiff with embroidery, meant for a foreign court.

“We shall see whether it is for you or not,” returned my godmother; and her quiet was more absolute than anger. “Mark my words. You will wear it this very evening.”

I believed I never should. A pink dress and I had no acquaintance—no kinship. It knew not my habits; I knew not its audacity. Yet she decreed also that I was to go with her and Graham to a concert that night: a great affair in the grand hall of the principal musical society; pupils of the Conservatoire were to perform; afterwards a lottery “au bénéfice des pauvres;” and, to crown all, the King, Queen, and Prince of Labassecour were to be present. Graham’s tickets had come with strict injunctions—attention to costume, punctuality by seven.

At six I was summoned upstairs. There was no struggle, no persuasion—only that steady, domestic tide by which one is carried when another will is in the house and has chosen its course. The pink went on; black lace softened it into shadow; the pins pricked, the ribbons were drawn, the folds smoothed with hands that smelled of starch and cold metal thimbles. I was pronounced fit for “grande tenue,” and commanded to look in the glass.

I did, and fear shook me; I turned away, and fear followed. Then seven struck; Dr. Bretton had come; my godmother and I went down. She wore brown velvet, rich and grave as earth after rain. I walked in her shadow and envied her those folds of dark majesty, which asked no one’s pardon.

Graham stood in the drawing-room doorway.

My uneasy prayer was, I do hope he will not think I have decked myself out to draw attention.

“Here, Lucy, are some flowers,” said he, and put a bouquet into my hand.

The scent rose at once—green stems bruised to freshness, some sharp sweetness of blossom, and that faint, watery chill flowers keep when they have been cut and carried. He glanced at me with a kind smile and a satisfied nod, and in that simple approval my shame loosened. He made no compliment that might have scorched; he offered no ridicule that might have frozen. The dress itself, too, was plain—no flounce, no foolish frill—only the light fabric and the bright tint that startled me; and since Graham found in it nothing absurd, my own eye learned, against its will, to accept it.

I had been so little used to public gala that I scarce knew what I expected; yet I liked the drive. In the close carriage on a cold but clear night, wrapped and sheltered, I felt the comfort of warmth gathered close like broth in a covered bowl. Outside, the wheels made their steady, softened thunder on stone; now and then iron rang sharper at a rut, and once, passing under bare trees, the wind threaded their branches with a dry whisper. The city’s noise rose and fell in layers—far bells, near voices, the short crack of a whip, the distant roll of other carriages—sound making its own weather around us.

Dr. John and his mother were in their happiest contention; they fenced with words as easily as they might have carved at a table together, and both were frank and kind to me, as if I were indeed of their blood. That atmosphere of friendship had its own flavour; it warmed the mind as surely as good food warms the body.

We passed through some of the best streets of Villette—brightly lit, far livelier now than at noon. Shop-windows blazed; the tide of life flowed abundant along broad pavements. I saw baskets of oranges and heaps of chestnuts; I caught a whiff of roasting from some corner where a vendor had set his brazier; then a gust of perfume from a lady’s muff; then damp stone and lamplight. And suddenly, like the sour remembrance of thin fare in the mouth after a rich mouthful, came the Rue Fossette—its walled garden, its school-house, its dark vast “classes,” where at this very hour I might have been wandering alone, gazing at stars through high blindless windows, listening to the distant voice reading in the refectory, monotonously turning over a “lecture pieuse.” Thus must I soon again listen and wander; the future laid its shadow across the present, timely as sobriety is timely in the midst of wine.

We fell into a current of carriages all flowing one way, and soon a great illuminated building blazed before us. We alighted under a portico in bustle and crowd; the press of bodies made a low surf of sound—steps, murmurs, laughter, an occasional sharp call. I do not remember more distinctly till I found myself mounting a majestic staircase, wide and easy, deeply carpeted with crimson. The colour seemed to swallow the noise; feet fell muffled, as on thick moss. Great doors waited above, closed solemnly, their panels also clothed in crimson.

How those doors rolled back I hardly knew; Dr. John managed such points, and the world of ceremony opened as if it had been trained to obey him. Within lay a hall grand, wide, high—circular walls sweeping round, a hollow dome above, all stained to the look of dead gold, relieved with cornicing and fluting and garlands—now burnished bright, now alabaster-white, now white and gold mingled in wreaths of gilded leaves and spotless lilies. Everywhere, where drapery hung, where carpets spread, where cushions lay, the chosen colour was deep crimson: the place glowed warm as a heart’s chamber, though it glittered like a treasury.

From the dome hung a mass that dazzled me—a cataract of light, streaming with drops, sparkling with facets, scattered with stars, and tinted as if gems had been dissolved into dew, or fragments of rainbows had been shattered and caught there. It was only the chandelier; yet to my country ignorance it looked like a marvel wrought by eastern genii, and I half expected a huge, dark, cloudy hand—the Slave of the Lamp—to hover in that perfumed lustre and guard it.

We moved on—whither I scarcely knew. At a turn we met another party coming towards us. For one flashing instant I saw: a handsome middle-aged lady in dark velvet; a gentleman who might have been her son—best face, finest figure I thought I had ever seen; and a third person in a pink dress with black lace.

I took them all for strangers, and received—strangely—an impartial impression. Then it shattered: it was a great mirror between pillars, and the party was our own. Thus, for the first and perhaps only time in my life, I was given that harsh little gift—seeing myself as others see me. I will not dwell on the result. It jarred like a wrong note, it stung like a pin driven too deep. It was not flattering; and yet—one might be thankful. It might have been worse.

At last we were seated where we could view the vast hall—dazzling, yet warm and cheerful. It filled and filled: women not perhaps very beautiful, but their dresses were perfect; and foreigners, even those ungraceful in domestic privacy, have an art of seeming graceful in public. The peignoir and papillote belong to home; for gala use they keep in reserve a slide, a bend, a carriage of head and arms, a controlled mien of mouth and eyes, put on with the toilette as jewels are put on.

Here and there I saw fine forms of a style of beauty I think England seldom breeds: firm-set, sculptural, without angles; as flexible as a marble caryatid; as complete, in their still stateliness, as a Phidian goddess. Their features had the low-country classic regularity—round where England is sharp, straight where England is expressive—calm to the point of chill. For their depth of passionless peace, one might look to a polar snow-field. Such women need little ornament and seldom wear any; smooth hair braided close is enough against smooth cheek and brow; their dress cannot be too simple; rounded arm and perfect neck ask no chain, no bracelet.

A Juno of this order sat full in our view—cold, blonde, and beauteous as the white column beside her, capitalled with gilding. She was the mark of many eyes and knew it, yet seemed proof to gaze as stone is proof to sunlight.

Seeing Dr. John’s attention drawn thither, I bent to him and murmured, “For the love of heaven, shield well your heart. You need not fall in love with that lady. You might die at her feet, and she would not love you again.”

“Very well,” said he. “And how do you know the spectacle of her grand insensibility might not be my strongest stimulus to homage? The sting of desperation is a wonderful irritant to my emotions. But”—he shrugged—“you know nothing about these things; I’ll address myself to my mother. Mamma, I’m in a dangerous way.”

“As if that interested me!” said Mrs. Bretton.

“Alas! the cruelty of my lot!” returned her son. “Never man had a more unsentimental mother: she never seems to think such a calamity can befall her as a daughter-in-law.”

“If I don’t, it is not for want of having that same calamity held over my head: you have threatened me with it for the last ten years. ‘Mamma, I am going to be married soon!’ was the cry before you were well out of jackets.”

“But, mother, one of these days it will be realized. All of a sudden, when you think you are most secure, I shall go forth like Jacob or Esau, or any other patriarch, and take me a wife—perhaps of these which are of the daughters of the land.”

“At your peril, John Graham! that is all.”

“This mother of mine means me to be an old bachelor. What a jealous old lady it is! But now just look at that splendid creature in pale blue satin—hair of paler brown, with ‘reflets satinés’ like the robe. Would you not feel proud, mamma, if I were to bring that goddess home some day and introduce her as Mrs. Bretton, junior?”

“You will bring no goddess to La Terrasse: that little château will not contain two mistresses—especially if the second be of the height, bulk, and circumference of that mighty doll in wood and wax, and kid and satin.”

“Mamma, she would fill your blue chair so admirably!”

“Fill my chair? I defy the foreign usurper! A rueful chair should it be for her. But hush, John Graham! Hold your tongue, and use your eyes.”

While they thus skirmished, the hall went on receiving party after party until the semicircle before the platform showed one dense mass of heads, sloping from floor to ceiling. The platform itself—wider than any stage—had been deserted half an hour since; now it overflowed. Around two grand pianos placed near the centre, a white flock of young girls, pupils of the Conservatoire, had noiselessly poured—muslin and pale ribbons, hands folded, faces set with that strained look which comes of fear masked as decorum.

Two gentlemen officered this virgin troop. One I knew: an artistic-looking man, bearded, with long hair, noted as a pianiste and the first music-teacher in Villette, who twice a week attended at Madame Beck’s pensionnat to give lessons to those few pupils rich enough to afford his instructions. His name was M. Josef Emanuel, and he was half-brother to M. Paul. The other gentleman—visible now in person—was M. Paul himself.

M. Paul amused me; I smiled inwardly as I watched him in his element—standing conspicuous before a wide, grand assemblage, arranging, restraining, overawing about a hundred young ladies. He was perfectly in earnest—energetic, intent, absolute. And yet what business had he there? What had he to do with music, he who could hardly distinguish one note from another? It was his love of display and authority that had brought him—a love not offensive because so naïve. It soon became plain his brother Josef was as much under his control as the girls. Never was such a little hawk of a man as that M. Paul! When noted singers and musicians began to appear—when stars rose—this comet-like professor set. Insufferable to him were all notorieties and celebrities: where he could not outshine, he fled.

All now waited for one compartment to be filled: a space clothed in crimson like staircase and doors, furnished with stuffed benches on each side of two regal chairs beneath a canopy.

A signal was given; the doors rolled back; the assembly rose; the orchestra burst out; and to the welcome of a choral flourish entered the King, the Queen, and the Court of Labassecour.

I had never before seen a living king or queen, and strained my eyes with a hunger almost childish. Yet the first sight always brings a vague surprise, bordering on disappointment: one expects majesty to sit permanently on a throne, crowned and sceptred. Looking out for a king and queen and seeing only a middle-aged soldier and a rather young lady, I felt half cheated, half pleased—as if the extraordinary had consented to be human.

I recall the King well: about fifty, a little bowed, a little grey, and with a face unlike any in that assembly. I had read nothing of him; no one had told me his habits; at first the strong hieroglyphics on his brow, round his eyes, beside his mouth, puzzled me. Soon, if I did not know, I felt their meaning. There sat a silent sufferer—a nervous, melancholy man. Those eyes had looked on the visits of a certain ghost; had waited long for the comings and goings of Hypochondria. Perhaps he saw her even now upon that platform, over against him, amid all that splendour. Hypochondria has that wont: to rise in the midst of thousands—dark as Doom, pale as Malady, well-nigh strong as Death. Her victim thinks to be happy one moment—“Not so,” says she; “I come.” And she freezes the blood in his heart and beclouds the light in his eye.

Some might have blamed the foreign crown for the fold on his brow; others early bereavement. Something there might be of both; yet both are embittered by that darkest foe—constitutional melancholy.

The Queen, his wife, knew it. The reflection of his grief seemed a subduing shadow on her own benignant face. She was mild, thoughtful, graceful; not beautiful, not of the solid marble-feeling order. Her shape was slender; her features, though distinguished, bore the stamp of royal lines too strongly to give unqualified pleasure—one could not help remembering ignoble phases such profiles have worn elsewhere: feeble, sensual, cunning, as the case might be. But the Queen’s eye was her own; pity and goodness and sweet sympathy gave it a light that blessed. She moved not as sovereign but as lady—kind, loving, elegant.

Her little son, the Prince of Labassecour, and the young Duc de Dindonneau, accompanied her. The child leaned on his mother’s knee; and throughout that evening I saw her watching the monarch beside her, conscious of his clouded abstraction, seeking to rouse him by drawing his attention to their boy. Often she bent to listen to the child’s remarks, then smiled and repeated them to his sire. The moody King would start, listen, smile—and as soon as his good angel ceased speaking, relapse. It was mournful and significant; and what struck me most was that, in all that aristocratic and bourgeois crowd, the peculiarity seemed invisible. I could not perceive one soul either struck or touched.

With the royal pair entered their court—two or three foreign ambassadors—and with them the élite of foreigners resident in Villette. They took possession of the crimson benches; the ladies sat; most of the men stood behind, a sable rank making dark foil to the splendour in front. The middle distance filled with matrons in velvet and satin, in plumes and gems. The benches nearest the Queen’s right hand were devoted to young girls—the bud of Villette aristocracy. Here were no jewels, no head-dresses, no velvet pile—purity, simplicity, and an aërial grace reigned: young heads simply braided; fair forms robed in white, pale rose, placid blue, suggesting angels—though truth must say several of these “jeunes filles” of sixteen or seventeen boasted contours as robust as an Englishwoman of five-and-twenty.

I recognised a couple of those pale buds: two of Madame Beck’s late pupils—Mesdemoiselles Mathilde and Angélique—girls who during their last school year should have been in first class, but whose brains never carried them beyond the second division. In English they had been under my charge, and hard work it was to make them translate rationally a page of The Vicar of Wakefield. For three months one had sat opposite me at table; I remembered with a kind of domestic astonishment the quantity of household bread and butter and stewed fruit she habitually consumed at second déjeuner—only to be exceeded by the fact that she would calmly pocket slices she could not eat. Such seraphs have hearty appetites under their white muslin wings; there is more of the kitchen in human nature than gala language admits.

Another of these fair creatures I knew—prettiest, or at least least demure and hypocritical in look—seated beside the daughter of an English peer, an honest though haughty girl; both had entered with the British embassy. My acquaintance had a slight, pliant figure unlike the foreign damsels; her hair was not braided close like a shell of satin, but looked like hair—long, curled, flowing. She chatted volubly, full of a light-headed satisfaction with herself and her place. I did not look at Dr. Bretton; I knew without looking that he too saw Ginevra Fanshawe. He grew quiet; he answered his mother briefly; he more than once suppressed a sigh.

Why should he sigh? He had confessed a taste for pursuing love under difficulties, and here were difficulties enough. His lady-love shone from a sphere above his own; he could not come near her; he could not be certain of winning even a look. I watched whether she would favour him. Our seat was not far from the crimson benches; eyes so quick and roving as Miss Fanshawe’s must soon light on us. They did—at least on Dr. and Mrs. Bretton. I kept rather in shade, not wishing immediate recognition. She looked steadily at Dr. John, then raised a glass to examine his mother; a minute later she whispered and laughed to her neighbour; and when the performance began her restless attention flew to the platform.

Of the concert itself I need not speak at length; I was ignorant—ignorance crasse—and my impressions had more wonder than knowledge. The young ladies at the two grand pianos, much frightened, made a tremulous exhibition. M. Josef Emanuel stood by them while they played; but he had not the tact, the influence, the peremptory power of his kinsman, who under similar circumstances would have compelled pupils to demean themselves with heroism and self-possession. M. Paul would have placed the hysteric débutantes between two fires—terror of the audience and terror of himself—and would have inspired courage of desperation by making the latter terror the greater. M. Josef could not do this.

After the white-muslin pianistes came a fine, full-grown, sulky lady in white satin. She sang. Her singing affected me like conjuring: I wondered how she did it—how she made her voice run up and down and cut such marvellous capers; but a simple Scotch melody, scraped by a rough street minstrel, has often moved me more deeply than these sumptuous tricks. Perhaps I am made to be reached by what is plain—like bread well baked, not like sugar spun into towers.

Then stepped forth a gentleman who, bending his body much towards the King and Queen and frequently bringing his white-gloved hand to the region of his heart, vented a bitter outcry against some “fausse Isabelle.” He seemed to solicit the Queen’s sympathy; but her Majesty listened, as it appeared to me, with the calm of courtesy rather than the earnestness of interest. His state of mind was harrowing; I was glad when he wound up his musical exposition and let the air cease vibrating with complaint.

Some rousing choruses struck me as the best part of the evening. Deputies from provincial choral societies were present—genuine, barrel-shaped native Labassecouriens—men who threw their voices out as farmers throw seed, with no mincing and no waste of breath on delicacy. Their hearty exertions had at least this good result: the ear drank a satisfying sense of power, as one drinks deep from a cup that is honestly filled. In that strong, communal sound—so unlike the thin, trained flutterings of frightened girls—I seemed to hear something older than fashion, something that belonged to the country’s own mouth and chest; and for a moment, amid gold walls and crimson hangings, I felt how easily the true native voice may be drowned under show—how quickly a people’s tone becomes mere ornament when courts and crowds applaud what is foreign, and do not even notice the sorrow sitting, uncheered, upon their King.

Three days—only three—had been allotted me away from the Rue Fossette; and I counted them as a housewife counts out eggs for a cake, knowing each one gone lessens what may be made. I watched the clock as if my gaze could salt and preserve the minutes; but time is no pantry-thing. It slid away, smooth and cold, while I stood over it like one powerless beside a boiling pot.

At breakfast Mrs. Bretton leaned towards me with that coaxing softness she could use like warm milk—simple, nourishing, and meant for comfort.

“Lucy will not leave us to-day,” she said. “She knows we can procure a second respite.”

“I would not ask for one if I might have it for a word,” I answered. “I long to have the good-by done. My trunk is packed and corded. I must go this morning—directly.”

Yet it appeared my going hung not on my will, but on Graham’s. He had said he would accompany me; and the day, as if spiteful, swallowed him up in engagements till dusk. When he came in at last, there followed a small combat—Mrs. Bretton and her son pressing me to remain one night more. Their kindness fretted me as oil frets flame. I could have cried outright with impatience to be free of the pain; I wanted the pang finished, as one on a scaffold may desire the descending stroke—not from love of it, but because suspense is a torment with teeth.

It was dark when Dr. John handed me down from the carriage at Madame Beck’s door. A lamp burned above the threshold; rain had been falling all day, a thin November drizzle that did not cleanse but only damped and darkened, and the lamplight lay on the wet pavement like a smear of pale butter on cold stone.

Just such a night it had been, not a year ago, when I first stopped at this very door—solitary and suppliant—waiting for its unclosing with a heart beating thick and loud, as if it were a pestle in a mortar. The paving-stones, too, seemed the same; I remembered their shapes with the odd exactness of one who had stared down to keep courage from running out at the eyes. On that former night I had briefly met him who stood with me now. I had never reminded him of that meeting; I had stored it away like a small, sweet conserve in a private cupboard of my mind, and there it was best kept.

Graham rang. The door opened instantly—for it was the hour when the half-boarders departed, and Rosine was alert as a cat at the pantry-latch.

“Don’t come in,” I said; but he stepped for a moment into the lighted vestibule. I had not wished him to see that the water stood in my eyes. He was too kind a nature to be shown sorrow when there was no remedy to offer. A physician, he must always wish to heal—even when healing was not in his power.

“Keep up your courage, Lucy,” he said. “Think of my mother and myself as true friends. We will not forget you.”

“Nor will I forget you, Dr. John.”

My trunk was brought in. We had shaken hands; he had turned to go. But he was not satisfied: the generous impulse in him would not be dismissed with so scant a leave-taking.

“Lucy,” he added, stepping after me, “shall you feel very solitary here?”

“At first I shall.”

“Well—my mother will soon call to see you; and meantime I’ll tell you what I’ll do. I’ll write—just any cheerful nonsense that comes into my head—shall I?”

His goodness struck me with that sudden sharp sweetness one tastes when thirsty and handed a draught one has not dared to ask for.

I smiled, shook my head. “Never think of it. Impose on yourself no such task. You write to me? You’ll not have time.”

“Oh, I will find or make time. Good-by!”

He was gone. The heavy door crashed to. The stroke had fallen. The pain was taken.

Allowing myself no leisure to think—swallowing tears as if they had been wine, burning and bitter—I went at once to Madame Beck’s sitting-room and paid the needful visit of ceremony. She received me with cordiality perfectly acted; she was even demonstrative, though brief. In ten minutes I was dismissed. From the salle-à-manger I proceeded to the refectory, where pupils and teachers were assembled for evening study; there, too, I had a welcome, and not, I think, quite hollow. That over, I was free to the dormitory.

“And will Graham really write?” I asked myself as I sank tired on the edge of the bed.

Then Reason—thin, cold, and patient as a knife—came stealing through the twilight of the long dim room and spoke in my ear.

“He may write once. So kind is his nature, it may stimulate him for once to make the effort. But it cannot be continued; it may not be repeated. Great were the folly that should build on such a promise—insane the credulity that should mistake a rain-pool, holding one draught in its hollow, for a perennial spring.”

I bowed my head. I sat thinking an hour longer. Reason kept whispering, and I felt her withered hand on my shoulder—felt her chill old lips touch my ear.

“If he should write, what then?” she muttered. “Do you meditate pleasure in replying? Brief be your answer. Hope no delight of heart—no indulgence of intellect. Grant no expansion to feeling—give holiday to no single faculty. Dally with no friendly exchange; foster no genial intercommunion…”

“But I have talked to Graham and you did not chide,” I pleaded, weakly, as if arguing for a morsel.

“No,” said she. “I needed not. Talk for you is good discipline. You converse imperfectly. While you speak, there can be no oblivion of inferiority—no encouragement to delusion. Pain, privation, penury stamp your language.”

“But where the bodily presence is weak and the speech contemptible,” I urged, “surely there cannot be error in making written language the medium of better utterance than faltering lips can achieve?”

“At your peril,” returned Reason, “you cherish that idea, or suffer its influence to animate any writing of yours.”

“But if I feel, may I never express?”

“Never!” declared Reason.

The word struck like iron on teeth. Never—never. She would not let me look up or smile or hope. She could not rest unless I were crushed and cowed, broken-in and broken-down; born only to work for bread, and to wait for death, and through all life to despond. Perhaps she was right; yet no wonder we are glad at times to defy her—to slip from under her rod and give a truant hour to Imagination, her soft bright foe—our sweet Help, our divine Hope. We shall and must break bounds at intervals, despite the revenge awaiting our return.

For Reason is vindictive as a devil. To me she was ever envenomed as a step-mother. If I obeyed her, it was chiefly in fear, not love. Long ago I should have died of her ill-usage—her stint, her chill, her barren board, her icy bed, her savage ceaseless blows—but for that kinder Power who holds my secret and sworn allegiance. Often has Reason turned me out by night, in mid-winter, on cold snow, flinging for sustenance the gnawed bone dogs had forsaken; sternly has she vowed her stores held nothing more for me, harshly denied my right to ask better things. Then looking up have I seen in the sky a head amidst circling stars, of which the midmost and brightest lent a ray sympathetic and attent. A spirit, softer and better than Human Reason, has descended with quiet flight to the waste—bringing all round her a sphere of air borrowed of eternal summer; bringing perfume of flowers which cannot fade—fragrance of trees whose fruit is life; bringing breezes pure from a world whose day needs no sun to lighten it. My hunger has this good angel appeased with food sweet and strange; tenderly has she assuaged insufferable fears—kindly given rest to deadly weariness—generously lent hope and impulse to paralysed despair. When I bend the knee to other than God, it shall be at her white and winged feet.

She remembered me that night; she saw me weep, and she came with comfort. “Sleep,” she said. “Sleep sweetly—I gild thy dreams!”

She kept her word and watched me through a night’s rest; but at dawn Reason relieved the guard. I awoke with a start. Rain dashed against the panes; the wind uttered a peevish cry at intervals, as though even the air were fretted by confinement. The night-lamp was dying on the black circular stand in the middle of the dormitory; day had already broken, thin and raw.

The pang of waking snatched me out of bed as if a giant hand had gripped my shoulder. How quickly I dressed in the cold! How deeply I drank of the ice-cold water in my carafe—my sharp cordial, to which, like other dram-drinkers, I had recourse when chagrin shook me.

Soon the bell rang its réveillée. Being dressed, I descended alone to the refectory. The stove was lit; the air there was warm. Through the rest of the house it was cold with the nipping severity of a continental winter. Though it was but the beginning of November, a north wind had brought a blight early over Europe. I remembered that when first I came those black stoves pleased me little; but now I began to associate with them a sense of comfort, and liked them as in England we like a fireside—dark, steady, and faithful in a draughty place.

I sat down before this comforter and fell into a deep argument with myself on life and its chances, on destiny and her decrees. My mind, calmer and stronger than the night before, made for itself imperious rules: forbidding, under deadly penalties, all weak looking back on happiness past; commanding patient journeying through the wilderness of the present; enjoining reliance on faith—a watching of the cloud and pillar that subdue while they guide, and awe while they illumine—hushing the impulse to fond idolatry, checking the longing outlook for a far-off promised land whose rivers perhaps are never to be reached save in dying dreams, whose sweet pastures are to be viewed only from some desolate summit like Nebo.

By degrees, a composite feeling—strength braided with pain—wound itself wirily round my heart, held it, restrained its throbbings, and made me fit for the day’s work. I lifted my head.

Beside the stove, piercing the wall, was a small window looking into the carré. As I looked up, a cap-tassel, a brow, two eyes filled a pane. Those eyes struck right against mine: they had been watching me. I had not, till that moment, known tears lay on my cheek; but I felt them then, exposed by another’s gaze.

This was a strange house, where no corner was sacred from intrusion—where not a tear could be shed, nor a thought pondered, but a spy was at hand to note and divine. And this new spy—out-door, male—what business brought him across the premises at this hour? No other professor would have dared to cross the carré before the class-bell rang.

M. Emanuel took no account of hours nor claims. Some book of reference in the first-class library he had occasion to consult; he had come to seek it. On his way he passed the refectory; he was a man who wore eyes before, behind, and on each side. He had seen me through the little window; he now opened the refectory door, and there he stood.

“Mademoiselle, vous êtes triste.”

“Monsieur, j’en ai bien le droit.”

“Vous êtes malade de cœur et d’humeur,” he pursued, half in French, half in English, with that cutting briskness of his. “You are at once mournful and mutinous. I see on your cheek two tears which I know are hot as two sparks, and salt as two crystals of the sea. While I speak you eye me strangely. Shall I tell you of what I am reminded while watching you?”

“Monsieur,” I said, striving to keep the tremor out of my voice, “I shall be called away to prayers shortly; my time for conversation is very scant at this hour—excuse—”

“I excuse everything,” he interrupted. “My mood is so meek, neither rebuff nor, perhaps, insult could ruffle it. You remind me, then, of a young she wild creature, new caught, untamed, viewing with a mixture of fire and fear the first entrance of the breaker-in.”

Unwarrantable accost. Rash and rude to a pupil; to a teacher inadmissible. He meant to provoke a warm reply; I had seen him vex the passionate to explosion before. In me his malice should find no gratification. I sat silent.

“You look,” he said, “like one who would snatch at a draught of sweet poison, and spurn wholesome bitters with disgust.”

“Indeed, I never liked bitters,” I answered at last, because the sting of his words forced speech. “Nor do I believe them wholesome. And to whatever is sweet—be it poison or food—you cannot, at least, deny its own delicious quality. Better, perhaps, to die quickly a pleasant death than drag on long a charmless life.”

“Yet,” he returned, “you should take your bitter dose duly and daily, if I had the power to administer it; and as to the well-beloved poison, I would, perhaps, break the very cup which held it.”

I turned my head sharply away—partly because his presence utterly displeased me, partly because I wished to shun questions; in my present mood, the effort of answering might overmaster self-command.

“Come,” said he, and his voice softened into an insinuation I liked no better than his sharpness, “tell me the truth—you grieve at being parted from friends—is it not so?”

I was silent. He came in, sat down on the bench about two yards from me, and persevered long—patiently for him—in attempts to draw me into talk. They were necessarily vain; I could not talk. At last I entreated him to let me alone. In uttering the request my voice faltered; my head sank on my arms and the table. I wept bitterly, though quietly. He sat a while longer. I did not look up nor speak till the closing door and his retreating step told me he was gone. Those tears relieved me as a storm relieves the air.

I had time to bathe my eyes before breakfast, and I suppose I appeared at that meal as serene as any other person—though not so jocund as the young lady who placed herself opposite me, fixed on me a pair of small eyes twinkling with glee, and stretched frankly across the table a white hand to be shaken.

Miss Fanshawe’s travels, gaieties, and flirtations agreed with her mightily. She had become quite plump; her cheeks were as round as apples, ripe and shining. I had last seen her in elegant evening attire; yet I do not know that she looked less charming now in her school-dress—a careless peignoir of dark blue, dimly and dingily plaided with black. That dusky wrapper gave her fairness a triumph; by contrast it made her skin look whiter, her bloom fresher, her golden tresses more like a bright heap of spun sugar.

“I am glad you are come back, Timon,” said she. Timon was one of her dozen names for me. “You don’t know how often I have wanted you in this dismal hole.”

“Oh, have you? Then, of course, if you wanted me, you have something for me to do—stockings to mend, perhaps.” I never gave Ginevra a minute’s or a farthing’s credit for disinterestedness.

“Crabbed and crusty as ever!” she cried. “I expected as much. But now—come, grand-mother—I hope you like coffee as much and pistolets as little as ever: are you disposed to barter?”

“Take your own way.”

Her way was always to make me convenient.
