She did not like the morning coffee—its school brewage not strong or sweet enough for her palate—but she had an excellent appetite for the pistolets, new-baked and very good, served in a fixed allowance to each. As my portion was more than I needed, I gave half to Ginevra; and she, in return, would sometimes give me part of her coffee. This morning I was glad of the draught. Hunger I had none; thirst parched me.

I do not know why I chose to give my bread rather to Ginevra than to another, nor why—when two had to share the convenience of one drinking-vessel, as sometimes happened on long walks into the country, when we halted for refreshment at a farm—I always contrived that she should be my convive, and rather liked to let her take the lion’s share, whether of white beer, sweet wine, or new milk. So it was; she knew it; and therefore, while we wrangled daily, we were never alienated.

After breakfast my custom was to withdraw to the first classe and sit and read—or think (oftenest the latter)—there alone, till the nine o’clock bell threw open all doors, admitted the rush of externes and demi-pensionnaires, and gave the signal for that bustle and business which, till five, had no relax.

I was seated there this morning when a tap came.

“Pardon, Mademoiselle,” said a pensionnaire, entering gently; she took from her desk some needed book or paper and withdrew on tip-toe, murmuring as she passed, “Que mademoiselle est appliquée!”

Appliquée indeed! The means of application were spread before me, but I did nothing; had done nothing; meant to do nothing. Thus does the world give us credit for merits we have not. Madame Beck deemed me a regular bas-bleu and warned me solemnly not to study too much, lest “the blood should all go to my head.” Everybody in the Rue Fossette held a superstition that “Meess Lucie” was learned—with the notable exception of M. Emanuel, who by means peculiar to himself had obtained a not inaccurate inkling of my real qualifications, and took quiet opportunities of chuckling in my ear over their scant measure.

For my part, I never troubled about the penury. I dearly liked to think my own thoughts. I had pleasure in reading a few books, but not many, preferring those on whose style or sentiment the writer’s individual nature was plainly stamped; I flagged inevitably over characterless volumes, however clever. I perceived well that, as far as my own mind was concerned, God had limited its powers and its action; thankful, I trust, for the gift bestowed, but unambitious of higher endowments.

The polite pupil was scarcely gone when, without tap or ceremony, in burst a second intruder. Had I been blind I should have known who it was. A constitutional reserve of manner had by this time worked, for me, a wholesome convenience in the house; rarely did I suffer now from rude intrusions. When first I came, a blunt German would clap my shoulder and ask me to run a race; or a riotous Labassecourienne seize my arm and drag me toward the playground with urgent proposals for the “Pas de Géant,” or for a romping hide-and-seek called “Un, deux, trois.” These attentions had ceased, without my being driven to the trouble of point-blank refusals. I had now no familiar demonstration to dread or endure save from one quarter; and as that was English I could bear it.

Ginevra Fanshawe made no scruple, at times, of catching me as I crossed the carré, whirling me round in a compulsory waltz, and enjoying the mental and physical discomfiture she induced. It was Ginevra who now broke in upon my supposed “learned leisure,” carrying a huge music-book under her arm.

“Go to your practising,” I said at once. “Away with you to the little salon!”

“Not till I have had a talk with you, chère amie. I know where you have been spending your vacation, and how you have commenced sacrificing to the graces, and enjoying life like any other belle. I saw you at the concert the other night, dressed—actually—like anybody else. Who is your tailleuse?”

“Tittle-tattle. My tailleuse? A fiddlestick. Sheer off, Ginevra; I don’t want your company.”

“But when I want yours so much, ange farouche, what does a little reluctance on your part signify? Dieu merci! we know how to manœuvre with our gifted compatriote—the learned ourse Britannique. And so, Ourson, you know Isidore?”

“I know John Bretton.”

“Oh, hush!” she cried, stopping her ears. “You crack my tympanums with your rude Anglicisms. But—how is our well-beloved John? Tell me about him. The poor man must be in a sad way. What did he say to my behaviour the other night? Wasn’t I cruel?”

“Do you think I noticed you?”

“It was a delightful evening. Oh, that divine de Hamal! And then—to watch the other sulking and dying in the distance; and the old lady—my future mamma-in-law! But I am afraid I and Lady Sara were a little rude in quizzing her.”

“Lady Sara never quizzed her at all,” I returned. “And for what you did—don’t make yourself uneasy. Mrs. Bretton will survive your sneer.”

“She may; old ladies are tough. But that poor son of hers! Do tell me what he said: I saw he was terribly cut up.”

“He said you looked as if at heart you were already Madame de Hamal.”

“Did he?” she cried, delight lighting her whole face. “He noticed that? How charming! I thought he would be mad with jealousy.”

“Ginevra, have you seriously done with Dr. Bretton? Do you want him to give you up?”

“Oh! you know he can’t do that. But wasn’t he mad?”

“Quite mad,” I assented; “as mad as a March hare.”

“Well—and how ever did you get him home?”

“How ever, indeed! Have you no pity on his poor mother and me? Fancy us holding him tight down in the carriage, and he raving between us, fit to drive everybody delirious. The very coachman went wrong somehow, and we lost our way.”

“You don’t say so? You are laughing at me. Now, Lucy Snowe—”

“I assure you it is fact—and fact, also, that Dr. Bretton would not stay in the carriage: he broke from us, and would ride outside.”

“And afterwards?”

“Afterwards—when he did reach home—the scene transcends description.”

“Oh, but describe it—you know it is such fun!”

“Fun for you, Miss Fanshawe? but”—and I made my face stern with a gravity she could not imitate—“you know the proverb: ‘What is sport to one may be death to another.’”

“Go on—there’s a darling Timon.”

“Conscientiously, I cannot, unless you assure me you have some heart.”

“I have—such an immensity, you don’t know!”

“Good. In that case you will be able to conceive Dr. Graham Bretton rejecting his supper in the first instance—the chicken, the sweetbread prepared for his refreshment, left on the table untouched. Then—but it is of no use dwelling on harrowing details. Suffice it to say that never, in the most stormy fits and moments of his infancy, had his mother such work to tuck the sheets about him as she had that night.”

“He wouldn’t lie still?”

“He wouldn’t lie still: there it was. The sheets might be tucked in, but the thing was to keep them tucked in.”

“And what did he say?”

When the house had sunk into its nightly order—dinner cleared away; the loud recreation-hour spent; the externes gone, the bell no longer clashing like a little factory’s heart; and the refectory lamp set to its quiet burn for study—I felt the whole building change its sound. Day had been a jangling of doors, shoes, voices, and commands; evening was a thinner music: distant murmurs stitched with long pauses, the hush between one footfall and another, the soft cough of a stove drawing, the far drip of water in a pipe. In such a hush, one dares to hear one’s own hopes; and I had a hope folded small and sealed, which I had carried all day as women carry a crust saved for later—pressed close, warmed by the body, guarded from greedy hands.

Madame Beck was established below in her salle-à-manger with her mother and friends; the house, for half an hour, seemed to have no eye on me. I glided to the kitchen, where the air was thick with that domestic perfume which makes a school almost bearable—burnt onion skins, warm bread, the metal scent of scoured pots, and in a corner a faint sourness of damp coffee-grounds (here used, with thrifty invention, for cleansing as other places use tea-leaves). My friend Goton, who could be coaxed by a soft word as readily as she could brown a ragout by a steady hand, gave me my bougie.

“Mais certainement, chou-chou,” she said, pressing it on me as if she were giving a sweetmeat to a child. “Vous en aurez deux, si vous voulez.”

I took the light—one slim candle, precious as a stolen minute—and went up, noiseless, to the dormitory, meaning to feast there alone on my letter as one eats in secret when one has too little and fears interruption.

But ill-luck—always a listener—had lain in wait. A pupil was already in bed, indisposed; and when my bougie threw its small circle of gold on muslin and nightcap borders, I recognized, crumpled in the sheets, the “figure chiffonnée” of Ginevra Fanshawe. She lay supine, a picture of delicacy; yet even in that stillness there was a watchfulness that vexed me—an eyelid’s faint quiver like the twitch of a cat feigning sleep. She was capable of waking at the very moment my heart would be most naked, and then pouring out chatter upon it as one pours cold water on hot sugar, spoiling all.

I would not be robbed so. I went down again, letter still unopened, and tried the class-rooms. They too betrayed me. It was the weekly sweeping and purification by candle-light: benches piled on desks; dust floating like a pale fog; the floor darkened with wet coffee-grounds, trampled into little muddy constellations; a scene of disorder where solitude could not be had, for even emptiness there was busy.

Baffled, but not beaten, I resolved to find a place no one used—some forgotten corner of the house, where even Madame’s vigilance did not reach. I took a key from where I knew it was kept, mounted three staircases one after another, and came at last to a narrow landing where the air turned colder, as if the warmth of living had been left below. A worm-eaten door yielded; I slipped through, and entered the garret.

It was deep, black, and wintry; the roof-beams above seemed to stoop; and the air smelt of damp wood, mould, and old cloth—a kind of poverty-scent, like clothing shut away with time and neglect. Here, surely, none would follow—not Madame herself. I shut the door, set my bougie on a doddered chest of drawers, and, shivering, drew a shawl about my shoulders. Then I took out my letter—my small stored portion—and broke the seal with hands that trembled as if they had been hungry long.

Will it be long—will it be short? I thought, wiping my eyes, for a soft dimness had come over them, like a south-wind shower that blinds by sweetness.

It was long.

Will it be cool—will it be kind?

It was kind.

Kind, to my bridled and disciplined expectation: kinder, perhaps, to my famished thought than the strict truth would warrant. Yet so little had I dared to hope, so much had I trained myself to fear, that even this simple good-nature tasted like a feast. There are people who sit daily at loaded tables and never know what a mouthful means; and there are others who, in a frosty garret, by a guttering candle, can be made richer than queens by a page of friendly words. I was that poor English teacher; and the letter—nothing more, in itself, than a kindly outpouring—seemed, in that instant, like grace.

Dr. John had written at length; had written with pleasure; had written in a mood so sunny it warmed the very paper. He recalled scenes we had shared, places we had visited, conversations we had held—little matters, trifles, the small daily bread of intimacy; and the cordial core of my delight was the conviction that he had not poured it out merely to content me, but to gratify himself. It might never happen again; indeed, every sober probability whispered it would not. But that belonged to the future. This present moment had no pain in it, no want, no blot. Full, pure, perfect, it blessed me so deeply I felt as if something gentle had leaned near—some passing minister of peace—and laid a cool wing on the hard, hot throb of my heart.

And yet—are there influences that begrudge human bliss? Are there presences, not human, that creep close when joy grows keen, to sour the air? For in that vast solitude something sounded.

At first it might have been only the settling of beams, the sigh of a draught under the slates; but then, most surely, I heard a stealthy foot—gliding—coming, as it seemed, from the black recess where the malefactor cloaks hung. I turned. The candle’s light was small, the room long; shadows gathered like old secrets in the corners. Then—reader, say what you will, call it nerves, call it madness, declare it dream: I saw, in the middle of that ghostly chamber, a figure all black and white. The skirts straight and narrow, black; the head bandaged and veiled, white.

An image like—a NUN.

A cry broke from me; my stomach turned cold. Had the shape advanced, I think I should have fallen senseless. It did not advance. It receded; and I, forgetting pride, forgetting all but flight, made for the door.

How I got down those stairs I cannot tell. Instinct drove me away from the refectory; I went straight to Madame’s sitting-room, and burst in as if the room were on fire.

“There is something in the grenier,” I said. “I have been there: I saw something. Go and look at it—all of you!”

The room, in my fear, seemed full; in truth, there were four: Madame Beck; her mother, Madame Kint, infirm and visiting; her brother, M. Victor Kint; and another gentleman with his back to me, speaking to the old lady. My terror must have bleached me: I felt my own face like chilled wax, my limbs shaking with an inward frost. They rose together, alarmed; they gathered round; they questioned; I urged them upward, and the sight of the men gave me a strange courage—as if a deeper voice and heavier step could rout what had glided and stared.

I turned to lead them; and then remembrance struck me like a blow. My letter—left on the drawers beside the bougie.

That letter was my portion, my hoarded sweetness; and for it I would defy flesh or spirit. I flew up again, faster because I heard them following. They had no choice but to come.

When I reached the garret-door, the darkness within was thick as a pit. My light was out. The candle, quenched, stood on the drawers like a dead thing. Someone—Madame, I think, with her cool sense—had brought a lamp; and as they came up, its ray pierced the black.

There was the bougie. But where was the letter?

In that moment the nun, the fear, the superstition—all fell away before a sharper anguish. I did not search for a ghost: I searched for paper. I dropped to my knees, groping on the floor, my hands sweeping dust and splinters, my throat torn with one cry.

“My letter! my letter!”

Cruel doom—to have one’s little comfort snatched away, preternaturally, before one has even finished tasting it. They spoke around me; they rummaged in corners; they muttered about cloaks disturbed and some breach in the skylight; I heard it as one hears talk through water. I only knew this: my letter was gone.

“Oh! they have taken my letter!” I sobbed, and I think I wrung my hands like a child.

“What letter, Lucy? My dear girl, what letter?” asked a voice close to my ear.

That voice—known, long-remembered in its very kindness—made my breath stop. I looked up.

In the dim garret, among the mouldy drawers and dangling cloaks, was the face of the writer of that letter—John Graham, Dr. Bretton himself.

Yes: he had been sent for that evening to prescribe for Madame Kint; he was the second gentleman in the salle-à-manger when I burst in, wild with fear.

“Was it my letter, Lucy?” he asked.

“Your own—yours—the letter you wrote to me. I came here to read it quietly. I couldn’t find another place to have it to myself. I saved it all day—never opened it till this evening. I scarcely glanced over it. I cannot bear to lose it. Oh, my letter!”

“Hush,” he said, and his tone—so firm, so full of warm authority—was like a hand laid on a fever. “Don’t cry and distress yourself so. What is it worth? Come out of this cold room. They will send for the police, no doubt, and examine further. We need not stay here. Come—down.”

He took my cold fingers in his warm hand and led me to a room where there was fire. The stove breathed steadily; its sound was the sound of safety. Dr. John and I sat before it. He spoke to me—soothing me with a goodness that did not ask leave, promising twenty letters for the one lost.

There are words that wound like knives—serrated, poisoned, leaving cuts that never quite heal. But there are also tones of consolation so fine the ear keeps their echo all life: kindnesses that, when darkness gathers, answer back from memory with undimmed shine. Whatever people told me later—that Dr. Bretton was not so perfect as I believed; that his nature lacked depth and endurance—was nothing to the truth I knew in that hour. He was as good to me as water to thirst, as sun to one shut in cold walls. I remember him heroic; heroic I will hold him.

He smiled and asked why I cared for his letter so very much. I thought—but did not say—that I prized it as I prized the blood in my veins. I only answered that I had so few letters to care for.

“I am sure you did not read it,” he said; “or you would think nothing of it.”

“I read it—only once. I wanted to read it again. I am sorry it is lost.” And I wept afresh, helplessly.

“Lucy—Lucy—my poor little god-sister (if such a kinship exists)—here—here is your letter. Why is it not better worth such tears, and such tenderly exaggerating faith?”

He drew it out as he spoke.

It was his curious, characteristic manoeuvre. His quick eye had seen the letter on the floor where I groped; his hand, as quick, had snatched it up; he had hidden it in his waistcoat pocket. Had my trouble been a shade cooler—had my tears been but a degree less true—I doubt whether he would have restored it at all. Dr. John could be amused by pain he did not deem deep.

Yet the joy of regaining made me forget the torment of the trick. My relief shone out, despite myself; I said little, but my face must have betrayed the whole feast of it—like a hungry creature suddenly handed back its bread.

“Are you satisfied now?” he asked.

“Satisfied—and happy,” I answered.

“Well then,” he went on, “how do you feel physically? Are you growing calmer? Not much: you tremble still like a leaf.”

I insisted I was composed; I no longer felt the first terror.

“Then you can tell me what you saw. Your account was vague. You only spoke of ‘something.’ Was it a man? An animal? What was it?”

“I will never tell exactly what I saw,” I said, “unless someone else sees it too. Otherwise I shall be discredited and accused of dreaming.”

“Tell me,” he urged. “I will hear it professionally. I look at you now as a doctor: your eye is too vivid and restless; your cheek has lost its blood; your hand will not steady. Come, Lucy—speak.”

“You would laugh.”

“If you don’t tell me, you shall have no more letters.”

“You are laughing now.”

“I will take away that single epistle again—being mine, I think I have a right to reclaim it.”

His raillery made me grave. I folded up the letter and covered it from sight, as one covers food from pilfering hands.

“You may hide it,” he said, “but I can possess it any moment I choose. You don’t know my skill in sleight of hand. I could be a conjuror. Mamma says sometimes, too, that I have a harmonizing property of tongue and eye; but you never saw that in me—did you, Lucy?”

“Indeed—indeed—when you were a mere boy I used to see both: far more then than now; for now you are strong, and strength dispenses with subtlety. But still—Dr. John—you have what they call here ‘un air fin,’ that nobody can mistake. Madame Beck saw it, and—”

“And liked it,” he broke in, laughing; “because she has it herself. But, Lucy, give me that letter—you don’t really care for it.”

I would not answer. A man in mirth must not always be humoured. And besides, there was a new sort of smile about his lips—sweet, yet it pained me without my knowing why; a new light in his eyes—not hostile, but not reassuring. Something in it made my heart draw back, as a tongue draws back from a too-bright blade.

I rose to go, and bid him good-night, a little sadly. His peculiar sensitiveness—his quick faculty for detecting what is unspoken—caught the complaint in my silence. He asked quietly if I was offended.

I shook my head.

“Permit me, then, to speak seriously before you go. You are in a highly nervous state. I feel sure—whatever control you have—that while alone this evening in that dismal, sepulchral garret—smelling of damp and mould, rank with cold—an air to breed catarrh and worse; a place you never ought to enter—you saw, or thought you saw, something calculated to impress the imagination. You are not subject to common terrors—robbers and such; but a thing bearing a spectral character might shake your mind. Be calm. This is a matter of nerves. Still—specify the vision.”

“You will tell nobody?”

“Nobody—most certainly. You may trust me as implicitly as you did Père Silas. Indeed, the doctor is perhaps the safer confessor of the two, though he has not grey hair.”

“You will not laugh?”

“Perhaps I may—to do you good; but not in scorn. Lucy, I feel as a friend towards you, though your timid nature is slow to trust.”

Now he did look like a friend. The strange sparkle was gone; the arched curves of lip, nostril, eyebrow softened; attention sobered his face. Won at last, I told him exactly what I had seen: the black and white figure, the white veiling, the fixed glitter of eyes. He already knew the house-legend; I had once related it to him on a mild October afternoon as we rode through Bois l’Etang, when the leaves fell in a soft whisper and the hoof-beats made a gentle percussion on the road.

He sat thinking; and while he thought, we heard the others coming down-stairs—a drift of voices and steps, the house’s soundscape thickening again with human talk.

“Are they going to interrupt?” he asked, glancing at the door with annoyance.

“They will not come here,” I answered; for we were in the little salon where Madame never sat at evening, and the stove’s warmth lingered only by chance.

They passed the door and went on to the salle-à-manger.

“Now,” he said, “they will talk about thieves and burglars. Let them. Say nothing. Keep your resolution of describing your nun to nobody. She may appear to you again: don’t start.”

“You think, then,” I whispered, with secret horror, “she came out of my brain, and is now gone in there, and may glide out again when I look not for her?”

“I think it a case of spectral illusion,” he replied. “I fear it results from long-continued mental conflict.”

“Oh, Dr. John—I shudder at being liable to such illusion! It seemed so real. Is there no cure—no preventive?”

“Happiness is the cure,” he said. “A cheerful mind the preventive: cultivate both.”

No mockery ever sounded to me so hollow as that counsel. Cultivate happiness! As if it were a crop one could sow, water, weed, and harvest. Happiness is not a potato to be planted in mould and fed with manure; it is a light that sometimes falls—rarely—out of heaven, like dew on a summer morning one did not order and cannot compel.

“Cultivate happiness,” I repeated briefly. “Do you cultivate happiness? How do you manage?”

“I am a cheerful fellow by nature,” he answered; “and ill-luck has never dogged me. Adversity gave me and my mother one passing scowl and brush, but we defied her—or rather laughed at her—and she went by.”

“There is no cultivation in all this.”

“I do not give way to melancholy.”

“Yes: I have seen you subdued by that feeling.”

“About Ginevra Fanshawe—eh?”

“Did she not sometimes make you miserable?”

“Pooh! stuff! nonsense! You see I am better now.”

If a laughing eye, bright with healthy energy, could prove it, he was better indeed. His very voice had a ring to it like metal struck clear.

“You do not look much amiss,” I granted.

“And why, Lucy, can’t you look and feel as I do—buoyant, courageous, fit to defy all the nuns and flirts in Christendom? I would give gold on the spot just to see you snap your fingers. Try the manœuvre.”

“If I were to bring Miss Fanshawe into your presence just now?”

“I vow she should not move me,” he said, “or she should move me by one thing only—true and passionate love. I would accord forgiveness at no less a price.”

“Indeed! a smile of hers would have been a fortune to you a while since.”

“Transformed, Lucy—transformed!” He rose, drawing on his gloves, and in the carriage of his head and the ease of his mien there was a liberty more than comfort—a disdain of past bondage. “Remember, you once called me a slave; but I am a free man now.”

“Ah, Doctor—Doctor! You said it was your nature to pursue love under difficulties—to be charmed by proud insensibility.”

He laughed. “My nature varies. The mood of one hour is sometimes the mockery of the next. Well, Lucy—will the Nun come again to-night, think you?”

“I don’t think she will.”

“Give her my compliments if she does—Dr. John’s compliments—and entreat her to have the goodness to wait a visit from him. Lucy—was she a pretty nun? Had she a pretty face? You have not told me that yet; and that is the really important point.”

“She had a white cloth over her face,” I said; “but her eyes glittered.”

“Confusion to her goblin trappings!” he cried, irreverent. “But at least she had handsome eyes—bright and soft?”

“Cold and fixed,” I answered.

“No, no—we’ll none of her. She shall not haunt you. Give her that shake of the hand if she comes again. Will she stand that, do you think?”

I thought it too cordial for a ghost to endure. And his smile—matching the kindness of his “Good-night”—was too human, too warm, too living for any spectre to bear.

Afterwards, what had they found in the garret? Very little. At first they spoke of cloaks disturbed; but Madame Beck told me later she thought they hung much as usual. As for a broken pane in the skylight, she said that aperture was rarely without some crack or gap; and a hail-storm had fallen not many days before.

Madame questioned me closely as to what I had seen. I described only an obscure figure in black; I would not breathe the word “nun,” sure that word would at once make it romance in her mind, and romance—so named—would be dismissed as folly. She charged me to speak of it to no servant, pupil, or teacher, and commended my discretion in coming to her private room rather than spreading terror through the refectory.

So the subject dropped.

And I was left, secretly and sadly, to turn it over alone: whether that strange thing belonged to this world, or to one beyond the grave; or whether it was only the child of malady—and I, the prey of that child.

To wonder sadly, had I said? No. For a space, sorrow was held back, as if some sudden gust had come upon a hollow in a wood—one of those secret dells where the grass lies spongy with mist, and the leaves keep a sickly, pale damp. Let but a storm, or an axe, make room in the boughs; let a slit be opened for air and sun; and what was a cold cup becomes a vessel brimmed with lustre. So, by some rent made in my own close-growing shade, light poured in; and I—who had been living on scant fare—took a new nourishment.

A new creed became mine: belief in happiness.

Three weeks had gone since that strange night in the garret; and now, in my little case, in the locked drawer upstairs, lay the first letter, and with it four others—each traced by the same firm hand, each sealed with the same clear seal, each bringing a draught of comfort so immediate it seemed not ink but cordial. After years, I read them again: kind letters—pleasant, because written by one well pleased; the last had a few lines, half-gay, half-tender, touched by feeling, but not subdued. Time mellowed them to a mild beverage; but then—fresh drawn from a source I honoured—each page was like a vintage poured out for the gods.

How did I answer? Under the dry check of Reason, or with the full impulse of Feeling?

I served two masters. I bowed in Rimmon’s house, and yet turned my heart to another shrine. I wrote two replies to every letter—one for my own relief, one for Graham’s perusal.

Feeling and I would thrust Reason out, shoot bolt and bar against her, spread paper, dip a pen deep, and pour forth, with a kind of hungry joy, our whole sincere heart—gratitude close-clinging, affection strongly adherent, attachment rooted and active. (Once for all, let me say I scorn the mean suspicion of what are called “warmer feelings.” Women do not kindle those flames where, from first to last of an acquaintance, they have never been cheated by even a glimmer of Hope. None launches into Love unless Hope’s star has risen, or been dreamed of, on Love’s troubled waters.) When I had written myself out—had told how gladly I would draw to my own lot whatever was painful in his destiny; how willingly I would stand, if I might, between him and storms—then, at that very point, the doors of my heart would shake. Bolt and bar would give. Reason would spring in, vigorous, revengeful; snatch the warm sheets; read; sneer; erase; tear up; re-write; fold; seal; direct; and send a curt page, proper and composed. She did right.

Nor did I live on letters only. I was visited; I was cared for. Once each week I was taken out to La Terrasse, and always made much of. Dr. Bretton did not fail to tell me why. “To keep away the nun,” he said. He was determined to dispute with her her prey. He professed a thorough dislike to her—chiefly for that white face-cloth and those cold grey eyes; the moment he heard those odious particulars, disgust, he declared, had roused opposition. He only wished she would look in upon me once more when he was present. But she never did. In short, he regarded me as a patient, and practised on me that cordial, attentive treatment where benevolence and skill make one method.

One evening—the first in December—I walked alone in the carré. It was six o’clock. The class-doors were closed; but within, the pupils, let loose for recreation, were making a small riot of laughter, shrieks, and racing feet: a domestic tempest, all noise and no danger, beating against the panels. Outside, the carré itself lay dark, save for the red glow under and about the stove: a low, steady furnace-light, like embers kept for the morrow’s bread. The wide glass-doors and long windows were frosted over, their whiteness stitched with starlight—fine, scattered points pricking through the blanched veil. The air promised clear cold, though there was no moon.

That I could remain there thus, in darkness, showed my nerves had gained tone. I thought of the nun; I hardly feared her; yet behind me the staircase climbed up—blind, black, landing after landing—to the haunted grenier. And still my heart gave one leap, and my pulse sprang, when I heard a breathing, a rustle, and saw, on the steps’ deep shadow, a deeper shadow move and descend. It paused at the class-door, then glided before me. At the same instant, from far off, came the clang of the great bell.

Life-like sounds bring life-like feelings. That figure was too round, too low for my gaunt nun. It was only Madame Beck on duty.

“Mademoiselle Lucy!” cried Rosine, bursting in with a lamp from the corridor. “On est là pour vous au salon.”

Madame saw me; I saw Madame; Rosine saw us both; there was no mutual recognition. I went straight to the salon.

There, as I owned I expected, I found Dr. Bretton—Graham—but in evening dress.

“The carriage is at the door,” said he. “My mother has sent it to take you to the theatre. She meant to go, but an arrival has prevented her; she said at once, ‘Take Lucy in my place.’ Will you go?”

“Just now? I am not dressed,” I cried, glancing at my dark merino as if it had suddenly become a disgrace.

“You have half an hour. I should have given notice, but I only determined since five, when I heard there was to be a genuine regale—a great actress.”

And he named her; and the name—then—was a trumpet through Europe. It is hushed now. The woman who bore it has long been covered by night and oblivion. But then her day, a day of Sirius, stood at its full height of heat and fervour.

“I’ll go. I will be ready in ten minutes,” I vowed—and flew, checked by no scruple such as might have checked another: the thought that to go anywhere with Graham, without Mrs. Bretton, might be objectionable. I could not have conceived it without kindling in myself a tyrannous shame. Besides, my godmother, knowing her son and knowing me, would as soon have chaperoned a sister with a brother, as watched our comings and goings with anxious guard.

There was no need of show. My dun mist crape would suffice. I went to seek it in the great oak-wardrobe of the dormitory, where hung no fewer than forty dresses. But reforms had come: some innovating hand had pruned the crowded press and carried divers garments to the grenier—my crape among them.

I must fetch it. I took the key and went aloft—fearless, almost thoughtless. I unlocked the door; I plunged in.

And then—the reader may believe or not—but that garret was not wholly dark. From one point shone a solemn light, like a star, yet broader; and it shone so plainly it revealed the deep alcove with part of the tarnished scarlet curtain drawn over it. Instantly, silently, it vanished: so did curtain and alcove; the whole end became black as night.

I made no search. I had no time—no will. Snatching my dress, happily hung near the door, I rushed out, relocked with convulsed haste, and darted down to the dormitory.

But I trembled too much to dress. Fingers so shaken cannot manage hairpins or hooks-and-eyes. I called Rosine and bribed her to help me. Rosine liked a bribe. She smoothed and plaited my hair with a practised neatness; set the lace collar mathematically straight; tied the neck-ribbon with exactness; did all like the neat-handed Phillis she could be when she chose. Then, candle in hand, she lighted me downstairs. At the last, I had forgotten my shawl; she ran back; and I stood in the vestibule with Dr. John waiting.

“What is this, Lucy?” he said, looking down at me keenly. “Here is the old excitement. Ha! the nun again?”

I utterly denied it. I was vexed to be suspected of a second illusion. He was sceptical.

“She has been, as sure as I live,” said he. “Her figure crossing your eyes leaves on them a peculiar gleam and expression not to be mistaken.”

“She has not been,” I persisted—for indeed, her apparition I could deny with truth.

“The old symptoms are there,” he affirmed: “a particular pale, and what the Scotch call a ‘raised’ look.”

He was so obstinate I told him what I had seen—the light, the curtain, the sudden blank. With him, of course, it was still the same cause: optics, nerves, malady. I did not believe him—not one bit; but I did not contradict. Doctors are so self-opinionated, so immovable in their dry, material views.

Rosine returned with the shawl, and I was bundled into the carriage.

The theatre was full—crammed to the roof. Royal and noble were there; palace and hotel had poured their inmates into those tiers, thronged and hushed. Even before the curtain rose, the place had its own soundscape: the low sea-swell of many breaths held in one cavern; the rustle of silk like reeds in a winter wind; the occasional thin ring of a dropped glass, startling in that gathered silence. I felt, keenly, the privilege of a place before the stage. I longed to see this being whose fame had made my anticipation severe and austere, yet riveted.

She rose at nine that December night: above the horizon I saw her come. She could shine yet with pale grandeur and steady might; but that star verged already on its judgment-day. Seen near, it was a chaos—hollow, half-consumed: an orb perished or perishing—half lava, half glow.

I had heard her called plain. I expected harsh bone, grim angles, something large, sallow. What I saw was the shadow of a royal Vashti: a queen, fair as day once, now pale like twilight, wasted like wax in flame.

For a while—a long while—I thought it only a woman, though a unique woman, moving in might and grace before the multitude. By-and-by I knew my mistake. There was on her something neither woman nor man. In each eye sat a devil. These forces bore her through the tragedy; they kept up her feeble strength, for she was frail; and as the action rose, they shook her with passions like a pit’s upheaving. They wrote HELL upon her straight, haughty brow. They tuned her voice to torment. They wrung her regal face to a demoniac mask. Hate and Murder and Madness, incarnate, stood there.

Marvellous: a mighty revelation.

Also low, horrible, immoral.

What were swordsmen bleeding out on arena sand? what bulls goring horses?—meeker sights, milder condiments, than this human creature torn by seven devils—devils that cried sore and rent the tenement they haunted, yet would not be exorcised.

Suffering had struck that stage-empress; and she stood before her audience neither yielding nor enduring nor, in any finite measure, resenting. She stood locked in struggle, rigid in resistance—draped, not dressed, in pale antique folds long and regular as sculpture, set against deepest crimson that flung her out white like alabaster—like silver—nay, like Death.

What hurts her becomes embodied at once; she attacks it as prey. Scarcely substance herself, she grappled with abstractions. Before calamity she was tigress: she rent woe, shivered grief in convulsed abhorrence. Pain bore no good fruit for her; tears watered no harvest of wisdom. Sickness, death itself, she faced with a rebel eye. Wicked, perhaps; strong, assuredly. Strength had conquered Beauty, had overcome Grace, and bound both at her side, captives peerlessly fair and docile. Even in frenzy, every movement was royally upborne. Her loose hair, in revel or war, was still an angel’s hair, glorious under a halo; fallen, insurgent, banished, she remembered the heaven where she rebelled. Heaven’s light followed her exile and disclosed the forlorn remoteness.

Vashti was not good, I had been told; and she did not look good—though spirit, a spirit from Tophet. Well: if so much unholy force can rise from below, may not one day an equal efflux of sacred essence descend from above?

What thought Dr. Graham?

For long intervals I forgot to look. Genius drew my heart out of its old orbit, as a fierce, cometary light draws a sunflower away from the south. I had seen acting, but never this: never anything that astonished Hope and hushed Desire; that outstripped Impulse and paled Conception; that showed power like a deep winter river, swollen, thundering in cataract, bearing the soul like a leaf on the steel sweep of its descent.

People had called Dr. Bretton serious and impassioned—too grave, too impressible. I never saw him so. He was impressionable as dimpling water, and as water, almost unimpressible: sun and breeze moved him; metal could not engrave, nor fire brand. He could think—and think well—but he was made rather for action than meditation. He could feel, vividly in his way; but his heart had no chord for enthusiasm. Sweet, bright influences his eyes and lips welcomed; for what belonged to storm—wild, intense, dangerous, flaming—he had no sympathy.

When at last I looked at him, it amused and enlightened me to discover he watched Vashti not with wonder, worship, nor dismay, but with intense curiosity. Her agony did not pain him; her wild moan—worse than a shriek—did not much move him; her fury revolted him somewhat, but not to horror. Cool young Briton! The pale cliffs of his England do not look down on the Channel more calmly than he watched that Pythian inspiration.

I longed to know his exact opinion, and asked.

At the sound of my voice he woke, as from a dream of his own thoughts. “How do you like Vashti?” I wished to know.

“Hm-m-m,” was the first answer—scarce articulate, yet expressive. Then a strange smile wandered round his lips, critical, almost callous. In a few terse phrases he gave his judgment—judged her as a woman, not an artist. It was a branding judgment.

That night was already marked in my book of life, not with white, but with a deep-red cross. Yet it was not ended; other memoranda were to be set down in tint indelible.

Towards midnight, when the tragedy blackened to the death-scene, and all held breath—when even Graham bit his under-lip, knit his brow, sat still and struck—when the whole theatre was hushed, all eyes centred, all ears leaned towards one quarter—nothing seen but the white form sunk on a seat, quivering in conflict with the last, worst-hated, visibly conquering foe—nothing heard but throes and gaspings, breathing mutiny, panting defiance—when it seemed an inordinate will convulsed a perishing frame and fought doom and death foot by foot, drop by drop, resisting to the latest the rape of every faculty—would see, would hear, would breathe, would live up to, within, well-nigh beyond the moment when death says to sense and being, “Thus far and no farther”—

Just then, behind the scenes, a stir ran like a bad omen: feet hurried; voices spoke. What was it? asked the whole house.

A flame, a smell of smoke replied.

“Fire!” rang through the gallery. “Fire!” repeated, re-echoed, yelled. And faster than pen can set it down came panic—rushing, crushing—a blind, selfish, cruel chaos. The sound of it was a new, monstrous thing: not music, not speech, but the raw roar of terror; the theatre’s carefully gathered hush broken into a savage industrial clamour, as if some machine had been loosed among human throats.

And Dr. John? I see him now, with comely courage and cordial calm.

“Lucy will sit still, I know,” said he, glancing down at me with serene goodness—the same repose of firmness I had seen by his mother’s hearth. Thus adjured, I think I would have sat still under a rocking crag. Besides, to sit still was my instinct; and at the price of my life I would not have moved to trouble him, thwart him, or make demands on his attention.

We were in the stalls. For a few minutes there was a terrible pressure round us—hot, suffocating, ruthless.

“How terrified are the women!” said he; “but if the men were not almost equally so, order might be maintained. This is a sorry scene. I see fifty selfish brutes at this moment, each of whom, if I were near, I could conscientiously knock down. I see some women braver than some men. There is one yonder—Good God!”

A young girl who had been clinging quietly to a gentleman before us was struck from his arms by a big, butcherly intruder and hurled under the crowd’s feet. Scarce two seconds lasted her disappearance. Graham rushed forward; he and the gentleman—a powerful man though grey-haired—united their strength to thrust back the throng. The girl’s head and long hair fell back over Graham’s shoulder; she seemed unconscious.

“Trust her with me; I am a medical man,” said Dr. John.

“If you have no lady with you, be it so,” was the answer. “Hold her, and I will force a passage: we must get her to the air.”

“I have a lady,” said Graham; “but she will be neither hindrance nor incumbrance.”

He summoned me with his eye. We had been separated by the press. Resolute to rejoin him, I penetrated the living barrier, creeping under where I could not pass between or over.

“Fasten on me, and don’t leave go,” he said; and I obeyed.

Our pioneer—this iron-grey Englishman—proved strong and adroit. He opened the dense mass like a wedge; with patience and toil bored through the flesh-and-blood rock—so solid, hot, suffocating—and brought us at last to the fresh, freezing night. The cold struck my lungs like a draught of pure water after smoke.

“You are an Englishman!” said he, turning shortly on Dr. Bretton when we reached the street.

“An Englishman. And I speak to a countryman?” was the reply.

“Right. Be good enough to stand here two minutes whilst I find my carriage.”

“Papa, I am not hurt,” said a girlish voice, faint but clear; “am I with papa?”

“You are with a friend, and your father is close at hand.”

“Tell him I am not hurt, except just in my shoulder. Oh, my shoulder! They trod just here.”

“Dislocation, perhaps!” muttered the doctor. “Let us hope there is no worse injury. Lucy, lend a hand one instant.”

I assisted while he arranged her drapery and position for ease. She suppressed a moan and lay quietly and patiently in his arms.

“She is very light,” said Graham, “like a child!” and in my ear he asked, “Is she a child, Lucy? Did you notice her age?”

“I am not a child—I am a person of seventeen,” returned the patient, demurely, with dignity; and then, directly, anxiety broke through: “Tell papa to come; I get anxious.”

The carriage drove up. Her father relieved Graham; but in the change of arms she was hurt and moaned again.

“My darling!” said the father tenderly; then, turning to Graham, “You said, sir, you are a medical man?”

“I am—Dr. Bretton, of La Terrasse.”

“Good. Will you step into my carriage?”

“My own carriage is here: I will seek it, and accompany you.”

“Be pleased then to follow us.” And he named his address: the Hôtel Crécy, in the Rue Crécy.

We followed. Their carriage drove fast. Graham and I were silent. It felt like an adventure written on the instant—one of those turns life takes, sudden as a knife in cloth.

Some little time was lost in finding our own equipage; but we reached the hotel perhaps ten minutes after the strangers. It was an hotel in the foreign sense: not an inn, but a collection of dwelling-houses—a vast lofty pile, with a huge arch to the street-door, leading through a vaulted way into a square built round on all sides. Sound changed there: the street’s alarm was left behind; the enclosed court held only the echo of wheels, the quick commands of servants, the sharp fall of doors—each noise struck clean, as if the stone kept account of it.

We mounted a wide public staircase and stopped at Numéro 2 on the second landing; the first floor, Graham said, was occupied by some “prince Russe.” We rang at a second great door and were admitted to handsome apartments. Announced by a servant in livery, we entered a drawing-room where an English fire glowed—coal snapping, flame steady and domestic—and foreign mirrors made the light double. Near the hearth stood a small group: a slight form sunk in a deep arm-chair, one or two women busy about it, and the iron-grey gentleman anxiously looking on.

People who live behind walls—school walls, convent walls, the walls a city sets up between one street and the next—are apt to be mislaid by those who move in open air. Not always from neglect. Life outside rushes like a river in thaw: it snatches letters out of hands, it shakes appointments loose, it carries away the small proofs by which one remembers one is still remembered. After a season of frequent messages—after a knot of little excitements that seem, by their very nature, to bind communication tighter—there will come, without warning, a hush. The post that used to knock will pass in silence; the familiar step will not sound on the stair. A blank begins; it does not explain itself.

There are reasons—excellent reasons—if only the shut-in knew them. The hermit counts his minutes by the tick of a clock that seems to have grown tired; he watches hours go past like travellers who drag their feet and stop too often. But those same days, outside, may be full of movement: errands, consultations, sudden illnesses, sudden recoveries, money-matters, journeys; the swirl of the world’s business which makes seven weeks seem not a winter but a handful of hurried mornings.

If the hermit is wise, he learns a difficult domestic art: to lay his feelings by, as one lays away fruit for a scant season; to fold his thoughts tight, like linen in a drawer, and shut the drawer. Destiny sometimes means us to imitate small creatures that sleep through frost. Make yourself compact, creep into the crevice of your own life, let the drift pile up; endure the cold. Say, because you must: It is right, for so it is.

Yet winter in a human chest is not like winter in a field; it does not always thaw cleanly. Sometimes spring returns, and the sealed place opens; air, sun, and south-wind come in, and the sound of running water is enough to raise one again. Sometimes the frost gets into the heart and never goes out, and what is left is only what scavengers find. Even that would be “right,” in the hard sense: mortals are mortals. “As well soon as syne.”

After that evening at the theatre came for me seven weeks as bare as seven sheets of unused paper. Not a line was written on one. Not a visit, not a token.

Midway through, my mind began to fill the white pages with its own dark ink. Something had happened at La Terrasse, I told myself: an accident, an illness. That middle point is always clouded for the solitary. Expectation strains the nerves as a tight string strains under a finger; doubts, kept down for days, heap themselves, gather weight, and roll back with a kind of revenge. Nights grew unkind. Sleep and I could not agree; I started from dozes as if some hand had shaken me. Dreams came in a sinister band—calamity leading them, desertion marching close behind—and they sat on my bed with cold breath and busy eyes. I did what I could; but one can be brave and still be a poor, pale, wasting creature.

Toward the end of the seven weeks I admitted what I had guarded against admitting: that these blanks were part of my lot; that they came from circumstance, from Fate; that they had an origin one must not question and a sequence one must not complain of. I did not wrong myself by any weak self-accusation. I knew enough justice to understand that suffering is not always a fault. And as to blaming others for silence, my reason acquitted them and my heart acquitted them too. Only, the road was heavy; I longed for a different weather.

I tried to fill my days with decent industry: I began a piece of lace-work so elaborate it ought to have had power to mesmerise the mind; I studied German till the syllables turned dry as husks; I set myself a course of reading in the library—those thick books whose pages smell of dust and old glue, whose knowledge, like dried peas, needs long soaking to be edible. I did all as orthodoxly as I could. The result was like gnawing a file for hunger, or drinking brine for thirst.

My hour of torment was the post-hour. I knew it by the air, by the sound in the passage, by the stir of the house; and I tried to trick myself—uselessly—out of that knowledge. I dreaded the nerve-shock of expectation, and then the sick drop after the ring when nothing followed for me.

I used to think of caged creatures—half-fed—waiting for their portion. So I waited for a letter. And to speak truth, and have done with the pretence of calm: I suffered. I had bitter fears and strange inward trials; hope failed in me as a flame fails when the wick is too short; despair came so near that it breathed through me like a cold draught in a closed room and made the heart seem to move under a weight. The letter—the well-beloved letter—would not come; and it was all of sweetness I had to look for.

At the extremity of want I went again and again to my small hoard: the five letters locked in their case. How splendid that month seemed which had dropped those five stars into my dark! Always at night I visited them. Not daring to ask each evening for a candle below, I bought a wax taper and matches; at study-hour I stole up to the dormitory, and there, with the taper’s thin honey-coloured flame, I feasted—like some famished guest at a Barmecide table—on what looked like bread and proved only air. It did not nourish me. I grew thin as a shadow; but I was not ill.

One night, reading late, I felt even this poor power fading: the letters, from too much poring, were losing sap and savour; my gold seemed turning to leaves in my hands. I was grieving over the disillusion when a quick tripping foot ran up the stairs. I knew Ginevra Fanshawe’s step. She had dined in town; she was returned to fetch shawl or ribbon from the wardrobe.

In she came, bright silk rustling, her shawl slipping from her shoulders, curls half undone by damp night-air, heavy on her neck. I had barely time to shut away my treasures and lock the bureau before she was at my side, out of humour.

“It has been a stupid evening: they are stupid people,” she began.

“Who? Mrs. Cholmondeley? I thought you always found her house charming.”

“I have not been to Mrs. Cholmondeley’s.”

“Indeed! Have you made new acquaintance?”

“My uncle de Bassompierre is come.”

“Your uncle de Bassompierre! Are you not glad? I thought he was a favourite.”

“You thought wrong: the man is odious; I hate him.”

“Because he is a foreigner? or for what other reason of equal weight?”

“He is not a foreigner. The man is English enough, goodness knows, and had an English name till three or four years ago; but his mother was a foreigner—a de Bassompierre—and some of her family are dead and have left him estates, a title, and this name: he is quite a great man now.”

“Do you hate him for that reason?”

“Don’t I know what mamma says about him? He is not my own uncle, but married mamma’s sister. Mamma detests him; she says he killed aunt Ginevra with unkindness: he looks like a bear. Such a dismal evening!” she ran on. “I’ll go no more to his big hotel. Fancy me walking into a room alone, and a great man fifty years old coming forwards, and after a few minutes’ conversation actually turning his back upon me, and then abruptly going out of the room. Such odd ways! I daresay his conscience smote him, for they all say at home I am the picture of aunt Ginevra. Mamma often declares the likeness is quite ridiculous.”

“Were you the only visitor?”

“The only visitor? Yes; then there was missy, my cousin: little spoiled, pampered thing.”

“M. de Bassompierre has a daughter?”

“Yes, yes: don’t tease one with questions. Oh, dear! I am so tired.”

She yawned, and without ceremony threw herself on my bed. “It seems Mademoiselle was nearly crushed to a jelly in a hubbub at the theatre some weeks ago.”

“Ah! indeed. And they live at a large hotel in the Rue Crécy?”

“Justement. How do you know?”

“I have been there.”

“Oh, you have? Really! You go everywhere in these days. I suppose Mother Bretton took you. She and Esculapius have the entrée of the de Bassompierre apartments: it seems ‘my son John’ attended missy on the occasion of her accident—Accident? Bah! All affectation! I don’t think she was squeezed more than she richly deserves for her airs. And now there is quite an intimacy struck up: I heard something about ‘auld lang syne,’ and what not. Oh, how stupid they all were!”

“All! You said you were the only visitor.”

“Did I? You see one forgets to particularize an old woman and her boy.”

“Dr. and Mrs. Bretton were at M. de Bassompierre’s this evening?”

“Ay, ay! as large as life; and missy played the hostess. What a conceited doll it is!”

Her sourness had a plain cause: incense had been withheld; homage diverted; coquetry had failed; vanity smarted. She lay there, fuming in her own perfume.

“Is Miss de Bassompierre quite well now?” I asked.

“As well as you or I, no doubt; but she is an affected little thing, and gave herself invalid airs to attract medical notice. And to see the old dowager making her recline on a couch, and ‘my son John’ prohibiting excitement, etcetera—faugh! the scene was quite sickening.”

“It would not have been so if the object of attention had been changed: if you had taken Miss de Bassompierre’s place.”

“Indeed! I hate ‘my son John!’”

“‘My son John!’—whom do you indicate by that name? Dr. Bretton’s mother never calls him so.”

“Then she ought. A clownish, bearish John he is.”

“You violate the truth in saying so; and as the whole of my patience is now spun off the distaff, I peremptorily desire you to rise from that bed, and vacate this room.”

“Passionate thing! Your face is the colour of a coquelicot. I wonder what always makes you so mighty testy à l’endroit du gros Jean? ‘John Anderson, my Joe, John!’ Oh, the distinguished name!”

My anger rose in a hot, useless wave. There is no grappling with such a creature—light as down, and as irritating. I put out my taper, locked my bureau, and left her there, since she would not leave me. Small-beer as she was, she had turned unendurably acid.

The morrow was Thursday, our half-holiday. Breakfast ended, I withdrew to the first classe. The dreaded post-hour drew near, and I waited for it as a person who believes in apparitions might wait the hour when a spectre crosses a room—fearing it, yet unable not to watch. Less than ever was a letter likely; yet the mere possibility tormented me.

It was a winter day of east wind, and I had come, by long trial, into a dreary fellowship with winds and their changes—an alliance the healthy seldom understand. North and east had for me a tyrannous influence: they sharpened pain, they blackened sorrow. South could soothe; west could sometimes cheer—unless it carried thunder-clouds, under whose weight and warmth all energy dies and the mind turns sick.

Bitter and dark as the January morning was, I remember running down—without bonnet—into the garden, and lingering among stripped shrubs and bare stems in the forlorn hope the postman’s ring might happen while I was out of hearing. There was a particular nerve in me—worn thin by long repetition of the same thought—which could scarcely bear that clang and its consequence: blankness. I stayed as long as I dared without attracting notice. I muffled my head in my apron; I stopped my ears, as if noise itself were torture.

At last I returned to the first classe. It was not yet nine; no pupils had entered. The first thing my eye caught was a white object lying on my black desk—a flat, pale shape like a leaf on charred ground. The post had come—unheard by me. Rosine had visited my little cell, and like an angel, left a token. That bright thing was a letter, a real letter. From three yards off I saw as much; and since I had but one correspondent on earth, it must be from him. He remembered me yet. Gratitude sent a deep pulse through my heart—like warm broth into a chilled body, like bread into a starving stomach.

I drew near, trembling, almost sure of a known hand; and found, instead, a pale female scrawl where I had looked for a firm masculine character. The blow was so sharp I spoke aloud: “This is cruel.”

I got over it. Life remains life, whatever its pangs: eyes still see, ears still hear, though the prospect of what delights be withdrawn, and the sound of what consoles be silenced.

I opened the billet. By that time I knew the hand well enough. It was dated “La Terrasse,” and it ran thus:

“DEAR LUCY,—It occurs to me to inquire what you have been doing with yourself for the last month or two? Not that I suspect you would have the least difficulty in giving an account of your proceedings. I daresay you have been just as busy and as happy as ourselves at La Terrasse. As to Graham, his professional connection extends daily: he is so much sought after, so much engaged, that I tell him he will grow quite conceited. Like a right good mother, as I am, I do my best to keep him down: no flattery does he get from me, as you know. And yet, Lucy, he is a fine fellow: his mother’s heart dances at the sight of him. After being hurried here and there the whole day, and passing the ordeal of fifty sorts of tempers, and combating a hundred caprices, and sometimes witnessing cruel sufferings—perhaps, occasionally, as I tell him, inflicting them—at night he still comes home to me in such kindly, pleasant mood, that really, I seem to live in a sort of moral antipodes, and on these January evenings my day rises when other people’s night sets in.

“Still he needs keeping in order, and correcting, and repressing, and I do him that good service; but the boy is so elastic there is no such thing as vexing him thoroughly. When I think I have at last driven him to the sullens, he turns on me with jokes for retaliation: but you know him and all his iniquities, and I am but an elderly simpleton to make him the subject of this epistle.

“As for me, I have had my old Bretton agent here on a visit, and have been plunged overhead and ears in business matters. I do so wish to regain for Graham at least some part of what his father left him. He laughs to scorn my anxiety on this point, bidding me look and see how he can provide for himself and me too, and asking what the old lady can possibly want that she has not; hinting about sky-blue turbans; accusing me of an ambition to wear diamonds, keep livery servants, have an hotel, and lead the fashion amongst the English clan in Villette.

“Talking of sky-blue turbans, I wish you had been with us the other evening. He had come in really tired, and after I had given him his tea, he threw himself into my chair with his customary presumption. To my great delight, he dropped asleep. (You know how he teases me about being drowsy; I, who never, by any chance, close an eye by daylight.) While he slept, I thought he looked very bonny, Lucy: fool as I am to be so proud of him; but who can help it? Show me his peer. Look where I will, I see nothing like him in Villette. Well, I took it into my head to play him a trick: so I brought out the sky-blue turban, and handling it with gingerly precaution, I managed to invest his brows with this grand adornment. I assure you it did not at all misbecome him; he looked quite Eastern, except that he is so fair. Nobody, however, can accuse him of having red hair now—it is genuine chestnut—a dark, glossy chestnut; and when I put my large cashmere about him, there was as fine a young bey, dey, or pacha improvised as you would wish to see.

“It was good entertainment; but only half-enjoyed, since I was alone: you should have been there.

“In due time my lord awoke: the looking-glass above the fireplace soon intimated to him his plight: as you may imagine, I now live under threat and dread of vengeance.

“But to come to the gist of my letter. I know Thursday is a half-holiday in the Rue Fossette: be ready, then, by five in the afternoon, at which hour I will send the carriage to take you out to La Terrasse. Be sure to come: you may meet some old acquaintance. Good-by, my wise, dear, grave little god-daughter.—Very truly yours,

“LOUISA BRETTON.”

A letter like that sets one to rights. I was not, perhaps, gay after reading it; but I was steadied, eased—like a room after a window is opened and stale air let out. My friends were well; Graham had not met danger; his mother had not sickened—those calamities my mind had been cooking nightly, as if grief were a broth forever simmering on a low flame. Their feeling for me, too, remained what it had been. And yet I could not help looking at Mrs. Bretton’s seven weeks and then at mine, and marvelling at the difference.

How wise it is for those set in an exceptional position to keep silence about the galling of it. The world understands perishing for lack of food: hunger is a common tongue. But the starvation that comes from solitary confinement—how the mind thins, how the nerves first burn and then go numb, how the senses, deprived of their natural nourishment, begin to invent phantoms—is too intricate for easy sympathy. Speak of it, and you might as well stand in a market-place and utter riddles in some dead dialect. Long may it be that few comprehend such themes; long may it be that physical privations alone are thought real, and the rest a fancy. Perhaps it is kinder so. In old days moral trial was a rarer mystery: in all Israel, there was but one Saul, and but one David to soothe him.

The keen still cold of morning sharpened, later, into a harsher breath from far wastes: the cold zone sighed over the temperate and froze it fast. A heavy sky—dull, thick with snow—sailed from the north and settled over expectant Europe. Toward afternoon the descent began. The storm was so dense and wild I feared no carriage would venture; but trust my godmother: she asked, and she would have her guest.

About six o’clock I was lifted from the carriage over front steps already blocked with drifted white, and set within the door of La Terrasse.

I ran through the vestibule and up-stairs to the drawing-room. There was Mrs. Bretton—a summer day in her own person, with warmth in her clasp and a kind kiss that thawed what the east wind had frozen. After months of bare boards, black benches, desks, and the sour breath of school stoves, the blue saloon struck my senses like richness: not only sight, but smell—the clean resinous scent of a good fire; the faint perfume of tea and wax and polished wood. The hearth glowed with a Christmas-like crimson splendour that dazzled me.

She held my hand awhile; she chatted; she scolded me for growing thinner; then declared the snow-wind had disordered my hair, and sent me up-stairs to make it neat and remove my shawl.

In my own little sea-green room I found a bright fire, and candles already lit: tall waxlights stood on either side of the great looking-glass. But between those candles, and before the glass, something moved—something that seemed to be dressing itself: slight, white, airy; like a winter spirit come to warm its hands at my hearth.

For one instant I thought of Graham’s spectral fancies. I watched, cautious. The thing wore white, sprinkled with small drops of scarlet; a red girdle defined its slenderness; in its hair was something leafy yet shining—a wreath with evergreen gloss. Frightful it was not; only strange in that quiet room.

It turned quickly. A large eye, under long dark lashes, flashed full upon me; the lashes softened the clear brilliance they guarded. The voice that came was low and gentle.

“Ah! you are come!” she breathed, and smiled slowly, looking at me with an intentness that felt like recognition.

I knew her then. One does not forget that cast of fine, delicate feature once seen.

“Miss de Bassompierre,” I said.

“No,” she answered, “not Miss de Bassompierre for you!” She did not explain at once; she let the moment ripen, as if taste must be given time upon the tongue.

“You are changed, but still you are yourself,” she said, coming nearer. “I remember you well—your countenance, the colour of your hair, the outline of your face….”

I moved toward the fire; she stood opposite and looked into me. While she gazed, her face grew more and more full of thought and feeling, until a dimness rose and quenched the clear light of her eyes.

“It makes me almost cry to look so far back,” she said. “But as to being sorry, or sentimental, don’t think it: on the contrary, I am quite pleased and glad.”

I was interested, yet utterly at fault; words would not come readily. At last I stammered, “I think I never met you till that night, some weeks ago, when you were hurt…?”

She smiled—a smile that seemed to bridge time. “You have forgotten then that I have sat on your knee, been lifted in your arms, even shared your pillow? You no longer remember the night when I came crying, like a naughty little child as I was, to your bedside, and you took me in. You have no memory for the comfort and protection by which you soothed an acute distress? Go back to Bretton. Remember Mr. Home.”

All at once the past unfolded—like a folded paper opened and smoothed flat.

“And you are little Polly?” I breathed.

“I am Paulina Mary Home de Bassompierre.”

Cheerful as my godmother was by nature, and as industrious as she could be, for our sakes, in setting a brightness upon the evening at La Terrasse, enjoyment did not thoroughly take hold of us till the winter itself announced deliverance. The wind had been crying all along the eaves like a creature shut out; the snow kept striking the panes with a dry, stinging sound, and, now and then, the blast—finding some hollow under the roof—set it to a long, organ-like moan. Those who sit warm in lamplight know that comfort can still be divided from the heart: imagination is not chained to the hearth; it will go pacing with the absent—out where the road is lost in drifts, where the gates are buried, where listening becomes pain.

At last, through that wild chorus, came the sharper, welcome notes—iron on stone, a horse’s hoof striking firm ground; a jingle of bit and chain; a voice lifted short against the wind. It was as if the whole house leaned to hear. We ran down into the hall; yet even while we hurried, the storm’s violence made us cautious. They entered with a warning gesture, as if they brought the weather with them.

Father and son came at last to the château: the Count de Bassompierre had ridden with Dr. Bretton. When the door opened, we saw not men, but two white shapes—two walking mounds of snow, the folds of their greatcoats stuffed, their hats and shoulders thick as if winter had laid hands on them and would not let go. Mrs. Bretton took them in with one glance, and her motherly authority rose like a staff.

“To the kitchen—instantly. Not one step on my staircase—at your peril—till you have left off that mask of Old Christmas.”

And so, without appeal, they were driven to the kitchen; and we, obedient only in the letter, followed them there. It was a large old Dutch kitchen, with dark beams, warm brick, a wide hearth where the flame did not merely burn but lived—crackling, hissing, and then dropping suddenly to a red, steady breathing. Pots hung ready; a copper pan caught the firelight on its belly; a smell of woodsmoke, and something faintly sweet—orange-peel, perhaps, and clove—lingered as if the room remembered former seasons.

The little Countess, white too with blown snow where she had dashed near the door, danced a circle round her equally white sire, clapping her hands.

“Papa, papa, you look like an enormous Polar bear!”

The bear shook himself; a shower of ice-dust flew out and pattered on the flags, and the sprite fled, squealing and laughing, only to return the next instant, bold again, eager to help tear away the arctic disguise. When the Count, at last, got free of his dreadnought of a greatcoat, he lifted it as if he would drop it upon her like an avalanche.

“Come, then,” she said, bending her head as if inviting doom.

He advanced it playfully; she bounded away—quick as a little chamois; soft too, with that velvet grace which makes speed look like play. Her laugh rang clear, silver on crystal. She took his hands—those cold, gloveless hands—and rubbed them hard between her own, as if she meant, by sheer affection, to force warmth back into the fingers. When she rose on tiptoe and reached his lips for a kiss, there shone round her a kind of light—no miracle, only that human radiance which love gives off without asking leave. The grave and reverend seigneur looked down on her as men look on what is indeed the apple of their eye.

“Mrs. Bretton,” said he, “what am I to do with this daughter—or daughterling? She neither grows in wisdom nor in stature. Don’t you find her pretty nearly as much the child as she was ten years ago?”

“She cannot be more the child,” returned Mrs. Bretton, “than this great boy of mine,”—for she was already at war with her son about some alteration of dress she deemed prudent, and which he resisted with laughing obstinacy, leaning against the Dutch dresser and keeping her at arm’s length.

“Come, mamma,” he said, “by way of compromise—and to get warmth inside as well as out—let us have a Christmas wassail-cup, and toast Old England here on the hearth.”

At that word the kitchen took on its ceremonial air. Mrs. Bretton herself moved with decision to the region of jars and spices, and instructed Martha in the mysteries as if they were ancient rites: the bowl was set to warm; sugar was measured; a cinnamon stick snapped and gave out its dry perfume; cloves pricked the air with a dark sweetness; and, when the liquor met the heat, it sent up a breath like winter made friendly—steam carrying ale and spice, orange and nutmeg, rich and rough together. The sound of it—the first low simmer, the spoon scraping the bottom, the small clink of ladle against rim—seemed to lay a soft hand over the wind’s howl outside.

She poured the draught into a Bretton flagon, and it was served round—reaming hot—by means of a small silver vessel which I recognized at once: Graham’s christening-cup, lifted now to mouths grown grave, and yet capable of holiday.

“Here’s to Auld Lang Syne!” cried the Count, holding the glancing cup aloft; and then, turning to Mrs. Bretton, he began, in that homely, mountain-scented Scots which carries the taste of heather and peat even when spoken in a foreign room:

“We twa ha’ paidlet i’ the burn
Fra morning sun till dine,
But seas between us braid ha’ roared
Sin’ auld lang syne.

“And surely ye’ll be your pint-stoup,
And surely I’ll be mine;
And we’ll taste a cup o’ kindness yet
For auld lang syne.”

“Scotch! Scotch!” cried Paulina. “Papa is talking Scotch; and Scotch he is—partly. We are Home and de Bassompierre, Caledonian and Gallic.”

“And is that a Scotch reel you are dancing, you Highland fairy?” asked her father. “Mrs. Bretton, there will be a green ring growing up in the middle of your kitchen shortly. I would not answer for her being quite cannie: she is a strange little mortal.”

“Tell Lucy to dance with me, papa; there is Lucy Snowe.”

Mr. Home—there was still quite as much plain Mr. Home about him as proud Count de Bassompierre—held out his hand to me with a kindness that had no condescension in it. He said he remembered me well; and, even had his memory been less trustworthy, my name had been so often on his daughter’s lips, and he had heard so many long tales of me, that I must seem an old acquaintance.

All had tasted the wassail but Paulina; nobody wished to profane her airy movement with a hot draught. Yet she would not be overlooked nor baulked of mortal privileges.

“Let me taste,” she demanded of Graham, as he was setting the cup on the dresser shelf out of her reach.

Mrs. Bretton and Mr. Home were already deep in talk. Dr. John, though he said little, had been watching the fairy’s dance, and he liked it. Not only the beauty and ease of those movements pleased his grace-loving eye; that liberty in his mother’s house charmed him because it set him at ease too: again she seemed a child for him—again almost his playmate. I wondered what his first words would be to her; they came with a half-mocking deference that revived the old days as surely as a tune revives a room.

“Your ladyship wishes for the tankard?”

“I think I said so. I think I intimated as much.”

“Couldn’t consent to a step of the kind on any account. Sorry for it, but couldn’t do it.”

“Why? I am quite well now: it can’t break my collar-bone again, or dislocate my shoulder. Is it wine?”

“No; nor dew.”

“I don’t want dew; I don’t like dew: but what is it?”

“Ale—strong ale—old October; brewed, perhaps, when I was born.”

“It must be curious: is it good?”

“Excessively good.”

He took it down, administered to himself a second dose, let his mischievous eyes confess extreme contentment, and replaced the cup with solemnity.

“I should like a little,” said Paulina, looking up. “I never had any old October: is it sweet?”

“Perilously sweet,” said Graham.

She continued to look up—exactly with the face of a child longing for a forbidden dainty. At last the Doctor relented, and—because indulgence is often sweetest to the giver—he let her taste from his hand. His eyes, always frank in revealing pleasurable feelings, smiled their confession; and he prolonged the sport by managing the cup so that only a drop at a time could reach the rosy lips that courted its rim. The ale smelled of spice and warmed grain; it had that deep, yeasty breath of barley long kept, and the wassail’s cinnamon and citrus rode above it like a holiday.

“A little more—a little more,” she said, petulantly touching his hand with one forefinger to coax a more generous tilt. “It smells of spice and sugar, but I can’t taste it; your wrist is so stiff, and you are so stingy.”

He indulged her, but whispered with mock gravity, “Don’t tell my mother or Lucy; they wouldn’t approve.”

“Nor do I,” she answered; and as soon as she had fairly assayed the draught, her tone altered as if some disenchanting potion had undone the fairy-work. “I find it anything but sweet; it is bitter and hot, and takes away my breath. Your old October was only desirable while forbidden. Thank you—no more.”

With a slight bend—careless, yet as graceful as her dance—she glided away to her father.

I believed her. The child of seven lived still in the girl of seventeen, ready to start up at a touch; but she was not ruled by it.

Graham looked after her a little baffled, a little puzzled. His eye returned to her more than once through the rest of the evening; she did not seem to notice him. When we went up to the drawing-room for tea, she took her father’s arm as if it were the natural place appointed to her. Her eyes and ears were dedicated to him. He and Mrs. Bretton did most of the talking; Paulina did the best listening—attending closely, prompting him with eager little questions that drew out recollection as one draws out a thread.

“And where were you at such a time, papa? And what did you say then? And tell Mrs. Bretton what happened on that occasion.”

She yielded no more effervescence of glee; the infant sparkle had exhaled with the kitchen heat. Now she was soft, thoughtful, docile. Her good-night was pretty to see. To Graham her manner had a touch of dignity; in her slight smile and quiet bow spoke the Countess. Graham bowed in return with gravity, as if unsure how to reconcile, in one image, the dancing fairy and the delicate dame.

Next morning we assembled round the breakfast-table, all of us shivering and fresh from the chill bravery of washing in winter water. Outside, the day made a noise like an adversary. Mrs. Bretton, after one look at the casements, pronounced a decree: nobody, unless driven by dire necessity, should quit her house.

Indeed, egress seemed next to impossible. The drift darkened the lower panes; and when one peered out, the sky looked vexed and dim, as if powdered glass were blown through it. The snowfall had ceased, but what had already fallen was being torn up again and whirled about by brief shrieking gusts, cast into a hundred fantastic shapes—now a wall, now a wave, now a sudden white blindness across the garden paths.

The Countess seconded the decree with zeal.

“Papa shall not go out,” she said, drawing her chair beside her father’s. “I will look after him. You won’t go into town, will you, papa?”

“Ay—and No,” he answered. “If you and Mrs. Bretton are very good to me, Polly—kind, you know, and attentive—if you pet me in a very nice manner and make much of me, I may be induced to wait an hour after breakfast and see whether this razor-edged wind settles. But, you see, you give me no breakfast; you offer me nothing; you let me starve.”

“Quick! please, Mrs. Bretton—pour out the coffee,” begged Paulina, while she set herself to the Count’s plate with the air of a small, passionate housekeeper. “Since he grew into a Count, he has needed so much attention.”

She split and prepared a roll with quick, neat fingers.

“There, papa—your pistolets are charged,” said she. “And there is some marmalade—just the same sort we used to have at Bretton, and which you said was as good as if it had been conserved in Scotland—”

“And which your little ladyship used to beg for my boy,” Mrs. Bretton interposed, looking at Graham with a mother’s treacherous delight. “Do you remember?
