How you would come to my elbow and touch my sleeve, whispering, ‘Please, ma’am, something good for Graham—a little marmalade, or honey, or jam?’”

“No, mamma,” broke in Dr. John, laughing and yet reddening. “It surely was not so. I could not have cared for these things.”

“Did he or did he not, Paulina?”

“He liked them,” Paulina asserted, without mercy.

“Never blush for it, John,” said Mr. Home. “I like them myself yet, and always did. And Polly showed sense in catering for a friend’s material comforts: it was I who put her in the way of such manners—nor do I let her forget them. Polly, offer me a small slice of that tongue.”

“There, papa: but remember you are only waited upon with this assiduity on condition of being persuadable, and reconciling yourself to La Terrasse for the day.”

“Mrs. Bretton,” said the Count suddenly, “I want to get rid of my daughter—to send her to school. Do you know of any good school?”

“There is Lucy’s place—Madame Beck’s.”

“Miss Snowe is in a school?”

“I am a teacher,” I said; and I was glad—strangely glad—of the chance to say it plain. For a little while, I had been feeling as if I sat among them under some borrowed light. Mrs. Bretton and her son knew my circumstances; the Count and his daughter did not. I spoke readily; yet, at the words, there rose in me—uninvoked—a swarm of thoughts, dim and thick; and I sighed in spite of myself.

Mr. Home kept his eyes on his plate for a time; he said nothing. Perhaps he had not caught my words; perhaps, being Scotch, and proud after his own fashion, he judged that on such a confession politeness should not fasten. Whether his pride was pseudo or dignity real, I leave wide and undecided. As it touched me, I can answer simply: then and always he showed himself a true-hearted gentleman.

He was by nature a feeler and a thinker; over his emotions lay a mellow melancholy, deepened—where trouble and bereavement had passed—into cloud. He did not know much about Lucy Snowe; what he knew he did not very accurately comprehend; his misconceptions of my character could have made me smile on another morning. But he saw my walk lay on the shady side of the hill. He gave me credit for keeping my course honestly straight. He would have helped me if he could; having no opportunity to help, he wished me well. When at last he did look at me, his eye was kind; and when he spoke, his voice was benevolent.

“Yours,” said he, “is an arduous calling. I wish you health and strength to win in it—success.”

His fair little daughter did not take the knowledge so quietly. She fixed on me wide eyes of wonder—almost dismay.

“Are you a teacher?” she cried; and, after a pause, as if tasting the idea and finding it sharp, “Well, I never knew what you were, nor ever thought of asking: for me, you were always Lucy Snowe.”

“And what am I now?” I asked, unable to forbear.

“Yourself, of course. But do you really teach here, in Villette?”

“I really do.”

“And do you like it?”

“Not always.”

“And why do you go on with it?”

Her father looked at her as if he might check such questioning; but he only said, indulgent, “Proceed, Polly—proceed with that catechism: prove yourself the little wiseacre you are. If Miss Snowe were to blush and look confused, I should have to bid you hold your tongue; and you and I would sit out this meal in disgrace. But she only smiles—so push her hard. Well, Miss Lucy, why do you go on with it?”

“Chiefly, I fear, for the sake of the money I get.”

“Not then from motives of pure philanthropy? Polly and I were clinging to that hypothesis as the most lenient way of accounting for your eccentricity.”

“No—no, sir. Rather for the roof of shelter I am thus enabled to keep over my head; and for the comfort of mind it gives me to think that while I can work for myself, I am spared the pain of being a burden to anybody.”

“Papa,” said Paulina, low and with feeling, “say what you will, I pity Lucy.”

“Take up that pity, Miss de Bassompierre,” he returned, not harshly but with a firm tenderness. “Take it up in both hands, as you might a little callow gosling squattering out of bounds without leave; put it back in the warm nest of the heart whence it issued, and receive in your ear this whisper: if my Polly ever came to know by experience the uncertain nature of this world’s goods, I should like her to act as Lucy acts—to work for herself, that she might burden neither kith nor kin.”

“Yes, papa,” she said, pensively and tractably. Yet her glance stole to me again, as if she could not help tasting compassion, even when bid to set it down.

“But this school,” pursued Mr. Home, changing grave to gay with the ease of a man who knows how to protect feeling by humour, “would Madame Beck admit my Polly, do you think, Miss Lucy?”

I answered that there needed only to try; Madame was fond of English pupils. “If you, sir,” I added, “will but take Miss de Bassompierre in your carriage this very afternoon, I think I can answer for it that Rosine, the portress, will not be very slow in answering your ring; and Madame, I am sure, will put on her best pair of gloves to receive you in the salon.”

“In that case,” said Mr. Home, “I see no necessity for delay. Mrs. Hurst can send what she calls her young lady’s ‘things’ after her; Polly can settle down to her horn-book before night; and you, Miss Lucy, I trust, will not disdain to cast an occasional eye upon her, and let me know, from time to time, how she gets on. I hope you approve of the arrangement, Countess de Bassompierre?”

The Countess hemmed and hesitated.

“I thought,” she said, “I thought I had finished my education—”

“That only proves how much we may be mistaken in our thoughts. I hold a far different opinion, as most of these will who have been auditors of your profound knowledge of life this morning. Ah, my little girl, thou hast much to learn; and papa ought to have taught thee more than he has done. Come—there is nothing for it but to try Madame Beck; and the weather seems settling, and I have finished my breakfast—”

“But, papa!”

“Well?”

“I see an obstacle.”

“I don’t at all.”

“It is enormous, papa; it can never be got over; it is as large as you in your greatcoat and the snowdrift on the top.”

“And, like that snowdrift, capable of melting?”

“No—of too solid flesh. It is just your own self. Miss Lucy, warn Madame Beck not to listen to any overtures about taking me, because, in the end, it would turn out that she would have to take papa too. As he is so teasing, I will tell tales about him. Mrs. Bretton—and all of you—listen. About five years ago, when I was twelve, he took it into his head that he was spoiling me; that I was growing unfitted for the world, and I don’t know what; and nothing would serve but I must go to school. I cried, and so on; but M. de Bassompierre proved hard-hearted—quite firm and flinty—and to school I went. What was the result? In the most admirable manner, papa came to school likewise. Every other day he called to see me. Madame Aigredoux grumbled; it was of no use; and so, at last, papa and I were both, in a manner, expelled. Lucy can tell Madame Beck this trait: it is only fair to let her know what she must expect.”

Mrs. Bretton demanded what the Count had to say in answer. As he made no defence, judgment was given against him, and Paulina triumphed.

Yet she had other moods besides archness and naïveté. After breakfast, when the two elders withdrew—I suppose to speak of certain of Mrs. Bretton’s affairs—and the Countess, Dr. Bretton, and I were left for a short while together, the child dropped from her as if laid aside with the napkin. With us, nearer her in age, she rose at once into the little lady. Even her face seemed to change: that candid play of feature and dimpled roundness she wore for her father yielded to a more thoughtful cast; the lines grew distinct, less mobile; the look steadied.

No doubt Graham noted it as well as I. He stood some minutes by the window, watching the snow worry the garden; the wind outside made a thin, saw-like music against the glass. Then he came to the hearth and tried conversation—but not with his usual ease. Topics did not rise readily; he chose fastidiously, hesitatingly, and so spoke infelicitously: of Villette, its inhabitants, its sights and buildings. Miss de Bassompierre answered in quite womanly sort—intelligently, with a manner not wholly stripped of individuality: here and there a glance, a quick gesture, a tone rather animated than measured recalled little Polly; yet over all was a fine polish, a calm courtesy, such as would forbid a less sensitive man than Graham to seize those childish relics as handles for intimacy.

Still, though Dr. Bretton remained subdued—sedate, for him—he was observant. Not one small impulse, not one natural break escaped him. He did not miss a characteristic movement, a hesitation, or a lisp. At times, when she spoke fast, she still lisped; and then she coloured, and, in a painstaking conscientious way—almost as amusing as the slight error—repeated the word more distinctly, as if correcting not only speech but self.

Whenever she did so, Dr. Bretton smiled.

Gradually the restraint on each side slackened. Had the conference but been prolonged, it would soon have grown genial: already to Paulina’s lip and cheek returned the wreathing, dimpling smile; she lisped once and forgot to correct it. And Dr. John—how he changed I can scarcely define; he did not grow gayer, no raillery sparkled, no levity darted; but he seemed to find the position more pleasant, and his comfort spoke in readier language, in tones more suave. Ten years ago, those two had always found abundance to say; the decade between had not narrowed experience nor impoverished intelligence. Some natures act thus upon each other: the more they say, the more they have to say. Out of association grows adhesion; out of adhesion, amalgamation.

Graham, however, must go. His profession makes demands that do not wait upon weather or inclination. He left the room; but before he could leave the house there was a return. I am sure he came back—not for the paper or card in his desk, which served as his excuse—but to assure himself, by one more glance, that Paulina’s aspect was truly such as memory was carrying away; that he had not been viewing her by some partial light and making a fond mistake. No: the impression held; indeed it gained. He took away a parting look—shy, but very soft—as beautiful and innocent as any little fawn lifting its head from fern, or lamb rousing from its meadow-bed.

When we were alone, Paulina and I kept silence for a time. We both took out work and set to it with mute diligence. The white-wood workbox of old days had been replaced by one inlaid with precious mosaic and furnished with golden implements. The tiny fingers that once trembled over the needle—tiny still—were now swift and skilful. Yet the same intent knitting of the brow remained; the same dainty mannerisms; the quick turns—now to replace a stray tress, now to shake from the silken skirt some imaginary atom of dust, some clinging fibre of thread.

That morning I was disposed for quiet. The austere fury of the day had an awing, hushing power. The passion of January—so white, so bloodless—was not spent: the storm had raved itself hoarse, yet seemed no nearer exhaustion. Had Ginevra Fanshawe been there, she would not have suffered me to muse and listen undisturbed; the presence just gone would have been her theme, and she would have rung changes on it till my thoughts were chased like leaves. But Paulina—though she cast once or twice towards me a quiet, penetrating glance from her dark full eye, her lips half parting as if speech were coming—saw, and delicately respected, my inclination to silence.

After that day, my life asked no sharp changes. I went out often, and with Madame Beck’s full consent; she approved, as she approved all things that promised her house an added credit, of the particular circle that opened itself to me—the château, the great hotel, the Rue Crécy.

She was never fulsome. In all that was worldly she had a cool head and a close hand; her self-interest burned without smoke. She contrived to show satisfaction without stooping to flatter, and, by some neat economy of phrase and manner, made it understood that she preferred her teachers to be seen where manners were refined and tongues well taught, rather than where they might be dulled and lowered. Only once did she give me a little speech that had the air of kindness. She sat in winter sun in the garden, her cup of coffee breathing beside her—coffee, strong and bitter, with that dark perfume that warms even a stone bench; the Gazette lay open under her small, quick fingers. I came to ask leave for the evening.

“Oui, oui, ma bonne amie: je vous donne la permission de cœur et de gré. Votre travail dans ma maison a toujours été admirable, rempli de zèle et de discrétion: vous avez bien le droit de vous amuser. Sortez donc tant que vous voudrez. Quant à votre choix de connaissances, j’en suis contente; c’est sage, digne, laudable.”

She closed her lips again, and the Gazette rose between us like a screen.

It will not be thought too grave a matter—yet to me it struck like theft in a chapel—that about this time the triply enclosed packet of five letters vanished from my drawer. I felt blankly chilled when I found the place empty; then, as quickly, I forced warmth back into my heart.

“Patience,” I whispered. “Say nothing. Wait. They will return.”

They did return. They had only made a short visit to Madame’s chamber; having endured their examination, they came back—every sheet, every fold, every seal, outwardly safe—by the next day.

What did she make of my correspondence? What estimate did she form of Dr. John Bretton’s firm, fluent hand—so English in its plain strength; of his thoughts, pithy without pretence, genial without any studied gallantry; of that half-humorous current which, for me, carried a rare taste of home, like a mouthful of honest bread after foreign sweetness? And of the kind words—few, not scattered like jewels in a fable, but rarer still for their spareness—how did she read them? Oh, Madame Beck—what was this to you?

I think the five letters found some favour in her eyes. One day after she had—borrowed them (for with so smooth a little woman, one must use smooth terms)—I surprised her watching me with a steady, thoughtful look: puzzled, but not unkind. It was between lessons; the pupils were out in the court, shrilling and stamping their play into the cold air, a small human tumult that made the stillness indoors feel deeper by contrast. Madame Beck and I remained alone in the first class. When I met her gaze, her thought pressed out into speech.

“Il y a,” said she, “quelquechose de bien remarquable dans le caractère Anglais.”

“How, Madame?”

She gave a little laugh and repeated “how,” in English—mocking, but lightly.

“Je ne saurais vous dire ‘how;’ mais, enfin, les Anglais ont des idées à eux, en amitié, en amour, en tout. Mais au moins il n’est pas besoin de les surveiller,” she added; and she got up and trotted away, compact and brisk as a well-fed pony.

“Then I hope,” I murmured, “you will let my letters alone for the future.”

But even as I formed the wish, something rushed into my eyes and drowned the room—the school benches, the winter garden, the pale sun. Never again would letters like those come to me. I had seen the last. That goodly river, on whose banks I had sat so long in thirst, had bent away; it would water other fields. Right, just, natural—there was no word to be spoken against it. Yet I had loved my Rhine, my Nile; I had almost worshipped my Ganges; and I grieved that the tide should roll estranged and vanish, mirage-like, leaving my little hut and acre sand-dry.

I wept—one heavy, brief shower. Then I said to myself: “The hope I mourn made me suffer long. It died late. After a death so lingering, the end should be welcome.”

I strove to make it so. Long pain had made patience a habit. In the end I closed my dead hope’s eyes, covered its face, and laid its limbs straight with a calm that surprised me.

But the letters must be put away—out of sight. Those who have been bereaved hoard relics as if they were sharp instruments; they lock them up, because it is not bearable to be pierced at every hour by the mere glance of a familiar fold of paper.

One empty holiday afternoon—a Thursday—I went to my treasure, meaning to decide its final disposal, and perceived, this time with an impulse of real anger, that it had again been handled. The packet lay there; but the ribbon had been untied and retied, and the drawer itself wore the look of having been visited.

That was too much. That Madame Beck should know the contents of my casket was unpleasant, but, perhaps, endurable; she had discretion and sense, and could see a thing in a true light. But the fear that she had communicated what she learned; that she had amused herself with a companion over papers which, to me, were sacred—this shocked me cruelly. And I saw reason to dread it. I guessed her confidant.

Her kinsman, M. Paul Emanuel, had been with her the night before; she was in the habit of consulting him as she consulted no one else. That morning, in class, he had thrown me a look that seemed borrowed from Vashti—blue, yet lurid, like a flash in a storm cloud. I had not at the moment understood it; I read it now. He was not apt to judge what concerned me with tolerance. Severe, suspicious—so I had always found him. The thought that those letters—mere friendly letters, yet the last of their kind—had fallen once, and might fall again, into his hands, jarred my very soul.

Where, in this strange house, could secrecy be made secure? What corner existed where a key was not a mockery and a padlock a jest?

In the grenier? No. Boxes there mouldered; drawers did not lock; damp ate ink, and rats and mice made free with everything. I could see the vermin’s teeth on my dear papers, could smell the must of ruined cloth, could imagine the writing blurring into nothing under a slow wetness. The grenier would not do. But where, then?

While I pondered, I sat in the dormitory window-seat. The afternoon was frosty and fair; the winter sun, already sinking, made a pale gleam on the tops of shrubs in the allée défendue. At the end of that forbidden walk stood the old pear-tree—the nun’s pear-tree—gaunt, grey, and stripped, like some ancient dryad’s skeleton. A thought struck me—one of those odd, fantastic thoughts that visit solitary people, and seem for a moment as practical as a plan.

I put on bonnet, cloak, and furs, and went out into the city.

By instinct, in a melancholy mood, I bent my steps towards the oldest quarter, where streets lay hoar and over-shadowed, and sound itself seemed aged—the hollow knock of a distant shoe on stone, the faint clink of a shutter-chain, the murmur of voices softened by thick walls and narrow lanes. Crossing a half-deserted place, I stopped before a broker’s shop—ancient, crowded with ancient things: tarnished brass, dulled glass, rusty locks, and vessels whose former uses had died with the hands that made them.

What I wanted was a metal box that might be soldered, or a thick glass jar that could be sealed air-tight. Among heaps and corners I found the latter: a stout bottle, its glass heavy, its mouth wide enough to receive my rolled papers. I bought it.

At home I made a little roll of the letters, wrapped them in oiled silk, bound them with twine, and put them in the bottle. Then I returned to the old Jew broker and had him stopper it, seal it, make it hermetic. As he worked, he glanced at me now and then from under frost-white eyelashes—suspicious, as if he scented a hidden crime. Perhaps, to him, this business of sealing words away looked like the burial of evidence. To me it was not pleasure, but a bleak, lonely satisfaction. The mood that drove me had something of the same quality as that which had once driven me to the confessional: the need to hide, to shut, to be absolved by concealment.

I walked back quickly and reached the pensionnat at dark, in time for dinner. The smell of soup in the corridor, the clatter of plates, the small chatter of girls—those ordinary domestic sounds came to me with a strange double meaning: life, continuing, indifferent; and my own secret act, carried like a weight beneath my shawl.

At seven the moon rose. At half-past seven, while pupils and teachers studied, while Madame Beck sat with her mother and children in the salle-à-manger, while the half-boarders had gone, while Rosine had left the vestibule and all grew still, I wrapped myself in a shawl, took the sealed jar, and stole out through the first-class door into the berceau, and thence into the allée défendue.

Methusaleh, the pear-tree, stood at the far end near my seat, dim and grey above the lower shrubs. Old as he was, he was sound in timber; only at the root there was a hollow—deep, hidden by ivy and creepers. I knew it well. I meant to hide my treasure there. Yet I was not only hiding treasure; I was burying grief. The thing I had lately wept over—my hope, wrapped and laid out—must be interred.

I cleared away the ivy and found the hole—large enough. I thrust the jar deep in. Then, from the tool-shed at the bottom of the garden, where building materials lay left by masons, I fetched a slate and some mortar, set the slate over the hollow, secured it with cement, covered all with black mould, and replaced the ivy. The earth had a cold, clean smell as I pressed it down—a smell like winter itself, like the cellar where roots are stored for lean months. When it was done, I leaned against the tree and lingered, as mourners linger, beside a newly made grave.

The air was very still, yet dim with a peculiar mist that turned moonlight into luminous haze. There was something in it—electrical, perhaps—that acted strangely on me. I felt as I had once felt in England, a year before, on a night when the aurora borealis streamed and swept round heaven—when, belated in lonely fields, I had watched that mustering of an army with banners; that quivering of serried lances; that swift ascent of messengers from below the north star to the dark keystone of the sky. I was not happy—far from it—but I was strong, as if reinforced by some stern element.

If life be war, it seemed my destiny to conduct it alone. I pondered how to break up my winter-quarters—to leave an encampment where food and forage failed. Perhaps to effect the change another pitched battle must be fought with fortune. If so, I had mind for the encounter: too poor to lose, I might, by God’s will, be destined to gain. But what road was open? What plan possible?

While I paused on this, the moon, dim hitherto, seemed to shine clearer. A ray lay white before me; a shadow became distinct. I looked more narrowly; whiter and blacker it grew; it took shape with an instantaneous transformation. About three yards from me stood a tall woman, sable-robed and snowy-veiled.

Five minutes passed. I neither fled nor shrieked. She was there still. I spoke.

“Who are you? and why do you come to me?”

She stood mute. She had no face—no features; all below her brow was masked with a white cloth. But she had eyes, and they viewed me.

I was not brave—yet I was desperate, and desperation will often do the work of courage. I advanced one step and stretched out my hand; I meant to touch her. She seemed to recede. I drew nearer; her silent recession became swift. A mass of shrubs—laurel and dense yew—intervened. I passed the obstacle; I looked. There was nothing.

I waited. “If you have any errand to men,” I said, “come back and deliver it.” Nothing answered; nothing re-appeared.

This time there was no Dr. John to whom I could have recourse. There was no one to whom I dared whisper, “I have again seen the nun.”

Paulina Mary sought me often in the Rue Crécy. In the old Bretton days she had never professed fondness for me; yet my society became, to her, an unconscious necessity. If I withdrew to my room, she would soon come trotting after, open the door, peep in, and say with her little peremptory accent, “Come down. Why do you sit here by yourself? You must come into the parlour.”

In the same spirit she urged me now. “Leave the Rue Fossette,” she said, “and come and live with us. Papa would give you far more than Madame Beck gives you.”

Mr. Home himself offered me a handsome sum—thrice my present salary—if I would become companion to his daughter. I declined. I think I should have declined even had I been poorer, with fewer resources and a narrower prospect. I had not that vocation. I could teach; I could give lessons; but to be a private governess or a companion was unnatural to me. Rather than take the former post in any great house, I would deliberately have taken a housemaid’s place—bought strong gloves, swept bedrooms and staircases, cleaned stoves and locks—in peace and independence. Rather than be a companion, I would have made shirts and starved.

I was no bright lady’s shadow—not Miss de Bassompierre’s. Overcast I often was by nature; subdued I could be: but the dimness must be voluntary—such as kept me docile at my desk among my now familiar pupils in Madame Beck’s first class; or alone by my bed in her dormitory; or in the alley and seat called mine in her garden. My qualities were not convertible to ornament another’s splendour; they could not be made the foil of any gem, the appendage of any greatness in Christendom.

Madame Beck and I, without assimilating, understood each other well. I was not her companion, nor her children’s governess; she left me free. She tied me to nothing—not to herself, not even to her interests. Once, when she had been called away a fortnight by illness in her family, and returned anxious lest her establishment had suffered, she found matters proceeding much as usual. She made each teacher a present. To my bedside she came at midnight and told me she had no present for me.

“I must make fidelity advantageous to the St. Pierre,” said she; “if I attempt to make it advantageous to you, there will arise misunderstanding between us—perhaps separation. One thing, however, I can do to please you—leave you alone with your liberty: c’est-ce que je ferai.”

She kept her word. Every slight shackle she had ever laid on me she removed, quietly. Thus I took a pleasure in voluntarily respecting her rules; a gratification in devoting double time, taking double pains, with the pupils she gave to my charge.

As for Mary de Bassompierre, I visited her with pleasure, though I would not live with her. My visits soon taught me it was unlikely that even my occasional society would long remain indispensable to her. M. de Bassompierre, however, seemed blind to any such possibility—unconscious, like a child, of the fitful beginnings of what, at its end, he might not approve.

Whether he would approve, I used to speculate. He was much taken up with scientific pursuits—keen and combative when roused upon them, but trustful in ordinary affairs. He still regarded his “daughterling” as a child, spoke of what should be done when “Polly” was a woman, when she should be grown. “Polly,” standing by his chair, would sometimes smile, take his honoured head between her little hands, and kiss his iron-grey locks; at other times she would pout and toss her curls; but she never said, “Papa, I am grown.”

She wore different moods for different people. With her father she truly was child-like—affectionate, merry, playful. With me she was serious, womanly as thought and feeling could make her. With Mrs. Bretton she was docile and reliant, but not expansive. With Graham—shy; very shy. Sometimes she tried to be cold; sometimes to avoid him. His step made her start; his entrance hushed her; his questions tangled her answers. When he left, she remained vexed with herself, disconcerted.

Even her father noticed.

“My little Polly,” he said once, “you live too retired. If you grow to be a woman with these shy manners, you will hardly be fitted for society. You really make a stranger of Dr. Bretton. How is this? Don’t you remember that, as a little girl, you used to be rather partial to him?”

“Rather, papa,” she echoed, dryly yet gently.

“And you don’t like him now? What has he done?”

“Nothing. Y—e—s, I like him a little; but we are grown strange to each other.”

“Then rub it off, Polly; rub the rust and the strangeness off. Talk away when he is here. Have no fear of him.”

“He does not talk much. Is he afraid of me, do you think, papa?”

“Oh, to be sure. What man would not be afraid of such a little silent lady?”

“Then tell him some day not to mind my being silent. Say it is my way, and that I have no unfriendly intention.”

“Your way, you little chatter-box? So far from being your way, it is only your whim.”

“Well, I’ll improve, papa.”

And very pretty was the grace with which next day she tried to keep her word. I saw her make an effort to converse with Dr. John on general topics. The attention brought into his face a pleased glow; he met her with caution, answering in his softest tones, as if some gossamer happiness hung between them, and he feared to break it by breathing too boldly. In her timid, earnest advance to friendliness there was, indeed, an exquisite, fairy charm.

When the Doctor had gone, she came to her father’s chair.

“Did I keep my word, papa? Did I behave better?”

“My Polly behaved like a queen. I shall be proud of her if this continues. By-and-by we shall see her receiving my guests with quite a calm grand manner. Miss Lucy and I must polish our best airs, lest we be thrown into shade. Still, Polly—there is a little flutter, a little stammer now and then, and even—to lisp as you lisped when you were six.”

“No, papa,” she interrupted, indignant. “That can’t be true.”

“I appeal to Miss Lucy. Did she not, in answering Dr. Bretton’s question whether she had ever seen the palace of the Prince of Bois l’Etang, say ‘yeth’ she had been there ‘theveral’ times?”

“Papa, you are satirical—you are méchant! I can pronounce all the letters of the alphabet as clearly as you can. But tell me—since you are so particular that I must be civil to Dr. Bretton—do you like him yourself?”

“To be sure. For old acquaintance’ sake I like him; then he is a very good son to his mother; besides being a kind-hearted fellow and clever in his profession. Yes—the callant is well enough.”

“Callant! Ah, Scotchman! Papa, is it the Edinburgh or the Aberdeen accent you have?”

“Both, my pet, both. And doubtless the Glaswegian into the bargain. It is that enables me to speak French so well: a gude Scots tongue always succeeds well at the French.”

“The French! Scotch again—incorrigible papa. You, too, need schooling.”

“Well, Polly, you must persuade Miss Snowe to undertake both you and me—to make you steady and womanly, and me refined and classical.”

The light in which M. de Bassompierre regarded “Miss Snowe” gave me inward amusement. What contradictory attributes are ascribed to us, according to the eye that looks! Madame Beck esteemed me learned and blue; Miss Fanshawe—caustic, ironic, cynical; Mr. Home—a model teacher, sedate and discreet, perhaps too strict and scrupulous, the very pattern of governess-correctness; while another person—Professor Paul Emanuel—never lost a chance to hint that mine was rather a fiery and rash nature: adventurous, indocile, audacious. I smiled at them all. If any one knew me, it was little Paulina Mary.

Since I would not be her nominal paid companion, she persuaded me to join her in some study as a settled means of sustaining communication. She proposed German—a language that, like myself, she found hard to master. We agreed to take lessons in the Rue Crécy from the same mistress; thus we were thrown together some hours each week. M. de Bassompierre seemed pleased: it met his approbation that Madame Minerva Gravity should share a portion of her leisure with his fair dear child.

The morrow, which I had counted on for indolent ease, woke with a stir in it, as if some great kitchen had been set to work before dawn and all the city were to be fed. It was the birthday of one of Labassecour’s young princes—the eldest, they said, the Duc de Dindonneau—and, in his honour, the schools had a holiday, and the chief Athénée an observance. The students were to assemble in the hall of public examinations, present a loyal address, and hear thereafter a professor deliver an oration.

Several of M. de Bassompierre’s learned friends—those grave “savants” who could season a dish of talk with Latin as deftly as with salt—were expected. The municipality would be there, and the Burgomaster Staas, and parents, kinsfolk, patrons: all Villette, in short, that liked to hear itself rustle and murmur in a crowded room. The Count, engaged by his friends, was to go; his daughter would go; and Paulina sent a small note to Ginevra and me, bidding us come early to join her.

In the Rue Fossette dormitory, while we dressed, Miss Fanshawe stopped with a pin poised, and fell suddenly into laughter—a peal that rang clear as the clink of a spoon against a glass.

“What now?” I asked; for she had turned and was staring at me as at some odd page in a book she could not construe.

“It seems so odd,” she said, in that half-honest, half-insolent openness which made even her impertinence taste of something wholesome—coarse bread, perhaps, but bread—“that you and I should now be so much on a level, visiting in the same sphere; having the same connections.”

“Why, yes,” said I; “I had not much respect for the connections you chiefly frequented awhile ago. Mrs. Cholmondeley and Co. would never have suited me at all.”

“Who are you, Miss Snowe?” she demanded—pure curiosity, undisguised as an uncovered dish.

“You used to call yourself a nursery governess; when you first came here you really had the care of the children in this house. I have seen you carry little Georgette in your arms, like a bonne—few governesses would have condescended so far—and now Madame Beck treats you with more courtesy than she treats the Parisienne, St. Pierre; and that proud chit, my cousin, makes you her bosom friend!”

“Wonderful!” I said, amused in spite of myself. “Who am I indeed? Perhaps a personage in disguise. Pity I don’t look the character.”

“I wonder you are not more flattered by all this,” she went on. “You take it with strange composure. If you really are the nobody I once thought you, you must be a cool hand.”

“The nobody you once thought me!” I repeated; and warmth rose to my face—as if hot broth had been brought too near the mouth. But I would not be angry. A school-girl’s “nobody” and “somebody” were terms flung out like crumbs—without weight, without nourishment. I told her I had met with civility; and asked what she saw in civility to throw any one into a fever.

“One can’t help wondering at some things,” she persisted.

“Wondering at marvels of your own manufacture. Are you ready at last?”

“Yes; let me take your arm.”

“I would rather not: we will walk side by side.”

When she took my arm, she leaned on me her whole weight; and as I was neither gentleman nor lover, I did not relish being made a prop.

“There, again!” she cried. “I thought, by offering to take your arm, to intimate approbation of your dress and general appearance: I meant it as a compliment.”

“You did? You meant, in short, to express that you are not ashamed to be seen in the street with me? That if Mrs. Cholmondeley should be fondling her lapdog at some window, or Colonel de Hamal picking his teeth on a balcony, and should catch a glimpse of us, you would not quite blush for your companion?”

“Yes,” she said, with that directness which was her best point—the salt that kept her character from spoiling outright.

I let my face do the commenting. It was not reverence it expressed.

“Scornful, sneering creature!” she went on, as we crossed a great square and entered the quiet, pleasant park—the nearest way to the Rue Crécy. The park had its own domestic sound—gravel underfoot, leaves rubbing softly like skirts in a corridor, birds calling as if they too kept holiday; a mercy after the city’s iron clatter. “Nobody in this world was ever such a Turk to me as you are!”

“You bring it on yourself. Let me alone; have the sense to be quiet; I will let you alone.”

“As if one could let you alone, when you are so peculiar and so mysterious!”

“The mystery and peculiarity being entirely the conception of your own brain—maggots—neither more nor less; be so good as to keep them out of my sight.”

“But are you anybody?” she persevered, thrusting her hand under my arm in spite of me; and my elbow pressed close against my side to bar the intrusion, as one bars a pantry-door against a pilfering cat.

“Yes,” I said. “I am a rising character: once an old lady’s companion, then a nursery-governess, now a school-teacher.”

“Do—do tell me who you are? I’ll not repeat it,” she urged, squeezing the captured arm and coaxing with ludicrous tenacity. She could not conceive how a person not bolstered by birth or money, not warmed at the hearth of some famous name, could stand upright and keep an honest look. For my own part, it sufficed me to be known where it mattered. Pedigree, station, recondite acquisition—these were lodgers of the third class in my mind; I gave them a back room, and would not let them strut into the parlour even if it stood empty. The world kept a different account; and perhaps the world is right. Yet I, too, am not wholly wrong.

There are people whom a lowered place degrades morally; to whom loss of connection costs loss of self-respect. Such may be justified in valuing station as a safeguard. We learn, as we live, to judge less sharply. Where we see an accumulation of little defences—round the prude’s virtue, round the man-of-the-world’s respectability—be sure they are needed.

Thus discoursing within, and outwardly fencing off Ginevra’s importunities, we reached the Hôtel Crécy. Paulina was ready; Mrs. Bretton was with her; and, under her escort and that of M. de Bassompierre, we were soon conducted to the place of assembly and seated well, at a convenient distance from the Tribune. The Athénée youth were marshalled before us; the municipality and its burgomaster held places of honour; the young princes with their tutors sat conspicuous; and the hall was crowded with the aristocracy and the first burghers.

Of the professor who was to speak I had scarcely thought. I expected some learned formality—half lecture to schoolboys, half honeyed compliment to princes—words that would fall like tepid water and leave no flavour.

The Tribune stood empty when we entered; then, in ten minutes, it filled; and in a second, a head, chest, and arms rose above the crimson desk. That head I knew. The close blackness of the cranium; the ample pallor of brow; the blue, kindled glance—details so domesticated in memory, and linked with so many whimsical associations, that their sudden apparition tickled my fancy to laughter. I confess I laughed till warmth came; then bent my head, and made my handkerchief and a lowered veil the only confidants of my mirth.

It pleased me—more than I should have owned—to see M. Paul there, lifted into public view: fierce and frank, dark and candid, testy and fearless, as when he reigned on his estrade in class. His presence was a surprise; though I knew he filled the chair of Belles Lettres, I had not expected him. With him in that Tribune, I knew we should not be dosed with syrup and starch. Yet for what was poured—suddenly, rapidly, continuously—upon that assembly, I was not prepared.

He spoke to princes, nobles, magistrates, burghers, with the same ease and pointed, choleric earnestness with which he harangued the three divisions of the Rue Fossette. He addressed the collegians not as schoolboys, but as future citizens and embryo patriots. The Europe to come had not yet declared itself; yet in him there seemed a spirit already awake. Who would have thought the flat, fat soil of Labassecour could yield such convictions? I need not map his opinions; but I believed the little man not more earnest than right. With all his fire, he was severe and sensible; he trod Utopian theories down, rejected wild dreams with scorn; but when he looked tyranny in the face—then a light opened in his eye worth seeing; and when he spoke of injustice, his voice rang, clear as a band-trumpet at twilight from the park—sound that takes the air like a blade and makes every listening nerve remember it has blood.

The audience did not all catch the flame in its purity; but some of the Athénée youth did. As he concluded, they gave him a long, loud cheer—ringing, sustained; for, with all his fierceness, he was their favourite.

When our party left the hall, he stood near the entrance. He saw and knew me; lifted his hat; offered his hand in passing, and said, “Qu’en dites vous?” That question—so like him—betrayed even in triumph his inquisitive restlessness, his want (as I counted it) of self-control. He should not have cared then what I thought, or what any one thought; but he did care, and was too natural to hide it, too impulsive to repress. If I blamed the eagerness, I liked the naïveté. I would have praised him; I had praise enough in my heart; but there were no words on my lips. I stammered something lame, and was glad when others came up with profuse congratulations and covered my poverty with their plenty.

A gentleman introduced him to M. de Bassompierre; and the Count, much gratified, asked him to join his friends and dine at the Hôtel Crécy. He declined—shy, always, of the advances of the wealthy; there was in him a sturdy independence like corded sinew under a plain sleeve—no parade, but strength felt when you came near. He promised, however, to step in with his friend M. A——, a French Academician, later in the evening.

At dinner that day, Ginevra and Paulina each looked, in her own fashion, very beautiful. Ginevra had the advantage in material charm; Paulina shone for attractions more subtle—light and eloquence of eye, grace of mien, variety of expression. Ginevra’s deep crimson dress set off her light curls, and harmonised with her rose-like bloom; she looked like a ripe fruit laid on red cloth. Paulina’s attire, close in fashion, faultlessly neat, was clear and white; it made the eye grateful, as one is grateful for clean linen on a table where good bread will be broken. Its very simplicity drew attention to the delicate life of her complexion, the soft animation of her face, the tender depth of her eyes, the brown shadow and bounteous flow of her hair—darker than her cousin’s, as were also the brows, lashes, the full irids, the large mobile pupils. Nature had traced Miss Fanshawe’s details slightly and carelessly; in Miss de Bassompierre’s she had wrought a fine finish, as if the hand that carved had meant the thing to last.

Paulina was awed by the savants, but not made dumb. She spoke modestly, with effort, yet with true sweetness and a fine sense that pierced through the courtliest phrases. More than once her father paused in his own discourse to listen, and looked at her with proud delight. It was M. Z——, very learned and very polished, who had drawn her into talk. I was charmed with her French—faultless structure, true idiom, pure accent. Ginevra, who had lived half her life on the Continent, could do nothing like it. Words never failed Miss Fanshawe; but accuracy and purity she neither possessed nor, in any number of years, would acquire. On language M. de Bassompierre was critical; and here too he was gratified.

Another observer sat down late—detained by professional exigency—Dr. Bretton. At the moment he took his seat, he quietly scanned both ladies; and more than once his guarded survey returned. His arrival roused Ginevra. Till then she had looked listless; now she smiled and became complacent, talked much—though rarely to the purpose, or rather with a purpose somewhat mortifyingly below the standard of the occasion. Her light disconnected prattle might once have gratified Graham; perhaps it pleased him still. Perhaps it was fancy that suggested, while his eye was filled and his ear fed, his taste and intelligence were not equally regaled. Certain it is, he yielded courteously all that was required; his manner showed neither coolness nor pique. Ginevra was his neighbour, and during dinner he almost exclusively confined his notice to her. She seemed satisfied, and went to the drawing-room in good spirits.

No sooner had we reached that refuge, however, than she again fell flat. Throwing herself on a couch, she denounced both the discours and the dinner as stupid affairs, and asked Paulina how she could bear such a set of prosaic gros-bonnets as her father gathered. But the instant the gentlemen were heard to move, her railings ceased as if a lid had been clapped on a pot. She sprang up, flew to the piano, and dashed at it with spirit. Dr. Bretton entered among the first and took up his station beside her.

I thought he would not long keep that post. There was a place near the hearth to which I expected to see him drawn; he only glanced that way, while others slid in to occupy it. The grace and mind of Paulina charmed these thoughtful Frenchmen. The fineness of her beauty, the soft courtesy of her manner, her immature but inbred tact, pleased their national taste; they gathered round her—not to talk science, which would have silenced her, but to touch on letters, arts, the texture of actual life. It soon appeared she had both read and reflected. I listened. I am sure Graham, though he stood a little apart, listened too; his hearing as well as vision was quick and discriminating. I felt he gathered the conversation, and that its mode suited him exquisitely—pleased him almost to pain.

In Paulina there was more force—of feeling and character—than most people thought; than Graham himself imagined; than she would ever show to those who did not wish to see. There is no excellent beauty, no accomplished grace, no reliable refinement, without strength as complete and trustworthy. As well look for fruit and blossom on a sapless tree as for charms that endure in a feeble nature. For a little while, beauty may bloom round weakness; but it cannot bear a blast. I, who had known her as a child, guessed by what good root her graces held to reality.

While Dr. Bretton listened and waited an opening in that magic circle, his glance wandered at intervals and lit by chance on me, where I sat in a quiet nook not far from my godmother and M. de Bassompierre, who, as usual, were deep in what Mr. Home called “a two-handed crack”—what the Count would have named a tête-à-tête. Graham smiled recognition, crossed the room, asked me how I was, told me I looked pale. I had my own smile at my own thought: it was about three months since Dr. John had spoken to me—a lapse of which he was not even conscious. He sat down and became silent. His wish was rather to look than to converse. Ginevra and Paulina were opposite; he could gaze his fill; he surveyed both forms—studied both faces.

More guests had entered since dinner—ladies and gentlemen, dropping in for evening talk; and among the gentlemen, by glimpses, I had already noticed, in an inner saloon seen only in vista, a severe dark outline hovering aloof—professorial, unmistakable. M. Emanuel knew many of the gentlemen present, but I think was a stranger to most of the ladies except myself. Looking towards the hearth, he could not but see me, and naturally moved to approach; but seeing Dr. Bretton also, he changed his mind and held back. Had he stopped there, no harm. But he puckered his brows, thrust out his lip, and looked so ugly that I turned my eyes away from the spectacle.

M. Joseph Emanuel had arrived—as well as his austere brother—and at this very moment was relieving Ginevra at the piano. What a master-touch succeeded her school-girl jingle! In what grand, grateful tones the instrument acknowledged the hand of a true artist! The room’s very noise seemed to cleanse itself under those chords; their measured swell made a kind of shelter, as if a roof were being built of sound against the harsh street’s ceaseless grind.

“Lucy,” began Dr. Bretton, breaking silence with a smile as Ginevra glided before him and cast a glance in passing, “Miss Fanshawe is certainly a fine girl.”

Of course I assented.

“Is there,” he pursued, “another in the room as lovely?”

“I think there is not another as handsome.”

“I agree with you, Lucy: you and I do often agree in opinion—in taste, I think; or at least in judgment.”

“Do we?” I said, somewhat doubtfully.

“I believe if you had been a boy, Lucy, instead of a girl—my mother’s god-son instead of her god-daughter—we should have been good friends: our opinions would have melted into each other.”

He had assumed a bantering air. A light, half-caressing, half-ironic, shone aslant in his eye. Ah, Graham! I have given more than one solitary moment to calculations of your estimate of Lucy Snowe. Was it always kind—always just? Had Lucy been the same within, but with the outward advantages of wealth and station, would your manner to her, your value for her, have been what they were? Yet I would not infer serious blame. My temperament was soon depressed; it fell if a cloud crossed the sun. Perhaps before severe equity I should stand more at fault than you.

Trying to keep down the unreasonable pain that thrilled my heart—made me feel, sharply, that while Graham could give to others a grave, manly interest, he had for Lucy only light raillery—I asked calmly, “On what points are we so closely in accordance?”

“We each have an observant faculty. You, perhaps, don’t give me credit for the possession; yet I have it.”

“But you were speaking of tastes: we may see the same objects, yet value them differently.”

“Let us bring it to the test. Of course you cannot but render homage to the merits of Miss Fanshawe: now, what do you think of others in the room?—my mother, for instance; or the lions yonder, Messieurs A—— and Z——; or, let us say, that pale little lady, Miss de Bassompierre?”

“You know what I think of your mother. I have not thought of Messieurs A—— and Z——.”

“And the other?”

“I think she is, as you say, a pale little lady—pale, certainly, just now, when she is fatigued with over-excitement.”

“You don’t remember her as a child?”

“I wonder, sometimes, whether you do.”

“I had forgotten her; but it is noticeable, that circumstances—persons—even words and looks that had slipped your memory may, under certain conditions, revive.”

“That is possible enough.”

“Yet,” he continued, “the revival is imperfect—needs confirmation; partakes so much of the dim character of a dream, or of the airy one of a fancy, that the testimony of a witness becomes necessary for corroboration. Were you not a guest at Bretton ten years ago, when Mr. Home brought his little girl, whom we then called ‘little Polly,’ to stay with mamma?”

“I was there the night she came, and also the morning she went away.”

“Rather a peculiar child, was she not? I wonder how I treated her. Was I fond of children in those days? Was there anything gracious or kindly about me—great, reckless, schoolboy as I was? But you don’t recollect me, of course?”

“You have seen your own picture at La Terrasse. It is like you personally. In manner, you were almost the same yesterday as to-day.”

“But, Lucy, how is that? Such an oracle really whets my curiosity. What am I to-day? What was I the yesterday of ten years back?”

“Gracious to whatever pleased you—unkindly or cruel to nothing.”

“There you are wrong; I think I was almost a brute to you, for instance.”

“A brute! No, Graham: I should never have patiently endured brutality.”

“This, however, I do remember: quiet Lucy Snowe tasted nothing of my grace.”

“As little of your cruelty.”

“Why, had I been Nero himself, I could not have tormented a being inoffensive as a shadow.”

I smiled; but I hushed a groan. Oh—if he would but let me alone—cease these allusions. His “quiet Lucy Snowe,” his “inoffensive shadow,” I gave back—not with scorn, but with an extreme weariness. The words had the cold pressure of lead. Let him not whelm me with such weight. Happily, he soon turned to another theme.

No one in the pensionnat doubted M. Paul Emanuel’s hatred of interruption. It was not a dislike merely, but an irritability so fine-drawn that the scrape of a chair, the click of a latch, the accidental drift of a skirt across the threshold, went to him like grit between the teeth. To cross his class while he taught was accounted a trial of courage; to cross it five times in one morning, as Rosine the portress had done, was to tempt Providence with French slippers for armour.

That morning I sat in the carré, with an embroidery-frame on my lap—one of those pieces a pupil begins with ardour, then abandons when the patient sameness of stitches refuses to flatter her vanity. My fingers kept the obedient rhythm of silk through linen, and my ears, less dutiful, took their pleasure from the voice next door. It rose and fell, sharpened and broadened, like a kitchen fire that suddenly catches a new draught: quiet coals one moment, a flare and crackle the next. There was a stout wall between me and the storm; and, besides, the glass-door to the court stood ready, a clean escape like a back-stair. I confess I listened with more curiosity than alarm.

Poor Rosine had no such refuge. Four times already she had been compelled to dart in, snatch a girl from beneath his very hand—“Mademoiselle, au piano!”—and vanish again as if the floor were hot. Each venture left her paler, her mouth emptier of words, her eyes fuller of the kind of fear that lives in servants who must obey two masters. When, for the fifth time, she approached, she came as one walks to the chopping-block.

“Mon Dieu! Mon Dieu!” she whispered at my door. “Que vais-je devenir? Monsieur va me tuer, je suis sûre; car il est d’une colère!”

She flung open the class-door.

“Mademoiselle La Malle au piano!”

Before she could retreat and pull it to, his voice shot out—hard, metallic, like the clang of a pan dropped in a silent scullery.

“Dès ce moment!—la classe est défendue. La première qui ouvrira cette porte, ou passera par cette division, sera pendue—fut-ce Madame Beck elle-même!”

The decree hung in the corridor like smoke after a burnt sauce: acrid, lingering, and impossible to pretend away. Ten minutes had scarcely passed when Rosine’s slippers shuffled again—only now the sound of them had the desperate soft haste of someone carrying boiling water.

“Mademoiselle,” she said, coming to my side as if my presence could shield her, “I would not for a five-franc piece go into that classe again just now. Monsieur’s lunettes are really terrible; and here is a commissionaire come with a message from the Athénée. I have told Madame Beck I dare not deliver it, and she says I am to charge you with it.”

“Me?” I returned, half amused, half affronted. “That is not in my duty. Come, Rosine—carry your own burden. Be brave—charge once more!”

“I, Mademoiselle? impossible! Five times I have crossed him this day. Madame must really hire a gendarme for this service. Ouf! Je n’en puis plus!”

“Bah! You are only a coward. What is the message?”

“Precisely of the kind with which Monsieur least likes to be pestered: an urgent summons to go directly to the Athénée—there is an official visitor—inspector—I know not what—arrived, and Monsieur must meet him. You know how he hates a must.”

Yes; I knew. Whatever tasted of constraint—an order, an obligation, a summons—was to him what a harsh, factory-throb is to a listening ear: a mechanical, imposed noise, insulting to his own chosen cadence. He would resist, not always from malice, but from that proud need to keep his soul’s footing on ground he had named his own.

Still, he must go. His duty, his interest, and Madame Beck’s quiet calculation all pointed one way. I took the message from Rosine, not without fear; yet in that fear there was also a stirring curiosity—like leaning over a pot to see whether the first bubbles have begun.

I opened the door, entered, and closed it behind me with what silence my unsteady hand could manage; for in that room, the manner of a fault could be worse than the fault itself. To rattle the latch, to hesitate, to leave a gap of light—those were provocations.

There he sat, bent above his desk upon the estrade, teaching arithmetic. Figures—those dry grains—never agreed with him; they parched his temper as stale bread parches the throat. The pupils’ heads were bent like stems under hail. He did not even raise his face at my entrance; to look up and acknowledge a breach of his law was an effort his anger refused him. It served me well. I walked the length of the room; and I have always found it easier to meet wrath close, where one can see its edge, than to endure it at a distance, where it looms and gathers.

I stopped at the estrade, directly before him. He continued his lesson as if I were air.

As I was not quite tall enough to see his countenance over the desk, I leaned and peeped round. At first I meant only to observe him: he had struck me, at my entrance, with a certain dark, fierce picturesqueness—like a black tiger in ill-humour. Twice I looked and withdrew, unchallenged. The third time, the instant my eye cleared the desk’s shadow, it was caught—pinned through the very pupil—by the “lunettes.” Rosine had spoken truth: those glasses had a blank, unmoving terror in them, more appalling than the living play of his naked eyes.

But proximity was my ally. The short-sighted “lunettes” could not well inspect a culprit under his nose. He took them off, and thus, oddly, we came nearer equality.

I was glad I did not truly fear him. When, in grim literal jest, he demanded cord and gibbet to hang the offender he had threatened, I could answer with my own weapon: I slipped from my work a needleful of thread and handed it round the corner of the desk with such civil readiness that it disarmed something in his surplus ire. I did not do it for the room to see; I merely attached the noosed thread to the barred back of his chair, as one might silently set in place a jesting garnish on a stern dish.

“Que me voulez-vous?” he said at last, in a growl that seemed to grind within his throat, his teeth clenched as though he had sworn no smile should escape.

“Monsieur,” I replied, “je veux l’impossible, des choses inouïes;” and then, without any tender preparation, I gave him the message, brisk and urgent, heightening its necessity—as one shakes a reluctant man by the shoulders with words.

He would not hear of it. He would not go; he would not leave his class; let all the officials of Villette send. He would not stir at the bidding of king, cabinet, and chambers together.

I knew, however, that his revolt was only his first seasoning—bitter, sharp, inevitable. In the end he must comply. So I stood silent, as though he had not spoken, and waited.

He demanded, “What more do you want?”

“Only Monsieur’s answer, to deliver to the commissionaire.”

He waved an impatient negative.

Then I made a bold movement. On the window-sill lay his bonnet-grec, in grim repose; I stretched my hand towards it. He followed my hand with a look that held both amazement and disdain.

“Ah!” he muttered, “if it came to that—if Miss Lucy meddled with my bonnet-grec—she might just put it on herself, turn garçon for the occasion, and benevolently go to the Athénée in my stead.”

With much respect, I laid the bonnet on the desk. Its tassel seemed to nod at me—solemn, admonitory, like a blackcap worn by some ancient judge.

“I’ll write a note of apology—that will do!” he said, still bent on evasion.

Knowing it would not do, I pushed the bonnet gently towards his hand. It slid down the smooth varnish of the desk—pushing before it the steel-framed “lunettes”—and, terrible to confess, those glasses went over the edge and fell upon the estrade.

Often had I seen them fall and survive. This time, by some spiteful turn of chance—my chance—it was different. When I picked them up, each clear pebble of glass was cracked into shivered stars.

Dismay seized me; but beneath dismay there was regret more real. I knew the value of those “lunettes.” His sight was peculiar; he called them his treasures. Holding them, ruined, I trembled, as if I had spilt something precious and irrecoverable—not wine, but the last of a rare oil one has guarded for years.

For some seconds I could not look at him. He spoke first.

“Là!” said he. “Me voilà veuf de mes lunettes! Now Mademoiselle Lucy will confess the cord and gallows are earned; she trembles in anticipation of her doom. Ah, traitress! traitress! You are resolved to have me quite blind and helpless in your hands!”

I lifted my eyes. Instead of fury, his face overflowed with a smile—coloured with a warm bloom I had once seen at the Hôtel Crécy. He was not angry—not even truly grieved. Under the real injury he showed clemency; under the real provocation, patience. And this mishap, which I had thought would ruin my errand, became my best assistance. While no harm had been done, he had been unmanageable; as soon as I stood before him a conscious offender, he grew graciously pliant, as if contrition were a seasoning he could not resist.

Still railing gently—“une forte femme… une Anglaise terrible… une petite casse-tout”—he declared he dared not disobey one who could work such havoc; it was like the Grand Emperor smashing the vase to inspire dismay. So he crowned himself with his bonnet-grec, took the broken “lunettes” from my hand with a clasp that pardoned and encouraged, bowed, and went off to the Athénée in excellent humour.

After such amiability, it is hard to hear that before night we quarrelled again; yet it happened, and I could not help it.

It was his custom sometimes—an admirable custom, too—to arrive of an evening without warning, burst upon the silent hour of study, and set up a sudden despotism over our tranquillity. Books were put away; work-bags brought out. He drew forth a thick volume or a handful of pamphlets, and, instead of the sleepy “lecture pieuse” drawled by a pupil, he poured into our conventual stillness some tragedy read grandly, acted with a fiery voice—some drama used as a vessel for his native passion, filled to the brim like a cup of potent brew. Or he would flash us a glimpse of current literature: a tale that had charmed Paris, a feuilleton that had waked laughter in salons—always pruning with a severe hand whatever could be deemed unfit for “jeunes filles.” I had noticed he could improvise whole paragraphs where he cut—clean, vigorous, irreproachable—better often than what he removed. It was a kind of translation, not from language to language, but from one moral lexicon to another; and he did it as a man does kneading—pressing, folding, shaping—until the coarse grain becomes fit for the oven.

That evening we sat quiet as nuns in retreat: pupils bent over tasks, teachers occupied with work. Mine was a small piece of fancy-work which, unusually, interested me; it was not mere time-killing. I meant it as a gift; the day of giving was near; my fingers hastened, drawing silk through with a little eager friction, as if the stitch itself could shorten the waiting.

We heard the sharp bell-peal we all knew; then the rapid step each ear could name. “Voilà Monsieur!” passed round in one breath; and the double door did not open—it split, as it always did for him, for “open” is too slow a word for his motion. He stood among us.

Two long study tables stretched under hanging lamps. Teachers sat at the centres; girls lined both sides—eldest nearest the lamp’s warmth, idlers and little ones pushed towards the dimmer ends. M. Paul’s habit was to hand a chair to some teacher—usually Zélie St. Pierre, the senior mistress—take her place, and thus secure the full lamp-light, which his near-sightedness demanded.

As usual, Zélie rose quickly, smiling with all her teeth—a smile that ran from ear to ear like a thin cut, lighting neither cheek nor eye. Either he did not see her, or chose not to. His new “lunettes” could excuse any oversight, and he used them. He passed Zélie, came straight to my side, and before I could rise, whispered, “Ne bougez pas,” and established himself between me and Miss Fanshawe—Ginevra—who would always contrive to be my neighbour, elbowing my ribs with her small, restless vanity, keeping herself “warm,” as she said, on winter evenings, till I sometimes protected myself with an artful pin at my girdle.

“Ne bougez pas” was easily commanded; it was less easily obeyed. I must make him room; I must ask the pupils to draw back that I might move. I swept aside my working things to clear space for his book, and edged away a little—not more than a yard, the allowance any reasonable man might accept. But M. Paul was never reasonable. Flint and tinder: the least friction sparked him.

“Vous ne voulez pas de moi pour voisin,” he growled. “Vous vous donnez des airs de caste; vous me traitez en paria.” He scowled. “Soit! je vais arranger la chose!”

“Levez-vous toutes, Mesdemoiselles!” he cried.

The girls rose. He drove them all to the other table, like a shepherd with a sharp stick. Then he placed me at one extremity of the long bench; brought me my work-basket, silk, scissors—every implement set in order; and fixed himself at the other end, a whole stretch away.

It was absurd; yet no one dared laugh. A giggle would have been a dropped plate in a tense kitchen—heard, noted, punished. As for me, I took it coolly. Isolated, cut off, I sat and minded my work, quiet, not unhappy. There was, indeed, a snug composure in it—new to me—as though his restless nature drew all fever out of the room like a magnet, leaving me only a calm that let the needle travel.

“Est-ce assez de distance?” he demanded.

“Monsieur en est l’arbitre,” I answered.

“Vous savez bien que non. C’est vous qui avez créé ce vide immense: moi je n’y ai pas mis la main.”

And with that he began to read.

For his misfortune, he had chosen a French translation of what he called “un drame de Williams Shackspire”—“le faux dieu,” he added, “de ces sots païens, les Anglais.” Had his temper been sweeter, perhaps his verdict would have been different; but to-night he spoke as a man speaks of a dish served ill: not content to say it is bad, he must insult the cook and the country besides.

The translation was indeed poor—thin where it should have been rich, pale where it should have bled. It had the wrong salt. It tasted to my mind like broth made from bones long boiled and strained twice: the nourishment gone, only a suggestion remaining. I said nothing; but the muscles about the eyes and mouth will sometimes betray what the tongue is forbidden to utter. His “lunettes” gleaned every stray look; I do not think they missed one. Soon he discarded them that his own eyes might blaze more freely; and though he sat at the cold north end to which he had exiled himself, he grew hotter there than reason would have allowed beneath the lamp’s very tropic.

When he had finished, it was doubtful whether he would depart in silence or let his anger escape. Silence was not his habit; yet what had I done, openly, to merit reproof? I had not spoken.

Supper was brought: bread, and milk diluted with tepid water—poor fare, softened and thinned, like many things in that house. In consideration of his presence, the rolls and glasses stood untouched until he spoke.

“Take your supper, ladies,” he said, busying himself with marginal notes to “Williams Shackspire.” They ate. I took my roll and glass too; but my work had seized me more strongly than hunger. I stayed in my seat of punishment and stitched while I chewed, sipping the warm, weakened milk, my whole manner easy—almost serenely domestic. The room had its evening sounds: the low talk at table, the clink of glass, the soft tearing of bread, the distant hush of the house settling. Beneath those human noises lay, as always, the heavier city—wheels, a far shout, the iron pulse of Villette. I felt, as I often did, that peace depends on what the ear must bear; and here, strangely, M. Paul’s presence—so rasping, so quick to spark—seemed to swallow up the jarring influences and leave me a pocket of quiet in which my needle could sing.

He rose and turned to the door. I thought he would go without another word.

He returned—perhaps only for the pencil-case left on the table. He took it, clicked the pencil in and out, broke its point against the wood, re-cut it, pocketed it—and then walked straight to me.

At the other table, teachers and girls talked freely; at meals they always did, their voices habitually fast and loud. The sound made a kind of crowded canopy under the lamp—busy, human, heedless. He came and stood behind me, and asked what I was making.

“A watchguard,” I said.

“For whom?”

“For a gentleman—one of my friends.”

He stooped; and then, close to my ear, he spoke in that low, cutting way which, in him, seemed less like speech than like a sharp whisper of steel.

Of all the women he knew, I was the one who could make herself the most consummately unpleasant. With me it was least possible to live on friendly terms. I had a “caractère intraitable,” perverse to a miracle. Whoever approached me with pacific intentions—crac!—I turned concord to discord, good-will to enmity. He, M. Paul, wished me well enough. He had never harmed me knowingly. He might claim to be at least a neutral acquaintance—guiltless of hostile sentiment; and yet—how I behaved to him! What pungent vivacities, what an impetus of mutiny, what a “fougue” of injustice!

At that I could not help widening my eyes a little, slipping in a small protest, half incredulous.

“Vivacities? Impetus? Fougue? I didn’t know—”

“Chut! à l’instant! There! there I went—vive comme la poudre!” He was sorry—very sorry. For my sake he grieved over this peculiarity. This “emportement,” this “chaleur”—generous, perhaps, but excessive—would yet do me mischief. It was a pity: I was not, he believed, wholly without good qualities. If only I would hear reason; be more sedate, more sober; less “en l’air,” less “coquette,” less taken by show; less prone to value outside excellence—to make much of attentions from people remarkable chiefly for so many feet of stature, “des couleurs de poupée,” “un nez plus ou moins bien fait,” and an enormous stock of fatuity—then I might yet prove useful, perhaps exemplary. But as it was—and here his voice caught, as though some feeling had stuck in his throat like a crumb that will not go down.

I might have looked up, might have held out my hand, might have offered one soft word to settle him; but I feared if I moved I should either laugh or cry, so strangely did the touching mingle with the absurd.

I thought him nearly done. He was not. He sat down, as if to speak more at ease—like a man settling to carve and serve, unwilling to leave his dish unfinished.

While he was on these painful topics, he said, he would dare my anger for my good, and refer to a change he had noticed in my dress. When he first knew me—when he used only to catch a passing glimpse from time to time—I satisfied him. The gravity, the austere simplicity, inspired the highest hopes. What fatal influence had lately impelled me to put flowers under my bonnet-brim, to wear “des cols brodés,” and even, on one occasion, to appear in a scarlet gown—he could conjecture, but would not yet openly declare.

This time I interrupted with a sharp indignation, and a sort of honest horror.

“Scarlet, Monsieur Paul? It was not scarlet. It was pink—and pale pink too—and further subdued by black lace.”

“Pink or scarlet, yellow or crimson, pea-green or sky-blue—it is all one. These are flaunting, giddy colours. And the lace—_that_ is but a colifichet de plus.” He sighed over my degeneracy. He could not be as particular as he wished; not possessing the exact names of these “babioles,” he might fall into small verbal errors which would give my sarcasm an opening and excite my unhappily sudden disposition. He would merely say, in general terms—and he knew these terms were correct—that my costume had lately assumed “des façons mondaines,” and it wounded him to see it.

What “façons mondaines” he discovered in my winter merino and plain white collar I could not tell; and when I asked, he said it was all too studied for effect—and besides, had I not a bow of ribbon at my neck?

“And if you condemn a bow of ribbon for a lady, Monsieur,” I said, holding up the little chainlet of silk and gold bright in the lamplight as if it were a thread of sunshine spun into cord, “you would necessarily disapprove of a thing like this for a gentleman?”

His only answer was a groan—whether for my levity, or for some deeper vexation, I could not then decide.

He sat silent a few minutes, watching the progress of the chain as I worked more assiduously than ever—stitch after stitch drawn tight, each loop made fast as memory is made fast when one binds it to an object meant to be worn close and daily. Then he asked, more low than before, whether what he had said would have the effect of making me entirely detest him.

I rose next morning while the dormitory still breathed its last, sour-sweet draught of night—lamp-oil, washed boards, the faint iron of cold. An hour before dawn, I finished my vigil kneeling on the floor by the central stand, working not by day’s honest breadth but by that dying wick’s reluctant glow, as if I were salting meat in a cellar and must not be seen.

All my poor wealth of colour—beads, silk, the very last lengths of thread—went under my fingers before the chain came to the weight and richness I had determined on. I made it double on purpose: I knew the taste I meant to meet; for certain spirits, contrariness is the surest seasoning, and nothing looks so persuasive as what has been laboured upon till it shines. When the last knot was drawn tight, when the braid lay pliant and firm as a well-plaited loaf, I wanted only a clasp. I had one, but it was the fastening of my sole necklace; I unhooked it as one takes the last pinch of spice from a jar kept for Sunday, and fixed it where it would do the most good. Then I coiled the guard compact, like a ribbon of burnished fruit-skin, and put it in a small box chosen for its insolent brilliance—tropic shell, nacarat-hued, warm as coral under milk, set round with a little coronet of blue stones that caught what light there was and answered it.

Inside the lid, with the point of my scissors, I scratched certain initials, neat and secret, as a cook will mark her own tin by a nick in the rim. I shut it, and the click sounded louder than it should have done in the sleeping house.

There are days in Madame Beck’s school that turn, with regularity, like the seasons of a kitchen: markets, masses, examinations, fêtes. The reader will remember Madame Beck’s anniversary—how, at each return, the establishment subscribed a handsome offering. This honour, full and formal, belonged to her alone; but in a modified form, by the natural drift of affection rather than any plotted ceremony, it had come to attach itself also to her kinsman and counsellor, M. Emanuel. He had made it plain that he would accept neither plate nor jewellery; the money-value—so much gold, so many stones—left him cold. Yet a slight tribute, plainly offered, touched him: a flower, a sketch, a thing made with one’s own patience and meaning, would please him more than a ring presented with bows and speeches. Such was he: not wise as his own generation counts wisdom, but capable of a childlike susceptibility to whatever dawns pure and lifts.

His fête fell on the first of March, a Thursday. The day came clear and sunny, with that thin, clean warmth that seems to rinse the very air. It was also the morning when we attended mass, and it carried besides the privilege of a half-holiday—an afternoon for walking, shopping, visits. The whole house, as if tuned by the same hand, woke to smartness. Collars—so often grey with work—came out white; the dingy classe-dress gave way to lighter stuffs. Mademoiselle Zélie St. Pierre, on this Thursday, went further: she appeared in a robe de soie—an article in economical Labassecour accounted as rash as buying strawberries in Lent. Nay, she sent for a coiffeur to dress her hair; and even dull noses could tell she had bedewed her handkerchief, her hands, with a new perfume—sharp-sweet, too assertive, like candied violets laid on hot metal.

Poor Zélie! It was her habit to say, at this time, that she was weary to death of seclusion and labour; that she longed for leisure, for means, for some one to work for her—a husband who would pay her debts (she was heavily entangled), supply her wardrobe, and leave her free, as she phrased it, to goûter un peu les plaisirs. Rumour had long declared her eye set on M. Emanuel. His eye was certainly often set on her. I had seen him sit and watch her, perseveringly, minute after minute, while the class composed in silence and he, on his estrade, seemed unoccupied. Under that basilisk attention she would writhe—half flattered, half at a loss; and he would follow every shade of her sensation with a keen, almost cruel acuteness. For in some cases he had an instinct that pierced like a skewer through a joint, finding the bone, the hidden gristle, the place where the meat is not sound. Under florid veilings he could discern the bare, barren places of spirit; the twisted tendency; the stain self-brought and carefully covered. Any acknowledged calamity he could pity and forgive; but where he met denial, where he found deceitful concealment, he could be merciless. He would snatch away the screen, hurry a shrinking creature up to the very peak of exposure, and show her there—false, naked, poor living lie—spawn of that horrible Truth which cannot be looked on unveiled. He believed it justice. I have doubted whether man has the right to do such justice upon man; more than once I have given tears to his victims and not spared keen reproach to himself. He deserved it; yet it was hard to shake his conviction that the work was righteous.

After breakfast and mass, the bell rang; the rooms filled; and classe offered, for once, a pretty spectacle. Teachers and pupils sat neat, orderly, expectant; in each hand, a bouquet of felicitation—spring flowers, fresh and damp at the stems, their scent rising and mingling: the green sting of cut leaves, the honey of forced blossoms, the faint earthy breath that clings to roots even when roots are gone. The air felt seasoned with sweetness, as if someone had opened a cupboard where sugared things are kept.

In my hands there was nothing.

I have always liked flowers best when they grow. Once gathered, they cease to please me; they look to me like lives torn up and sentenced—rootless, perishing. Their likeness to living makes me sad. I never offer flowers to those I love; I never wish to receive them from hands dear to me. Zélie marked my empty hands; she could not believe I had been so remiss. Her eye ran over and round me with greedy curiosity: surely I must have some solitary symbolic blossom tucked away—some shy knot of violets to win praise for taste, commendation for ingenuity. But the unimaginative Anglaise proved barer than the Parisienne’s fears: I sat literally unprovided, as leafless as winter timber. When she ascertained it, Zélie smiled with satisfaction.

“How wisely you have acted to keep your money, Miss Lucie,” she said. “Silly I have gone and thrown away two francs on a bouquet of hot-house flowers!”

And she displayed her splendid nosegay with pride.
