Hush—there was a step in the corridor: the step. Prompt as always, but to our fancy this morning it had in it something more than mere vehemence of nerve; we flattered ourselves it carried a friendly promise. It did.

He entered with a mood that made him, in that already well-lit room, like an added sunbeam. The morning light played amongst our plants and laughed on the walls; it seemed to take an extra lustre from his benignant salute. Like one who understands the ceremony of a table and dresses for it—not to boast, but to honour—he had put aside the soot-dark paletôt whose vague folds gave him a conspirator’s air. His figure (such as it was) was set off by a civilized coat and a silken vest not unpretty to behold. The defiant bonnet-grec had vanished; bare-headed he came in, carrying a Christian hat in his gloved hand. The little man looked well—very well. His blue eye held a clearness of amity; his dark complexion carried a glow of good feeling that passed in place of beauty. One ceased to note that his nose, though far from small, had no fine shape; that his cheek was thin, brow square, mouth no rose-bud. He was accepted as he stood; his presence seemed the reverse of insignificant.

He went to his desk, set down hat and gloves.

“Bon jour, mes amies,” said he—and though it was not jovial, nor unctuous, it had that tone he reserved for words his heart consented to utter. That heart did speak sometimes. Irritable, it was not ossified; in its core lay a tenderness beyond a man’s tenderness—something that humbled him to little children and bound him, in spite of himself, to girls and women; he could not deny the affinity, nor quite deny that, on the whole, he was better with them than with his own sex.

“We all wish Monsieur a good day,” said Mademoiselle Zélie, who constituted herself spokeswoman of the assembly, “and present to him our congratulations on the anniversary of his fête.”

Advancing with only so many twists of affectation as were, to her, the indispensable machinery of motion, she laid her costly bouquet before him. He bowed over it.

Then followed the long train of offerings. Pupils swept past with that gliding step foreigners practise, leaving their tributes as they went; each adjusted her separate gift with dexterity, so that when the last nosegay lay on the desk it formed the apex of a blooming pyramid—spreading, towering, exuberant enough, at last, to eclipse the hero behind it. Ceremony ended, seats were resumed; we sat in dead silence, expectant of a speech.

Five minutes might have passed, and the hush remained unbroken; ten—and still no sound.

Many must have begun to wonder what Monsieur waited for; and well they might. Voiceless and viewless, stirless and wordless, he kept his station behind the piled flowers.

At last a voice issued—deeper than his usual, as if it spoke from a hollow place.

“Est-ce là tout?”

Zélie looked round.

“You have all presented your bouquets?” she inquired of the pupils.

Yes; all had given—from eldest to youngest, from tallest to smallest. The senior mistress signified as much.

“Est-ce là tout?” came again; and the intonation, deep before, had now sunk lower.

“Monsieur,” said Mademoiselle St. Pierre, rising, and speaking with her own sweet smile, “I have the honour to tell you that, with a single exception, every person in classe has offered her bouquet. For Meess Lucie, Monsieur will kindly make allowance; as a foreigner she probably did not know our customs, or did not appreciate their significance. Meess Lucie has regarded this ceremony as too frivolous to be honoured by her observance.”

“Famous!” I muttered between my teeth. “You are no bad speaker, Zélie, when you begin.”

The answer vouchsafed from the estrade was only a hand, emerging from behind the pyramid, gesturing as if to deprecate words, to enjoin silence.

A moment after, the form followed. Monsieur emerged from his eclipse; stepping to the front of his estrade, he fixed his gaze straight upon the vast mappe-monde opposite, as if that painted globe could give him patience; and demanded a third time, now in tones genuinely tragic:

“Est-ce là tout?”

Even then I might have set all right. In my hand, tight-held, I had the ruddy little shell-box with its blue stones—my secret tribute, my labour. I had fully purposed to step forward and slip it into his hand. But first the comic side of his behaviour tempted me to delay; then Mademoiselle St. Pierre’s interference provoked contumacy. The reader has had no reason to ascribe to me pretensions to perfection; he will not be surprised to learn I felt too perverse to defend myself from any imputation the Parisienne chose to insinuate. Besides, M. Paul took my defection so seriously—made tragedy of it—that he deserved, I thought, to be vexed. I kept my box and my countenance, and sat as insensible as a stone.

“It is well!” dropped at length from his lips; and as he said it, the shadow of some paroxysm—wrath, scorn, resolve—passed over his brow, rippled his mouth, lined his cheeks. He swallowed down further comment and launched into his customary discours.

I do not remember the discours; I did not listen. The very act of gulping down his vexation, of dismissing it abruptly, gave me a sensation that half counteracted the ridiculous effect of the reiterated “Est-ce là tout?”

Toward the close, my attention was caught again, and in a manner which diverted it at first, then pricked it sorely. By some little accident—my thimble fell; I stooped to regain it, and struck the crown of my head against the sharp corner of my desk—petty mishaps that made a small bustle. M. Paul, already strained, became irritated; flinging away the dignity and self-control he never long consented to wear, he broke forth into the strain that gave him ease.

Somehow, in the course of his speech, he had crossed the Channel and landed on British ground. There I found him when I began to listen.

With a quick cynical glance round the room—a glance meant to scathe as it crossed me—he fell with fury upon les Anglaises. Never had I heard English women handled as he handled them that morning. He spared neither mind, morals, manners, nor appearance. He railed at their tall stature, long necks, thin arms, slovenly dress; at their pedantic education; at their impious scepticism; at their insufferable pride; at their pretentious virtue. Over that last he ground his teeth and looked as if, had he dared, he would have said things worse. He was spiteful, acrid, savage—and, as a natural consequence, detestably ugly.

“Little wicked venomous man!” I thought. “Am I going to harass myself with fears of displeasing you, of hurting your feelings? No. You shall be indifferent to me, as the shabbiest bouquet in your pyramid.”

I grieve to say I could not carry out the resolution. For a time his abuse found me stolid; I bore it a quarter of an hour. But he was determined to sting. He fastened not only upon women, but upon our greatest names and best men—sullying the shield of Britannia, dabbling the union jack in mud. With vicious relish he brought up the spiciest continental historical falsehoods—than which nothing can be more offensive. Zélie and the whole class became one grin of vindictive delight; it is curious how these clowns of Labassecour secretly hate England. At last I struck a sharp stroke on my desk, opened my lips, and let loose this cry:

“Vive l’Angleterre, l’Histoire et les Héros! A bas la France, la Fiction et les Faquins!”

The class was struck of a heap; they thought me mad. The Professor put up his handkerchief and smiled fiendishly into its folds. Little monster of malice! He believed he had the victory because he had made me angry. In a second he grew good-humoured. With great blandness he resumed the subject of his flowers; talked poetically and symbolically of sweetness, perfume, purity; made Frenchified comparisons between the jeunes filles and the blossoms before him; paid Mademoiselle St. Pierre a full-blown compliment on the superiority of her bouquet; and ended by announcing that on the first really fine, mild, balmy spring morning he intended to take the whole class out to breakfast in the country—such of the class, he added with emphasis, as he could count amongst the number of his friends.

“Donc je n’y serai pas,” I declared, involuntarily.

“Soit!” was his response; and gathering his flowers in his arms, he flashed out of classe. I, consigning my work, scissors, thimble, and the neglected little box to my desk, swept upstairs. I do not know whether he felt hot and angry; I confess that I did.

Yet my anger was strangely evanescent. I had not sat an hour on the edge of my bed—picturing and re-picturing his look, manner, words—before I smiled at the whole scene. A little pang of regret came with the smile: the box had not been offered. I had meant to gratify him. Fate would not have it so.

In the afternoon, remembering that desks in classe were not inviolate repositories, and thinking it well to secure the box—because of the initials I had cut inside the lid, P. C. D. E., for Paul Carl (or Carlos) David Emanuel; these foreigners must always have a string of baptismals—I went down to the schoolroom.

It slept in holiday repose. The day-pupils were gone home; the boarders were out walking; the teachers (save the surveillante of the week) were in town, visiting or shopping. The suite of divisions was vacant; so was the grande salle, with its huge solemn globe hanging in the midst, its many-branched chandeliers, and its horizontal grand piano closed and silent, as if enjoying its mid-week Sabbath. In that stillness the building’s sounds stood out—boards settling, a distant door’s sigh, the faint echo of street-noise filtered through thick walls; and I could not help thinking how different a mind feels when spared the continual clatter of voices and footsteps. A school is a kind of managed din, and even its order has its own oppression.

I wondered to find the first classe door ajar; that room was usually locked when empty and accessible only to Madame Beck and myself, who held a duplicate key. I wondered more, as I approached, to hear a vague movement as of life—a step, a chair stirred, the sound of a desk opening.

“It is only Madame Beck doing inspection duty,” I decided.

The half-open door permitted certainty. I looked.

Not Madame Beck’s inspecting garb—not the shawl and clean cap—but a man’s coat and close-shorn dark head. He sat in my chair; his olive hand held my desk open; his nose was lost among my papers. His back was toward me; there was no room for doubt. The attire of ceremony had been discarded; the cherished ink-stained paletôt was resumed; the perverse bonnet-grec lay on the floor as if dropped from a hand too busy to care.

I knew—had long known—that M. Emanuel’s hand was on intimate terms with my desk. It raised and lowered the lid, ransacked and arranged the contents almost as familiarly as my own. He did not wish the fact to be dubious: he left signs of each visit palpable and unmistakable. Yet I had never caught him in the act. Watch as I would, I could not detect the hours and moments of his coming. I saw only the brownie’s work: exercises left overnight full of faults and found next morning carefully corrected; loans that came capriciously, full welcome and refreshing. Between a sallow dictionary and worn-out grammar would spring, as if grown in the dark, a fresh interesting new work, or a classic mellow and sweet in its ripe age. Out of my work-basket would peep a romance, under it would lurk a pamphlet or magazine from which last evening’s reading had been taken.

Impossible to doubt the source; and one trait, condemning and traitorous, settled the question beyond dispute: they smelt of cigars. This, at first, had shocked me. I used to open the window with bustle and air my desk; with fastidious finger and thumb I would hold the peccant brochures out to the breeze as if smoke were moral infection. I was cured of that formality suddenly. Monsieur caught me at it one day, understood the inference, and in an instant relieved my hand of its burden; another moment, and he would have thrust the same into the glowing stove. It happened to be a book I was bent on reading; for once I proved more decided and quicker than he. I recaptured the spoil; and, having saved that volume, I never hazarded a second.

With all this, I had never been able to arrest, in his visits, the freakish, friendly, cigar-loving phantom.

But now at last I had him. There he was—the very brownie; and there, curling from his lips, was the pale blue breath of his Indian darling. He was smoking into my desk; no wonder the books betrayed him. Provoked at that particular, yet pleased to surprise him—pleased, with the mixed feeling of a housewife who discovers at last her strange elfin ally busy in the dairy at the untimely churn—I stole forward softly, stood behind him, and bent with precaution over his shoulder.

My heart smote me. After this morning’s hostility, after my seeming remissness, after the puncture to his feelings and the ruffling of his temper—he, willing to forget and forgive, had brought me a couple of handsome volumes, whose titles and authorship promised interest. As he sat bending above the desk, he stirred its contents with a gentle careful hand; disarranging, but not harming. My morning’s anger melted as easily as salt in warm broth. Whatever else I might say, I did not dislike Professor Emanuel.

I think he heard me breathe. He turned suddenly. His temperament was nervous, yet he never started, and seldom changed colour; something hardy in him caught and held itself.

“I thought you were gone into town with the other teachers,” said he, as if gripping his self-possession lest it slip. “It is as well you are not. Do you think I care for being caught? Not I. I often visit your desk.”

“Monsieur, I know it.”

“You find a brochure or tome now and then; but you don’t read them, because they have passed under this?”—and he touched his cigar.

“They have, and are no better for the process; but I read them.”

“Without pleasure?”

“Monsieur must not be contradicted.”

“Do you like them, or any of them—are they acceptable?”

“Monsieur has seen me reading them a hundred times, and knows I have not so many recreations as to undervalue those he provides.”

He looked at me—quick, searching, as if he listened not only for words but for what they could not translate.

“I mean well,” he said. “And if you see that I mean well, and derive some little amusement from my efforts, why can we not be friends?”

“A fatalist would say—because we cannot.”

“This morning,” he went on, “I awoke in a bright mood, and came into classe happy; you spoiled my day.”

“No, Monsieur—only an hour or two of it, and that unintentionally.”

“Unintentionally! No. It was my fête-day; everybody wished me happiness but you. The little children of the third division gave each her knot of violets, lisped each her congratulation: you—nothing. Not a bud, leaf, whisper—not a glance. Was this unintentional?”

“I meant no harm.”

“Then you really did not know our custom? You were unprepared? You would willingly have laid out a few centimes on a flower to give me pleasure, had you been aware that it was expected? Say so, and all is forgotten, and the pain soothed.”

“I did know that it was expected: I was prepared; yet I laid out no centimes on flowers.”

You are not to conclude—because a man has once been gentle—that gentleness will henceforth sit upon him like a settled habit, and make his daily temper smooth as kneaded dough. M. Paul’s nature had its own sharp leaven. He was little, yet full of combustibles; often over-wrought, and then irritability rose in him like heat in an over-stoked oven. Worse than mere quickness, there ran in his veins a tincture of jealousy—no soft jealousy of the heart only, but that narrower, head-bound mistrust which watches, calculates, and, when it cannot understand, suspects.

Sometimes, as I sat at my desk under his eye, and saw him knit his brow over an exercise that did not contain half the faults he desired, I thought of Napoleon. The comparison came, not from grandeur, but from a certain shameless impatience of magnanimity. M. Paul could quarrel with a whole city of learned women and never feel his dignity the poorer for it. A knot of blunders pleased him as a cluster of nuts pleases a squirrel: he liked something hard to crack; he liked to hear it crack.

I remember clearly the hot passage he had with Madame Panache, a woman hired for a time by Madame Beck to teach history. She was clever in the way of those who can marshal facts and make them glitter by arrangement; words obeyed her as kitchen implements obey a practiced hand—ready, bright, clattering. Her person was robust and ample; many would have called her “a fine woman.” Yet there were points in her bustling presence that affronted M. Paul’s capricious sense like a harsh note in a quiet room.

It was the sound first. Her voice, flung up the carré, echoed from stone to door to stair-rail, loud as a market-call; it struck him the way iron-shod wheels strike a narrow street—setting one’s teeth on edge. Her step, too—long, free, almost a stride along the corridor—made him snatch up his papers and vanish, as if mere hearing her approach fouled his air.

One day, with a malicious relish, he thrust himself into her class. In a moment—quick as flame runs along a spill—he had caught her method, and found it differed from a pet plan of his own. With little ceremony and less courtesy, he pointed out what he called her errors. Perhaps he expected her to fold like softened pastry; instead she rose, acrid and splendidly affronted, and reprimanded him roundly for interference.

He might still have withdrawn with dignity. He did not. He threw down defiance as a gauntlet on stone. Madame Panache—bellicose as Penthesilea—snatched it up. She snapped her fingers in his face; she rushed upon him with a tempest of words. M. Emanuel could be eloquent; Madame Panache was voluble. The establishment rang for days with their antagonism, as if the very walls had been tuned to dispute.

What amazed me was his seriousness. He did not laugh at her wounded amour-propre; he hated her with full intent. He pursued her vindictively and implacably, denying himself rest, denying himself even the calm enjoyment of his meals or his cigar, until she was rooted out of the school. He conquered; but the laurels did not shade him gracefully.

Once I ventured to hint as much. To my surprise he allowed I might be right, yet added that when brought into contact with persons of coarse self-complacency—Madame Panache being his specimen—he could not govern himself; an unspeakable aversion drove him to a war of extermination.

Three months later I heard she had met with reverses and might be truly distressed for want of employment. Then, with the same energy he had used to expel, he used to assist: he forgot his hatred and moved heaven and earth to find her a place. She came to thank him and make up old differences; but ten minutes of the old loud voice, the old forward manner, set his nerves jangling as if a tin pail had been dropped on a flagged floor. He started up and bowed her—rather himself—out of the room.

That love of power, that eager grasp at supremacy, stayed in him. He was not always to be yielded to. Sometimes it was necessary—right—to resist; to stand still, look up into his eyes, and tell him his demands went beyond reason, that his absolutism verged on tyranny.

Nothing, perhaps, disturbed him like the first dawning of talent within his reach. He watched it struggle into life with a scowl; he held back his hand. He would not aid the birth. When the first conflict was over—when he saw breath drawn and life brighten in the eye—he did not yet foster. “Prove yourself true ere I cherish you,” was his ordinance; and he made proof difficult. He strewed thorns, briers, flints; he exacted ordeals without tears, without fear, keeping the austerest watch over the pain-pressed pilgrim. When at last he allowed a rest, he would still prise open the eyelids with pitiless finger and thumb and peer, as if to read the smallest print of Vanity, Pride, or Falsehood in the very tissues of the brain and heart. If he let one sleep, it was only to wake him suddenly for new tests: irksome errands when weary, trials of temper, sense, and health. Only when every severe assay had been applied and endured—when corrosive aquafortis had failed to tarnish the ore—did he stamp it, silent and clouded, with approval.

I speak by knowledge. Until lately M. Paul had not been my professor; he had given me no lessons. But about the time the last chapter closed, he happened to hear me confess an ignorance—arithmetic, I think—which he said would have disgraced a charity-school boy. He took me in hand at once: examined me, found me abundantly deficient, gave me books, appointed tasks. At first he did it with pleasure—almost exultation—condescending to say he believed I was “bonne et pas trop faible,” though, by adverse circumstance, “as yet in a state of wretchedly imperfect mental development.”

The beginning of all effort with me is always a hard crust to break. I never could, even in forming an acquaintance, show average quickness at once. Each new page of my life has opened with a depressing passage. While that passage lasted, M. Paul was kind—very good, very forbearing. He saw the sharp pain and heavy humiliation my own incapacity inflicted. When tears of shame and effort blurred my sight, his eyes would moisten too. Burdened as he was, he would steal half his brief recreation to help me; and his tenderness then was like warm broth to one faint with fasting—simple, sustaining, unexpectedly sweet.

But strange grief: when the heavy dawn began to lift; when my faculties struggled free, and energy came; when I doubled and trebled the tasks he set, thinking to please him—his kindness hardened. The beam in his eyes turned to a spark. He fretted, opposed, curbed me. The more I did, the less content he seemed. Sarcasms—severe enough to amaze and puzzle—harassed me; then came bitter innuendoes against “pride of intellect.” I was threatened, vaguely, with some doom if I trespassed the limits proper to my sex and conceived a contraband appetite for unfeminine knowledge.

Alas, I had no godlike thirst for discovery. If I loved anything, it was because it pleased someone I cared for to see me try. Yet when M. Paul sneered, I wanted those lofty hungers more keenly; injustice stirred ambition; it gave wings. At first the sneer made my heart ache; by-and-by it warmed my blood and drove my pulses faster. Whatever my powers—feminine or not—God had given them; I resolved to be ashamed of no faculty of His bestowal.

The combat grew sharp. I believed I had lost his affection; he treated me strangely. In his most unjust moods he insinuated I had deceived him when I seemed “faible,” that I had feigned incapacity. Then, with a sudden turn, he would accuse me of imitations and impossible plagiarisms—declaring I had extracted pith from books I had never heard of, books which, had I attempted them, would have sent me into a sleep as deep as Eutychus.

Once, when he pressed such an accusation, I rose against him. I gathered an armful of his books from my desk, filled my apron, and poured them in a heap on his estrade at his feet.

“Take them away, M. Paul,” I said, “and teach me no more. I never asked to be made learned; you compel me to feel that learning is not happiness.”

Then I returned to my place, laid my head on my arms, and would not speak to him for two days. He had pained and chagrined me. His affection—when it was sweet—was a new pleasure, dear and incomparable. Now, seeming withdrawn, it made his lessons tasteless. Yet the books were not taken away. They were restored, carefully, to their places; he came as usual. He made his peace somehow—too readily perhaps. I ought to have held out longer, but when he looked kind and good and held out his hand, memory would not keep sharp hold of his oppressive moments. Besides, reconcilement has its own flavour: like bread shared after hunger, it is too easy to accept.

On a certain morning, a message came from my godmother, inviting me to attend a notable lecture in the public rooms before described. Dr. John brought the message himself and delivered it verbally to Rosine, who did not scruple to follow M. Emanuel into the first class and, in his presence, plant herself squarely before my desk, hand in apron-pocket, and repeat it saucily and aloud, ending with: “Qu’il est vraiment beau, Mademoiselle, ce jeune docteur! Quels yeux—quel regard! Tenez! J’en ai le cœur tout ému!”

When she was gone my professor demanded why I suffered “cette fille effrontée, cette créature sans pudeur,” to address me so. I had no soothing reply. Rosine, in whose skull reverence and reserve had been sparingly portioned, spoke thus continually; and besides—what she said of the young doctor was true enough. Graham was handsome; his eyes were fine; his glance had power to thrill. The observation shaped itself into sound before I checked it.

“Elle ne dit que la vérité,” I said.

“Ah! vous trouvez?”

“Mais, sans doute.”

The day’s lesson then was such as made us glad when it ended. At its close the released pupils rushed out, half-trembling, half-exultant. I too was going—when a mandate to remain stopped me. I muttered that I wanted fresh air—the stove glowed, the class was over-heated. An inexorable voice commanded silence; and this salamander—no room ever seemed too hot for him—sat down between my desk and the stove (a place where he ought to have felt broiled, but did not) and confronted me with a Greek quotation.

In M. Emanuel’s soul rankled a chronic suspicion that I knew both Greek and Latin. As some say monkeys can speak if they would but hide the faculty for fear of harm, so he imagined in me a store of knowledge craftily concealed. I was credited with the privileges of a classical education; I had revelled on Hymettus honey; a golden hoard lay hived in memory to sustain my efforts and secretly nourish my wits.

To surprise this supposed secret he tried a hundred expedients: to wheedle, threaten, startle it out of me. Sometimes he placed Greek and Latin books in my way and watched, as if tempting a prisoner with forbidden armour. Sometimes he rolled out sweet, sounding lines—classic tones falling musically from his lips, for his voice was good, wide in compass, rich in modulation, matchless in expression—while he fixed on me a vigilant, piercing, often malicious eye, as though waiting for some start, some glow, some betrayal.

None came. Not understanding, I could neither be charmed nor annoyed. Baffled—almost angered—he still clung to his delusion. My susceptibilities were called marble; my face a mask. It was as if he could not accept the homely truth and take me as I was. People must have some delusion; if it is not given them ready-made, they will invent exaggeration for themselves.

At moments I wished his suspicion were true. There were hours when I would have given my right hand to possess the treasures he ascribed to me—if only to punish his testy crotchets by fulfilling his worst fears with a sudden blaze of acquirements, and crush, coldly and cruelly, the mocking spirit out of Paul Carl David Emanuel. But no such triumph was in my power.

To-day, as usual, the quotations fell harmless. He shifted ground. “Women of intellect” became his theme: here he was at home. A “woman of intellect,” it seemed, was a lusus naturae—an unlucky accident—fit nowhere, useful nowhere, wanted neither as wife nor worker. Beauty forestalled her in the first office. He believed, with his whole soul, that lovely, placid, passive feminine mediocrity was the only pillow on which manly thought could rest its aching temples; and for work, male mind alone could work to any good practical result—hein?

This “hein?” was bait, meant to draw contradiction. I only answered, “Cela ne me regarde pas: je ne m’en soucie pas;” and then—because my body had its own plain claims and my senses, whatever my mind might be, were not made of stone—I asked, “May I go, Monsieur? They have rung the bell for the second déjeuner.”

“What of that? You are not hungry?”

Indeed I was. I had eaten nothing since breakfast at seven; if I missed this bell, I should have nothing till dinner at five. He said he was in the same plight, but I might share with him. He broke in two the brioche meant for his own refreshment and gave me half.

The crumb was tender; it yielded with that faint, sighing tear that fresh buttered bread makes when you part it; the crust carried a delicate sweetness, faintly yeasty, warm with the scent of baked milk and eggs. For all his harshness, there was in that act a kindliness more nourishing than the cake itself. His bark was worse than his bite.

Yet the formidable attack was still to come.

While I ate, I could not forbear confessing my secret wish—that I truly knew all he accused me of knowing. Did I sincerely feel myself an ignoramus? he asked, softened. Had I answered meekly and simply, I believe his hand would have been stretched out and we should have been friends on the spot. But I replied:

“Not exactly. I am ignorant, Monsieur, of what you ascribe to me; but I sometimes— not always—feel a knowledge of my own.”

“What did I mean?” he demanded sharply.

I could not explain in one breath—how a person may know certain inward measures, certain shades of feeling, certain mute recognitions that no grammar names and no lexicon holds; how one’s true dialect is sometimes not words but sensations—heat and cold, hunger and satiety, shame and courage. I evaded by turning to what my senses offered me then.

He had finished his half of the brioche. The fragment had not satisfied me; surely it had not satisfied him. From the refectory, across the court, came a smell—baked apples: the soft, domestic perfume of fruit yielding to heat, sugar melting into syrup, spice lifting in the steam. It was as if the whole building had, for a moment, remembered a farmhouse kitchen—quiet, patient, and humane—where food is not merely swallowed but tended.

I asked if he did not perceive that agreeable odour. He confessed he did. I said, if he would let me out by the garden-door and permit me to run across the court, I would fetch him a plateful; I added that they must be excellent, for Goton had a good method of baking—or rather stewing—fruit: a little spice, sugar, and a glass or two of vin blanc. Might I go?

“Petite gourmande!” he said, smiling. “I have not forgotten how pleased you were with the pâté à la crème I once gave you; and you know very well that to fetch apples for me will be the same as getting them for yourself. Go then, but come back quickly.”

At last he liberated me—on parole.

My plan was simple: go swiftly, return with good faith, set the plate within the door, and vanish at once—leave consequences for later. But his intolerably keen instinct had foreseen me. He met me at the threshold, hurried me back, and fixed me in my seat again as if I were a truant pupil.

He took the plate from my hand—apples split and collapsed into their own amber juice, the skins wrinkled, the flesh all perfume and softness; I could see the grains of sugar clinging, hear the faint click of spoon on china. He divided what had been meant for himself and ordered me to eat my share.

I complied ill; and vexed by my reluctance, he opened a masked, dangerous battery.

He returned to an unreasonable proposition with which he had before afflicted me: that on the next public examination-day I should—foreigner as I was—take my place on the first form among the first-class pupils and improvise, in French, a composition on any subject a spectator might dictate, without grammar or lexicon.

I knew what would follow. Nature had denied me the impromptu faculty. In public I was, by nature, a cypher. Even alone, my best hours were not under the meridian sun: I needed the fresh hush of morning, or the recluse peace of evening, to coax from the Creative Impulse one sign of presence, one proof of force. With me that impulse was the most intractable, capricious, maddening of masters—himself always excepted. Sometimes, under circumstances apparently propitious, it would not speak when questioned, would not hear when appealed to, would not be found when sought: it would stand cold, indurated, granite—a dark Baal with carven lips and blank eyeballs, breast like a tomb’s stone face. Then, suddenly—at some turn, some sound, some long-trembling sob of wind, some rushing past of an unseen current like electricity—it would wake unasked, stir strangely alive, rush from its pedestal like a perturbed Dagon, calling to its votary for sacrifice, for blood, for breath, regardless of hour or scene—promising oracles, filling the temple with a hum of prophecy, yet giving half the meaning to fateful winds and grudging even the desperate listener a miserable remnant, as if each word were a drop of ichor wrung from its own dark veins.

And this tyrant I was to chain—to make it improvise on a school estrade between a Mathilde and a Coralie, under Madame Beck’s eye, for the pleasure of a bourgeois of Labassecour!

On this subject M. Paul and I had battled before—strong battle, with confused noise of demand and rejection, exaction and repulse. To-day he rated me soundly. The obstinacy of my whole sex, he said, was concentrated in me. I had an “orgueil de diable.” I feared to fail. What mattered if I failed? Who was I that I should not fail like my betters? It would do me good to fail. He wanted to see me worsted; I knew it. At last he paused for breath.

Would I speak now and be tractable?

Never. Law itself should not compel me. I would pay a fine, undergo imprisonment, rather than write for show and to order, perched up on a platform.

Could softer motives influence me? Would I yield for friendship’s sake?

Not a whit—not a hair’s breadth. No friendship had the right to exact it; no true friendship would harass thus.

Then, with that supreme sneer of his—lip curling, nostrils opening, eyelids contracting—he said there was but one form of appeal to which I would listen, and of that it was not for him to make use.

“Under certain persuasions, from certain quarters, je vous vois d’ici,” said he, “eagerly subscribing to the sacrifice, passionately arming for the effort.”

Making a simpleton, a warning, an example of myself before a hundred and fifty papas and mammas of Villette.

Patience broke. I cried out that I must be liberated—that I must get into air—I was almost in a fever.

“Chut!” said the inexorable. It was a pretext to run away. He was not hot, with the stove close at his back; how could I suffer, screened by his person?

I told him I did not understand the constitution of salamanders; I knew nothing of their natural history. I was a phlegmatic islander; sitting in an oven did not agree with me. At least might I step to the well for a glass of water? The sweet apples had made me thirsty.

If that was all, he would do my errand.

He rose and went to fetch it.

The door was only on the latch behind me. I did not waste the chance. Before his return, his half-worried prey had escaped.

Spring had edged forward, and, as if the season had remembered some old caprice, it turned suddenly warm. The air lay on the town like a newly-heated cloth laid over bread: close, sweet, and a little enervating. With the change came, for me, that loosening of strength which heat can bring—one restless night making a whole day thin and colourless; the smallest effort drawing the sap out of me as if I had been a plant set too near a window.

On a Sunday afternoon I walked the half-league to the Protestant church. The streets of Villette were quieter than on working days, yet not silent; from open shutters came the clink of crockery, the brisk slap of a carpet shaken, a child’s call flung down a stairwell, and—always—some thin, metallic hum from the city’s industrious heart, as if even devotion could not buy an hour’s true hush. When I returned I was spent; and I fled, as I often fled, to my solitary sanctuary, the first classe. There I sat down with relief, and made of my desk a pillow for my arms and head, thinking only: rest, rest—let me be as still as flour lying in its bin.

A little while I listened to the bees’ low labouring music in the berceau, that long green passage where the sound of wings and leaf is like a domestic hymn. I watched through the glass door and the tender, lightly-strewn foliage of spring: Madame Beck, and a gay party of friends whom she had entertained at dinner after morning mass, were walking in the centre-alley under orchard boughs all in blossom. The bloom had that pure, warm tint which makes one think of snow at sunrise—pink in its whiteness, and white in its pink, as if the tree had chosen to flower in light.

Among that little company my eye, I remember, fastened on one figure: a handsome young girl I had seen before at Madame Beck’s, Mademoiselle Sauveur by name. I had been told—vaguely, as one is told things in a house where many people listen—that she was a filleule, a god-daughter, of M. Emanuel’s; and that between her mother, or her aunt, or some near female kin, and the Professor, there had existed of old a special friendship. She was always richly dressed—wealth, they said, sat behind her like a servant—and to-day her bright silk caught and threw back every tremble of sun and leaf-shade. M. Paul was not of the holiday band, but I had seen her with him before; and, as far as distant observation could judge, she enjoyed him with the frank ease of a ward with an indulgent guardian. I had seen her run up, put her arm through his, hang upon him; and once—only once, but it had struck keenly—there shot through me a curious sensation: an anticipatory discomfort, one of those presentiments that taste like cold iron on the tongue. I refused to dwell on it, as one refuses to put a strange thing into the mouth again.

While I followed the gleam of her robe through blossoms and young emerald leaves, my eyes grew dazzled; they closed. The warmth, the hum of bees and birds, the dim schoolroom odour of chalk and worn wood warmed by sun—everything lulled me. At last, I slept.

Two hours stole over me. When I woke, the sun had slipped behind the high houses; the garden and the room were grey; the bees were gone homeward; the flowers, as if they had folded their napkins, were closing. The party of guests had vanished too; each alley was void, as after a meal when chairs are pushed back and the last glass has been carried away.

I woke not chilled, as I should have been after sitting so still, but strangely at ease. My cheek and arms were not benumbed by the hard desk. No wonder. Instead of bare wood, a thick shawl had been carefully folded beneath my head and arms; another shawl—both taken from the corridor where such comforts hung for any who chose to claim them—was wrapped warmly round me. Someone had come in; someone had seen my dropped state; someone had moved with a step so quiet, a hand so gentle, that even my day-sleep had not been disturbed.

Who had done this? Which teacher? which pupil? None, except St. Pierre, bore me ill-will; yet which of the rest had the habit—the very instinct—of such tender usefulness? As to Ginevra Fanshawe, bright creature, she was not gentle; had she meddled, she would have tugged me up with a laugh, making a jest of my weariness. At last I said: it is Madame Beck’s doing; she has entered, seen me asleep, and thought I might take cold. She would not have her useful machine damaged. She valued me as one values a good oven that bakes evenly: not beloved, perhaps, but not to be allowed to crack.

“And now,” I thought, “I will take a walk. The evening is fresh.”

I opened the glass door and stepped into the berceau. At my own alley I paused. Had it been dark—had it been true dusk—I should scarcely have ventured there; for I had not forgotten the curious illusion of vision (if illusion it were) experienced in that place some months ago. But a ray of declining sun still burnished the grey crown of Jean Baptiste; the birds were not all yet hidden in tufted shrubs and thick wall-ivy. The garden had that late-day sound which is almost as much silence as sound: a far carriage-wheel softened by distance; a leaf turning over; a sparrow scolding once, then quiet.

I paced up and down, and my mind slipped into its old furrow—the same thoughts I had pondered that night when I buried my glass jar: how to make an advance in life, how to take one step more towards independence. The plan had never died; it only lay under other days, like yeast under a cloth, waiting warmth.

“Living costs little,” I said to myself, “in this economical town of Villette, where people are more sensible than I understand they are in dear old England—less worried about appearance, less fevered with display; where no one is ashamed to be homely and saving. House-rent, chosen prudently, need not be high. When I have saved one thousand francs, I will take a tenement: one large room, two or three smaller. I will furnish the first with benches and desks, a black tableau, an estrade for myself; a chair and table; a sponge; white chalks. I will begin with day-pupils, and work my way upwards. Madame Beck began no higher—she has said so often enough—and where is she now? These premises, this garden, bought with her money; a competency secured for old age; an establishment to make careers for her children.”

The thought went on, and with it came a sterner taste—like the bitter edge of burnt sugar.

“Courage, Lucy Snowe. With self-denial and economy now, and steady exertion by-and-by, an object in life need not fail you. Do not complain that such an object is selfish, limited, without interest. Labour for independence until you have proved, by winning that prize, your right to look higher. But afterwards—afterwards—is there nothing more? No true home? Nothing dearer than myself, that might draw from me better things than I care to culture for myself alone? Nothing at whose feet I could lay down the whole burden of human egotism, and take up, gladly, the nobler charge of living for others?”

It was as if I had set a table for one, then looked up and found the place opposite laid for no one.

“I suppose,” I thought, “the orb of my life is not to be so rounded: the crescent-phase must suffice.” Very good. I saw a huge mass of my fellow-creatures in no better circumstance; I saw that many men, and more women, hold their span on conditions of denial and privation. I found no reason to be among the few favoured. Yet I believed in some blending of hope and sunshine sweetening the worst lots. I believed this life is not all; neither the beginning nor the end. I believed while I trembled; I trusted while I wept.

So that account was closed. It is right, now and then, to look one’s life-ledger bravely in the face and settle it honestly; and he is a poor self-swindler who, while he reckons, sets down under “happiness” what is misery. Call anguish—anguish; despair—despair; write both in strong characters with a resolute pen. Falsify—put “privilege” where you should write “pain”—and see if your mighty creditor accepts the coin. Offer the strongest—if the darkest angel of God’s host—water when he has asked blood: will he take it? Not a whole pale sea for one red drop.

I paused before Methusaleh, the giant patriarch of the garden. Leaning my brow against his knotty trunk, I let my foot rest on the stone sealing the small sepulchre at his root. There, once, I had buried a passage of feeling; and now memory, like a hand reaching down into earth, touched it again. Dr. John rose up as he always rose—warm affection; faith in his excellence; delight in his grace. What had become of that curious, one-sided friendship: half marble, half life; truth on one side, perhaps a jest on the other?

Was it dead? I could not tell; but it was buried. Sometimes I thought the tomb unquiet, and dreamed of disturbed earth, of hair still golden and living, thrust through coffin-chinks. Had I been too hasty? The question came with cruel sharpness after some brief chance interview with him. He had still kind looks, a warm hand; his voice still held that pleasant tone for my name. I never liked “Lucy” so well as when he uttered it.

But time taught me what my heart would not: this benignity, this cordiality, this music, belonged in no shape to me. It was part of himself; honey of temper; balm of mellow mood. He gave it as ripe fruit gives sweetness to the rifling bee; he diffused it as sweet plants shed perfume. Does the nectarine love the bee or bird it feeds? Is sweetbriar enamoured of the air?

“Good-night, Dr. John; you are good, you are beautiful; but you are not mine. Good-night, and God bless you!”

The words left my lips in sound; I heard them spoken. Then, close—very close—I heard an echo.

“Good-night, Mademoiselle; or rather, good-evening—the sun is scarce set. I hope you slept well?”

I started, but was only discomposed a moment; I knew the voice, and the speaker.

“Slept, Monsieur! When? where?”

“You may well inquire when—where. It seems you turn day into night, and choose a desk for a pillow; rather hard lodging—?”

“It was softened for me, Monsieur, while I slept. That unseen, gift-bringing thing which haunts my desk remembered me. No matter how I fell asleep; I awoke pillowed and covered.”

“Did the shawls keep you warm?”

“Very warm. Do you ask thanks for them?”

“No.” He said it curtly; then, as if he could not help himself, he added, “You looked pale in your slumbers. Are you home-sick?”

“To be home-sick, one must have a home; which I have not.”

“Then you have more need of a careful friend. I scarcely know any one, Miss Lucy, who needs a friend more absolutely than you; your very faults imperatively require it. You want so much checking, regulating, and keeping down.”

That thought of “keeping down” never left M. Paul’s head. The most habitual subjugation would, in my case, have failed to cure him of it. I listened, and did not strive to be too submissive; his occupation would have been gone had I left him nothing to keep down.

“You need watching, and watching over,” he pursued, “and it is well for you that I see this, and do my best to discharge both duties. I watch you and others closely—constantly—nearer and oftener than you or they think. Do you see that window with a light in it?”

He pointed to a lattice in one of the college boarding-houses.

“That,” said he, “is a room I have hired, nominally for a study—virtually for a post of observation. There I sit and read for hours; it is my way—my taste. My book is this garden; its contents are human nature—female human nature. I know you all by heart. Ah! I know you well—St. Pierre, the Parisienne—cette maîtresse-femme—my cousin Beck herself.”

“It is not right, Monsieur.”

“Comment? it is not right? By whose creed? Does some dogma of Calvin or Luther condemn it? What is that to me? I am no Protestant. My rich father—though I have known poverty, and once starved for a year in a garret in Rome, starved wretchedly, often on a meal a day, and sometimes not that—yet I was born to wealth—my rich father was a good Catholic; and he gave me a priest and a Jesuit for tutor. I retain his lessons; and to what discoveries, grand Dieu, have they not aided me!”

“Discoveries made by stealth seem to me dishonourable discoveries.”

“Puritaine!” He laughed. “I doubt it not. Yet see how my Jesuit’s system works. You know the St. Pierre?”

“Partially.”

He laughed again, sharper. “You say right—‘partially’; whereas I know her thoroughly; there is the difference. She played before me the amiable; offered me patte de velours; caressed, flattered, fawned. Now, I am accessible to a woman’s flattery—accessible against my reason. Though never pretty, she was—when I first knew her—young, or knew how to look young. Like all her countrywomen, she had the art of dressing—she had a certain cool, easy, social assurance which spared me the pain of embarrassment—”

“Monsieur, that must have been unnecessary. I never saw you embarrassed in my life.”

“Mademoiselle, you know little of me. I can be embarrassed as a petite pensionnaire; there is a fund of modesty and diffidence in my nature—”

“Monsieur, I never saw it.”

“Mademoiselle, it is there. You ought to have seen it.”

“Monsieur, I have observed you in public—on platforms, in tribunes, before titles and crowned heads—and you were as easy as you are in the third division.”

“Mademoiselle, neither titles nor crowned heads excite my modesty; and publicity is very much my element. I like it; I breathe in it freely;—but—but—” He checked, and something in his voice shifted, as if a string had been touched too hard. “In short, here is the sentiment brought into action at this very moment; however, I disdain to be worsted by it. If, Mademoiselle, I were a marrying man—which I am not; and you may spare yourself the trouble of any sneer you may be contemplating—and found it necessary to ask a lady whether she could look upon me as a future husband, then would it be proved that I am as I say—modest.”

I believed him then; and in believing, I honoured him with such sincerity of esteem that my heart ached, as if admiration itself could wound.

“As to the St. Pierre,” he went on, recovering his habitual tone, “she once intended to be Madame Emanuel; and I don’t know whither I might have been led, but for yonder little lattice with the light. Ah, magic lattice! what miracles of discovery hast thou wrought! Yes: I have seen her rancours, her vanities, her levities—not only here, but elsewhere. I have witnessed what bucklers me against all her arts. I am safe from poor Zélie.”

He spoke as a man might speak of a dish he had once been tempted to eat—sweet-scented, handsomely garnished, but found, after tasting, to be tainted; and the very remembrance of the flavour now turned his stomach.

“And my pupils,” he recommenced, “those blondes jeunes filles—so mild and meek—I have seen the most reserved romp like boys, the demurest snatch grapes from the walls, shake pears from the trees. When the English teacher came, I saw her, marked her early preference for this alley, noticed her taste for seclusion, watched her well, long before she and I came to speaking terms. Do you recollect my once coming silently and offering you a little knot of white violets when we were strangers?”

“I recollect it. I dried the violets, kept them, and have them still.”

“It pleased me when you took them peacefully and promptly, without prudery—that sentiment which I ever dread to excite, and which, when revealed in eye or gesture, I vindictively detest. To return. Not only did I watch you; but often—especially at eventide—another guardian angel was noiselessly hovering near. Night after night my cousin Beck has stolen down yonder steps, and glidingly pursued your movements when you did not see her.”

“But, Monsieur, you could not from the distance of that window see what passed in this garden at night?”

“By moonlight I possibly might with a glass—I use a glass—but the garden itself is open to me. In the shed at the bottom, there is a door leading into a court which communicates with the college; of that door I possess the key, and thus come and go at pleasure. This afternoon I came through it, and found you asleep in classe; again this evening I have availed myself of the same entrance.”

I could not help saying, “If you were a wicked, designing man, how terrible would all this be!”

His attention seemed incapable of being arrested by that view. He lit his cigar; the tiny scratch and flare sounded loud in the deepening garden, a brief violence against the soft ecology of evening. The smell of tobacco pushed into the damp sweetness of leaves; the faint rasp of his inhalation and exhale made a rhythm like a small machine beginning to work. He leaned against a tree and looked at me with that cool, amused composure he had when his humour was tranquil.

I thought proper to go on sermonizing him. He often lectured me by the hour; I did not see why I should not speak my mind for once. I told him my impression of his Jesuit system—how the knowledge it brought was bought too dear; how coming and going by stealth degraded his own dignity; how it turned the garden, which ought to be a place where one might breathe honestly, into a theatre of spying.

“My dignity!” he cried, laughing. “When did you ever see me trouble my head about my dignity? It is you, Miss Lucy, who are digne. How often, in your high insular presence, have I taken pleasure in trampling upon what you are pleased to call my dignity—tearing it, scattering it to the winds—in those mad transports you witness with such hauteur, and which I know you think very like the ravings of a third-rate London actor.”

“Monsieur, I tell you every glance you cast from that lattice is a wrong done to the best part of your own nature. To study the human heart thus is to banquet secretly and sacrilegiously on Eve’s apples. I wish you were a Protestant.”

Indifferent to the wish, he smoked on. After a space—smiling, yet thoughtful—he said rather suddenly:

“I have seen other things.”

“What other things?”

He took the weed from his lips and threw the remnant amongst the shrubs, where for a moment it lay glowing in the gloom.

“Look at it,” said he. “Is not that spark like an eye watching you and me?”

He took a turn down the walk; and when he returned his voice had a different grain, as if the garden’s damp had sunk into it.

“I have seen, Miss Lucy, things to me unaccountable—things that have made me watch all night for a solution, and I have not yet found it.”

The tone sent a thrill along my veins; he saw me shiver.

“Are you afraid? Whether is it of my words, or that red jealous eye just winking itself out?”

“I am cold,” I said. “The night grows dark and late; the air is changed; it is time to go in.”

“It is little past eight, but you shall go in soon. Answer me only this question.”

Yet he paused before he put it. Dusk had come on with clouds; drops of rain began to patter through the trees, at first like a cautious hand trying the roof, then more frequent. The garden’s soundscape altered: leaf-noise thickened; distant wheels hissed; somewhere water began to drip steadily from a gutter, making a measured, mournful beat. I hoped he would feel the change and relent, but he was absorbed.

“Mademoiselle, do you Protestants believe in the supernatural?”

“There is a difference of theory and belief on that point amongst Protestants as amongst other sects,” I answered. “Why, Monsieur, do you ask such a question?”

“Why do you shrink and speak so faintly? Are you superstitious?”

“I am constitutionally nervous. I dislike the discussion of such subjects. I dislike it the more because—”

“You believe?”

“No: but it has happened to me to experience impressions—”

“Since you came here?”

“Yes; not many months ago.”

“Here?—in this house?”

“Yes.”

“Bon! I am glad of it. I knew it, somehow, before you told me. I was conscious of rapport between you and myself. You are patient, and I am choleric; you are quiet and pale, and I am tanned and fiery; you are a strict Protestant, and I am a sort of lay Jesuit: but we are alike—there is affinity between us. Do you see it, Mademoiselle, when you look in the glass? Do you observe that your forehead is shaped like mine—that your eyes are cut like mine? Do you hear that you have some of my tones of voice? Do you know that you have many of my looks? I perceive all this, and believe that you were born under my star.”

His words had that curious power of dominance, not merely over thought but over the very language one might use to answer him—as if he would have me speak in his lexicon, not mine. It pricked me; and yet, beneath that prick, there was something—some recognition—that made my breath shorten.

“Yes,” he repeated, “you were born under my star. Tremble! for where that is the case with mortals, the threads of their destinies are difficult to disentangle; knottings and catchings occur—sudden breaks leave damage in the web. But these ‘impressions,’ as you say with English caution. I, too, have had my ‘impressions.’”

“Monsieur,” I said, steadier than I felt, “tell me them.”

“I desire no better, and intend no less. You know the legend of this house and garden?”

“I know it. Yes. They say that hundreds of years ago a nun was buried here alive at the foot of this very tree, beneath the ground which now bears us.”

“And that in former days a nun’s ghost used to come and go here.”

“Monsieur—what if it comes and goes here still?”

“Something comes and goes here. There is a shape frequenting this house by night, different to any forms that show themselves by day. I have indisputably seen a something, more than once; and to me its conventual weeds were a strange sight—saying more than they can do to any other living being. A nun!”

“Monsieur, I, too, have seen it.”

“I anticipated that. Whether this nun be flesh and blood, or something that remains when blood is dried and flesh is wasted, her business is as much with you as with me, probably. Well, I mean to make it out; it has baffled me so far, but I mean to follow up the mystery. I mean—”

Instead of finishing, he lifted his head suddenly. In the same instant I did likewise; we both looked towards the high tree shadowing the great berceau, its boughs resting on the roof of the first classe. From that quarter came a strange, inexplicable sound—an inward heave, a rending—like strong arms struggling under a blanket, like a living body wrenching itself free. There scarce stirred a breeze; yet that heavy tree was convulsed while feathery shrubs stood still, as if the disturbance belonged not to weather but to will.

For some minutes among wood and leafage the tearing and labouring went on. Dark as it was, it seemed to me that something more solid than either night-shadow or branch-shadow blackened out of the boles. Then the struggle ceased. What birth succeeded this travail? What Dryad was born of these throes?

We watched, fixed. A sudden bell rang in the house—the prayer-bell—its clang cutting through rain and leaf-sound, commanding the air as a dominant language commands a crowd.

Instantly, into our alley, there came, out of the berceau, an apparition, all black and white. With a sort of angry rush—close, close past our faces—swept swiftly the very NUN herself.

Never had I seen her so clearly. She looked tall of stature, and fierce of gesture. As she went, the wind rose sobbing; the rain poured wild and cold; the whole night seemed to feel her.

Where, then, was Paulina Mary?

My visits to the Hôtel Crécy—those measured approaches to its great doors and its great people—had been interrupted for a space; the Count de Bassompierre and his daughter had been away, dividing their weeks between provincial air and Parisian glitter. I learned of their return as one learns of spring—by accident, by the sudden gentleness in the light, by a softness in the day that seems to lay its hand on your wrist and bid you walk slower.

It was one of those April afternoons when the sun has the temper of warmed butter: not hot, but yielding; not fierce, but golden. I had been wandering a quiet boulevard where lindens stood in orderly rows, their buds still tight, yet already perfuming the air with that faint green promise one smells before one sees. Underfoot, the broad path held the muffled roll of wheels and the clipped percussion of hooves; overhead, the leaves were not yet loud enough to make their own music, so that every small sound—bridle-chain, rider’s laugh, a distant vendor’s call—rang clear and separate, as if the city, for once, consented to speak in a civilized tone.

Ahead of me a little knot of riders had stopped, meeting in the middle of the smooth avenue as though the whole world were made for their recognition. On one side sat a middle-aged gentleman and a young lady; on the other, a young man—handsome, erect, and animated to the very edge of restraint.

Even at a distance, the young lady’s grace declared itself like a signature. Her horse moved with that trained, collected step which does not waste sound; the leather creaked discreetly; the bit chimed like a small bell. She wore her elegance as one wears a scent: close, natural, and costly without protest. And yet, as I drew nearer, I felt I knew her—as if some old domestic memory, stored with the smell of linen and the tone of a child’s voice, had risen up and walked abroad in velvet.

I recognized them all—the Count Home de Bassompierre, his daughter, and Dr. Graham Bretton.

Graham’s face—Dr. John’s face—was lit as I had seldom seen it lit: joy, true and warm, yet kept within that manly barrier he carried everywhere, like a polished case over a fervent instrument. He had the look of one who is at once conquered and proud of the conquest; subdued, and yet made taller by submission. He was, in truth, the man of the world to his marrow. It was not enough for him to feel; the world must approve the feeling. It was not enough for him to choose; society must smile upon the choice, or else he distrusted his own judgement and called his measures false.

In Paulina he found what his nature demanded. The pearl was pure—yes; but he could not admire the gem and forget the setting. Had he met her without that shining framework—without the authoritative protection of her father, without wealth’s quiet declarations, without fashion’s careful consecration and taste’s finishing touch—she would have seemed to him a pretty creature, perhaps to be regarded, perhaps to be praised; but it was this visible sanction, this atmosphere of consequence, this sheltering prestige, which drew his homage from admiration into allegiance. Here was refinement, guarded and crowned. Here was beauty not exposed, but enthroned.

He did homage to her as to a sovereign.

And Paulina—Paulina smiled, not with the consciousness of power, but with feeling; something soft slept in her eyes, like warmth under snow.

They separated. Graham rode away quickly, as if the earth were too slow for him and the air itself scarce swift enough. He passed me at speed, seeing nothing on either hand.

Then I heard a voice—musical, friendly, clear as a silver spoon against a glass.

“Papa, there is Lucy!”

In a moment she had turned. “Lucy, dear Lucy—do come here!”

I went to her at once. She threw back her veil and leaned down from the saddle to kiss me, her cheek cool with open air, her breath carrying that faint clean scent of riding—leather, violets, and the cold sweetness of spring.

“I was coming to see you to-morrow,” she said; “but now to-morrow you will come and see me.” She named the hour, and I promised.

The next evening found me in her room—she and I shut into that private place, apart from the house’s ceremonial spaces. I had not seen her since the time when her claims had been weighed, silently and strangely, against those of Ginevra Fanshawe, and had prevailed as decisively as virtue over vanity.

Paulina had much to tell—of roads, of cities, of the provinces and the capital; she spoke with animation, yet her clear, soft voice never hurried itself into harshness. Even when her words came fast, they came like good kitchen-work: quick, sure, and clean—no waste, no clatter, no spilling of meaning. The dusk deepened while she talked; in the grate a fire began to show itself as a low red glow, like embers under ash. Outside, the boulevard’s evening noises faded into a quieter ecology—the distant wheels softened, the footfalls grew fewer, and the house itself seemed to hold its breath.

By-and-by she ended her narrative abruptly, as if she had reached a place in her own mind where the path forked and she dared not choose aloud. Silence followed: a restless silence; the kind that has a pulse in it.

Then she turned to me with a half-appealing diffidence.

“Lucy—”

“Well; I am at your side.”

“Is my cousin Ginevra still at Madame Beck’s?”

“Your cousin is still there; you must be longing to see her.”

“No—not much.”

“You want to invite her to spend another evening?”

“No…” The pause that followed was delicate and pained. “I suppose she still talks about being married?”

“Not to any one you care for.”

“But of course she still thinks of Dr. Bretton? She cannot have changed her mind on that point, because it was so fixed two months ago.”

“Why, you know, it does not matter. You saw the terms on which they stood.”

“There was a little misunderstanding that evening, certainly; does she seem unhappy?”

“Not she. To change the subject. Have you heard or seen nothing of, or from, Graham during your absence?”

“Papa had letters from him once or twice about business, I think. He undertook the management of some affair which required attention while we were away. Dr. Bretton seems to respect papa, and to have pleasure in obliging him.”

“Yes: you met him yesterday on the boulevard; you would be able to judge from his aspect that his friends need not be painfully anxious about his health?”

She smiled faintly, and the sound was like the soft crackle of a fire taking hold.

“Papa seems to have thought with you. I could not help smiling. He is not particularly observant, you know, because he is often thinking of other things than what pass before his eyes; but he said, as Dr. Bretton rode away, ‘Really it does a man good to see the spirit and energy of that boy.’ He called Dr. Bretton a boy; I believe he almost thinks him so, just as he thinks me a little girl; he was not speaking to me, but dropped that remark to himself.”

Then again, with that same appealing note—lower now—

“Lucy…”

At the same instant she left her chair and came to sit on the stool at my feet. She took my hand between hers. In the firelight her fingers looked pale and fine; they moved with an unconscious precision, as if accustomed to gentle tasks—sorting ribbons, tying knots, turning pages without tearing them. She was dear to me. I have not often been prodigal of such confession; but intimate intercourse, close inspection, had shown in Paulina a nature delicate, intelligent, sincere, and my regard for her lay deep and quiet.

“What have you to ask of Lucy?” I said. “Be brave, and speak out.”

Her eyes met mine and fell; her cheek did not merely blush upon the surface—there was an inward heat gathering, as though some hidden oven had been stoked too high.

“Lucy, I do wish to know your thoughts of Dr. Bretton. Do, do give me your real opinion of his character—his disposition.”

“His character stands high, and deservedly high.”

“And his disposition? Tell me about his disposition,” she urged; “you know him well.”

“I know him pretty well.”

“You know his home-side. You have seen him with his mother; speak of him as a son.”

“He is a fine-hearted son—his mother’s comfort and hope, her pride and pleasure.”

At each favourable word she stroked my hand, a small caress, timid and grateful, as if she were smoothing some precious cloth and feared to bruise it.

“In what other way is he good, Lucy?”

“Dr. Bretton is benevolent—humanely disposed towards all his race. He would have benignity for the lowest savage, or the worst criminal.”

“I heard some gentlemen—some of papa’s friends—talking about him, say the same. They say many of the poor patients at the hospitals, who tremble before some pitiless and selfish surgeons, welcome him.”

“They are right. I have witnessed as much. He once took me over a hospital; I saw how he was received.”

Her eyes lifted for a moment, full of the softest gratitude, and then—again—hesitated. She seemed to wish the room dimmer, the hour later, the very air more secret.

“How quiet and secluded we feel here!” I said, to reassure her.

“Do we? Yes; it is a still evening, and I shall not be called down to tea; papa is dining out.”

She still held my hand and played with the fingers, unconsciously dressing them now with her rings, now circling them with a twine of her own beautiful hair. At last she pressed my palm against her hot cheek. When she spoke, her voice—naturally liquid as a lark’s—was cleared and steady, though her breath trembled upon the words.

“You must think it rather strange that I should talk so much about Dr. Bretton—ask so many questions—take such an interest, but—”

“Not at all strange; perfectly natural; you like him.”

“And if I did,” she said quickly, with a little spark of pride and pain, “is that a reason why I should talk? I suppose you think me weak, like my cousin Ginevra?”

“If I thought you one whit like Madame Ginevra, I would not sit here waiting for your communications. I would get up, walk at my ease about the room, and anticipate all you had to say by a round lecture. Go on.”

“I mean to go on,” she retorted; and for an instant, with that petulance and sensitiveness, she was the little Polly of Bretton again. “What else do you suppose I mean to do?

“If,” she continued, emphatic and pale with earnestness, “if I liked Dr. John till I was fit to die for liking him, that alone could not license me to be otherwise than dumb—dumb as the grave—dumb as you, Lucy Snowe—you know it—and you know you would despise me if I failed in self-control, and whined about some rickety liking that was all on my side.”

“It is true,” I answered, “I little respect women or girls who are loquacious either in boasting the triumphs, or bemoaning the mortifications, of feelings. But as to you, Paulina, speak—for I earnestly wish to hear you. Tell me all it will give you pleasure or relief to tell: I ask no more.”

“Do you care for me, Lucy?”

“Yes, I do, Paulina.”

“And I love you. I had an odd content in being with you even when I was a little, troublesome, disobedient girl; it was charming to me then to lavish on you my naughtiness and whims. Now you are acceptable to me, and I like to talk with and trust you. So listen, Lucy.”

She settled herself against my arm—gently, as if she knew what weight is and what it costs to carry. The fire murmured; somewhere far below a door shut with a sound like a soft drum. In the momentary hush, the house’s life—so many voices trained to obedience—seemed to fall away, leaving us with the small honest sounds that make intimacy safe.

“A few minutes since,” she said, “you asked whether we had not heard from Graham during our absence, and I said there were two letters for papa on business; this was true, but I did not tell you all.”

“You evaded?”

“I shuffled and equivocated, you know. However, I am going to speak the truth now; it is getting darker; one can talk at one’s ease. Papa often lets me open the letter-bag and give him out the contents. One morning, about three weeks ago, you don’t know how surprised I was to find, amongst a dozen letters for M. de Bassompierre, a note addressed to Miss de Bassompierre.

“I spied it at once, amidst all the rest. The handwriting was not strange; it attracted me directly. I was going to say, ‘Papa, here is another letter from Dr. Bretton;’ but the ‘Miss’ struck me mute. I actually never received a letter from a gentleman before.

“Ought I to have shown it to papa, and let him open it and read it first? I could not for my life, Lucy. I know so well papa’s ideas about me: he forgets my age; he thinks I am a mere school-girl; he is not aware that other people see I am grown up—as tall as I shall be. So, with a curious mixture of feelings—some of them self-reproachful, and some so fluttering and strong I cannot describe them—I gave papa his twelve letters—his herd of possessions—and kept back my one, my ewe-lamb.”

As she spoke, the whole scene came vividly before me: a bright breakfast-room, the crisp sound of paper, a father’s inattentive hand receiving his business like daily bread; a daughter’s lap hiding a single sealed note as if it were a warm loaf snatched from the oven before it cooled. The everyday act—sorting letters—made sudden by its small transgression, and sacred by its tremor.

“It lay in my lap during breakfast,” she went on, “looking up at me with an inexplicable meaning, making me feel myself a thing double-existent—a child to that dear papa, but no more a child to myself.

“After breakfast I carried my letter up-stairs; and having secured myself by turning the key in the door, I began to study the outside of my treasure. It was some minutes before I could get over the direction and penetrate the seal; one does not take a strong place of this kind by instant storm—one sits down awhile before it, as beleaguers say.”

She smiled, but the smile shook.

“Graham’s hand is like himself, Lucy, and so is his seal—all clear, firm, and rounded—no slovenly splash of wax—a full, solid, steady drop—a distinct impress; no pointed turns harshly pricking the optic nerve, but a clean, mellow, pleasant manuscript that soothes you as you read. It is like his face—just like the chiselling of his features. Do you know his autograph?”

“I have seen it: go on.”

“The seal was too beautiful to be broken, so I cut it round with my scissors. On the point of reading the letter at last, I once more drew back voluntarily; it was too soon yet to drink that draught—the sparkle in the cup was so beautiful—I would watch it yet a minute.

“Then I remembered all at once that I had not said my prayers that morning. Having heard papa go down to breakfast a little earlier than usual, I had been afraid of keeping him waiting, and had hastened to join him as soon as dressed, thinking no harm to put off prayers till afterwards. Some people would say I ought to have served God first and then man; but I don’t think heaven could be jealous of anything I might do for papa.”

Her voice grew lower, and the room seemed to draw closer around it, as if even the curtains listened.

“I believe I am superstitious. A voice seemed now to say that another feeling than filial affection was in question—to urge me to pray before I dared to read what I so longed to read—to deny myself yet a moment, and remember first a great duty. I have had these impulses ever since I can remember.

“I put the letter down and said my prayers, adding, at the end, a strong entreaty that whatever happened, I might not be tempted or led to cause papa any sorrow, and might never, in caring for others, neglect him. The very thought of such a possibility so pierced my heart that it made me cry. But still, Lucy, I felt that in time papa would have to be taught the truth, managed, and induced to hear reason.”

In the pauses between her sentences, I heard the fire’s small crack and sigh, and beyond the glass, faint street sounds—Paris breathing itself to sleep. The city’s noise, even subdued, seemed an intrusion, an industrial impatience pressing against our quiet, as if commerce and movement resented a girl’s private devotion. Yet within the room, her words restored a gentler soundscape—prayer, paper, the hush of a locked door—an ecology of secrecy and reverence.

“I read the letter,” she said at last.

Her fingers tightened on mine.

“Lucy, life is said to be all disappointment. I was not disappointed. Ere I read, and while I read, my heart did more than throb—it trembled fast—every quiver seemed like the pant of an animal athirst, laid down at a well and drinking; and the well proved quite full, gloriously clear; it rose up munificently of its own impulse; I saw the sun through its gush, and not a mote, Lucy—no moss, no insect, no atom—in the thrice-refined golden gurgle.”

She paused, as if tasting again that draught—careful, reverent, unwilling to spill a drop by speaking too roughly. There was something in her choice of images—well-water, sunlight, purity—that spoke of a mind trained to guard what is delicate. It made me think how feelings, like recipes handed down, can be ruined by one careless substitution: a too-bold word, a too-public confession, the wrong person allowed to taste.

“Life,” she continued, “is said to be full of pain to some. I have read biographies where the wayfarer seemed to journey on from suffering to suffering; where Hope flew before him fast, never alighting so near, or lingering so long, as to give his hand a chance of one realizing grasp. I have read of those who sowed in tears, and whose harvest, so far from being reaped in joy, perished by untimely blight, or was borne off by sudden whirlwind; and, alas! some of these met the winter with empty garners, and died of utter want in the darkest and coldest of the year.”

“Was it their fault, Paulina,” I asked softly, “that they of whom you speak thus died?”

“Not always their fault. Some of them were good endeavouring people. I am not endeavouring, nor actively good, yet God has caused me to grow in sun, due moisture, and safe protection—sheltered, fostered, taught, by my dear father; and now—now—another comes. Graham loves me.”

For some minutes we sat in silence on that summit. The fire made a low, steady sound, like a pot kept at the gentlest simmer; the room smelt of warm wood and the faintest trace of tea-leaf lingering in the air from some earlier hour. Love—declared—rested among such homely particulars, as if it meant to live not only in letters, but in daily bread.

“Does your father know?” I asked at last.

“Graham spoke with deep respect of papa, but implied that he dared not approach that quarter as yet; he must first prove his worth. He added that he must have some light respecting myself and my own feelings ere he ventured to risk a step in the matter elsewhere.”

“How did you reply?”

“I replied briefly, but I did not repulse him. Yet I almost trembled for fear of making the answer too cordial: Graham’s tastes are so fastidious. I wrote it three times—chastening and subduing the phrases at every rescript; at last, having confected it till it seemed to me to resemble a morsel of ice flavoured with ever so slight a zest of fruit or sugar, I ventured to seal and despatch it.”

“Excellent, Paulina. Your instinct is fine; you understand Dr. Bretton.”

“But how must I manage about papa? There I am still in pain.”

“Do not manage at all. Wait now. Only maintain no further correspondence till your father knows all, and gives his sanction.”

“Will he ever give it?”

“Time will show. Wait.”

“Dr. Bretton wrote one other letter, deeply grateful for my calm, brief note; but I anticipated your advice by saying, that while my sentiments continued the same, I could not, without my father’s knowledge, write again.”

“You acted as you ought to have done; so Dr. Bretton will feel. It will increase his pride in you—his love for you—if either be capable of increase. Paulina, that gentle hoar-frost of yours, surrounding so much pure, fine flame, is a priceless privilege of nature.”

“You see I feel Graham’s disposition,” she murmured. “I feel that no delicacy can be too exquisite for his treatment.”

“It is perfectly proved that you comprehend him; and then—whatever Dr. Bretton’s disposition, were he one who expected to be more nearly met—you would still act truthfully, openly, tenderly, with your father.”

“Lucy, I trust I shall thus act always. Oh, it will be pain to wake papa from his dream, and tell him I am no more a little girl!”

“Be in no hurry to do so, Paulina. Leave the revelation to Time and your kind Fate. I have noticed, too, the gentleness of her cares for you: doubt not she will benignantly order the circumstances, and fitly appoint the hour.”

She leaned her head a little closer against my arm, and in that slight movement I felt the whole weight of what she feared: not scandal, not refusal alone, but the power of words—how a father’s vocabulary for his child might be too small to hold her womanhood.
