To be called “little girl” was not merely affection; it was dominion, it was a language that kept her in a certain place. And she, with her fine sense of the untranslatable, knew that to change a name is to change a world.

“Yes,” I went on, “I have thought over your life just as you have yourself thought it over; I have made comparisons like those to which you adverted. We know not the future, but the past has been propitious.

“As a child I feared for you; nothing that has life was ever more susceptible than your nature in infancy. Under harshness or neglect, neither your outward nor your inward self would have ripened to what they now are. Much pain, much fear, much struggle would have troubled the very lines of your features, broken their regularity, harassed your nerves into the fever of habitual irritation; you would have lost in health and cheerfulness, in grace and sweetness.

“Providence has protected and cultured you—not only for your own sake, but I believe for Graham’s. His star, too, was fortunate: to develop fully the best of his nature, a companion like you was needed. There you are—ready. You must be united. I knew it the first day I saw you together at La Terrasse. In all that mutually concerns you and Graham there seems to me promise, plan, harmony.

“I do not think the sunny youth of either will prove the forerunner of stormy age. I think it is deemed good that you two should live in peace and be happy—not as angels, but as few are happy amongst mortals. Some lives are thus blessed: it is God’s will; it is the attesting trace and lingering evidence of Eden.

“Other lives run from the first another course. Other travellers encounter weather fitful and gusty—breast adverse winds—are belated, and overtaken by the early closing winter night. Neither can this happen without the sanction of God; and I know that, amidst His boundless works, is somewhere stored the secret of this last fate’s justice. I know that His treasures contain the proof as the promise of its mercy.”

Paulina did not answer at once. She only held my hand closer, as if to bind my words to her pulse, and in the quiet room—kept safe from the louder, harsher world—we let the fire speak for us, with its patient domestic sound, while outside the city’s distant noise rolled on, indifferent, like a tide against a shore that refuses to be worn away.

On the first of May, the whole house—twenty boarders and four teachers—received orders that sounded like a trumpet-call and a kitchen-bell struck at once: up at five; dressed by six; ready to march under the command of M. le Professeur Emanuel. He meant, at last, to keep his promise and take his flock out of Villette to breakfast in the country.

I, it is true, had once been told—by tone, by look, by a very intelligible coldness—that I was not invited to that projected fête. When I now ventured to recall this, and to ask quietly how it stood, my ear was caught and smartly pulled: the answer came by that sharp pressure. I did not protest; I only learned. He leaned nearer, as if the other ear also were within reach of discipline.

“Je vous conseille de vous faire prier,” he said; and the menace of his voice made the counsel imperial.

One Napoleonic compliment was enough. I made up my mind to be of the party.

Morning broke with a calm that was almost domestic in its gentleness: birds stitched their small clear notes through the garden like bright thread; the dew lay as a thin mist—cool on the cheek, promising heat later, like milk still faintly chilled before it warms near the stove. We all felt it would be warm; and there was a pleasure, half liberty, in folding away heavier things, in choosing what suited a sunny season.

The girls came down in clean print dresses and light straw bonnets—each bonnet trimmed with that French gift of making the unpretending look exactly right, as if good taste were a sort of daily bread. Nobody rustled in faded silk; nobody clung to a second-hand “best” like a relic. It was all fresh and honest, like linen aired in the morning wind.

At six the bell rang—merry, metallic, impatient—and we poured down the stairs, through the carré, along the corridor, into the vestibule. There stood our Professor. He had put away the savage paletôt and the severe bonnet-grec; he wore instead a belted blouse that made him look younger, and a straw hat that did not fight the sunshine. He gave us the kindest good-morrow; and most of us, receiving it, paid him back with a smile that was almost thanks.

We were marshalled, set in order, and soon we started. The streets were quiet; the boulevards were fresh and peaceful as fields. I believe we were happy as we walked. This chief of ours—capable, when he chose, of striking fear into a heart as if with a sudden clang—could also, by some secret touch, set happiness moving through a whole line of girls like a brisk air through curtains.

He did not lead in front nor stalk behind; he walked up and down our ranks, speaking to each, talking much to his favourites, yet not wholly forgetting even those he disliked. For my own part, I wished—on private grounds—to keep slightly out of his direct notice. I was paired with Ginevra Fanshawe; my arm bore the dear pressure of that angel’s very substantial limb. She continued in excellent case; and to support the burden of her loveliness through a warm day was no slight labour. Many times I wished, with honest fatigue, there had been less of the charming commodity.

Still, I made her useful. I interposed her always between myself and M. Paul—shifting as I heard him coming up on the right or the left, placing Miss Fanshawe like a bright screen, a bouquet held up to hide one’s own blush.

My motive had its colour. The new print dress I wore was pink. Under our present convoy, this made me feel much as I have felt when, wearing a shawl with a red border, obliged to cross a meadow where a bull pastured. For a while, the shifting system, and certain arts with my black silk scarf—shaken out, spread wide, fringes arranged like a little barricade—served; but by-and-by he discovered that, come he on which side he would, Miss Fanshawe remained his neighbour.

Their acquaintance had never run smooth. His temper always crisped at her English accent; their dispositions did not fit—they jarred the moment they touched. He held her empty and affected; she thought him bearish, meddling, and repellent. At last, after he had changed his place the sixth time and found the same result, he thrust his head forward, fixed his eyes on mine, and demanded, with impatient suspicion—

“Qu’est-ce que c’est? Vous me jouez des tours?”

The words were scarcely out when, with his quickness, he seized the root of my manoeuvre. In vain I shook out the long fringe and spread the broad end of my scarf.

“A-h-h! c’est la robe rose!” broke from him—so sudden, so oddly indignant, it affected me like the abrupt low of some lord of the meadow.

“It is only cotton,” I urged, hurriedly; “and cheaper, and washes better than any other colour.”

“Et Mademoiselle Lucy est coquette comme dix Parisiennes,” he retorted. “A-t-on jamais vu une Anglaise pareille. Regardez plutôt son chapeau, et ses gants, et ses brodequins!”

Those articles were like my companions’; not one whit smarter—perhaps plainer than most. But he had found his text; I felt the sermon coming, and I began to chafe under it. Yet it passed as a summer storm sometimes does—threatening much, discharging little. I had but one flash of sheet-lightning, a single bantering smile from his eyes; and then, quite softly—

“Courage!—à vrai dire je ne suis pas fâché, peut-être même suis-je content qu’on s’est fait si belle pour ma petite fête.”

“Mais ma robe n’est pas belle, Monsieur—elle n’est que propre.”

“J’aime la propreté,” said he.

So the sun of his good humour triumphed; it consumed little scudding clouds before they stained his morning.

And now we were in the country—among what they called “les bois et les petits sentiers.” A month later these woods and lanes would be dusty and of doubtful seclusion; now they were May-green and at rest, with leaf-shade still thin enough to let the light sift through like flour through a sieve. The air smelled of damp earth warming, of sap, of crushed grass; every footfall had its own small sound—gravel clicking, twigs snapping, the soft brush of skirts—and above it the birds held their bright, living orchestra. It was a soundscape to heal the mind: no iron wheels shrieking, no factory throb, no city’s endless metallic fret. Only breath, step, and song.

We reached a well—planted round, in Labassecour taste, with an orderly circle of lime-trees. Here we halted. On the green swell of ground surrounding the well we were ordered to sit; Monsieur took his place in our midst and allowed us to gather round him in a knot. Those who liked him more than they feared came close (chiefly the little ones); those who feared more than they liked kept away; those in whom affection had given even fear a pleasurable zest held the greatest distance.

He began to tell us a story.

He could narrate—well. His diction had that strength children love and learned men envy: simple, yet not poor; plain, yet never bare. He tinted a twilight scene—so quietly, with such sure touch, that it fixed itself in me like a remembered scent. I have never seen from any artist’s pencil the very picture he made with words.

I had no impromptu faculty; that deficiency made me marvel the more at his. He was not a man to write books; yet I have heard him fling away, careless as a sower, handfuls of mental wealth which books seldom hold. His mind was my library: when it opened to me, I entered bliss. Bound and printed volumes often wearied me; they fagged and blinded; but his spoken pages were a collyrium to the eyes of the spirit. Over them, inward sight grew clear and strong.

His story ended, he came to the little knoll where Ginevra and I sat apart. In his usual way—never waiting for an opinion to be offered—he asked abruptly—

“Were you interested?”

“Yes.”

“Was it good?”

“Very good.”

“Yet I could not write that down,” said he.

“Why not, Monsieur?”

“I hate the mechanical labour; I hate to stoop and sit still. I could dictate it, though, with pleasure, to an amanuensis who suited me. Would Mademoiselle Lucy write for me if I asked her?”

“Monsieur would be too quick; he would urge me, and be angry if my pen did not keep pace with his lips.”

“Try some day; let us see the monster I can make of myself under the circumstances. But just now there is no question of dictation; I mean to make you useful in another office. Do you see yonder farm-house?”

“Surrounded with trees? Yes.”

“There we are to breakfast; and while the good fermière makes the café au lait in a caldron, you and five others, whom I shall select, will spread with butter half a hundred rolls.”

At that word—breakfast—there rose in my imagination not merely hunger but a whole scene of domestic labour: hot milk climbing in a great pot, coffee bruised and brewed till it sent up its deep brown perfume, butter softening to a spreadable gloss, bread waiting like a white crowd to be made ready for mouths and laughter. It pleased me that he did not plan a mere outing; he planned a meal, and a meal requires hands.

He formed his troop into line again and marched us straight on the farm, which, on seeing our force, surrendered without capitulation.

Clean knives and plates appeared; fresh butter, yellow as a primrose-heart, was set out; and half-a-dozen of us—chosen by our Professor—set to work under his directions to prepare for breakfast a huge basket of rolls. The baker had been ordered beforehand to provision the farm in anticipation of our coming; the rolls lay there in their piled abundance, the crusts delicate and pale, the insides still tender with that faint scent of yeast and oven-heat that clings to newly baked bread like a secret.

Coffee and chocolate were already hot. Cream came, thick and cool; eggs, new-laid, were added—shells still with a farmyard freshness, the yolks promising that deep gold which only a hen’s honest feeding can make. M. Emanuel—always generous—would have ordered “jambon” and “confitures” as well; but some of us, presuming perhaps on our influence, insisted it would be reckless waste of victual.

He railed at us, calling us “des ménagères avares;” but we let him talk and managed the economy our own way. It seemed to me that, even in this, there was a small struggle between two languages of power: his—lavish, authoritative, impatient of counting; ours—prudent, domestic, careful of what a household must remember. Waste is a kind of forgetfulness; and I have always feared forgetfulness.

With what a pleasant countenance he stood on the farm-kitchen hearth looking on! The kitchen had its own music: the low simmer in the caldron, the clink of knife on plate, the faint crack as a roll opened, the quick rustle of aprons, and our voices—bright, unforced, not drowned by any modern roar. One could think in such a place. One could breathe.

He was a man made happy by seeing others happy; he liked movement, animation, abundance, and enjoyment about him. We asked where he would sit. He told us we knew well he was our slave and we his tyrants, and that he dared not even choose a chair without our leave. So we set him in the farmer’s great chair at the head of the long table and put him into it.

Well might we like him—passions, hurricanes, and all—when he could be so benignant and docile at times. At his worst, it was only his nerves that were irritable, not his temper that was radically bad; soothe, comprehend, comfort him, and he was a lamb; he would not harm a fly. Only to the very stupid, the perverse, the unsympathizing, was he dangerous.

Mindful always of his religion, he made the youngest say a little prayer before we began breakfast, crossing himself as devotedly as a woman. I had never seen him pray before, or make that pious sign. He did it so simply, with such child-like faith, that I smiled—pleased without knowing why. His eyes met my smile; he stretched out his hand kindly.

“Donnez-moi la main! I see we worship the same God, in the same spirit, though by different rites.”

Most of his brother professors were emancipated free-thinkers, infidels, atheists; many were men whose lives would not bear scrutiny. He was rather like a knight of old—religious in his way and of spotless fame. Innocent childhood, beautiful youth were safe beside him. His vivid passions and keen feelings were held in check by pure honour and artless piety: those were the charm that kept the lion couchant.

That breakfast was merry; and the merriment was not mere vacant clatter. M. Paul originated, led, controlled, heightened it. His social temper played unfettered and unclouded. Surrounded only by women and children, there was nothing to cross him; he had his own way—and it was a pleasant way. The coffee went round—dark, fragrant, and strong; the chocolate came, thicker, sweeter, with that warm, faintly spiced breath which clings to it. The rolls, buttered half to their edges, shone; eggs broke open with quiet richness; cream whitened cups. I watched mouths and hands; I listened to laughter rising and falling with the same ease as birdsong outside; and it seemed to me that nourishment—shared, prepared, and received together—bound us more securely for that hour than any rule in Madame Beck’s house.

When the meal ended, the party were free to run in the meadows. A few stayed to help the farmer’s wife put away her earthenware. I was among those; I liked the order of it—the stacking of plates, the wiping of cups, the quiet economy after abundance.

M. Paul called me from among the helpers to come out and sit near him under a tree, whence he could watch the troop gambolling over a wide pasture, and read to him while he took his cigar. He sat on a rustic bench; I sat at the root of the tree. The grass smelled bruised and green; the sunlight lay warm on the ground. Even the small sounds—the creak of the bench, the far cry of a child, the steady whisper of leaves—seemed arranged for content.

I read from a pocket classic—Corneille. I did not like it; he did, finding beauties I could never be brought to perceive. While I read, he listened with a sweetness of calm the more impressive because his nature was generally impetuous. Deep happiness filled his blue eye and smoothed his broad forehead.

I too was happy—happy with the bright day, happier with his presence, happiest with his kindness.

By-and-by he asked if I would not rather run to my companions than sit there.

“No,” I said. “I feel content to be where you are.”

He asked whether, if I were his sister, I should always be content to stay with a brother such as he.

“I believe I should,” I answered; and I felt it.

Again he inquired whether, if he were to leave Villette and go far away, I should be sorry.

At that, I dropped Corneille and made no reply.

“Petite sœur,” said he, “how long could you remember me if we were separated?”

“That, Monsieur, I can never tell,” I said; “because I do not know how long it will be before I shall cease to remember everything earthly.”

“If I were to go beyond seas for two—three—five years, should you welcome me on my return?”

“Monsieur, how could I live in the interval?”

“Pourtant j’ai été pour vous bien dur, bien exigeant.”

I hid my face with the book. Tears fell upon its page—tears with no right to be there, blurring Corneille’s severe lines as if my own heart insisted on writing over them in a language no one teaches and no one translates. I asked him why he talked so. He said he would talk so no more; he tried to cheer me with the kindest encouragement.

Yet the gentleness with which he treated me during the rest of the day went to my heart. It was too tender; it had something mournful in it. I would rather he had been abrupt, whimsical, irate as was his wont. That kindness felt like a hand laid on a wound before the knife.

When hot noon arrived—the day turning out as we had guessed, glowing as June—our shepherd collected his sheep from the pasture and led us softly home. But the farm lay a whole league from Villette. The children, especially, were tired with their play; most spirits flagged at the prospect of that mid-day walk over chaussées flinty, glaring, and dusty. The thought of returning through that hard brightness made the body shrink; even the ears seemed to dread the rasp of feet on dry road.

This, however, had been foreseen. Just beyond the farm boundary we met two spacious vehicles coming to fetch us—such conveyances as are hired for school-parties. With good management, room was found for all; and in another hour M. Paul made safe consignment of his charge at the Rue Fossette.

It had been a pleasant day. It would have been perfect but for that breath of melancholy that had dimmed its sunshine for a moment.

The tarnish returned that same evening.

About sunset I saw M. Emanuel come out at the front door with Madame Beck. They paced the centre-alley for nearly an hour, speaking earnestly. He looked grave, restless; she wore an amazed, expostulatory, dissuasive air. I wondered what lay under their words. When Madame re-entered as it darkened, leaving her kinsman still lingering in the garden, I said to myself—

“He called me ‘petite sœur’ this morning. If he were really my brother, how I should like to go to him now and ask what presses on his mind. See how he leans against that tree—arms crossed, brow bent. He wants consolation; Madame does not console: she only remonstrates. What now—?”

From stillness to action he started. M. Paul came striding down the garden—erect, quick. The carré doors stood open. I thought he was perhaps going to water the orange-trees in their tubs, as he sometimes did; but, reaching the court, he turned abruptly towards the berceau and the first-classe glass door.

There—in that first classe—I was. From thence I had watched him. Yet there I could not find courage to await his approach. He had turned so suddenly; he strode so fast; his face looked so strange. The coward within me grew pale, shrank, and—without waiting to hear reason, and hearing the shrubs crush and the gravel crunch to his advance—she was gone on the wings of panic.

I did not pause until I had taken sanctuary in the oratory, empty now. There, listening with beating pulses and an unaccountable, undefined apprehension, I heard him pass through the schoolrooms, clashing the doors impatiently as he went. I heard him invade the refectory where the “lecture pieuse” held us under hallowed constraint. I heard him pronounce the words—

“Où est Mademoiselle Lucie?”

And just as, summoning courage, I was preparing to go down and do what, after all, I most wished in the world—to meet him—the wiry voice of St. Pierre replied glibly and falsely—

“Elle est au lit.”

He passed, with a stamp of vexation, into the corridor. There Madame Beck met him—captured, chid, convoyed him to the street-door, and finally dismissed him. When the street-door closed, amazement at my own perverse proceeding struck me like a blow.

From the first I had felt it was me he wanted—me he was seeking—and had not I wanted him too? What then had carried me away? What had rapt me beyond his reach? He had something to tell; he was going to tell me that something. My ear strained to hear it, and I had made the confidence impossible.

Yearning to listen and console, while I believed audience and solace beyond hope—no sooner did opportunity arrive, sudden and full, than I evaded it as I would have evaded the levelled shaft of mortality.

My insane inconsistency had its reward. Instead of the comfort, the certain satisfaction, I might have won—had I but put choking panic down and stood firm two minutes—there was now dead blank, dark doubt, drear suspense.

I took my wages to my pillow, and passed the night counting them.

Madame Beck sent for me on Thursday afternoon, and, with that quiet promptitude in her eye which always made a request wear the air of an order, asked whether I had anything to hinder me from going into town to execute some little commissions at the shops.

I had none. To be useful—especially in matters requiring neatness and exactitude—was at least to have a place, and a purpose; and so I accepted, and was presently furnished with a list—wools in such a shade as would not “bleed” in washing, silks to match certain patterns, skeins of embroidering-thread, tapes, slides, tassels: all the fussy, finger-staining furnishings by which young girls learn patience, and women learn economy.

I dressed for a day whose sky hung low, sultry and leaden—air thick as if it had been boiled down to a syrup, and then forgotten on the fire. I had drawn the spring-bolt of the street-door, my hand already on the latch to go out, when Madame’s voice recalled me to the salle-à-manger.

“Pardon, Meess Lucie!” she cried, as if a thought had darted upon her at the very instant. “I recollect one more errand for you, if your good-nature will not deem itself over-burdened?”

I hastened to protest—there are forms of refusal which women like her do not pardon; besides, I was indeed disengaged.

She ran into the little salon and came back with a basket—pretty, light, and contrived for a lady’s hand rather than a servant’s—filled with hothouse fruit, such fruit as seems less grown than fabricated: skins polished to a blush, fragrance forced to a sweetness almost too full, leaves around them dark-green and wax-like, with pale, starry blossoms of some foreign plant pinned in for show. The scent rose warm and faintly cloying—summer coaxed out of glass and fire, as if Nature had been bribed.

“There,” said she, placing it on my arm with an air of kindness that kept its edge of command. “It is not heavy; it will not shame your neat toilette as if it were a household detail. Do me the favour to leave this little basket at the house of Madame Walravens, with my felicitations on her fête. She lives down in the old town, Numéro 3, Rue des Mages. The walk is rather long—but you have the afternoon; do not hurry. If you are not back in time for dinner, I will order a portion to be saved, or Goton—who is fond of you—will toss up some trifle for your especial benefit: an omelette, perhaps, or some soup quickly strengthened with herbs. You shall not be forgotten, ma bonne Meess. And oh! please—” she called me back once more as I moved away—“be sure to insist on seeing Madame Walravens herself, and give the basket into her own hands. She is punctilious. Adieu! Au revoir!”

So at last I got away.

In the shops my list consumed more time than my steps. To choose wools is to handle half the flocks of the country—each skein with its own rough breath of dye and lanolin; to match silks is to strain the eye upon gleam and undertone; and embroidering-thread, fine as a spider’s cable, entangles itself like thought. I stood among drawers and trays, amid the whisper of measuring-tapes and the dry music of paper unrolled and smoothed; the city outside sounded muffled, as if the air, already heavy with storm, had begun to thicken the very noise. At length, however, the patterns for slippers and bell-ropes and cabas were chosen, the tassels and slides selected; all that little “tripotage” was off my mind. Nothing remained but the fruit and the felicitations.

I liked the prospect of the long walk into the grim Basse-Ville; and I liked it no worse because the evening sky, over the city, was settling into a mass like black-blue metal heated at the rim and slowly inflaming to a dull, labouring red. There are weathers that command exertion—high wind, for instance, that wrestles with one’s limbs and calls out strength I do not possess without pain. But rain—rain asks only resignation. It takes you as you are; it drenches garment and hair; and in return it purifies the town. It sweeps the broad streets as clean as a scullery-maid’s pail; it empties the pavements; it hushes wheels and cries; it petrifies the living city into a silent, wet monument. Let then the floods descend—only I must first rid myself of this basket of fruit, this burden of warm, perfumed sweetness, which in such a storm seemed almost absurdly delicate.

An unknown clock from an unknown tower—Jean Baptiste’s voice was now too distant to be heard—was tolling the third quarter past five when I reached the street and house Madame Beck had named. It was hardly a street; rather a piece of a square. Grass grew between broad grey flags; the houses were large, old, with an air of shut-up pride; behind them rose the dark suggestion of trees—gardens, perhaps, where former owners had walked in leisure. Antiquity brooded there; trade and bustle were banished. Rich men had once held that quarter, and greatness had sat there; but wealth had long since stretched its gilded wings and flown, leaving its old nests—either to shelter penury, or to stand empty and mouldering through winters.

Across this deserted place the first great drops were beginning to fall—slow, heavy drops, darkening the stones like little bruises. In all that open space I saw no sign of life except an infirm old priest, bending and propped on a staff—the very emblem of decay. He had issued from the house I sought; and when I paused before the door, just closed after him, and rang, he turned to look at me. He did not soon avert his gaze. Perhaps I, with my basket of forcing-house fruit, and my youth—youth without authority—appeared incongruous in that solemn, forsaken square.

The door was opened, not by a plump, ruddy bonne, but by a very old woman in an antique peasant costume—cap at once hideous and costly, with long flaps of native lace; jacket and petticoat of stout cloth; sabots like little boats. The dress suited the place; it matched the grey flags and the ancient doors. Her face, however, did not soothe as her costume did. I had seldom seen anything more sourly cantankerous. She would scarcely reply to my inquiry for Madame Walravens; and I believe she would have snatched the basket from my arm—fruit and all—had not the priest, hobbling back, checked her.

His deafness, or seeming deafness, made my task awkward. I repeated, more than once, that I must see Madame Walravens herself; that my orders were to place the basket in her own hands. At last he understood. He addressed the aged bonne—not in French, but in the aboriginal tongue of Labassecour: the very sound of it struck my ear with a strange power, like an old song half remembered. It was thicker, earthier than French, with consonants that seemed to have grown out of fields and barns. The bonne, who had treated my French as if it were impertinence, listened to that dialect as to law; her whole manner softened—no, not softened, but yielded. Language, I saw, is command; and the tongue of the soil holds dominion where the polished speech of cities may knock and be left on the doorstep.

I was at last admitted. The priest himself escorted me up-stairs and ushered me into a sort of salon, and there left me.

The room was large, with a fine old ceiling and windows of coloured glass, almost church-like; but it was bare and desolate, and, under the thick shadow of the coming storm, looked lowering—like a hall waiting for a funeral that had been delayed. A smaller room opened within; there the blind of the single casement was closed. In the deep gloom I could scarcely distinguish furniture; only outlines—an upright chair, a little table, something like a framed picture on the wall.

By-and-by that picture seemed to shift. To my bewilderment, it shook, sank, rolled back, and vanished, leaving an arched opening—an arched passage beyond, and a winding stair, cold stone, uncarpeted, unpainted, like a stair in a donjon. Down those steps came a tap, tap—as of a stick. Then a shadow fell, and last of all a presence.

Was it a body? It seemed less a body than an obstruction—something that darkened the arch. It drew nearer, and I saw it well, and comprehension came with a chill.

Well might this be named the quarter of the Magi; well might those turrets overlooking it take for godfathers three mystic sages of dead and dark art. Here hoar enchantment prevailed. That vanishing picture, that stone passage, that winding stair—these were stage-properties of a fairy tale; and the chief figure was its malevolent fairy.

She might have been three feet high; yet she had no shape—only angles and hollows, a heap of brocade and jewels arranged upon deformity. Her skinny hands rested on each other, pressing the gold knob of a wand-like ivory staff. Her face—large, set not on shoulders but before her breast, as if she had no neck—was scored with a century; her eyes seemed older still: malign, unfriendly, thick grey brows above, livid lids around. They looked at me as if my existence were an offence.

She wore a gown of bright blue brocade, full-tinted as a gentian, with satin foliage in a large pattern; over it a costly shawl, gorgeously bordered, so large that its many-coloured fringe swept the floor. But her chief splendour was her jewels: long clear earrings with a lustre no glass could borrow; rings on her skeleton hands, thick gold hoops set with stones—purple, green, blood-red. Hunchbacked, dwarfish, doting—she was adorned like a barbarian queen. A silver beard bristled her chin.

“Que me voulez-vous?” she demanded, hoarsely—the voice rather of male than female old age.

I delivered the basket and Madame Beck’s message.

“Is that all?” she asked.

“It is all,” I answered.

“Truly—it was well worth while,” she returned. “Go back to Madame Beck and tell her I can buy fruit when I want it; et quant à ses félicitations, je m’en moque!”

And she turned her back.

At that instant thunder broke, and lightning blazed broad over salon and inner room. It was as if the tale insisted upon its accompaniment: the wanderer decoyed into the enchanted house, the spell-wakened tempest rising outside. The air had its own soundscape now—no longer the distant commerce of streets, but the great, blunt percussion of heaven and the white tearing hiss of rain. Modern noise is a needless cruelty; but the storm’s uproar is primitive and, in its way, cleansing—an orchestra older than any city, yet capable of drowning it like a basin held under a tap.

What, in all this, was I to think of Madame Beck? She owned strange acquaintances. She sent gifts to an unpropitious shrine, and the uncouth being she honoured received both offering and congratulation with scorn. There went that sullen Sidonia, tapping her ivory staff on the mosaic parquet, muttering venomously as she disappeared.

The rain now fell in sheets. The sky, ruddy a while ago, had turned, through all its blackness, deadly pale, as if terrified by its own violence. Forked bolts lanced through vertical torrents; red zigzags interlaced a descent blanched like white metal; and all burst from a firmament swollen and dark.

Notwithstanding my late boast that I did not fear a shower, I did not like to go out under this waterspout. I left the inhospitable salon and sought the cold staircase; on the landing there was a seat. There I waited, listening: the rain’s continuous drumming, the thunder’s nearer crashes, the wind’s sudden shoves against shutters—each sound had its place, its pitch, its threat. In such moments the ear becomes the surest organ; it reads danger before the eye can see it.

Somebody glided along the gallery above. It was the old priest.

“Indeed, Mademoiselle shall not sit there,” he said. “It would displease our benefactor if he knew a stranger was so treated in this house.”

He begged me, with such earnest courtesy, to return to the salon, that without discourtesy I could not refuse. But instead of the bare larger room, he led me into the smaller inner chamber. He partially withdrew the blind, and the place revealed itself—less a boudoir than an oratory: solemn, close, dedicated rather to remembrance than comfort. It had that particular smell of old devotion—wax, dry wood, time itself—like linen laid away with lavender in a chest no one opens.

He sat down as if to keep me company; but he did not talk. He took out a book, fixed his eyes on the page, and moved his lips in whispering—prayer, litany. A yellow electric light from the storm-sky gilded his bald head while his figure remained in deep purple shade. He sat still as sculpture. Only when a fiercer bolt or harsher, closer rattle spoke of nearing danger did he raise his eyes—less in fear than in awe.

I, not being crushed by terror, could observe. To speak truth, I began to fancy that this old priest resembled Père Silas, before whom I had once knelt in the church of the Béguinage. The thought was vague—my confessor I had seen only in dusk and in profile—yet there seemed a likeness; I thought too I recognized the voice. As I watched him, he betrayed by a lifted look that he felt my scrutiny; and I turned my gaze to the room.

Beside a cross of curiously carved old ivory, yellow with age, above a dark-red prie-dieu furnished with a rich missal and ebon rosary, hung the picture whose dim outline had drawn my eyes before—the picture which had moved and rolled away with the wall. In the gloom I had taken it for a Madonna. Now, in clearer light, it proved a woman’s portrait in a nun’s dress. The face was pale, young, touched with the dejection of grief or ill health. Not beautiful; not even intellectual. Its amiability was the amiability of weakness—of a frame too frail for passion, of habits trained to acquiescence. Yet I looked long, and could not choose but look.

The priest, without lifting his head, as if his eyes never left the page, perceived where my attention rested. In a slow, distinct voice he let fall four observations, each like a bead told off:

“She was much beloved.

“She gave herself to God.

“She died young.

“She is still remembered—still wept.”

“By that aged lady—Madame Walravens?” I asked, thinking to find, in bereavement, a key to the old woman’s desperate ill-humour.

He shook his head, half smiling.

“No, no,” said he. “A grand-dame’s affection for her children’s children may be great, and her sorrow lively; but it is only the affianced lover—whom Fate, Faith, and Death have trebly denied the bliss of union—who mourns what he has lost, as Justine Marie is still mourned.”

He seemed to wish me to question him. I asked who had lost, and who still mourned, Justine Marie. He answered with a little narrative, not without romance, told while the storm, as if weary of its own violence, began to subside. He spoke with that French art of sentiment which pulls emotion into fine threads; I confess I should have found the truth more impressive had it been told with less handling and more simplicity. Yet the old man, though pious and gentle, had in him a certain shrewdness: when he lifted his eyes, he looked at me out of their corners with a subtlety one would hardly have expected to survive seventy years. Still, I believe he was a good man.

His tale concerned a former pupil—his benefactor—who had loved this pale Justine Marie, daughter of rich parents, when his own worldly prospects warranted the hope of a well-dowered hand. Then disaster came. The pupil’s father—once a rich banker—failed, died, and left debts and destitution. The son was forbidden to think of Marie. Above all, the hunchbacked grand-dame—Madame Walravens—opposed the match with a violence which deformity seemed to have sharpened into something demoniac.

Justine Marie, mild and weak, had neither the treachery to be false nor the force to be steadfast. She gave up her first suitor; but refusing to accept a second with a heavier purse, she withdrew to a convent and died there in her noviciate.

Lasting anguish, the priest said, had taken possession of the faithful heart that had worshipped her; and the truth of that love and grief showed itself afterward in deeds that, even to me, listening, were touching.

Years after Justine Marie’s death, ruin came upon her house also. Her father—a jeweller by nominal calling, but one who dealt on the Bourse—was entangled in financial transactions that brought exposure and ruinous fines. He died of grief for the loss and shame for the infamy. His old mother—this very Madame Walravens—and his bereaved wife were left penniless, and might have died of want; but the once-despised suitor, hearing of their condition, came with singular devotedness to the rescue. He took on their insolent pride the revenge of pure charity—housing them, caring for them, befriending them as tenderly and efficiently as a son.

The mother—on the whole a good woman—died blessing him. The strange, loveless, misanthrope grandmother lived on, supported entirely by this self-sacrificing man. Her, who had blighted his hope and denied him domestic happiness, awarding him long mourning and cheerless solitude, he treated with a respect a good son might offer a kind mother.

He had brought her to this house; and, said the priest, tears rising frankly to his eyes, “here, too, he shelters me—his old tutor—and Agnes, a superannuated servant of his father’s family. To our sustenance, and to other charities, I know he devotes three parts of his income, keeping only the fourth to provide himself with bread and the most modest accommodation. By this arrangement he has rendered it impossible ever to marry. He has given himself to God and to his angel-bride as much as if he were a priest, like me.”

He wiped his tears before those last words; and when he pronounced them he lifted his eyes to mine for an instant. Though veiled, the glance shot a meaning that struck me—like a pin passed through cloth to fasten something down.

Certain people—Romanists, especially—are strange beings. Such a one, whom you know no more than the last Inca, knows you and all your concerns; and says to you so and so, not because the impulse happens to seize him, but because a plan has been laid. An errand suddenly remembered; a basket of fruit; an address in an ancient square; the old priest “accidentally” crossing as I arrived; his intervention with the bonne; his reappearance on the stairs; my introduction to this oratory; the portrait; the narrative volunteered—each incident, taken alone, might seem chance, like loose beads in a palm. But I had seen the rosary on the prie-dieu. I felt the string somewhere. I could not yet find the knot.

Perhaps my abstraction looked suspicious. He interrupted gently.

“Mademoiselle,” said he, “I trust you have not far to go through these inundated streets?”

“More than half a league.”

“You live—?”

“In the Rue Fossette.”

“Not—” he spoke with sudden animation—“not at the pensionnat of Madame Beck?”

“The same.”

“Donc,” he cried, clapping his hands. “Donc, vous devez connaître mon noble élève—mon Paul?”

“Monsieur Paul Emanuel, Professor of Literature?”

“He—and none other.”

A brief silence followed. The hidden clasp of the necklace seemed suddenly to give under pressure; I felt the connection become palpable.

“Was it of M. Paul you have been speaking?” I asked. “Was he your pupil—and the benefactor of Madame Walravens?”

“Yes; and of Agnes, the old servant. And moreover”—he laid an emphasis as if to seal the sentence—“he was and is the lover, true, constant, eternal, of that saint in heaven—Justine Marie.”

“And who, father, are you?” I continued. The question was scarcely needed; I had prepared myself for the answer.

“I, daughter, am Père Silas,” he said; “that unworthy son of Holy Church whom you once honoured with a noble and touching confidence—showing me the core of a heart, and the inner shrine of a mind whereof, in solemn truth, I coveted the direction, in behalf of the only true faith. Nor have I for a day lost sight of you; nor for an hour failed to take in you a rooted interest. Passed under the discipline of Rome, moulded by her high training, inoculated with her salutary doctrines, inspired by the zeal she alone gives—I realize what then might be your spiritual rank, your practical value; and I envy Heresy her prey.”

I half realized myself, for an instant, as if already in that condition—disciplined, moulded, inoculated. “Not so,” thought I; but I restrained any protest and sat quiet.

“I suppose M. Paul does not live here?” I asked, turning to what seemed to me the only matter worth pursuing.

“No. He comes occasionally—to worship his beloved saint, to make his confession to me, and to pay his respects to her he calls his mother. His own lodging consists but of two rooms. He has no servant. And yet he will not suffer Madame Walravens to dispose of those splendid jewels with which you see her adorned, and in which she takes a puerile pride—the ornaments of her youth, and the last relics of her son the jeweller’s wealth.”

How often, I said to myself, has this man—this M. Emanuel—seemed to me to lack magnanimity in trifles; and yet how great he is in great things!

I did not count, among his greatness, either confession or saint-worship. But self-denial, carried out in bread and rent and daily living—self-denial that keeps other people warm while it leaves oneself poorly housed—that I could understand. It is easy to offer one’s feelings; it is harder to portion out one’s income like a loaf and give away the larger share.

“How long is it since that lady died?” I asked, looking again at Justine Marie.

“Twenty years. She was somewhat older than M. Emanuel. He was then very young; for he is not much beyond forty.”

“Does he yet weep her?”

“His heart will weep her always,” said Père Silas. “The essence of Emanuel’s nature is—constancy.”

He spoke the word as if it were doctrine, and also warning.

Now the tempest had spent itself. The rain still fell, but without rage; the sun broke out pale and watery through a cleft in the hot firmament, as if exhausted by its own lightnings. A longer delay would leave me no daylight for the return. I rose, thanked the father for his hospitality and his tale, and was answered benignantly with a “pax vobiscum,” which I could accept, for it seemed uttered with true benevolence—though I liked less the mystic phrases that hung about him like incense.

“Daughter, you shall be what you shall be!” he added.

It was an oracle that made me shrug as soon as I had got outside the door. Few of us know certainly what we shall come to; but for all that had happened, I had good hope of living and dying a sober-minded Protestant. There is a hollowness within, and a flourish without, in that “Holy Church” which tempts me but moderately.

I went on my way through wet, darkening streets, pondering many things. Whatever Romanism may be, there are good Romanists. This man, Emanuel, seemed of the best—touched with superstition, influenced by priestcraft, yet wondrous for fond faith, for pious devotion, for sacrifice of self, for charity unbounded. It remained to see how Rome, by her agents, handled such qualities—whether she cherished them for their own sake and for God’s, or put them out to usury, and made booty of the interest.

“Oubliez les Professeurs.” Madame Beck said it as one tells a child to put aside a toy and be quiet; she meant calm, she meant obedience, she meant me to go on with my portioned days as though nothing had been stirred. She was a wise woman; yet she should not have spoken those words. They were a match struck where tinder lay.

Forget him? They took a singular way to teach me forgetting: they praised him into a figure I could not dismiss. They showed me goodness till it shone; they scrubbed his name clean, as a careful cook scours a kettle till it gives back light; and then they spoke—Père Silas with his grave patience, Madame Beck with her composed emphasis—of the manner of his loving. Before, I had only guessed at what he could be; now they set before me, with a priest’s slow exactness, a history made of fidelity and renunciation, watched beside a tomb through twenty years, as steadily as one watches a hearth through a long winter.

It was not the hollow incense people burn for pleasure. It had the weight of deeds. He had tied up his own wishes, portioned out his earnings, and let what might have been vengeance go, that he might carry a cross for those who were once dear to her who was dead. The story did what stories do when they touch the quick: it altered the very air I breathed. The schoolroom smelled the same—chalk, ink, the faint starch of washed linen—but my mind would not keep its former flavour; it had taken a new seasoning, sharp and persistent.

As for Justine Marie, I knew her without seeing her. There were girls like her in Madame Beck’s classes: pale as watered milk, slow, kind by habit rather than ardour—safe, neutral, good enough, and never splendid. If angels’ wings had been fastened on such shoulders, I knew what poet-fancy had glued them there; if a halo had been painted above that forehead, I knew in whose ardent eyes the circle had been lit.

Was I to tremble before a dead nun? Was the image of pale, vowed, buried womanhood to rise and stand for ever between us? And what of those charities that drank up his money? What of that heart, sworn to a kind of virginity?

Madame Beck—Père Silas—you should not have laid those questions in my way. They became, at once, the knotted riddle, the obstacle like a barred door, and also the spur. For a week, night and day, I fell asleep upon them and woke to them. There was no answer in the whole world except where one small, dark man moved about—sat, stood, lectured—under the odd head-piece of a bonnet-grec, and within the poor girth of an ink-stained paletôt, with that look of quick temper and quicker feeling.

After the Rue des Mages, I wanted to see him again. It seemed that, now I had been forced to imagine what lay behind his choler, his face must read differently; I longed to trace there the mark of that old devotedness, the half-knightly, half-saintly courage Père Silas had sketched. They had made him my Christian hero; and I wanted—simply, strongly—to look at him under that name.

Opportunity did not linger. The next day my new impressions were tried, and in a fashion neither heroic nor tender, but lively enough, and humiliating.

About three o’clock, the first class had settled to its usual stillness. Madame Beck herself was teaching—orderly, lucid, competent; her voice, even, trained, went on like the measured beat of a kitchen clock that keeps all labour in rule. Released from responsibility by her presence, I sat bent over my desk drawing—copying, line by line, the elaborate patience of an engraving. I had then a practical notion of art: to imitate until one had produced a Chinese finical facsimile of steel or mezzotint. It was tedious, and I was pleased; the labour soothed me as kneading soothes—press, fold, press again—till the mind grows quiet in the rhythm.

Then, without warning, peace was cleft by the wild inburst of a paletôt.

My pencils, my copy, my careful little progress—crushed together in a fist—vanished from before me. I myself was shaken out of my chair as if some excited cook, impatient for spice, had shaken a withered nutmeg from a box: one sharp jerk and out it drops. Chair and desk were seized—one under each sleeve—and carried away; in a breath I, with my poor furniture, was transported into the grand salle, that vast room used for dancing and chorus lessons. There we were set down with an emphasis that seemed to forbid the smallest hope of retreat.

When my wits came back in broken pieces, I saw, before me, two men—gentlemen, no doubt. One dark, one fair; one stiff, half-military, in a braided surtout; the other with the careless air of student or artist. Both wore moustache, whiskers, imperial—an abundant forest of hair about faces that looked cold through it. Near them stood M. Emanuel, apart, as if refusing fellowship; his countenance was all choler, and he held out his hand with that tribune gesture of his, as if he stood in a court.

“Mademoiselle,” said he, “your business is to prove to these gentlemen that I am no liar. You will answer, to the best of your ability, such questions as they shall put. You will also write on such theme as they shall select. In their eyes, it appears, I hold the position of an unprincipled impostor. I write essays; and, with deliberate forgery, sign to them my pupils’ names, and boast of them as their work. You will disprove this charge.”

Grand ciel! It was the show-trial I had so long evaded, dropped on my head like thunder. These two braided, sneering personages were none other than the college professors—Messieurs Boissec and Rochemorte: pedants, sceptics, fops, with that commercial air of men who think knowledge a stamped coin and would test it with their teeth. It seemed M. Paul had been rash enough to exhibit something I had written—something he had never praised, never even mentioned to me, and which I had supposed forgotten. The essay was not remarkable in itself; it only looked so beside the average work of foreign school-girls. In an English school it might have passed like plain bread on a crowded table: eaten, perhaps approved, and never spoken of again. These two had questioned its genuineness, insinuated a cheat; and I was dragged forward to witness to truth, while they took their pleasure in the torture of examination.

They began with classics. A dead blank. They went to French history. I hardly knew Mérovée from Pharamond. They tried me in various ‘ologies; I could only shake my head and say, always, “Je n’en sais rien.”

After a pause, expressive enough, they ventured on matters of general information—subjects I did know, on which I had reflected. I felt ideas surge up, quick as water in a rising well; but when I tried to draw them out, there were no words. My tongue, which could have served me in solitude, failed me in public. Whether I could not speak or would not, I scarcely knew. My nerves were wrong, and my humour crossed; I stood like a cook with hands full of ingredients and no fire to set them to—everything ready, nothing possible.

I heard the braided-surtout professor whisper to the other, “Est-elle donc idiote?”

“Yes,” I thought, “for such as you, an idiot she is, and will always be.”

But I suffered; I suffered sharply. I saw the damp gather on M. Paul’s brow. In his eye there was reproach—passionate, and sad. He would not believe that I was wholly without that prompt, popular cleverness which makes answers leap; he thought I could, if I would. I could not make him understand that my thoughts, like herbs gathered fresh, needed time to be washed, sorted, and cut; they would not at a word be thrown into boiling speech.

At last, to release us all, I stammered, “Gentlemen, you had better let me go; you will get no good of me; as you say, I am an idiot.”

I wish I could have spoken with calm dignity, or that I had held my tongue. My voice betrayed me—thin, shaken. I saw the judges cast on M. Emanuel a look of triumph, and I heard the tremor of my own words; then, choking, I burst into tears. They were not the tears of grief, but of anger. Had I been a man and strong, I would have challenged that pair where they stood. I would rather have been scourged than thus made to show emotion.

The incapables! Could they not see, in that composition they called a forgery, the crude hand of a novice? The theme had been classical—new to me. When M. Paul dictated it, I heard it for the first time. I had no seasoned store laid by; no knowledge planted in spring, grown in summer, harvested in autumn, and stored through winter. Whatever I needed, I must go out and gather—wild herbs from hedge and bank—bring them home fresh and green, shred them hurriedly into the pot, and hope that the flavour would serve. I read books, searched facts, built a skeleton out of dry bones, and then clothed it, and tried to breathe into it life. The research was anxious; my inward repugnance to flaw or falsity made me cautious; but the material was not mellow in me. These men mistook the patient cookery of a hungry mind for the ease of a rich scholar.

They would not let me go. I must write before them. I sat down; dipped my pen into ink with a shaking hand; looked at the white paper through eyes half blinded with tears. One of the judges began to apologize in that mincing voice that pretends pity while it enjoys power.

“Nous agissons dans l’intérêt de la vérité. Nous ne voulons pas vous blesser.”

Scorn steadied me as salt steadies a trembling hand.

“Dictate, Monsieur,” I answered.

Rochemorte named the theme: “Human Justice.”

Human Justice! A cold abstraction, blank as an empty dish, giving off no savour, suggesting nothing. There stood M. Emanuel—sad as Saul, stern as Joab—while his accusers triumphed in their moustaches and their braid.

I looked at the two faces, and in looking, memory—sharp as the scent of smoke that suddenly recalls a forgotten hearth—darted its light. Those visages, peering out of their forest of hair, were the same I had seen, projected in full gaslight behind the pillars of a portico on the night of my desolate arrival in Villette. These—these were the heroes who had chased a friendless foreigner beyond her strength, hunted her breathless through a quarter of the town.

“Pious mentors,” I thought. “Pure guides for youth. If ‘Human Justice’ were what she ought to be, you would scarce sit where you sit.”

The idea once seized, I fell to work. “Human Justice” no longer stood as a pale phrase: she rushed before me in a new guise—a red, random beldame, arms akimbo, squatting in her own house, a den of confusion. Servants called to her for orders she would not give; beggars waited at her door, starving, unnoticed; children—sick, quarrelsome—crawled about her feet and yelled in her ears for cure, sympathy, redress. She cared for none of them. She had her warm seat by the fire, her short black pipe, her bottle of Mrs. Sweeny’s soothing syrup. She smoked, she sipped, she enjoyed her little paradise; and when the cries of the suffering pierced her ears too keenly, she seized poker or hearth-brush and settled the weak, the wronged, the sickly. But if the offender was strong, lively, violent—she only menaced, then plunged her hand into her deep pouch and flung a liberal shower of sugar-plums.

So I sketched “Human Justice,” hurried and keen, and set it before Messieurs Boissec and Rochemorte. M. Emanuel read it over my shoulder. I waited no comment. I curtsied to the trio and withdrew.

After school, M. Paul and I met again. The meeting did not run smooth; there was a crow to pluck. That forced examination lay in my throat like a bone. A crabbed dialogue ended with his calling me “une petite moqueuse et sans-cœur,” and with his stormy departure.

Yet I did not want him to go quite away. I only wished him to feel that such a transport as he had indulged could not be taken without cost. So I was not sorry, soon after, to see him in the garden, working in the berceau. The place had its own domestic hush: the soft scrape of spade in soil, the whisper of leaves, the faint drip from yesterday’s rain still lingering in the trellis. In that gentler sound-world, the mind breathes more healthfully; it is not stunned by the clang and trumpet of male voices in a hall, but soothed by earth and growth.

He approached the glass door; I drew near. We spoke first of the flowers growing round it—common things, but real. By-and-by he laid down his spade; by-and-by he began again, and passed to other subjects, and at last touched what he knew must be spoken.

Conscious that his proceeding had been extravagant, he half apologized; he half regretted the fitfulness of his moods; yet he hinted that allowance should be made.

“But,” said he, “I can hardly expect it at your hands, Miss Lucy; you know neither me, nor my position, nor my history.”

His history. The word fell like a seed into ground ready.

“No, Monsieur,” I said at once. “Of course, as you say, I know neither your history, nor your position, nor your sacrifices, nor any of your sorrows, or trials, or affections, or fidelities. Oh, no! I know nothing about you; you are for me altogether a stranger.”

“Hein?” he murmured, lifting his brows.

“You know, Monsieur, I only see you in classe—stern, dogmatic, hasty, imperious. I only hear of you in town as active and wilful, quick to originate, hasty to lead, but slow to persuade, and hard to bend. A man like you, without ties, can have no attachments; without dependants, no duties. All we, with whom you come in contact, are machines, which you thrust here and there, inconsiderate of their feelings. You seek your recreations in public, by the light of the evening chandelier: this school and yonder college are your workshops, where you fabricate the ware called pupils. I don’t so much as know where you live; it is natural to take it for granted that you have no home, and need none.”

“I am judged,” said he. “Your opinion of me is just what I thought it was. For you I am neither a man nor a Christian. You see me void of affection and religion, unattached by friend or family, unpiloted by principle or faith. It is well, Mademoiselle; such is our reward in this life.”

“You are a philosopher, Monsieur; a cynic philosopher.” My eye went, in spite of me, to his paletôt, and he, seeing, brushed its dim sleeve with a quick hand. “Despising the foibles of humanity—above its luxuries—independent of its comforts.”

“Et vous, Mademoiselle? vous êtes proprette et douillette, et affreusement insensible, par-dessus le marché.”

“But, in short, Monsieur, now I think of it, you must live somewhere? Do tell me where; and what establishment of servants do you keep?”

His under-lip projected with a scornful force, and he broke out—

“Je vis dans un trou! I inhabit a den, Miss—a cavern, where you would not put your dainty nose. Once, with base shame of speaking the whole truth, I talked about my ‘study’ in that college: know now that this ‘study’ is my whole abode; my chamber is there and my drawing-room. As for my ‘establishment of servants’” (he mimicked my voice cruelly) “they number ten; les voilà.”

And he spread his ten fingers close under my eyes.

“I black my boots,” he pursued savagely. “I brush my paletôt.”

“No, Monsieur, it is too plain; you never do that,” I could not help saying.

“Je fais mon lit et mon ménage; I seek my dinner in a restaurant; my supper takes care, of itself; I pass days laborious and loveless; nights long and lonely; I am ferocious, and bearded and monkish; and nothing now living in this world loves me, except some old hearts worn like my own, and some few beings, impoverished, suffering, poor in purse and in spirit, whom the kingdoms of this world own not, but to whom a will and testament not to be disputed has bequeathed the kingdom of heaven.”

“Ah, Monsieur; but I know!”

“What do you know? many things, I verily believe; yet not me, Lucy!”

“I know that you have a pleasant old house in a pleasant old square of the Basse-Ville—why don’t you go and live there?”

“Hein?” Again that mutter, half astonished, half suspicious.

“I liked it much, Monsieur; with the steps ascending to the door, the grey flags in front, the nodding trees behind—real trees, not shrubs—trees dark, high, and of old growth. And the boudoir-oratoire—you should make that room your study; it is so quiet and solemn.”

He eyed me closely; a half-smile and a half-colour crossed him, as if warmth tried to break through the discipline of his face.

“Where did you pick up all that? Who told you?” he asked.

“Nobody told me. Did I dream it, Monsieur, do you think?”

“Can I enter into your visions? Can I guess a woman’s waking thoughts, much less her sleeping fantasies?”

“If I dreamt it, I saw human beings as well as a house. I saw a priest—old, bent, grey—and a domestic, old too and picturesque; and a lady, splendid but strange; her head would scarce reach to my elbow—her magnificence might ransom a duke. She wore a gown bright as lapis-lazuli—a shawl worth a thousand francs; she was decked with ornaments so brilliant I never saw any with such a beautiful sparkle; but her figure looked as if it had been broken in two and bent double. She seemed to have outlived the common years of humanity, and to have attained those which are only labour and sorrow. She was become morose—almost malevolent; yet somebody, it appears, cared for her in her infirmities—somebody forgave her trespasses, hoping to have his trespasses forgiven. They lived together, these three people—the mistress, the chaplain, the servant—all old, all feeble, all sheltered under one kind wing.”

He covered the upper part of his face with his hand, but did not hide his mouth. About it I saw hovering an expression I liked—softened, reluctant, almost grateful.

“I see you have entered into my secrets,” said he, “but how was it done?”

So I told him: the errand to the Rue des Mages, the storm that had detained me, the abruptness of the lady, the kindness of the priest.

“As I sat waiting for the rain to cease,” I said, “Père Silas whiled away the time with a story.”

“A story! What story? Père Silas is no romancist.”

“Shall I tell Monsieur the tale?”

“Yes: begin at the beginning. Let me hear some of Miss Lucy’s French—her best or her worst—I don’t much care which: let us have a good poignée of barbarisms, and a bounteous dose of the insular accent.”

“Monsieur is not going to be gratified by a tale of ambitious proportions, and the spectacle of the narrator sticking fast in the midst. But I will tell him the title—the ‘Priest’s Pupil.’”

“Bah!” he said, and the swarthy flush dyed his cheek again. “The good old father could not have chosen a worse subject; it is his weak point. But what of the ‘Priest’s Pupil?’”

“Oh! many things.”

“You may as well define what things. I mean to know.”

“There was the pupil’s youth, the pupil’s manhood—his avarice, his ingratitude, his implacability, his inconstancy. Such a bad pupil, Monsieur! so thankless, cold-hearted, unchivalrous, unforgiving!”

“Et puis?” he asked, taking a cigar, as if he needed smoke between himself and what he felt.

“Et puis,” I went on, “he underwent calamities which one did not pity—bore them in a spirit one did not admire—endured wrongs for which one felt no sympathy; finally took the unchristian revenge of heaping coals of fire on his adversary’s head.”

“You have not told me all,” he said.

“Nearly all, I think: I have indicated the heads of Père Silas’s chapters.”

“You have forgotten one—that which touched on the pupil’s lack of affection—on his hard, cold, monkish heart.”

“True; I remember now. Père Silas did say that his vocation was almost that of a priest—that his life was considered consecrated.”

“By what bonds or duties?”

“By the ties of the past and the charities of the present.”

“You have, then, the whole situation?”

“I have now told Monsieur all that was told me.”

Some minutes passed, meditative, while the garden made its small sounds—leaf against leaf, the far muffled murmur of the city beyond the walls, and nearer, our own breathing, which, when one listens, has its separate language, untranslatable into any word.

Then he said, very quietly, yet with an emphasis that compelled truth:

“Now, Mademoiselle Lucy, look at me, and with that truth which I believe you never knowingly violate, answer me one question. Raise your eyes; rest them on mine; have no hesitation; fear not to trust me—I am a man to be trusted.”

I raised my eyes.

“Knowing me thoroughly now—all my antecedents, all my responsibilities—having long known my faults, can you and I still be friends?”

“If Monsieur wants a friend in me,” I answered, “I shall be glad to have a friend in him.”

“But a close friend I mean—intimate and real—kindred in all but blood. Will Miss Lucy be the sister of a very poor, fettered, burdened, encumbered man?”

Words failed me; yet I suppose I answered. He took my hand, and my hand found comfort in the shelter of his. His friendship did not feel like a brittle thing—some delicate sugar-work that breaks under a touch; it came upon me, at once, with the imagined support of rock.

“When I talk of friendship,” he repeated, “I mean true friendship.”

I could hardly believe such earnest words had been spoken to me, that such an anxious kindness looked from his eyes. If he truly wished my confidence and would truly give me his, it seemed life could offer nothing beyond. In a moment I felt made rich—made safe—substantially happy, as one feels when, after long fare of thin bread, a warm dish is set down, honest and sustaining. To fix the reality, I asked:

“Is Monsieur quite serious? Does he really think he needs me, and can take an interest in me as a sister?”

“Surely, surely,” said he. “A lonely man like me, who has no sister, must be but too glad to find in some woman’s heart a sister’s pure affection.”

“And dare I rely on Monsieur’s regard? Dare I speak to him when I am so inclined?”

“My little sister must make her own experiments,” he said. “I will give no promises. She must tease and try her wayward brother till she has drilled him into what she wishes. After all, he is no inductile material in some hands.”

While he spoke, the tone of his voice, the altered light of his eye, gave me a pleasure I had not known before. I envied no girl her lover, no bride her bridegroom, no wife her husband. I was content with this—my voluntary, self-offering friend. If he would prove reliable—and he looked reliable—what beyond his friendship could I ever covet? Yet, even in content, a fear stirred, as it will when one has once been waked from happiness like from a dream: if all should melt again—if this, too, should prove but a vision, gone with the changing of the air?

Besides Madame Beck’s mother, there was yet another power with a voice sharp enough to cut through any covenant M. Paul and I might fancy we had made. We lived beneath a vigilance that never slept. Rome—silent as incense, patient as stone—watched her son through the mystic lattice where I had once knelt, and whither he drew near month by month: the sliding panel of the confessional.

Yes, he had long been my friend; yes, he had shown, more than once, a certain partiality in feeling. Yet I had hungered to hear him say it plainly, warmly, with that earnestness which makes a word into a vow. I had liked his modest hesitations, his tender deference, his trust that longed to rest and seemed grateful when it was taught how. He had called me “sister.” It was well. He might call me what he pleased, so long as he confided in me. I would be his sister on one condition only—that he should not, one day, invite me to fill that relation towards some future wife of his; and as he stood tacitly vowed to celibacy, the snare appeared unlikely.

Most of the succeeding night I went over our evening’s interview as one might keep returning to a remembered flavour—first sweet, then strange, then troubling, yet impossible to set aside. I watched for morning; I listened for the bell; I rose, dressed, and found prayers and breakfast intolerably slow. All the hours loitered until the lesson of literature, and my wish was simple: to understand what this new, named alliance meant; to see how brotherly he would be when we met again; to test what sisterhood lay in me; to discover whether I could summon a sister’s courage, and he a brother’s frankness.

He came; and life, made perversely, will not match expectation.

All that day he never accosted me. His lesson was given more quietly than usual, more mildly, and with a gravity that chilled. He was fatherly to his pupils; he was not brotherly to me. I expected, before he left the classe, a smile—at least a word. There was neither. For my portion fell one nod—hurried, shy.

“This distance,” I argued, “is accidental; involuntary; patience, and it will pass.” It did not pass. It continued for days; it increased. I smothered surprise and swallowed whatever else began to rise.

Well might I have asked, when he offered fraternity, “Dare I rely on you?” Well might he, knowing himself, have withheld a pledge. True, he had bade me make my own experiments—to tease and try him. Vain injunction! Privilege nominal and unattainable. Some women could have used it. Nothing in my instincts placed me amongst that brave band. Left alone, I was passive; repulsed, I withdrew; forgotten—my lips could not shape a reminder, nor my eyes dart one. Somewhere there had been an error in my reckoning; time must disclose it.

The customary evening came when he was to give me a lesson. One evening in seven he had long bestowed on me—generously, steadily—devoting it to the week’s review and the week to come. On such occasions my schoolroom was anywhere: wherever the pupils and teachers were, or close to them; often in the large second division, where, when the day-pupils were gone and the boarders sat in a knot near the surveillante’s estrade, one could choose a quiet nook and pretend the world had narrowed into paper and ink.

At the proper hour I gathered my books and papers, pen and ink, and went to the large division.

In classe there was no one, and it lay in cool, deep shadow; but through the open double doors the carré was visible—full of pupils and full of light. The westering sun blushed over hall and figures; its ruddy warmth fused wall-colours and the variegated dresses into a single glow, like fruit steeping in syrup. The girls were seated, working or studying; in the midst stood M. Emanuel, speaking good-humouredly to a teacher. His dark paletôt and jetty hair caught crimson reflections; his Spanish face, when he turned it, answered the sun’s kiss with an animated smile. I took my place at a desk and set my hands in order upon my things as if order could steady a heart.

Near the windows the orange-trees and other plants—bright with bloom—had basked all day and now asked water. He had a taste for gardening; he liked to tend and foster living green things. I had long thought that the slow labour among shrubs—with spade or watering-pot—quieted his nerves, as kneading quiets a cook’s impatience: the same hands that could be quick with chalk and rod could also be exact and gentle with root and leaf. Now he looked to the orange-trees, the geraniums, the gorgeous cactuses, and revived them with the refreshment their drought required. His lips at the same time held his precious cigar—the first necessity and prime luxury of his life; its blue wreaths curled among the blossoms in the warm evening light, spoiling, for me, the sweetness with a sharp, foreign bitterness.

He spoke no more to pupils or mistresses, but gave endearing words to a small, spanieless thing—if one may coin a word—which nominally belonged to the house, but in truth owned him for master. She was delicate, silky, loving, lovable, trotting at his side, looking up into his face with an attachment that needed no language. Whenever he dropped his bonnet-grec or handkerchief—sometimes in play—she crouched beside it like a tiny lion guarding a flag.

There were many plants; and as the amateur gardener fetched water from the well with his own active hands, his work spun on. The great school-clock ticked. Another hour struck. The carré and its youthful circle lost the illusion of sunset. Day drooped. My lesson, I perceived, must to-night be short. But the orange-trees, cacti, camellias—all were served now. Was it my turn?

Alas! in the garden there were more favourites: rose-bushes, choice flowers. Little Sylvie’s glad bark and whine followed the receding paletôt down the alleys. I put up some of my books; I should not want them all. I sat and waited, feeling twilight creep like cold water along the floor of my patience.

Sylvie emerged again, frisking—heralding the returning paletôt. The watering-pot was set by the well; its duty done. How glad I was! Monsieur washed his hands in a little stone bowl. There was scarcely time now for any lesson; soon the prayer-bell must ring. Still—we should meet; he would speak; I might read in his eyes the riddle of that shyness which had turned into distance.

His ablutions done, he stood, slowly re-arranging his cuffs, looking at the horn of a young moon set pale in an opal sky, glimmering faint on Jean Baptiste’s oriel. Sylvie watched his contemplative stillness and could not bear it; she whined, jumped, tried to break the hush. He looked down.

“Petite exigeante,” said he; “you must not be forgotten one moment, it seems.”

He lifted her, sauntered across the court within a yard of the windows near which I sat—sauntered lingeringly, fondling the little creature in his bosom, calling her tender names in a tender voice. On the front-door steps he turned; again he looked at the moon, at the grey cathedral, over spires and roofs fading into a blue sea of night-mist. He tasted the sweet breath of dusk and noted the folded bloom of the garden. Then, suddenly, he looked round; a keen beam from his eye rased the white façade of the classes, swept the long line of windows. I think he bowed; if he did, I had no time to return it. In a moment he was gone; the moonlit threshold lay pale and shadowless before the closed door.

I gathered up the unused heap from my desk and carried it back to its place in the third classe. The prayer-bell rang; I obeyed.

The morrow would not restore him to the Rue Fossette: it was his college day. I taught; I endured the intermediate hours; I saw evening coming and braced myself for its heavy ennui. Whether it was worse to sit with my co-inmates or alone, I had not weighed; I took, by nature, the latter. If comfort existed, no heart or head in this house could yield it; only under the lid of my desk might it lodge—nestling between leaves, clinging to a pencil-point, the nib of a pen, or tinting the black pool in the ink-glass. With a heavy heart I opened the desk; with a weary hand I turned its contents.

One by one, well-accustomed books—volumes sewn in familiar covers—came out and went back. They had no charm; they could not comfort. Then: something new—a pamphlet in lilac. I had not seen it before; and I had arranged my desk this very day, this very afternoon. It must have been slipped in within the last hour, while we were at dinner.

I opened it. What was it? What did it mean to say to me?

It was neither tale nor poem, neither essay nor history. It did not sing, relate, or discuss. It preached—and persuaded.

Small as it was, it had its own spell: its voice—honeyed, unctuous—bound attention at once. It preached Romanism; it persuaded to conversion. Here were no thunders, no blasting breath of displeasure. The Protestant was to turn Papist, not so much from dread of the heretic’s hell, as from longing for the comfort, indulgence, tenderness Holy Church offered. She threaten? She coerce? Oh dear no; she only wished to guide and win.

It was milk for babes; the mild effluence of a mother’s love towards her youngest—addressed to those whose head is reached through the heart. It appealed not to intellect; it sought to win the affectionate through their affections, the sympathising through their sympathies. St. Vincent de Paul gathering orphans about him could not have spoken more sweetly.

One inducement to apostasy was held out with particular care: the Catholic who had lost dear friends could enjoy the solace of praying them out of purgatory. The writer did not touch the firmer peace of those whose belief dispenses with purgatory altogether; but I thought of it, and, on the whole, preferred that doctrine as the most consolatory. The little book amused rather than displeased me. It was canting, sentimental, shallow; yet something in its soft, coaxing art cheered my gloom and made me smile—as if I watched the gambols of an unlicked wolf-cub muffled in fleece and practising the bleat of a lamb. It reminded me of certain Methodist tracts read in childhood—flavoured with the same seasoning of excitation to fanaticism. He that wrote it was perhaps no bad man; and though trained cunning—system’s cloven hoof—showed itself perpetually, I paused before accusing him of insincerity. His judgment, however, wanted surgical props: it was rickety.

I smiled over this dose of maternal tenderness coming from the ruddy old lady of the Seven Hills; smiled, too, at my own disinclination—almost disability—to meet these melting favours. I glanced at the title-page and read: “Père Silas.” A fly-leaf bore, in small but clear pencil characters well known to me: “From P. C. D. E. to L—y.” When I saw that, I laughed—but not in my former spirit. I was revived.

A mortal bewilderment cleared from head and sight. The Sphinx-riddle was answered. The conjunction of those two names—Père Silas and Paul Emanuel—gave the key. The penitent had been with his director; permitted to withhold nothing; suffered to keep no corner of his heart sacred to God and himself. Our late interview had been drawn from him; he had avowed the covenant of fraternity, spoken of his adopted sister. How could such a covenant be sanctioned? Fraternal communion with a heretic! I seemed to hear Père Silas annulling the pact, warning of perils, entreating, enjoining reserve—nay, commanding, by the authority of his office and by the memory of all M. Emanuel held most dear, the enforcement of that new system whose frost had pierced to the marrow of my bones.

These are not pleasant conjectures. Yet, by comparison, they were welcome: the vision of a ghostly troubler hovering in the background was as nothing beside the fear of spontaneous change arising in M. Paul himself.

At this distance I cannot be sure how much these conjectures were self-suggested, and how far they were confirmed from another quarter. Help was not wanting.
