This evening brought no bright sunset. West and east were one cloud; no summer night-mist, blue yet rose-tinged, softened the distance. A clammy fog from the marshes crept grey round Villette. Rain had drizzled all afternoon and still fell—fast, quiet, persistent—ticking on leaves and window-frames with a sound that made the house feel hollow. It was no weather for rambling under dripping trees; so I started to hear Sylvie’s sudden bark in the garden—her bark of welcome. Surely she was not accompanied; yet that glad, quick bark was never given save in homage to one presence.

Through the glass door and arching berceau I commanded the deep vista of the allée défendue. Thither rushed Sylvie, glistening through gloom like a white guelder-rose. She ran to and fro, whining, springing, harassing little birds among the bushes. I watched five minutes; no fulfilment followed. I returned to my books; her bark ceased.

Again I looked up. She stood not many yards distant, wagging her feathery tail as fast as muscle could work, watching intently the operations of a spade plied fast by an indefatigable hand. There was M. Emanuel, bent over the soil, digging in wet mould among rain-laden shrubs, working hard—as if his day’s pittance were still to earn by literal sweat.

In that sign I read a ruffled mood. He would dig thus in frozen snow on the coldest winter day when urged inwardly by painful emotion—nervous excitation or sad self-reproach. He would dig by the hour, brow knit, teeth set, never lifting his head, never opening his lips.

Sylvie watched until tired. Then, scampering devious—snuffing and sniffing everywhere—she discovered me in classe. Instantly she flew barking at the panes, urging me forth to share either her pleasure or her master’s toil; she had seen me walk that alley with M. Paul and doubtless judged it my duty to join him now, wet as it was.

She made such a bustle that at last he looked up and, of course, perceived why and at whom she barked. He whistled to call her off; she barked louder—bent on having the glass door opened. Tired, I suppose, of her importunity, he threw down his spade, approached, and pushed the door ajar.

Sylvie burst in, sprang to my lap, and with paws at my neck—and little nose and tongue somewhat too busy about my face, mouth, and eyes—flourished her tail over the desk and scattered books and papers far and wide. The small animal’s warm breath smelled of wet garden earth and the faint sour of rain; her coat held the damp as herbs hold dew.

M. Emanuel advanced to still the clamour and repair the wreck. He gathered up the books; he captured Sylvie and stowed her away under his paletôt, where she nestled quiet as a mouse, her head just peeping forth. She was tiny and had the prettiest innocent face, silkiest long ears, and the finest dark eyes. I never saw her without thinking of Paulina de Bassompierre; forgive the association—it would occur.

He petted and patted her. The endearments were natural; she invited affection by beauty and vivid life.

While caressing the little dog, his eye roved over the papers just replaced. It settled on the religious tract. His lips moved; he half checked an impulse to speak—as if a promise of silence tied him and his nature strained against it. If he had vowed never to address me more, his better self broke the vow; with a second effort he spoke.

“You have not yet read the brochure, I presume? It is not sufficiently inviting?”

I replied that I had read it.

He waited, as if wishing me to offer an opinion unasked. Unasked, however, I was in no mood to make concessions or advances. If any were demanded, let the docile pupil of Père Silas make them—not I. His eye rested on me gently; in the blue ray there was mildness, solicitude, a shade of pathos; meanings composite and contrasted—reproach melting into remorse. He seemed, for an instant, to hunger for some emotion in me. I could not show it. Another minute, and I might have betrayed confusion; so I bethought myself of quill-pens and drew some from my desk, beginning soberly to mend them.

I knew this would turn his mood. He never liked to see me mend pens. My knife was always dull; my hand unskilful; I hacked and chipped. On this occasion I cut my finger—half on purpose. I wanted to restore him to his natural state, to set him at ease, to hear him chide.

“Maladroit!” he cried at last. “She will make mincemeat of her hands.”

He put Sylvie down, making her lie quiet beside his bonnet-grec; then, taking my pens and penknife, he began to slice, nib, and point with the accuracy and quickness of a machine. The sound of the blade on quill—small, crisp, orderly—was like the calming crackle of a clean fire.

“Did you like the little book?” he asked.

Suppressing a yawn, I said I hardly knew.

“Had it moved you?”

“I think it made me a little sleepy.”

After a pause—impatient, incredulous—he broke out that it was of no use taking that tone with him. Bad as I was—and he would be sorry to name all my faults at a breath—God and nature had given me “trop de sensibilité et de sympathie” not to be profoundly affected by an appeal so touching.

“Indeed!” I answered, rousing myself. “I was not affected at all—not a whit.”

And in proof I drew from my pocket a perfectly dry handkerchief, still clean and folded.

Then came a string of strictures, more piquant than polite. I listened with zest. After two days of unnatural silence, it was better than music to hear M. Paul haranguing again in his old fashion. While he spoke, I solaced myself—and Sylvie—with the contents of a bonbonnière which his gifts kept supplied with chocolate comfits. The sweet cracked under the tooth; the cocoa rose warm and bitter after the sugar; it grounded me in my body when my mind would have flown into worry. It pleased him to see even such a trifle from his hand duly appreciated. He looked at me and the spaniel as we shared the spoil; then he put up his penknife. Touching my hand with the bundle of newly cut quills, he said:

“Dites donc, petite sœur—speak frankly—what have you thought of me during the last two days?”

I took no notice. The purport made my eyes fill. I bent over Sylvie, caressing her with an attention too steady to be casual. He leaned over the desk, bending towards me.

“I called myself your brother,” he said. “I hardly know what I am—brother—friend—I cannot tell. I know I think of you—I feel I wish you well—but I must check myself; you are to be feared. My best friends point out danger, and whisper caution.”

“You do right to listen to your friends. By all means be cautious.”

“It is your religion—your strange, self-reliant, invulnerable creed—whose influence seems to clothe you in, I know not what, unblessed panoply. You are good—Père Silas calls you good, and loves you—but your terrible, proud, earnest Protestantism—there is the danger. It expresses itself by your eye at times; and again it gives you certain tones and certain gestures that make my flesh creep. You are not demonstrative, and yet just now—when you handled that tract—my God! I thought Lucifer smiled.”

“Certainly I don’t respect that tract—what then?”

“Not respect that tract? But it is the pure essence of faith, love, charity! I thought it would touch you: in its gentleness, I trusted it could not fail. I laid it in your desk with a prayer. I must indeed be a sinner: Heaven will not hear the petitions that come warmest from my heart. You scorn my little offering. Oh, cela me fait mal!”

“Monsieur, I don’t scorn it—at least, not as your gift. Monsieur, sit down; listen to me. I am not a heathen. I am not hard-hearted. I am not unchristian. I am not dangerous, as they tell you. I would not trouble your faith. You believe in God and Christ and the Bible, and so do I.”

“But do you believe in the Bible? Do you receive Revelation? What limits are there to the wild, careless daring of your country and sect? Père Silas dropped dark hints.”

By dint of persuasion I made him half-define these hints; they amounted to crafty slanders—little poisoned needles, slipped into language as one might slip a bitter herb into a dish meant to please. That night M. Paul and I talked seriously and closely. He pleaded; he argued. I could not argue—a fortunate incapacity; it needed but a triumphant, logical opposition to effect what his director wished. But I could talk in my own way—the way he was used to—whose meanderings he could follow, whose pauses he could fill, whose strange stammerings were strange to him no longer. At ease with him, I defended my creed as I could; I lulled, in some degree, his prejudices.

He was not satisfied when he went away; hardly was he appeased. Yet he was made thoroughly to feel that Protestants were not necessarily the irreverent pagans his director had insinuated; he was brought to comprehend something of their manner of honouring the Light, the Life, the Word; and he was enabled, partly, to perceive that while their veneration for things venerable was not like that cultivated in his Church, it had its own—perhaps deeper—power, its own more solemn awe.

It was all very well for Paulina to declare—small, composed, and resolute—that no more letters should pass between her and Dr. Bretton until her father had sanctioned the intercourse. She said it as one might say a grace before meat: quietly, with a pure intention. But Dr. Bretton could not live within a league of the Hôtel Crécy and not find his feet—by some inevitable, homing instinct—upon its threshold. Both lovers meant, at first, to be distant. They kept their intention so far as open demonstration went; but the heart has its own geography, and in feeling they soon drew very near.

In his presence there came upon her a kind of sunshine that altered even the air of rooms. There are lights that do not glitter; they warm. Under that warmth, whatever in Graham was best sought Paulina as naturally as a hungry man seeks bread. All his faculties—always active, always eager for nutriment—found, with her, a sustenance he had not guessed he wanted: not the showy confection of praise, but the slow, wholesome fare that strengthens. He had admired before—Miss Fanshawe, for instance—with an admiration where the intellect played little part; but now his whole intellect, and his highest tastes, were brought into question, awakened, exercised, fed.

Paulina did not, that I could see, set herself to lure him into books, as a clever hostess leads a guest from one dish to another. She had no scheme of improving him; she thought him perfect, as a devout parishioner thinks the altar perfect. It was Graham who, almost by accident at first, spoke of some volume he had been reading; and when her answer chimed with his—clear, exact, and richly toned—he talked on. He talked more and better, perhaps, than he had ever talked before. She listened with delight and responded with animation; and in each successive answer he seemed to hear a music growing finer to his sense—an accent suggestive and persuasive that opened for him a scarce-known storehouse within, and showed him, with a kind of sweet severity, unsuspected power in his own mind and—what was better—latent goodness in his heart.

Each liked the way in which the other spoke: voice, diction, expression pleased. Each relished, keenly, the flavour of the other’s wit; they met each other’s meaning with strange quickness, their thoughts matching like carefully chosen pearls laid side by side upon a thread. Graham had mirth by nature, a wholesome, abundant cheer; Paulina, unstimulated, inclined to be thoughtful and pensive. But now she seemed merry as a lark. In her lover’s genial presence she glanced like some soft, glad light. I wondered to see how beautiful she grew in her happiness. That gentle ice of hers—that reserve she had depended on—where was it now? Graham would not long bear it. He brought with him a generous influence that thawed the timid, self-imposed restriction, and made her—without losing modesty—free.

Now were the old Bretton days talked over. At first, perhaps, brokenly, with a smiling diffidence; then with opening candour and a confidence that grew as it was justified. Graham had made for himself a better opportunity than any I could have offered. He had earned independence of the collateral help that disobliging Lucy had refused. All his reminiscences of “little Polly” found their proper expression in his own pleasant tones, by his own kind and handsome lips.

More than once, when we were alone, Paulina would tell me how curious and wonderful it was to discover the richness and accuracy of his memory in this matter—how, while he looked at her, recollections seemed suddenly quickened in his mind, as though his gaze struck a hidden spring.

He reminded her that she had once gathered his head in her arms, caressed his leonine graces, and cried out, “Graham, I do like you!” He told her how she would set a footstool beside him and climb, by its aid, to his knee. At this day, he said, he could recall the sensation of her little hands smoothing his cheek, or burying themselves in his thick mane. He remembered the touch of her small forefinger placed half tremblingly, half curiously, in the cleft in his chin; the lisp, the look with which she named it “a pretty dimple;” then the way she would seek his eyes and question why they pierced so, telling him he had a “nice, strange face; far nicer, far stranger, than either his mamma or Lucy Snowe.”

“Child as I was,” Paulina said to me, “I wonder how I dared be so venturous. To me he seems now all sacred. His locks are inaccessible; and, Lucy, I feel a sort of fear when I look at his firm marble chin—at his straight Greek features. Women are called beautiful, Lucy; he is not like a woman, therefore I suppose he is not beautiful; but what is he, then? Do other people see him with my eyes? Do you admire him?”

“I’ll tell you what I do, Paulina,” I answered once, when her questions pressed hard. “I never see him. I looked at him twice or thrice about a year ago, before he recognised me; and then I shut my eyes. And if he were to cross their balls twelve times between each day’s sunset and sunrise, except from memory, I should hardly know what shape had gone by.”

“Lucy, what do you mean?” she asked, under her breath.

“I mean that I value vision, and dread being struck stone blind.”

It was best to answer her strongly at once—to silence for ever those tender, passionate confidences which left her lips sweet honey and sometimes dropped in my ear molten lead. To me, she commented no more on her lover’s beauty.

Yet speak of him she would. Sometimes shyly, in brief, quiet phrases; sometimes with a tenderness of cadence and music of voice exquisite in itself, but which chafed me at times miserably. Then I know I gave her stern looks and words. But cloudless happiness had dazzled her native clear sight; she only thought Lucy—fitful.

“Spartan girl! Proud Lucy!” she would say, smiling at me. “Graham says you are the most peculiar, capricious little woman he knows; but yet you are excellent: we both think so.”

“You both think you know not what,” I replied. “Have the goodness to make me as little the subject of your mutual talk and thoughts as possible. I have my sort of life apart from yours.”

“But ours, Lucy, is a beautiful life—or it will be; and you shall share it.”

“I shall share no man’s or woman’s life in this world, as you understand sharing. I think I have one friend of my own, but am not sure; and till I am sure, I live solitary.”

“But solitude is sadness.”

“Yes; it is sadness. Life, however, has worse than that. Deeper than melancholy lies heart-break.”

“Lucy, I wonder if anybody will ever comprehend you altogether.”

There is, in lovers, a certain infatuation of egotism: they will have a witness of their happiness, cost that witness what it may. Paulina had forbidden letters; yet Dr. Bretton wrote. She had resolved against correspondence; yet she answered—were it only to chide. She showed me these letters; with something of the spoiled child’s wilfulness, and of the heiress’s imperiousness, she made me read them.

As I read Graham’s, I scarcely wondered at her exaction or understood her pride. They were fine letters—manly and fond, modest and gallant. Hers must have appeared to him beautiful. They had not been written to show her talents; still less, I think, to express her love. On the contrary, it appeared she had proposed to herself the task of hiding that feeling and bridling her lover’s ardour. But how could such letters serve such a purpose? Graham was become dear as her life; he drew her like a powerful magnet. For her there was influence unspeakable in all he uttered, wrote, thought, or looked. With this unconfessed confession her letters glowed; it kindled them from greeting to adieu.

“I wish papa knew; I do wish papa knew!” began now to be her anxious murmur. “I wish, and yet I fear. I can hardly keep Graham back from telling him. There is nothing I long for more than to have this affair settled—to speak out candidly; and yet I dread the crisis. I know—I am certain—papa will be angry at first. I fear he will dislike me almost. It will seem to him an untoward business; it will be a surprise, a shock. I can hardly foresee its whole effect on him.”

The fact was, her father—long calm—was beginning to be a little stirred. Long blind on one point, an importunate light was beginning to trespass on his eye.

To her he said nothing; but when she was not looking at him—or perhaps not thinking of him—I saw him gaze and meditate on her, as an old man watches the last sheaf in his field and knows other hands may soon gather it.

One evening—Paulina was in her dressing-room, writing, I believe, to Graham; she had left me in the library, reading—M. de Bassompierre came in. He sat down. I was about to withdraw; he requested me to remain—gently, yet with a manner that showed he wished compliance. He had taken his seat near the window at some distance from me. He opened a desk and took out what looked like a memorandum-book. Of this book he studied a certain entry for several minutes, as though the date were something he had lost and must recover with effort.

“Miss Snowe,” said he, laying it down, “do you know my little girl’s age?”

“About eighteen, is it not, sir?”

“It seems so. This old pocket-book tells me she was born on the fifth of May, in the year 18—, eighteen years ago. It is strange; I had lost the just reckoning. I thought of her as twelve—fourteen—an indefinite date; but she seemed a child.”

“She is about eighteen,” I repeated. “She is grown up; she will be no taller.”

“My little jewel!” he said, in a tone which penetrated like some of his daughter’s accents.

He sat very thoughtful.

“Sir, don’t grieve,” I ventured; for I knew his feelings, utterly unspoken as they were.

“She is the only pearl I have,” he said; “and now others will find out that she is pure and of price: they will covet her.”

I made no answer. Dr. Bretton had dined with us that day. He had shone both in converse and in looks. Something—under the stimulus of a high hope—had unfolded in his whole manner which compelled attention. I think he had purposed, on that day, to indicate the origin of his endeavours and the aim of his ambition. Slow in remarking, M. de Bassompierre was logical in reasoning. Having once seized the thread, it guided him through a long labyrinth.

“Where is she?” he asked.

“She is up-stairs.”

“What is she doing?”

“She is writing.”

“She writes, does she? Does she receive letters?”

“None but such as she can show me. And—sir—she—they have long wanted to consult you.”

“Pshaw! They don’t think of me—an old father. I am in the way.”

“Ah, M. de Bassompierre—not so—that can’t be. But Paulina must speak for herself; and Dr. Bretton, too, must be his own advocate.”

“It is a little late. Matters are advanced, it seems.”

“Sir, till you approve, nothing is done—only they love each other.”

“Only!” he echoed.

Invested by fate with the part of confidante and mediator, I was obliged to go on. “Hundreds of times has Dr. Bretton been on the point of appealing to you, sir; but, with all his high courage, he fears you mortally.”

“He may well—he may well fear me. He has touched the best thing I have. Had he but let her alone, she would have remained a child for years yet. So. Are they engaged?”

“They could not become engaged without your permission.”

“It is well for you, Miss Snowe, to talk and think with that propriety which always characterizes you; but this matter is a grief to me. My little girl was all I had: I have no more daughters and no son. Bretton might as well have looked elsewhere; there are scores of rich and pretty women who would not, I daresay, dislike him. He has looks, and conduct, and connection. Would nothing serve him but my Polly?”

“If he had never seen your Polly, others might and would have pleased him—your niece, Miss Fanshawe, for instance.”

“Ah! I would have given him Ginevra with all my heart; but Polly!—I can’t let him have her. No—I can’t. He is not her equal,” he affirmed, rather gruffly. “In what particular is he her match? They talk of fortune! I am not an avaricious or interested man, but the world thinks of these things—and Polly will be rich.”

“Yes, that is known,” said I. “All Villette knows her as an heiress.”

“Do they talk of my little girl in that light?”

“They do, sir.”

He fell into deep thought. I ventured to say, “Would you, sir, think any one Paulina’s match? Would you prefer any other to Dr. Bretton? Do you think higher rank or more wealth would make much difference in your feelings towards a future son-in-law?”

“You touch me there,” said he.

“Look at the aristocracy of Villette—you would not like them, sir?”

“I should not—never a duc, baron, or vicomte of the lot.”

“I am told many of these persons think about her, sir,” I went on, gaining courage on finding I met attention rather than repulse. “Other suitors will come, therefore, if Dr. Bretton is refused. Wherever you go, I suppose, aspirants will not be wanting. Independent of heiress-ship, it appears to me that Paulina charms most of those who see her.”

“Does she? How? My little girl is not thought a beauty.”

“Sir, Miss de Bassompierre is very beautiful.”

“Nonsense!—begging your pardon, Miss Snowe, but I think you are too partial. I like Polly: I like all her ways and all her looks—but then I am her father; and even I never thought about beauty. She is amusing, fairy-like, interesting to me. You must be mistaken in supposing her handsome.”

“She attracts, sir: she would attract without the advantages of your wealth and position.”

“My wealth and position! Are these any bait to Graham? If I thought so—”

“Dr. Bretton knows these points perfectly, as you may be sure, M. de Bassompierre, and values them as any gentleman would—as you would yourself under the same circumstances—but they are not his baits. He loves your daughter very much; he feels her finest qualities, and they influence him worthily.”

“What! has my little pet ‘fine qualities?’”

“Ah, sir! did you observe her that evening when so many men of eminence and learning dined here?”

“I certainly was rather struck and surprised with her manner that day; its womanliness made me smile.”

“And did you see those accomplished Frenchmen gather round her in the drawing-room?”

“I did; but I thought it was by way of relaxation—as one might amuse one’s self with a pretty infant.”

“Sir, she demeaned herself with distinction; and I heard the French gentlemen say she was ‘pétrie d’esprit et de graces.’ Dr. Bretton thought the same.”

“She is a good, dear child, that is certain; and I do believe she has some character. When I think of it, I was once ill; Polly nursed me; they thought I should die. She, I recollect, grew at once stronger and tenderer as I grew worse in health. And as I recovered—what a sunbeam she was in my sick-room! Yes; she played about my chair as noiselessly and as cheerful as light. And now she is sought in marriage! I don’t want to part with her,” said he, and he groaned.

“You have known Dr. and Mrs. Bretton so long,” I suggested. “It would be less like separation to give her to him than to another.”

He reflected rather gloomily.

“True. I have long known Louisa Bretton,” he murmured. “She and I are indeed old, old friends; a sweet, kind girl she was when she was young. You talk of beauty, Miss Snowe! She was handsome, if you will—tall, straight, and blooming—not the mere child or elf my Polly seems to me. At eighteen, Louisa had a carriage and stature fit for a princess. She is a comely and a good woman now. The lad is like her; I have always thought so, and favoured and wished him well. Now he repays me by this robbery! My little treasure used to love her old father dearly and truly. It is all over now, doubtless—I am an incumbrance.”

The door opened. His “little treasure” came in.

She was dressed, so to speak, in evening beauty. That animation which sometimes comes with the close of day warmed her eye and cheek; a tinge of summer crimson heightened her complexion; her curls fell full and long on her lily neck; her white dress suited the heat of June. Thinking me alone, she had brought in her hand the letter just written—folded but unsealed. I was to read it. When she saw her father, her tripping step faltered, paused; the colour in her cheek flowed rosy over her whole face.

“Polly,” said M. de Bassompierre, in a low voice, with a grave smile, “do you blush at seeing papa? That is something new.”

“I don’t blush—I never do blush,” she affirmed, while another eddy from the heart sent up its scarlet. “But I thought you were in the dining-room, and I wanted Lucy.”

“You thought I was with John Graham Bretton, I suppose? But he has just been called out: he will be back soon, Polly. He can post your letter for you; it will save Matthieu a ‘course,’ as he calls it.”

“I don’t post letters,” she said rather pettishly.

“What do you do with them, then?—come here and tell me.”

Both her mind and gesture hesitated a second—as if to ask, Shall I come?—but she approached.

“How long is it since you became a letter-writer, Polly? It only seems yesterday when you were at your pot-hooks, labouring away absolutely with both hands at the pen.”

“Papa, they are not letters to send to the post in your letter-bag; they are only notes, which I give now and then into the person’s hands, just to satisfy.”

“The person! That means Miss Snowe, I suppose?”

“No, papa—not Lucy.”

“Who then? Perhaps Mrs. Bretton?”

“No, papa—not Mrs. Bretton.”

“Who, then, my little daughter? Tell papa the truth.”

“Oh, papa!” she cried with earnestness, “I will—I will tell you the truth—all the truth; I am glad to tell you—glad, though I tremble.”

She did tremble. Growing excitement, kindling feeling, and also gathering courage shook her.

“I hate to hide my actions from you, papa. I fear you and love you above everything but God. Read the letter; look at the address.”

She laid it on his knee. He took it up and read it through; his hand shaking, his eyes glistening meantime.

He re-folded it and viewed the writer with a strange, tender, mournful amaze.

“Can she write so—the little thing that stood at my knee but yesterday? Can she feel so?”

“Papa, is it wrong? Does it pain you?”

“There is nothing wrong in it, my innocent little Mary; but it pains me.”

“But, papa, listen! You shall not be pained by me. I would give up everything—almost,” she corrected herself; “I would die rather than make you unhappy; that would be too wicked!”

She shuddered.

“Does the letter not please you? Must it not go? Must it be torn? It shall, for your sake, if you order it.”

“I order nothing.”

“Order something, papa; express your wish; only don’t hurt, don’t grieve Graham. I cannot, cannot bear that. I love you, papa; but I love Graham too—because—because—it is impossible to help it.”

“This splendid Graham is a young scamp, Polly—that is my present notion of him. It will surprise you to hear that, for my part, I do not love him one whit. Ah! years ago I saw something in that lad’s eye I never quite fathomed—something his mother has not—a depth which warned a man not to wade into that stream too far; now, suddenly, I find myself taken over the crown of the head.”

“Papa, you don’t—you have not fallen in; you are safe on the bank. You can do as you please; your power is despotic. You can shut me up in a convent and break Graham’s heart to-morrow, if you choose to be so cruel. Now, autocrat—now czar—will you do this?”

“Off with him to Siberia, red whiskers and all,” he said. “I don’t like him, Polly, and I wonder that you should.”

“Papa,” said she, “do you know you are very naughty? I never saw you look so disagreeable, so unjust, so almost vindictive before. There is an expression in your face which does not belong to you.”

“Off with him!” pursued Mr. Home—who certainly did look sorely crossed and annoyed, even a little bitter. “But, I suppose, if he went, Polly would pack a bundle and run after him; her heart is fairly won—won, and weaned from her old father.”

“Papa, I say it is naughty—it is decidedly wrong—to talk in that way. I am not weaned from you, and no human being and no mortal influence can wean me.”

“Be married, Polly! Espouse the red whiskers. Cease to be a daughter; go and be a wife!”

“Red whiskers! I wonder what you mean, papa. You should take care of prejudice. You sometimes say to me that all the Scotch, your countrymen, are the victims of prejudice. It is proved now, I think, when no distinction is to be made between red and deep nut-brown.”

“Leave the prejudiced old Scotchman; go away.”

She stood looking at him a minute. She wanted to show firmness, superiority to taunts. Knowing her father’s character, guessing his few foibles, she had expected some such scene; it did not take her by surprise, and she desired to let it pass with dignity, reliant upon reaction. Her dignity stood her in no stead. Suddenly her soul melted in her eyes; she fell on his neck.

“I won’t leave you, papa; I’ll never leave you. I won’t pain you! I’ll never pain you!” was her cry.

“My lamb! my treasure!” murmured the loving though rugged sire. He said no more for the moment; indeed, those two words were hoarse.

The room was now darkening. I heard a movement, a step without. Thinking it might be a servant coming with candles, I gently opened the door to prevent intrusion. In the ante-room stood no servant. A tall gentleman was placing his hat on the table, drawing off his gloves slowly—lingering, waiting, it seemed to me. He called me neither by sign nor word; yet his eye said, “Lucy, come here.” And I went.

Over his face a smile flowed while he looked down on me. No temper save his own would have expressed by a smile the sort of agitation which now fevered him.

“M. de Bassompierre is there—is he not?” he inquired, pointing to the library.

“Yes.”

“He noticed me at dinner? He understood me?”

“Yes, Graham.”

“I am brought up for judgment, then—and so is she?”

“Mr. Home is talking to his daughter.”

“Ha! These are sharp moments, Lucy!”

He was quite stirred up. His young hand trembled. A vital suspense now held, now hurried, his breath; in all this trouble his smile never faded.

“Is he very angry, Lucy?”

“She is very faithful, Graham.”

“What will be done unto me?”

“Graham, your star must be fortunate.”

“Must it? Kind prophet! So cheered, I should be a faint heart indeed to quail. I think I find all women faithful, Lucy. I ought to love them, and I do. My mother is good; she is divine; and you are true as steel. Are you not?”

“Yes, Graham.”

“Then give me thy hand, my little god-sister: it is a friendly little hand to me, and always has been. And now for the great venture. God be with the right. Lucy, say Amen!”

He turned and waited till I said “Amen!”—which I did to please him. The old charm, in doing as he bid me, came back. I wished him success; and successful I knew he would be. He was born victor, as some are born vanquished.

“Follow me!” he said; and I followed him into Mr. Home’s presence.

“Sir,” he asked, “what is my sentence?”

The father looked at him; the daughter kept her face hid.

“Well, Bretton,” said Mr. Home, “you have given me the usual reward of hospitality. I entertained you; you have taken my best. I was always glad to see you; you were glad to see the one precious thing I had. You spoke me fair; and meantime—I will not say you robbed me—but I am bereaved; and what I have lost, you, it seems, have won.”

“Sir, I cannot repent.”

“Repent! Not you! You triumph, no doubt. John Graham, you descended partly from a Highlander and a chief, and there is a trace of the Celt in all you look, speak, and think. You have his cunning and his charm. The red—(well then, Polly, the fair) hair, the tongue of guile, and brain of wile, are all come down by inheritance.”

But it is not thus with all. What then? Let His will be done—done it will be, whether we bow to it with meekness or break ourselves against it. There are powers that urge the world forward, visible and veiled; they have their charge, and they fulfil it. If proof must be written, it will be written—if need be—in fire and in blood. We read that scripture in the scorched seams of the earth, in the red sap of the vine, in the quick pulse under our own ribs when joy is plucked away. Sufferer, do not faint at the heat of such evidence. Wayfarer, tighten what is loose; look up; go on. Pilgrims and fellow-mourners, keep close company. For most of us the road runs dark through this wilderness; let our step be even, our burden borne, our banner held. For staff—promise. For present help—providence. For end—home. Let us run that we may obtain; let us endure; let us keep the faith, and trust the issue: “Art thou not from everlasting, mine Holy One? WE SHALL NOT DIE!”

On a Thursday morning we were gathered, as usual, in classe for the literature lesson. July lay warm upon the city; the glass-door stood ajar, and a fresh current came in from the garden, lifting the leaves of the plants trained round the lintel. They bent and waved as if they had gossip to whisper—tidings drawn from outside air and carried, green-tongued, into our close routine. Even the soundscape of the room was softened: the scratch of pens, the small rustle of paper tied with ribbon, the quiet breathing of girls who had been schooled into stillness. The compositions lay ready—white bundles, neatly bound—waiting for the quick hand and quicker eye of the Professor who always came with a kind of keen weather about him: swiftness, fire, a step that struck sparks from the boards.

The hour came. We expected that step.

Instead, the door opened, and it was Madame Beck who entered—quietly, cautiously, with a light shawl drawn round her shoulders as if she had stepped in from a chill, though the day was hot.

She went straight to M. Emanuel’s desk and stood before it. Her voice, when she spoke, was low and firm; her gaze fixed, not roaming.

“This morning there will be no lesson of literature.”

She paused—two minutes, perhaps—long enough for the room to feel the gap where a voice should have been. Then she went on.

“It is probable the lessons will be suspended for a week. I shall require at least that space of time to find an efficient substitute for M. Emanuel. Meanwhile, it shall be our study to fill the blanks usefully.

“Your Professor, ladies, intends, if possible, duly to take leave of you. At the present moment he has not leisure for that ceremony. He is preparing for a long voyage. A very sudden and urgent summons of duty calls him to a great distance. He has decided to leave Europe for an indefinite time. Perhaps he may tell you more himself. Ladies, instead of the usual lesson with M. Emanuel, you will, this morning, read English with Mademoiselle Lucy.”

She bowed—courteous as a rule-book—drew her shawl closer, and passed out.

Silence dropped at once, heavy as a coverlet. Then the room stirred. A murmur travelled desk to desk; one or two began to cry. It spread—not deep, not noble, but contagious. Sobs broke the air in little gusts, and with each gust discipline loosened as if a hand had slipped off the reins.

Habit and duty—those old, hard-fed servants—rose in me quickly. I stood, took the book, fixed my voice into its usual tone, and brought the room to order. I made the English reading long, close, exacting. I held them to it, as one holds dough to the board and kneads because to stop would be to think; because the hands can bear what the heart cannot. I remember a sharp impatience at the sobbing girls. Their distress had that hysteric edge which frays rather than moves; I told them so, with a dryness that half ridiculed. I was severe; I could not endure the tears, that gasping sound. It seemed to call something in me to the surface which I dared not let rise.

One weak-minded, low-spirited pupil persisted when the rest had been shamed into quiet. Relentless necessity made me accost her so that she conquered the convulsion. She might have had a right to hate me—except that, when school was over and the others had gone, I ordered her to stay, and when we were alone I did what I had never done to one among them before: I pressed her to my heart and kissed her cheek. The poor child broke again at that tenderness, more bitterly; and I—afraid of what it unlocked—sent her away at once.

All that day I stuffed with occupation, minute upon minute, as one stuffs a jar to the brim so there shall be no room for air. I would have sat up all night if the light might have been kept burning; but night was a treacherous larder, cold and empty, and it left bad effects, preparing me ill for the next day’s ordeal—gossip.

The news went through the school like a spoon tapping the rim of every cup. At first there was a little reserve—surprise wears modesty for an hour—but it soon fell off. Teachers, pupils, the very servants mouthed the name of “Emanuel.” That he—whose connection with the school was as old as its beginning—should withdraw thus suddenly, startled all. They talked until talk itself yielded a kind of intelligence: by the third day it was said he would sail in a week; then that he was bound for the West Indies.

I watched Madame Beck for disproof or confirmation as one watches a hearth for a draught: the slightest change of flame, the faintest shift of smoke. But her face was smooth, unperturbed; her eyes disclosed nothing but commonplace.

“This secession is an immense loss to me,” she said publicly—in classe, at dinner, audibly to Zélie St. Pierre. “I do not know how I shall fill up the vacancy. I am so used to my kinsman; he has become my right hand. What shall I do without him? I opposed the step, but M. Paul has convinced me it is his duty.”

Why was it his duty? The question burnt in me like spice on a raw tongue. I had impulses—sudden, fierce—to catch her sleeve as she passed, to grip and say: Stop. Finish the matter. Why must he go into banishment? But Madame always addressed some other teacher; she never looked at me, never seemed conscious that I could care.

The week wore on. Nothing more was said of his coming to take leave; no one seemed anxious; no one questioned whether he would appear; they gossiped incessantly, and never touched that point which was life to me. As for Madame—she could see him when she chose, speak to him as she pleased. What should she care if he came into the schoolroom or not?

The week was consumed. We were told his destination—“Basseterre in Guadaloupe.” The business, they added, concerned a friend’s interests, not his own. I thought as much.

“Basseterre in Guadaloupe.” I slept little, and whenever sleep caught me it was only to be broken by those words, pronounced over my pillow or written across the dark in jagged characters of red and violet light. Even the night had its acoustics: the house creaked, a distant cart rumbled on the stones, a shutter clicked, and every small sound seemed to arrange itself into that one refrain—Basseterre, Guadaloupe—as if the city were learning a foreign word and repeating it without understanding, wearing it down with use.

For what I felt there was no help; how could I help feeling? He had been kind of late—hourly kinder. It was a month since we had settled the theological difference; in that time no quarrel had risen. Our peace was not that cold daughter of divorce; we had not lived aloof. He came oftener; he talked with me more; he spent hours with me with temper soothed, with eye content, with manner home-like and mild. Kind subjects had grown between us. He inquired into my plans of life; I told him. The school project pleased him; he made me repeat it more than once, though he called it an Alnaschar dream—my basket of eggs carried too gaily. Still, the mutual understanding was settling, fixing; feelings of union and hope made themselves profoundly felt; affection, deep esteem, dawning trust—each had fastened its bond.

What quiet lessons I had then! No more taunts on my “intellect,” no more threats of grating public exhibitions. In place of the jealous gibe, and the more jealous half-passionate eulogy, came mute, indulgent help; fond guidance; a tender forbearance which forgave but never praised. Sometimes he would sit many minutes and not speak at all; and when dusk or duty brought separation, he would leave with words like, “Il est doux, le repos! Il est précieux le calme bonheur!”

One evening—not ten days ago—he joined me while I walked in my alley. He took my hand. I looked up; I thought he meant to arrest my attention.

“Bonne petite amie,” he said softly; “douce consolatrice!”

Through his touch and with his words came a new feeling—a strange thought finding its path. Could he be becoming more than friend or brother? Did his look say something beyond fraternity? His eyes had more to speak; his hand drew me forward; his lips stirred to interpret—

No. Not now. The twilight alley was broken by an interruption, double and ominous: two forms faced us, bodeful as a shadow and its echo—a woman’s and a priest’s—Madame Beck and Père Silas.

The Père’s aspect I shall never forget. At first it wore a kind of Jean-Jacques sensibility, stirred by the affection he had surprised; then instantly it darkened with the jaundice of ecclesiastical jealousy. He spoke to me with unction; he looked on his pupil with sternness. As for Madame Beck, she saw nothing—nothing; though her kinsman, in her presence, retained the hand of the heretic foreigner, clasping it close and fast.

After that, when the sudden announcement of departure struck, my first reception of it was disbelief. Only repetition—many tongues, many minds accepting—forced it upon me. Of that week of suspense—blank, burning days—bringing no word from him, no explanation: I remember, but cannot describe its passage.

The last day broke. Now he would visit us; now he would come and speak farewell—or vanish mute and be seen no more.

That alternative seemed to exist in the mind of not one creature in the school. All rose at the usual hour; all breakfasted; all went to ordinary duty with the same trained phlegm. The house moved on its rails, tame, oiled, oblivious. I could scarcely breathe in an atmosphere so stagnant, so smothering. Would no one lend me a voice? Had no one a wish, a word, a prayer to which I could say Amen?

They had been unanimous in demanding trifles—a holiday, a treat, a remitted lesson. They would not now band together to besiege Madame Beck and insist upon a last interview with a master who had been loved—loved as they could love. Yet what is the love of the multitude? A sound that rises loud and falls at once, like applause in a theatre—ready for the next act.

I knew where he lived; the distance was scarcely a stone’s throw. Had he been in the next room—unsummoned, I could make no use of my knowledge. To follow, seek, remind, recall—there I had no faculty. M. Emanuel might have passed within reach of my arm; had he passed silent and unnoticing, silent and stirless I should have let him go.

Morning wasted; afternoon came. I thought all was over. My heart trembled; my blood ran troubled; I was sick, scarcely knowing how to keep at my post. Yet the little world plodded on indifferent—jocund even. Those very pupils who, seven days before, had sobbed at the news, now seemed to have forgotten both it and their emotion. Habit had swallowed feeling, as a loud street swallows birdsong.

A little before five—dismissal hour—Madame Beck sent for me to her chamber to read and translate an English letter, and to write the answer. Before we began, I noticed she closed both doors softly; she even shut and fastened the casement, though the day was hot and she generally required free air. Why this precaution? Suspicion leapt in me—keen, almost fierce. Did she wish to exclude sound? What sound?

I listened as I had never listened—like a wolf in winter, nose in the snow, sensing prey; hearing far off the traveller’s tramp. Yet I could listen and write. Halfway through the letter I heard a tread in the vestibule; my pen stopped. No bell had rung: Rosine—acting, no doubt, by orders—had anticipated such awakening. Madame saw me halt. She coughed, made a bustle, spoke louder. The tread passed towards the classes.

“Proceed,” she said.

My hand was fettered, my ear enchained, my thoughts carried off captive.

The classes were in another building; a hall parted them from the dwelling-house. Even so, through distance and partition, I heard a sudden stir of numbers—one whole division rising at once.

“They are putting away work,” said Madame.

It was indeed the hour; but why that sudden hush—that instant quelling of the tumult, as if a presence had entered and drawn a net over sound?

“Wait, Madame—I will see what it is.”

I set down my pen and left her. Or tried. She would not be left: powerless to detain, she rose and followed—close as my shadow. On the last stair I turned.

“Are you coming, too?” I asked.

“Yes,” she said, meeting my glance with a peculiar look—clouded, yet resolute.

We went on, not together, but she in my steps.

He had come.

Entering the first classe, I saw him—once more the form most familiar, standing in the centre of the girls. I do not doubt they had tried to keep him away; but he was there.

The pupils stood in a semicircle. He passed round, giving farewells—pressing each hand, touching each cheek with his lips. Foreign custom permitted it at such parting; yet it seemed to me, in that room, a ceremony solemn enough to last a lifetime. The air carried small sounds—skirts brushing, benches shifting, the soft kiss of farewell—each one a little cut.

Madame Beck’s closeness goaded me. I felt her breath at my neck and shoulder; I shrank under it, fevered. He was approaching; the semicircle was almost travelled. He came to the last pupil; he turned—

And Madame stepped before me.

Suddenly she magnified her proportions, amplified her drapery. She eclipsed me. I was hidden. She knew my weakness; she could calculate the moral paralysis that could strike me in crisis—the total default of self-assertion. She hastened to her kinsman, broke upon him volubly, mastered his attention, hurried him to the glass-door opening on the garden.

I think he looked round. Had I caught his eye, courage might have rushed in to aid feeling; there might have been a charge—perhaps a rescue. But already the room was confusion: the semicircle broke into groups; my figure was lost among thirty more conspicuous. Madame had her will. She got him away, and he had not seen me; he thought me absent.

Five o’clock struck. The loud dismissal-bell rang—metallic, tyrannous, cutting through every other sound. The school separated; the room emptied.

There is, in my memory, an entire darkness over some minutes I then passed alone—a grief inexpressible over a loss unendurable. What should I do—what should I do—when all my life’s hope was torn by the roots out of my riven, outraged heart?

What I should have done I know not, when a little child—the least in the school—broke with simplicity into the silent centre of that inward storm.

“Mademoiselle,” lisped the thin treble voice, “I am to give you that. M. Paul said I was to seek you all over the house, from the grenier to the cellar, and when I found you, to give you that.”

And the child delivered a note—the little dove dropped on my knee, its olive leaf plucked off. There was neither address nor name, only these words:

“It was not my intention to take leave of you when I said good-by to the rest, but I hoped to see you in classe. I was disappointed. The interview is deferred. Be ready for me. Ere I sail, I must see you at leisure, and speak with you at length. Be ready; my moments are numbered, and, just now, monopolized; besides, I have a private business on hand which I will not share with any, nor communicate—even to you.—PAUL.”

“Be ready.” Then it must be this evening. Was he not to go on the morrow? Of that I was certain: I had seen the date of his vessel’s departure advertised. I would be ready. Yet could that meeting be achieved? Time was short; the schemers watchful, active, hostile. The way of access looked strait as a gully, deep as a chasm; Apollyon straddled across it, breathing flames. Could my Greatheart overcome? Could my guide reach me?

Who could tell? Yet courage began—small, stubborn—to feed on that note, as one feeds on a crust when there is nothing else. It seemed to me I felt a pulse of his heart beating true to the whole throb of mine.

I waited my champion, and Apollyon came trailing his Hell behind him. If Eternity held torment, I do not think its form would be fiery rack, nor its nature despair. I think it would be suspense. Imagine a day among those days which never dawn and will not set: an angel entering Hades—standing, shining, smiling—delivering a prophecy of conditional pardon, kindling a doubtful hope of bliss, not now, but at an hour unlooked for; revealing, in his own grandeur, the height and compass of promise; then, towering, becoming a star and vanishing into his own Heaven. His legacy would be suspense—a worse boon than despair.

All that evening I waited—trusting the dove-sent olive leaf, yet, in the midst of trust, terribly fearing. Fear pressed heavy, cold and peculiar; I knew it for the partner of a rarely-belied presentiment. The first hours dragged. In spirit I clung to the flying skirts of the last; yet they passed like drift-cloud, like wrack scudding before storm.

Day burned away like a Yule-log—hot, consuming, red at the heart. Its crimson close perished; and I was left bent among cool blue shades, over pale ashen gleams of night.

Prayers were over; it was bed-time. My co-inmates retired. I still remained in the gloomy first classe, forgetting—disregarding—rules I had never broken before.

How long I paced I cannot tell. I must have been afoot many hours. Mechanically I moved benches and desks, making myself a path down the length of the room, as if I were laying out a narrow furrow in hard ground and must keep to it because it was the only way forward. There I walked; and when I was certain the whole household lay abed, quite out of hearing—there, at last, I wept. Reliant on Night, confiding in Solitude, I kept my tears sealed no longer. They heaved my heart; they tore their way. In this house, what grief could be sacred?

Soon after eleven—a very late hour in the Rue Fossette—the door unclosed quietly, not stealthily. A lamp’s flame invaded the moonlight, and Madame Beck entered with the same composed air as if she came on ordinary business at an ordinary season. She did not address me at once. She went to her desk, took her keys, and seemed to seek something. She loitered over the feigned search long—too long. She was calm—too calm; my mood could scarcely endure the pretence. Two hours earlier I had left behind my wonted respects and fears; now no yoke could be borne, no curb obeyed.

“It is more than time for retirement,” she said. “The rule of the house has already been transgressed too long.”

I gave no answer; I did not check my walk. When she came in my way I put her out of it.

“Let me persuade you to calm, Meess; let me lead you to your chamber,” she said, trying to speak softly.

“No,” I answered. “Neither you nor another shall persuade or lead me.”

“Your bed shall be warmed. Goton is sitting up still. She shall make you comfortable: she shall give you a sedative.”

“Madame,” I broke out, “you are a sensualist. Under all your serenity, your peace, your decorum, you are an undenied sensualist. Make your own bed warm and soft; take sedatives and meats, and drinks spiced and sweet, as much as you will. If you have any sorrow or disappointment—and, perhaps, you have—nay, I know you have—seek your own palliatives in your own chosen resources. Leave me. Leave me, I say!”

“I must send another to watch you, Meess: I must send Goton.”

“I forbid it. Let me alone. Keep your hand off me, and my life, and my troubles. Oh, Madame! in your hand there is both chill and poison. You envenom and you paralyze.”

“What have I done, Meess? You must not marry Paul. He cannot marry.”

“Dog in the manger!” I said—for I knew she secretly wanted him, had always wanted him. She called him “insupportable,” railed at him for a “dévot;” she did not love, but she wanted to marry that she might bind him to her interest. Deep into some of her secrets I had entered—how, I know not; by intuition, by inspiration that came I know not whence. Living with her had taught me too that, unless with an inferior, she must ever be a rival. She was my rival, heart and soul—secretly, under the smoothest bearing—unknown to all save her and myself.

For two minutes I stood over her, feeling that the whole woman was in my power; for in certain moods—stimulated states like that instant—her habitual disguise, mask and domino, became to me a mere network full of holes. I saw underneath a being heartless, self-indulgent, ignoble.

She retreated quietly. Meek and self-possessed—though uneasy—she said that if I would not be persuaded to rest, she must reluctantly leave me. Which she did at once, perhaps more glad to get away than I was to see her vanish.

This was the sole flash—truth-extorting—rencontre that ever passed between me and Madame Beck. It was never repeated. It did not change her manner to me; I do not know that she revenged it; I do not know that she hated me the worse for my fell candour. I think she bucklered herself with her strong mind’s secret philosophy and resolved to forget what it irked her to remember. To the end of our mutual lives there occurred no repetition of, no allusion to, that fiery passage.

Fascinated as by a basilisk with three heads, I could not quit that little knot of people under the interlaced trees. The very soil seemed to have taken my feet for its own; the branches made a roof that promised concealment; the night—soft, warm, and close as a pantry after baking—whispered protection. An officious lamp, like a serving-boy with a single hurried errand, flashed one pale beam, showed me an obscure seat that would pass for empty, and then was gone again into the gaiety.

Let me set down, briefly and plainly, what—during this dark fortnight—I had been gathering in silence from Rumour, concerning the origin and object of M. Emanuel’s departure. It was no romance of devotion, no pilgrimage of pure zeal; it began in Mammon and ended in Interest, and between alpha and omega there was nothing but bargaining.

Madame Walravens—hideous as some squat idol set up in a niche and fed with incense—had, nevertheless, the weight of an idol among her worshippers. Once she had been rich—very rich; and though at present she did not command her money, she was likely, one day, to be rich again. In Basseterre, in Guadaloupe, she held a large estate: a dowry brought sixty years ago, sequestered since her husband’s failure; now, they supposed it clear of claims, and, if only tended by a competent agent, capable of being made, in a few years, largely productive. They talked of it as people talk of a fruit-tree long neglected, whose branches—if pruned, grafted, and defended from blight—might yet be made to load the basket: a plantation of canes, perhaps; coffee; whatever in that fierce sun turns labour into sweetness and coin.

Père Silas took an interest in the prospective harvest for the sake of the Church, whereof Magloire Walravens was a devout daughter. Madame Beck—distantly related to the hunchback, and knowing her without family of her own—had long brooded, like a thrifty housewife over winter stores, on contingencies; harshly treated as she was, she never ceased to court her, for interest’ sake. Priest and directress were thus, for money reasons, equally and sincerely attentive to the nursing of that West Indian estate.

But the distance was great, and the climate hazardous. The competent and upright agent wanted—must be a devoted man: a man who could live without applause, and work without being watched; who could bear fever and solitude, and still keep accounts like a conscience. Just such a man had Madame Walravens retained for twenty years in her service, blighting his life, and then living on him like an old fungus; such a man Père Silas had trained, taught, and bound to him by gratitude, habit, and belief. Such a man Madame Beck knew, and could in some measure influence.

“My pupil,” said Père Silas, “if he remains in Europe, runs risk of apostasy, for he has become entangled with a heretic.”

Madame Beck made her private comment also, and cherished in her own breast her secret reason for desiring expatriation. The thing she could not obtain, she desired not another to win: rather would she destroy it.

As to Madame Walravens, she wanted her money and her land, and knew Paul—if he liked—could make the best and faithfullest steward. So the three self-seekers banded and beset the one unselfish. They reasoned, appealed, implored; on his mercy they cast themselves; into his hands they confidingly thrust their interests. They asked but two or three years of devotion—after that, he should live for himself: and one of the number, perhaps, wished that in the meantime he might die.

No living being ever humbly laid advantage at M. Emanuel’s feet, or confidingly put it into his hands, that he spurned the trust or repulsed the repository. What might be his private pain, his inward reluctance to leave Europe—what calculations for his own future—none asked, or knew, or reported. For me, all that was blank. His conferences with his confessor I might guess; the part duty and religion were made to play in the persuasions used, I might conjecture. He was gone, and had made no sign: there my knowledge closed.

With my head bent and my forehead on my hands, I sat amidst grouped trunks and branching brushwood. Whatever talk passed in the circle near me I could have heard, had there been motive. They gossiped about dresses, music, illuminations, the fine night; the very words sounded like the clink of glasses in a room where one is hungry and has no place at table. I listened for one phrase, one crumb dropped from a careless tongue—“It is calm weather for his voyage; the Antigua will sail prosperously.” No such remark fell. Neither the Antigua, nor her course, nor her passenger was named.

Perhaps the light chat scarcely interested old Madame Walravens more than it did me. She appeared restless, turning her head this way and that, looking through the trees and among the crowd, expectant and impatient. “Où sont-ils? Pourquoi ne viennent-ils?” I heard her mutter more than once; and at last, determined to have an answer, she spoke aloud—brief, simple—and it sent a shock through me as if a cold hand had been laid on my neck.

“Messieurs et mesdames,” said she, “où donc est Justine Marie?”

Justine Marie! The dead nun. Where was she? In her grave, Madame Walravens—what can you want with her? You shall go to her, but she shall not come to you. Thus I should have answered, had the response lain with me. But nobody seemed of my mind; nobody started, or frowned, or even looked surprised. The strangest question received the quietest commonplace reply.

“Justine Marie,” said one, “is coming; she is in the kiosk; she will be here presently.”

Out of that question and answer sprang a change in the chat—chat still: desultory, familiar gossip. Hint, allusion, jest, went round; but all so broken, so dependent on persons not named and circumstances not defined, that listen as intently as I would—listen I did now, with a fated interest—I could make out no more than that some scheme was on foot in which this ghostly Justine Marie—dead or alive—was concerned. They seemed grasping at her for some reason; there was talk of a marriage, of a fortune; for whom I could not quite seize—perhaps for Victor Kint, perhaps for Josef Emanuel: both bachelors. Once I thought the raillery fell thickest on a young fair-haired foreigner of the party, called Heinrich Mühler.

Amidst all the badinage, Madame Walravens obtruded from time to time hoarse, cross-grained speeches; her impatience diverted only by an implacable surveillance of Désirée, who could not stir but the old woman menaced her with her staff, as if guarding a dish on a crowded sideboard.

“La voilà!” suddenly cried one of the gentlemen. “Voilà Justine Marie qui arrive!”

That moment was peculiar for me. The painted nun on the panel rose before me; the sad love-story, long whispered in the house like old kitchen legends told over kneading-trough and fire, came back; I saw again the vision of the garret, the apparition of the alley, the strange birth of the berceau. A presentiment of discovery pressed on my heart; a conviction that some disclosure was at hand. And indeed, when imagination once runs riot, where do we stop? The barest winter tree, the humblest hedge-munching animal, a passing cloud, a struggling moonbeam—Fancy will clothe all in spirituality and make a phantom of it.

With solemn force, the expectation of mystery breaking up, I leaned forward and looked.

“She comes!” cried Josef Emanuel.

The circle opened, as if to admit a welcome member. A torch chanced to be carried past; its blaze, aiding the pale moon, lit to perfection the little stage where my dread had been rehearsing its scene. Surely those near me must have felt some portion of my suspense. For me—my life stood still.

It is over. The moment and the nun are come. The crisis and the revelation are passed by.

The flambeau still glares within a yard, held up in a park-keeper’s hand; its long tongue of flame almost licks the figure of the Expected—there—full in my sight. What is she like? What does she wear? How does she look? Who is she?

There are many masks in the park to-night; and as the hour wears late, a feeling of revelry and mystery spreads abroad, so that you would scarcely discredit me, reader, were I to say she is like the nun of the attic, wears black skirts and white head-clothes, looks the resurrection of the flesh, a risen ghost.

All falsities—all figments. We will not deal in that gear. Let us be honest, and cut, as heretofore, from the homely web of truth.

Homely, though, is an ill-chosen word. What I see is not precisely homely. A girl of Villette stands there—a girl fresh from her pensionnat: comely, with the beauty indigenous to this country. She looks well-nourished—fair and fat of flesh, round-cheeked, good-eyed, abundant-haired. She is handsomely dressed. She is not alone; her escort consists of three persons—two elderly, whom she calls “Mon Oncle” and “Ma Tante.” She laughs, chats; good-humoured, buxom, and blooming, she looks at all points the bourgeoise belle.

So much for Justine Marie; so much for ghosts and mystery. Not that the last was solved—this girl certainly was not my nun: what I had seen in garret and garden must have been taller by a span.

We have looked at the city belle; we have cursorily glanced at the respectable old uncle and aunt. Have we a stray glance to give the third member of this company? We ought, reader: he has claims on us; we do not meet him now for the first time.

I clasped my hands very hard; I drew my breath very deep. I held in the cry, devoured the ejaculation, forbade the start. I spoke and stirred no more than a stone; but I knew what I looked on. Through the dimness left in my eyes by many nights’ weeping, I knew him.

They said he was to sail by the Antigua. Madame Beck said so. She lied; or she had uttered what was once truth, and failed to contradict it when it became false. The Antigua was gone—and there stood Paul Emanuel.

Was I glad? A huge load left me, as if some iron pot had been lifted from my breast. Was it a fact to warrant joy? I know not. Ask first what circumstances attended this respite. How far did this delay concern me? Were there not those whom it might touch more nearly?

After all, who might this young girl—this Justine Marie—be? Not a stranger: she was known to me by sight; she visited in the Rue Fossette; she was often at Madame Beck’s Sunday parties. She was a relation of both the Becks and Walravens; she derived her baptismal name from the sainted nun who would have been her aunt had she lived; her patronymic was Sauveur. She was an heiress and an orphan; M. Emanuel her guardian—some said her godfather.

The family junta wished this heiress married to one of their band. Which was it? Vital question—which was it?

I felt very glad now that the drug administered in the sweet draught had filled me with a possession which made bed and chamber intolerable. I have always, through my whole life, liked to penetrate to the real truth; to seek the goddess in her temple and dare the veil. It is not that truth comes without pain—often it strikes like the first knife that opens a too-full loaf, letting out steam and heat that smart the hand; but to see and know the worst is to take from Fear her main advantage.

The Walravens’ party, augmented in numbers, now became very gay. The gentlemen fetched refreshments from the kiosk. All sat down on the turf under the trees; they drank healths and sentiments; they laughed, jested. M. Emanuel underwent some raillery—half good-humoured, half, I thought, malicious—especially on Madame Beck’s part. I soon gathered that his voyage had been deferred of his own will, without the concurrence—nay, against the advice—of his friends. He had let the Antigua go, and had taken his berth in the Paul et Virginie, appointed to sail a fortnight later.

It was his reason for this resolve they teased him to assign, and which he would only vaguely indicate as “the settlement of a little piece of business which he had set his heart upon.” What was this business? Nobody knew. Yes—there was one who seemed at least partly in his confidence: a meaning look passed between him and Justine Marie.

“La petite va m’aider—n’est-ce pas?” said he.

The answer was prompt enough, God knows.

“Mais oui, je vous aiderai de tout mon cœur. Vous ferez de moi tout ce que vous voudrez, mon parrain.”

And this dear “parrain” took her hand and lifted it to his grateful lips.

At that demonstration, I saw the light-complexioned young Teuton, Heinrich Mühler, grow restless, as if the sound of that kiss on fingers were a note that jarred him. He grumbled a few words. M. Emanuel actually laughed in his face; and with the ruthless triumph of the assured conqueror, drew his ward nearer.

M. Emanuel was very joyous that night. He seemed not one whit subdued by the change of scene and action impending. He was the life of the party: a little despotic, perhaps, determined to be chief in mirth as well as in labour, yet proving from moment to moment his right of leadership. His was the wittiest word, the pleasantest anecdote, the frankest laugh. Restlessly active after his manner, he multiplied himself to wait on all; but I saw which was his favourite. I saw at whose feet he lay on the turf; whom he folded carefully from the night air, as one wraps a delicate thing away from damp; whom he tended, watched, and cherished as the apple of his eye.

Still hint and raillery flew thick; and still I gathered that while M. Paul should be absent, working for others, these others—who were not quite ungrateful—would guard for him the treasure he left in Europe. Let him bring them an Indian fortune; they would give him in return a young bride and a rich inheritance. The saintly consecration, the vow of constancy, was forgotten: the blooming and charming Present prevailed over the Past; and at length, his nun was indeed buried.

Thus it must be. The revelation was come. Presentiment had not been mistaken in her impulse; there is a kind of presentiment that never is mistaken. It was I who had for a moment miscalculated: not seeing the true bearing of the oracle, I had thought she muttered of vision, when in truth her prediction touched reality.

I might have paused longer upon what I saw; I might have deliberated ere drawing inferences. Some would have held the premises doubtful, the proofs insufficient; some slow sceptics would have examined incredulously ere accepting the project of a marriage between a poor, unselfish man of forty and his wealthy ward of eighteen. But far from me such shifts and palliatives—far from me such coward fleeing from the swift-footed Fact.

No. I hastened to accept the whole plan. I extended my grasp and took it all in, gathered it to me with a rage of haste, folded it round me as the soldier struck on the field folds his colours about his breast. I invoked Conviction to nail upon me the certainty—abhorred while embraced—to fix it with the strongest spikes. And when the iron had entered well my soul, I stood up, as I thought, renovated.

In my infatuation, I said: “Truth, you are a good mistress to your faithful servants. While a Lie pressed me, how I suffered! Even when Falsehood was sweet—flattering to fancy, warm to feeling—it wasted me with hourly torment. The persuasion that affection was won could not be divorced from dread that, by another turn of the wheel, it might be lost. Truth stripped away Falsehood, Flattery, Expectancy—and here I stand—free.”

Nothing remained but to take my freedom to my chamber, carry it to my bed, and see what I could make of it.

The play was not yet quite played out; I might have waited and watched longer that love-scene under the trees—that sylvan courtship. Had there been nothing of love in the demonstration, my Fancy—generous and creative in this hour—could have modelled for it the sharpest lineaments and given it deepest colour of passion. But I would not look. I had fixed my resolve; I would not violate my nature. And then—something tore me so cruelly under my shawl, something dug into my side like a talon—some vulture, strong in beak and claw—I must be alone to grapple with it. I think I never felt jealousy till now.

It was not like enduring the endearments of Dr. John and Paulina—against which, while I sealed eyes and ears, withdrew my thoughts, my sense of harmony still acknowledged a charm. This was an outrage. The love born of beauty was not mine; I had nothing in common with it: I dared not meddle. But another love—venturing diffidently into life after long acquaintance, furnace-tried by pain, stamped by constancy, consolidated by affection’s pure and durable alloy, submitted by intellect to intellect’s own tests, and finally wrought up, by his own process, to his own unflawed completeness—this Love, that laughed at Passion with his fast frenzies and hot hurried extinction—in this Love I had a vested interest. Whatever tended to its culture or its destruction, I could not view impassibly.

I turned from the group of trees and the merrie companie in its shade. Midnight was long past; the concert was over; crowds thinned. I followed the ebb.

Leaving the radiant park and well-lit Haute-Ville—still well lit, for it seems this was to be a nuit blanche in Villette—I sought the lower quarter. And there, the moonlight—forgotten in the park—flowed in again upon perception. High she rode, calm and stainless. The music and mirth of the fête, the fire and bright hues of lamps, had outdone her for an hour; but now her glory and silence triumphed. The rival lamps were dying. She held her course like a white fate.

How different the soundscape here—the deep peace unvexed by drum and bugle. In the upper town, those brassy mouths had clanged and clamoured as if man could purchase joy by noise; here, the night had its own language: the measured tread of a homeward burgher; the far, soft shutting of a door; the faint wash of one’s own dress against the stones. It seemed to me that this quiet was not mere absence but a kind of health—an acoustic shelter where the mind could hear itself. I thought how easily a city’s true memory is drowned: not by forgetting alone, but by being shouted down; how the native cadence of a street—its old footsteps, its low talk—dies when every festival must be louder than the last, when art is hawked like a fairing and forced to compete with clatter.

These oil-twinkling streets were still. I liked them for their lowliness and peace. Homeward-bound pedestrians passed now and then, making little noise and soon gone. So well I loved Villette under that aspect that I would not willingly have re-entered under a roof—but I was bent on pursuing my strange adventure to a successful close, and regaining my bed in the great dormitory before Madame Beck returned.

Only one street lay between me and the Rue Fossette; as I entered it, for the first time that night, the sound of a carriage tore up the peace. It came very fast. How loud sounded its rattle on the paving—an iron violence that seemed to insult the narrowness and sleep of the place. The street was confined; I kept carefully to the causeway. The carriage thundered past; and what did I see—or fancy I saw—as it rushed by? Surely something white fluttered from that window—surely a hand waved a handkerchief. Was that signal meant for me? Am I known? Who could recognise me? It was not M. de Bassompierre’s carriage, nor Mrs. Bretton’s; and besides, neither the Hôtel Crécy nor the château of La Terrasse lay in that direction. I had no time for conjecture. I must hurry home.

Gaining the Rue Fossette, reaching the pensionnat, all was still. No fiacre had yet arrived with Madame and Désirée. I had left the great door ajar; should I find it thus? Perhaps the wind—or some accident—might have thrown it to with force enough to start the spring-bolt. In that case, hopeless became admission; my adventure must issue in catastrophe.

I lightly pushed the heavy leaf. Would it yield?

Yes. As soundless, as unresisting, as if some propitious genius had waited within upon a sesame-charm. Entering with bated breath, quietly making all fast, mounting the staircase as softly as bare feet in a sickroom, I sought the dormitory and reached my couch.

Ay—I reached it, and drew a free inspiration. The next moment, I almost shrieked—almost, but not quite, thank Heaven.

Throughout the dormitory, throughout the house, reigned the stillness of death. All slept; and in such hush it seemed none dreamed. Stretched on nineteen beds lay nineteen forms, motionless. On mine—the twentieth—nothing ought to have lain: I had left it void, and void should have found it. What then did I see between the half-drawn curtains? What dark usurping shape—supine, long, strange? A robber, crept in through the open street-door, lying there in wait? It looked very black. I think it looked—not human. A wandering dog, perhaps, that had come in from the street and nested there? Would it spring if I approached? Approach I must.

Courage. One step.

My head reeled; for by the faint night-lamp I saw stretched on my bed the old phantom—the NUN.

A cry might have ruined me. Be the spectacle what it might, I could afford neither scream nor swoon. Besides, I was not overcome. Tempered by late incidents, my nerves disdained hysteria. Warm yet from illuminations, music, thronging thousands—lashed up by a new scourge—I defied spectra. Without exclamation I rushed on the haunted couch. Nothing leaped out or stirred; all the movement was mine. So was all the life, the reality, the substance, the force—as my instinct knew.

I tore her up—the incubus. I held her on high—the goblin. I shook her loose—the mystery. Down she fell—down all around me—in shreds and fragments—and I trode upon her.

Here again—behold the branchless tree, the unstabled Rosinante; the film of cloud, the flicker of moonshine. The long nun proved a long bolster dressed in a long black stole, artfully invested with a white veil. The garments, strange as it may seem, were genuine nun’s vestments, disposed by some hand with studied care to deceive the eye in dusk. Whence came these clothes? Who contrived this artifice? These questions still remained.

To the head-bandage was pinned a slip of paper, bearing in pencil these mocking words:

“The nun of the attic bequeaths to Lucy Snowe her wardrobe. She will be seen in the Rue Fossette no more.”

And what—and who—was she that had haunted me? She whom I had actually seen three times? Not a woman of my acquaintance had that stature. She was not of a female height. Not to any man I knew could the machination, for a moment, be attributed.

The morning after that strange Midsummer night came in with a brightness so ordinary it seemed, by its very innocence, to mock the ferment within doors. The sun rose as if to preside over nothing more momentous than the ripening of currants and the steady swelling of peas in their pods. Heat already lived in the brickwork; it would soon draw from it that baked, mineral scent which mingles, in enclosed gardens, with the bruised sweetness of herbs when you brush past them. In the Rue Fossette, however, few noses were lifted to the air, and few ears attended to the sparrows and the thin, contented hiss of leaves. The house listened for other sounds—footsteps where none came, a door that had not creaked, a voice that did not answer when called.

At matins there was a gap—an absence seated like a cold plate at a family table. At breakfast, one coffee-cup remained untouched; the steam from the others rose, thinned, and vanished, and that small fading seemed a sign. When the beds were made, a bolster was found laid lengthwise, capped and night-gowned—an effigy stuffed with linen, a jest or a stratagem left in place of a girl. And when the music-mistress arrived, punctual as the striking of a clock, her pupil—so accomplished, so confident of her own claims on the world—failed, for once, to present herself.

Ginevra Fanshawe had disappeared.

The pensionnat was searched as a kitchen is searched when something is missing that must be found before the dish can be served: drawers pulled open, cupboards sounded, corners hunted, the very air suspected. Up-stairs and down, through dormitory, class-room, corridor—no trace; not so much as a scrap of paper; not a pin dropped in haste. The house’s usual soundscape—bells, lessons, the measured step of routine—was broken into sharp consultations, sudden scurries, the hiss of whispers, and, beneath them, Madame Beck’s breathing, altered from its habitual composure to a quick restraint that betrayed fear.

Never had I seen her so pale. Here was not merely a truant: here was a blow to her interest, her reputation, her command. She demanded to know by what outlet the fugitive had fled. Doors were examined, bolts tried, windows tested; no casement was found unfastened, no pane broken. The building presented itself like a sealed pantry—close, locked, irreproachable. Yet something living had been taken out of it in the night.
