And still, for me, a particular recollection persisted with the steadiness of a flavor that will not be masked once the palate has recognized it. To facilitate a certain enterprise, a certain great door had been drawn softly to its place—closed, yes, but neither bolted nor made secure. Along with this came back the memory of a thundering carriage-and-pair encountered, and that puzzling signal of a waved handkerchief. From these facts, and from others kept to myself, I could draw but one inference.

It was elopement.

I held my certainty for a time, because certainty, when it carries scandal in its hands, is not to be flung down like a loaf on a bare board. Yet Madame Beck’s dismay deepened; and at length I spoke. I alluded to M. de Hamal’s suit; and as I expected, she was perfectly aware of that affair. She had discussed it long since with Mrs. Cholmondeley, and, with that admirable art of shifting weight from her own shoulders, had contrived to lay her responsibility in the business upon that lady.

Now she had recourse to Mrs. Cholmondeley and to M. de Bassompierre. The Hôtel Crécy, we found, was already alive with the news. Ginevra had written to her cousin Paulina—vaguely, coquettishly, yet with enough meaning to set a family ear pricked—to signify hymeneal intentions. Communications had also been received from the de Hamal side. M. de Bassompierre took up the track with the tenacity of a man who cannot endure to be outwitted in his own house; he followed, he hurried, he questioned. He overtook them too late.

In the course of the week the post brought me a note. It explained more than one point—explained, with that airy insolence which is Ginevra’s way of making mischief seem like a dance.

“DEAR OLD TIM (short for Timon),—

“I am off you see—gone like a shot. Alfred and I intended to be married in this way almost from the first; we never meant to be spliced in the humdrum way of other people; Alfred has too much spirit for that, and so have I—Dieu merci!

“Do you know, Alfred, who used to call you ‘the dragon,’ has seen so much of you during the last few months, that he begins to feel quite friendly towards you. He hopes you won’t miss him now that he has gone; he begs to apologize for any little trouble he may have given you. He is afraid he rather inconvenienced you once when he came upon you in the grenier, just as you were reading a letter seemingly of the most special interest; but he could not resist the temptation to give you a start, you appeared so wonderfully taken up with your correspondent. En revanche, he says you once frightened him by rushing in for a dress or a shawl, or some other chiffon, at the moment when he had struck a light, and was going to take a quiet whiff of his cigar, while waiting for me.

“Do you begin to comprehend by this time that M. le Comte de Hamal was the nun of the attic, and that he came to see your humble servant? I will tell you how he managed it. You know he has the entrée of the Athénée, where two or three of his nephews, the sons of his eldest sister, Madame de Melcy, are students. You know the court of the Athénée is on the other side of the high wall bounding your walk, the allée défendue. Alfred can climb as well as he can dance or fence: his amusement was to make the escalade of our pensionnat by mounting, first the wall; then—by the aid of that high tree overspreading the grand berceau, and resting some of its boughs on the roof of the lower buildings of our premises—he managed to scale the first classe and the grand salle. One night, by the way, he fell out of this tree, tore down some of the branches, nearly broke his own neck, and after all, in running away, got a terrible fright, and was nearly caught by two people, Madame Beck and M. Emanuel, he thinks, walking in the alley. From the grande salle the ascent is not difficult to the highest block of building, finishing in the great garret. The skylight, you know, is, day and night, left half open for air; by the skylight he entered. Nearly a year ago I chanced to tell him our legend of the nun; that suggested his romantic idea of the spectral disguise, which I think you must allow he has very cleverly carried out.

“But for the nun’s black gown and white veil, he would have been caught again and again both by you and that tiger-Jesuit, M. Paul. He thinks you both capital ghost-seers, and very brave. What I wonder at is, rather your secretiveness than your courage. How could you endure the visitations of that long spectre, time after time, without crying out, telling everybody, and rousing the whole house and neighbourhood?

“Oh, and how did you like the nun as a bed-fellow? I dressed her up: didn’t I do it well? Did you shriek when you saw her: I should have gone mad; but then you have such nerves!—real iron and bend-leather! I believe you feel nothing. You haven’t the same sensitiveness that a person of my constitution has. You seem to me insensible both to pain and fear and grief. You are a real old Diogenes.

“Well, dear grandmother! and are you not mightily angry at my moonlight flitting and run away match? I assure you it is excellent fun, and I did it partly to spite that minx, Paulina, and that bear, Dr. John: to show them that, with all their airs, I could get married as well as they. M. de Bassompierre was at first in a strange fume with Alfred; he threatened a prosecution for ‘détournement de mineur,’ and I know not what; he was so abominably in earnest, that I found myself forced to do a little bit of the melodramatic—go down on my knees, sob, cry, drench three pocket-handkerchiefs. Of course, ‘mon oncle’ soon gave in; indeed, where was the use of making a fuss? I am married, and that’s all about it. He still says our marriage is not legal, because I am not of age, forsooth! As if that made any difference! I am just as much married as if I were a hundred. However, we are to be married again, and I am to have a trousseau, and Mrs. Cholmondeley is going to superintend it; and there are some hopes that M. de Bassompierre will give me a decent portion, which will be very convenient, as dear Alfred has nothing but his nobility, native and hereditary, and his pay. I only wish uncle would do things unconditionally, in a generous, gentleman-like fashion; he is so disagreeable as to make the dowry depend on Alfred’s giving his written promise that he will never touch cards or dice from the day it is paid down. They accuse my angel of a tendency to play: I don’t know anything about that, but I do know he is a dear, adorable creature.

“I cannot sufficiently extol the genius with which de Hamal managed our flight. How clever in him to select the night of the fête, when Madame (for he knows her habits), as he said, would infallibly be absent at the concert in the park. I suppose you must have gone with her. I watched you rise and leave the dormitory about eleven o’clock. How you returned alone, and on foot, I cannot conjecture. That surely was you we met in the narrow old Rue St. Jean? Did you see me wave my handkerchief from the carriage window?

“Adieu! Rejoice in my good luck: congratulate me on my supreme happiness, and believe me, dear cynic and misanthrope, yours, in the best of health and spirits,

“GINEVRA LAURA DE HAMAL, née FANSHAWE.

“P.S.—Remember, I am a countess now. Papa, mamma, and the girls at home, will be delighted to hear that. ‘My daughter the Countess!’ ‘My sister the Countess!’ Bravo! Sounds rather better than Mrs. John Bretton, hein?”

So ended her explanation: with titles on her tongue as naturally as sugar, and with that strange confidence that naming a thing—Countess, marriage, happiness—makes it secure against all after-tastes.

Those who delight in moral reckonings will expect, perhaps, that so light a creature should be brought in time to heavy expiation. Doubtless a share of suffering lay ready somewhere in her future, as it lies ready for all; yet, of what I knew, I can only say this.

I saw her towards the close of her honeymoon. She called on Madame Beck, and sent for me into the salon. She rushed into my arms laughing—warm with life, scented with the newness of her condition, bright as a confection dressed for a feast. Her curls were longer, her cheeks rosier than ever; her white bonnet and Flanders veil, her orange-flowers and bride’s dress, became her mightily; she was all airy whiteness and soft rustle, and her voice rang in the room like quick silver struck on glass—too clear, too restless to be called music, yet impossible to ignore. There are sounds that do not nourish; they stimulate like strong coffee taken too late.

“I have got my portion!” she cried at once. Ginevra ever clung to the substantial; there was a trading element in her composition, much as she professed to scorn the bourgeoise. “And uncle de Bassompierre is quite reconciled. I don’t mind his calling Alfred a ‘nincompoop’—that’s only his coarse Scotch breeding; and I believe Paulina envies me, and Dr. John is wild with jealousy—fit to blow his brains out—and I’m so happy! I really think I’ve hardly anything left to wish for—unless it be a carriage and an hotel, and, oh! I—must introduce you to mon mari. Alfred, come here!”

And Alfred appeared from the inner salon, where he had been talking with Madame Beck and receiving from her a mixture of congratulation and reprimand, as if she seasoned her approval with salt that it might keep. I was presented under my various names: the Dragon, Diogenes, Timon. The young Colonel was very polite. He offered me a neatly turned apology for the ghost-visits and the rest, ending with the declaration that “the best excuse for all his iniquities stood there,” pointing to his bride.

Then the bride sent him back to Madame Beck as one sends away a servant for another dish, and took possession of me. She proceeded, in her unrestrained spirits—girlish, giddy, willful—to overwhelm and almost suffocate me with her nonsense. She thrust forward her ring again and again; she called herself Madame la Comtesse de Hamal, and made me listen to the sound of it a score of times, as if repetition could hammer it into truth. I said little. I gave her only the crust and rind of my nature. It sufficed her; she expected nothing finer from me, and indeed seemed to relish my dryness as a condiment to her own froth. The more impassive and prosaic my mien, the more merrily she laughed, like someone delighted to hear a sharp utensil scrape a plate.

Soon after his marriage, M. de Hamal was persuaded to leave the army—the surest way, it was thought, of weaning him from certain associates and habits that cost more than they yielded. A post of attaché was procured for him, and he and his young wife went abroad.

I thought she would forget me then, but she did not. For many years she kept up a capricious, fitful correspondence. At first it was all herself and Alfred; then Alfred receded, as summer fruit recedes when a newer harvest comes in, and she wrote chiefly of herself and a certain newcomer—one Alfred Fanshawe de Bassompierre de Hamal—who began to reign in his father’s stead. There were boastings about this infant, amplifications of miracles of precocity, as if motherhood were a legend she could recite and embellish; and when I received her extravagances with my customary phlegm, she reproached me fiercely. I did not know what it was to be a mother; the sensibilities of the maternal heart were Greek and Hebrew to me; I was an unfeeling thing.

In due course the young gentleman took his degrees in teething, measles, hooping-cough. That was a terrible time for me: her letters became a shout of affliction—never woman so visited by calamity, never human being in such need of sympathy. At first I was frightened and wrote back pathetically. Soon I perceived there was more cry than wool in the matter, and relapsed into my natural, cruel insensibility. As for the boy, he weathered each storm like a hero. Five times he was “in articulo mortis,” and five times he revived.

In later years, ominous murmurings rose against Alfred the First. M. de Bassompierre had to be appealed to; debts had to be paid, some of them of that dismal and dingy order called debts of honour. Complaints and difficulties grew frequent. Under every cloud—whatever its shape—Ginevra still called out lustily for sympathy and aid; she had no notion of meeting distress single-handed. Yet in some fashion, from some quarter, she was pretty sure to obtain her will. And so she went on—fighting the battle of life by proxy; and, on the whole, suffering as little as any human being I have ever known.

Was I to set down, before I shut the book, an account of those two loud companions I had brought home from the fête—Freedom and Renovation—those sturdy fellows who had strode at my side under lanterns and fireworks, and sworn they would deliver me from the old servitude?

I put them to proof the next day, as one tests flour by touch and scent before risking the whole loaf. Freedom—who had strutted most—proved, on a nearer view, a beggar in fine clothes: he had excuses ready, and empty hands. Renovation never answered at all. In the night he had died—quietly, without even the decency of a struggle—leaving me to wake to the same narrow room, the same close air, the same rack of suspense tightened anew.

Then came back that corrosive vigil: Shall I yet see him before he goes? Will he remember? Will he come? Will this hour, this half-hour, bring him—or must I again endure the wrench of a farewell unspoken, the uprooting that tears out both hope and doubt, leaving only the raw cavity where the heart has been?

It was the Feast of the Assumption. No class was held. After mass, the boarders and teachers streamed away on holiday, to take their goûter at some farm-house; one could almost taste beforehand the country bread and milk, the brown eggs, the fruit warm from hedges, and hear, in fancy, the pleasant rural hush broken only by cart-wheels and birds. I did not go. Two days only remained before the Paul et Virginie sailed, and I clung to my last chance as a castaway to his last plank.

Some joiners’ work had been set down for the first classe—a bench or desk to mend—such things done best when the rooms are empty. I sat solitary, intending to escape to the garden when the men came, but too listless even to obey my own plan. At length I heard footsteps.

Labassecourien work is seldom solitary; they do in pairs what one could do alone, as if each nail demanded witnesses. So, while I was tying my bonnet—its ribbons hanging idle from my fingers—I noted, with the dreary leisure of one imprisoned, that only one man approached. I noted, too, he wore shoes, not sabots. It must be the master-carpenter, I thought, come to inspect before sending his journeymen. I drew my scarf about me. He opened the door. My back was toward it. A quick, light thrill—too swift for thought—ran through me. I turned.

The doorway was filled; my eyes stamped the image upon my brain: M. Paul.

How many prayers fall back from Heaven as dry leaves from a closed gate! Yet once—perhaps once in a life—some golden thing is dropped full into the lap, complete, flawless from Fruition’s mint.

He wore travel-dress—a surtout guarded with velvet—such as a man chooses when he means to set out. He looked well, cheerful, and, above all, kind; he came in with eagerness, and in one second was close, all amity, all sunshine. It might be the brightness of a bridegroom; it might be merely his own brave habit of carrying light where he can. Whatever the cause, I could not meet his warmth with coldness. If this were indeed the last moment, I would not waste it in proud distance. I loved him too well to let Jealousy—thin witch—stand between us and a humane farewell. A cordial word, a gentle look, would be provision for a long famine; I would take it and taste it, and not let pride spill the cup.

It would be short—so I told myself. A hand held for two minutes; a word as he had said to the assembled pupils; perhaps—only perhaps—the lightest touch of his lips on my cheek, once, and then the gulf: the wide separation I could not cross to go to him, and across which he might never glance back.

He took my hand. With the other he pushed back my bonnet. He looked into my face—and his luminous smile went out, as if someone had laid a wet cloth upon a flame. His lips moved into a mute language—almost maternal—such as one uses over a child suddenly found altered by illness or want.

“Paul, Paul!” cried a woman’s hurried voice behind. “Paul, come into the salon; I have yet a great many things to say to you—conversation for the whole day—and so has Victor; and Josef is here. Come, Paul, come to your friends.”

Madame Beck—drawn by vigilance or by that instinct which never slept in her—pressed near, so near she almost thrust her person between him and me.

“Come, Paul!” she reiterated; her eye grazed me with its hard ray, keen as a steel point. She pushed against her kinsman. I thought he would recede; I thought he would go.

Something in me—held down, strained long—snapped. What broke loose was not decorum, but pain.

“My heart will break!”

It seemed literal heart-break; yet, under the strain, another fountain yielded. One breath from him—only the whisper, “Trust me!”—lifted a weight and opened an outlet. I wept: deep sobs, icy shivers, strong trembling—yet relief in the very storm.

“Leave her to me; it is a crisis: I will give her a cordial, and it will pass,” said Madame Beck, with her calm.

To be left to her and her cordial was to be left to the poisoner and her bowl. When M. Paul answered—deep, harsh, brief—

“Laissez-moi!”

In that grim sound there was a music—strange, strong, life-giving.

“Laissez-moi!” he repeated; his nostrils widened; the muscles of his face quivered.

“But this will never do,” said Madame, stern.

More sternly he returned—“Sortez d’ici!”

“I will send for Père Silas: on the spot I will send for him,” she threatened, pertinacious.

“Femme!” cried the Professor—no longer in his deep tones, but high, sharp with excitement. “Femme! sortez à l’instant!”

He was roused. I loved him then in his wrath with a passion beyond what I had yet known—because the wrath was for me, and because beneath it was something steadfast that would not be handled.

“What you do is wrong,” she persisted; “an act characteristic of men of your unreliable, imaginative temperament; a step impulsive, injudicious—”

“You know not what I have of steady and resolute in me,” said he; “but you shall see. Modeste,” he added, less fiercely, “be gentle, be pitying, be a woman; look at this poor face, and relent. You know I am your friend, and the friend of your friends. Of sacrificing myself I made no difficulty; but my heart is pained by what I see; it must have and give solace. Leave me.”

There was such bitterness, such command, in that leave me, that I marvelled she delayed. Still she stood—stone against fire—meeting his eye with her own fixed forbiddance. She opened her lips to retort. I saw a light leap up over his face; he moved—how, I scarcely know, for it seemed still within courtesy; he gave his hand; it scarcely touched her—and she ran, whirled from the room, was gone, and the door shut, in a second.

The flash passed. He smiled, told me to wipe my eyes, waited quietly till the storm in me sank, dropping now and then a word like cool water on heated stone. Soon I sat beside him again—not desperate now, not desolate; not friendless, not sick of life and craving death.

“It made you very sad then to lose your friend?” he said.

“It kills me to be forgotten, Monsieur,” I answered. “All these weary days I have not heard from you one word; and I was crushed with the possibility—growing to certainty—that you would depart without saying farewell.”

“Must I tell you again, as I told Modeste Beck, that you do not know me? Must I show you my character? You will have proof that I can be a firm friend? Without proof, this hand will not lie still in mine, it will not trust my shoulder? Good. The proof is ready. I come to justify myself.”

“Say anything, teach anything, prove anything, Monsieur. I can listen now.”

“Then, first, you must go with me a good distance into the town. I came on purpose to fetch you.”

I did not question, did not bargain with fear, did not ask for explanations. I retied my bonnet. I was ready.

He took me by the boulevards. Under the lime-trees the air was greener and cooler; their leaves made a roof that held the day’s heat in check, and the trunks—pale, columnar—stood like sentinels in calm. On benches set along the walk, he made me sit more than once. He did not ask if I was tired; he looked, and drew his conclusion with that practical tenderness of his.

“All these weary days,” he repeated, with a gentle mimicry of my voice and foreign accent—playful, never cruel. “‘All these weary days’ I have not for one hour forgotten you. Faithful women err in this: they think themselves the sole faithful. On a very fervent truth to myself, I, too, till lately scarce dared count on faith from any quarter; but—look at me.”

I lifted my eyes. They were happy now; they could not help betraying the heart.

“Well,” he said, after scrutinizing me a moment, “there is no denying that signature: Constancy wrote it. Her pen is of iron. Was the record painful?”

“Severely painful,” I said. “Withdraw her hand, Monsieur; I can bear its inscribing force no more.”

“Elle est toute pâle,” he murmured to himself; “cette figure-là me fait mal.”

“Ah! I am not pleasant to look at—?”

The question slipped out as if it had been waiting long behind my teeth. I had always carried, like a hidden bruise, the dread of outward deficiency; at that moment it throbbed and made itself heard.

A great softness came over his face. His violet eyes glistened under their dark lashes. He started up. “Let us walk on.”

“Do I displease your eyes much?” I urged, driven by a necessity that was not vanity alone, but hunger for some assurance.

He stopped and gave me a short, strong answer—one that subdued and satisfied. From that hour I knew what I was for him; and what I might be for the rest of the world, I ceased painfully to care.

Where we wandered I could scarcely tell. The walk was long and yet seemed brief. M. Emanuel spoke of his voyage—he thought to stay away three years. On his return from Guadaloupe he looked for release from liabilities and a clear course. And what would I do in the interval? Had I dropped the idea of keeping a little school of my own?

“Indeed I had not,” I said. “I was doing my best to save what would enable me to begin.”

He did not like leaving me in the Rue Fossette; he feared I should miss him there too much, feel desolate, grow sad. That was true; yet I promised to endure.

“Still,” he added, speaking low, “there is another objection. I should wish to write to you sometimes. It would not be well to have uncertainty about letters. And in the Rue Fossette—in short, our Catholic discipline in certain matters—though justifiable and expedient—might, under peculiar circumstances, become liable to misapplication—perhaps abuse.”

“But if you write,” I said, “I must have your letters; and I will have them. Ten directors, twenty directresses, shall not keep them from me. I am a Protestant: I will not bear that kind of discipline. Monsieur, I will not.”

“Doucement—doucement,” he replied. “We will contrive a plan. We have resources. Soyez tranquille.”

He paused.

We were returning now, and had reached the middle of a clean faubourg. The houses were small, but had a look of decency and domestic hope—white steps scrubbed, windows bright, little vines trained where there was room to grow. Before a neat white door-step M. Paul halted.

“I call here,” he said.

He did not knock. He took a key from his pocket, opened, and entered at once. He ushered me in and shut the door. No servant appeared. The vestibule was small but freshly painted; at the end a French window stood, with vines trained about its panes—tendrils and green leaves kissing the glass. The house held a silence like a held breath.

He opened an inner door and showed me a parlour—a tiny salon—pretty as a shell. The walls were tinted like a blush; the floor waxed to a mild shine; a bright carpet lay square in the centre. A little round table gleamed like the mirror above the hearth. There was a small couch, a chiffonnière half-open to show porcelain, a French clock, a lamp, some biscuit ornaments. In the deep recess of the single ample window stood three green pots of plants, each in bloom, as if the room kept a private summer. In one corner a small marble-topped guéridon held a work-box, and beside it a glass of violets in water. The lattice was open; the air breathed in fresh; the violets lent their sweet, cool scent—clean and living, like a promise.

“Pretty, pretty place!” I said; and he smiled to see me pleased.

“Must we sit down here and wait?” I whispered, half-awed by the hush.

“We will first peep into one or two other nooks of this nutshell,” he answered.

“Dare you take the freedom of going all over the house?” I asked.

“Yes, I dare,” he said quietly.

He showed me a little kitchen: a small stove and oven; bright brasses—few, but scoured till they held the light; two chairs and a table; a cupboard with a diminutive but well-chosen set of earthenware. The place smelled not of grease, but of clean metal and dry wood—as if it waited to be awakened by honest cooking.

“There is a coffee service of china in the salon,” he observed, as I looked at the green-and-white plates and the cups and jugs to match.

Up a narrow, clean staircase he let me glimpse two little sleeping-rooms—pretty cabinets, modest, orderly. Then he led me down again, and we halted, with a certain ceremony, before a larger door than any yet opened.

He produced a second key, fitted it, and opened.

“Voici!” he cried.

I stood in a good-sized room—scrupulously clean, though barer than the rest. The boards were well scoured and left without carpet; two rows of green benches and desks ran with an alley between, ending in an estrade with a teacher’s chair and table; behind them a tableau. Two maps hung on the walls; in the windows a few hardy plants stood like patient pupils. In short, a miniature classe—complete, neat, pleasant.

“It is a school then?” I said. “Who keeps it? I never heard of an establishment in this faubourg.”

“Will you have the goodness to accept a few prospectuses for distribution in behalf of a friend of mine?” he asked, drawing from his pocket some quires and placing them in my hand.

I looked. I read. In fair print:

“Externat de demoiselles. Numéro 7, Faubourg Clotilde. Directrice, Mademoiselle Lucy Snowe.”

And what did I say to M. Paul Emanuel?

There are junctures one cannot recall—crises that whirl in the mind like a wheel spun too fast, leaving only a blur of light. I cannot remember the thoughts or words of the ten minutes that followed; but the first thing distinct is this: I was speaking very fast, repeating—

“Did you do this, M. Paul? Is this your house? Did you furnish it? Did you get these papers printed? Do you mean me? Am I the directress? Is there another Lucy Snowe? Tell me—say something!”

He would not speak. His pleased silence, his laughing downward look, his attitude—those are clear to me still.

“How is it? I must know all—all!” I cried.

The packet of papers fell on the floor. He had extended his hand; I had fastened on it as if it were a hold above deep water.

“Ah! you said I had forgotten you all these weary days,” he said. “Poor old Emanuel! These are the thanks he gets for trudging about three mortal weeks—from house-painter to upholsterer, from cabinet-maker to charwoman. Lucy and Lucy’s cot—the sole thoughts in his head!”

I hardly knew what to do. I caressed the soft velvet of his cuff, then stroked the hand it surrounded. It was his foresight—his goodness—his silent, strong, effective goodness—that overcame me. The assurance of his sleepless interest broke on me like sudden day; and his look—tender, fond, daringly tender—shook me in a way I cannot translate into any language but trembling.

Yet in the midst of that sweetness, the practical in me rose—like a housewife measuring flour even while her heart sings.

“The trouble!” I exclaimed. “And the cost! Had you money, M. Paul?”

“Plenty of money!” he said heartily. “The disposal of my large teaching connection put me in possession of a handsome sum; and with part of it I determined to give myself the richest treat that I have known or shall know. I like this. I have reckoned on this hour day and night lately. I would not come near you, because I would not forestall it. Reserve is neither my virtue nor my vice. If I had put myself into your power, and you had begun with your questions of look and lip—‘Where have you been, M. Paul? What have you been doing? What is your mystery?’—my solitary first and last secret would presently have unravelled itself in your lap. Now,” he pursued, “you shall live here and have a school; you shall employ yourself while I am away; you shall think of me sometimes; you shall mind your health and happiness for my sake, and when I come back—”

There he left the future blank, as if it were too sacred to fill with common ink.

I promised. I promised to work hard and willingly. “I will be your faithful steward,” I said; “I trust at your coming the account will be ready. Monsieur, monsieur, you are too good!”

The words were poor for what I felt—speech is brittle and cold; it shivers when one would mould it. He watched me still, and gently lifted his hand to stroke my hair; it touched my lips in passing. I pressed it close, paid it tribute. He was my king; and that hand—so bountiful, so human—made homage both joy and duty.

The afternoon slipped away; evening shaded the quiet faubourg. M. Paul claimed my hospitality: he had been afoot and occupied since morning and needed refreshment. He said I should offer him chocolate in my pretty gold-and-white service. He went out and ordered what was needful from the restaurant; he placed the guéridon and two chairs on the balcony outside the French window, under the screening vines. With what shy joy I accepted my part as hostess, arranged the salver, served the benefactor-guest—feeling, in that small domestic ritual, the beginning of an identity I had never dared name.

Behind the house were gardens, and beyond them fields. The air was still, mild, and fresh. Sound, too, was blessedly clean there: no harsh roll of heavy carts, no clatter of commerce, no iron shouting—only a light breeze making a soft, leaf-borne speech; and, in a garden near, the steady rise and fall of a jet from a well—water talking to itself in silver syllables. That gentle acoustic world soothed like a hand laid on a fevered brow; it made the heart’s listening less frantic. Over poplars, laurels, cypresses, and roses, the moon looked up—so halcyon that the spirit trembled under her quiet. A star shone subject beside her with the unemulous ray of pure love. Near us a pale statue leaned over the play of waters.

M. Paul talked—his voice modulated so it did not bruise the evening. It mingled with the whisper of vines, the hush-sigh of foliage, the continual thin music of the fountain—nature’s unbought art, unadvertised, unexhibited, healing because it asked no audience.

Our meal was simple: chocolate, rolls, and a plate of fresh summer fruit—cherries and strawberries bedded in green leaves. Yet it was what we both liked better than a feast. The chocolate had that deep, dark fragrance—warm, faintly bitter, sweetened into comfort; the rolls broke with a tender crack; the berries, cool and perfumed, stained the tongue with June. I found an inexpressible delight in tending him—placing, pouring, watching the steam lift, hearing the small sounds of cup and spoon that, in such quiet, become intimate as whispers.

I asked whether his friends—Père Silas and Madame Beck—knew what he had done, whether they had seen my house.

“Mon amie,” he said, “none knows what I have done save you and myself. The pleasure is consecrated to us two—unshared and unprofaned. To speak truth, there has been to me in this matter a refinement of enjoyment I would not make vulgar by communication. Besides,” he added, smiling, “I wanted to prove to Miss Lucy that I could keep a secret. How often has she taunted me with lack of dignified reserve and needful caution! How many times has she saucily insinuated that all my affairs are the secret of Polichinelle!”

It was true; I had not spared him. Magnificent-minded, grand-hearted, dear, faulty little man—he deserved candour, and from me had it.

Still I questioned—practical again: to whom did the house belong? Who was my landlord? What was the rent? He gave me these particulars at once in writing; he had foreseen all.

The house was not his—of that I had been sure. He could earn, but he was not made to hoard; he needed, by nature, a treasurer. The tenement belonged, he said, to a citizen in the Basse-Ville—a man of substance; and then he startled me by adding, “a friend of yours, Miss Lucy, a person who has a most respectful regard for you.”

To my pleasant surprise I found the landlord was none other than M. Miret—the short-tempered, kind-hearted bookseller who had found me a seat that night in the park. He was rich in his station, much respected, and owned several houses in this faubourg. The rent was moderate—scarce half what it would have been nearer the centre of Villette.

“And then,” observed M. Paul, “should fortune not favour you—though I think she will—I have the satisfaction to think you are in good hands. M. Miret will not be extortionate. The first year’s rent you have already in your savings; afterwards Miss Lucy must trust God, and herself. But now—what will you do for pupils?”

“I must distribute my prospectuses,” I answered.

Man cannot prophesy. Love is no oracle. Fear is a fertile cook: it will take one bitter root and, by long simmering, persuade you it has made a whole banquet of calamity.

For years I had tasted, beforehand, the very iron of those coming absences. I lay down in imagination beneath the huge wheel, certain I should feel the crushing—certain it would grind bone to meal. I heard it too: the heavy approach, the solemn thunder of an advancing fate; and in that sound my heart had already been bruised.

Yet when the chariot came, it did not, as I had pictured, descend with a visible horror. It passed—high, sullen, and broad—like a shadow traversing noon. It left a chill, a thinning of the light, a momentary dimness on the air; and then it was gone, and I, the prostrate votary, still breathed. Strange, but true: the anticipatory crunch had been almost all the torture.

M. Emanuel was away three years.

Reader, they were the three happiest years of my life.

You start at the paradox. You think happiness cannot be kneaded out of separation. Listen, then, and I will tell you how the days rose, one by one, like loaves in a quiet oven—how the very ordinary acts of keeping house, keeping school, keeping faith, became a kind of daily rite, rich with scent and sound, and warmed by a fire that burned beyond seas.

I began my school. I worked—worked hard; not with the dry appetite of ambition, but as one serves in a beloved kitchen when the master has stepped out and left the keys in your hand. I held myself steward of his property; and because he had trusted me, I resolved to render, God willing, a good account. At first my pupils were burghers’ children; by-and-by a higher class entered. The externat took root. Its growth was not sudden, like a forced fruit, but steady, like grain that is well sown in a field and watched with patience through weathers.

In the middle of the second year, chance—one of those plain, domestic chances by which Providence sometimes drops a coin into the flour-bin—put into my hands an additional hundred pounds. A letter came from England, and with it the notes, clean and crisp as if newly counted. They were sent by Mr. Marchmont, cousin and heir to my dear dead mistress. He had been recovering from a dangerous illness; and this money, he said, was a peace-offering to his conscience, reproaching him in the matter of certain papers or memoranda discovered after his kinswoman’s death—papers that named or recommended Lucy Snowe. Mrs. Barrett had given him my address.

How far his conscience had been sinned against, I did not inquire. I asked no questions; I took the cash and made it useful. With that hundred pounds I ventured to take the house adjoining my own. I would not leave the dwelling M. Paul had chosen—where he had left me, and where he expected to find me again. I extended, as one extends a table when more guests arrive than were first invited. My externat became a pensionnat; and that also prospered.

The secret of my success did not lie so much in any endowment of mine, as in a new state of circumstances—a changed life, a relieved heart. The spring that moved my energies lay far away beyond seas, on an Indian isle. At parting he had left me a legacy: not merely in money, though he was generous, but in thought—such a thought for the present, such a hope for the future, such a motive for a persevering, laborious, enterprising, patient, brave course—I could not flag.

Few things shook me now. Few things had power to vex, intimidate, or depress. Most things pleased. Mere trifles had a charm: the creak of a stair that meant a child was coming down safely; the hush after lessons when slates were put away; the soft, steady ticking of the clock in the schoolroom; the faint, homely perfume of warmed milk when the boarders took their supper. I began to understand how a life can be seasoned—how, if the heart is not pinched by dread, even plain fare tastes of comfort.

Do not think this genial flame fed wholly on a parting promise, like a lamp kept alive by one last spoon of oil. A generous provider supplied bounteous fuel. I was spared all chill, all stint; I was not suffered to fear penury; I was not tried with suspense. By every vessel he wrote; and his letters were like parcels of sustenance sent across the ocean—real food that nourished, living water that refreshed. He wrote as he gave, and as he loved: in full-handed, full-hearted plenitude.

There was no sham in him, no hollow unreal. Apology never dropped her slippery oil from his lips; never, by his pen, did she proffer coward feints and paltry nullities. He gave neither stone nor excuse—neither scorpion nor disappointment. He sat down, took pen and paper, because he loved Lucy, and had much to say; because he was faithful and thoughtful; because he was tender and true. When his letters came, the house itself seemed to breathe differently: the paper held the salt of sea-air, the faint tang of foreign sun, and—dearer than perfume—the remembered cadence of his voice. I would read and reread, listening, as one listens for a known step in a corridor; and the very silence after seemed full, because it had been answered.

And was I grateful? God knows. I believe that scarce a living being so remembered, so sustained, could do otherwise than be grateful to the death.

Adherent to his own religion—in him was not the stuff of the facile apostate—he freely left me my pure faith. He did not tease nor tempt. He wrote, “Remain a Protestant. My little English Puritan, I love Protestantism in you. I own its severe charm. There is something in its ritual I cannot receive myself, but it is the sole creed for ‘Lucy.’”

All Rome could not put into him bigotry, nor the Propaganda itself make him a real Jesuit. He was born honest, not false—artless, not cunning—a freeman, not a slave. Tenderness had rendered him ductile in a priest’s hands; affection, devotedness, sincere pious enthusiasm had sometimes blinded his kind eyes, made him abandon justice to himself to do the work of craft, and serve ends not his own; but such faults are costly to the owner, and so rare to find, one scarcely knows whether they will not one day be reckoned amongst the jewels.

So passed the three years, not like three blank winters, but like three seasons of husbandry: sowing, tending, reaping; setting things in order; making the small daily acts of care into a continuing promise. And now the term was done. His return was fixed.

It was autumn. He was to be with me before the mists of November came.

My school flourished. My house was ready. I had made him a little library, filling its shelves with the books he had left in my care; I had cultivated—out of love for him, for I was naturally no florist—the plants he preferred, and some were yet in bloom. Even the kitchen, though it knew my plain hand, had been made more exact: the copper scoured, the pantry ranked, the jars labelled—little domestic histories written in ink and tied with string, as if careful naming could keep things from being lost. There are words that preserve a taste; there are tastes that cannot be carried into another tongue. Often, as I marked the French names for herbs and the English names in my head, I felt the slight power hidden in a dominant lexicon—how a thing changes when it must answer to a different word; how, in the mere act of translation, something delicate dies. I could not give M. Paul my whole inward sense in any language; yet I prepared what I could: bread, order, warmth, welcome.

I thought I loved him when he went away; I loved him now in another degree: he was more my own.

The sun passed the equinox; the days shortened; the leaves grew sere; but—he was coming. Frosts appeared at night; November sent his fogs in advance; the wind took its autumn moan; but—he was coming.

I know the language of skies; I have noted their signs since childhood. And, too, I know the language of sound: the way a house changes its speech when a storm gathers; the way air begins to ring, and then to roar, as if some immense, careless industry had been set loose above the roofs. In such noise the mind cannot keep its peace. It is not only fear; it is assault. The wind is a tyrant that will not be reasoned with.

The skies hung full and dark. A wrack sailed from the west. The clouds cast themselves into strange forms—arches and broad radiations. There rose resplendent mornings—glorious, royal, purple as a monarch in state; the heavens were one flame; so wild they rivalled battle at its thickest—so bloody they shamed Victory in her pride.

God watch that sail! Oh, guard it!

The wind shifted to the west. “Peace, peace, Banshee,” I said—keening at every window. It would rise, it would swell; it shrieked out long. Wander as I might through the house that night, I could not lull the blast. The advancing hours made it strong; by midnight, all sleepless watchers heard and feared a wild south-west storm.

That storm roared, frenzied, for seven days. It did not cease till the Atlantic was strewn with wrecks. It did not lull till the deeps had gorged their full of sustenance. Not till the destroying angel of tempest had achieved his perfect work would he fold the wings whose waft was thunder, the tremor of whose plumes was storm.

“Peace, be still!” Oh, a thousand weepers, praying in agony on waiting shores, listened for that voice; but it was not uttered—not uttered till, when the hush came, some could not feel it; till, when the sun returned, his light was night to some.

Here pause: pause at once. There is enough said.

Trouble no quiet, kind heart. Leave sunny imaginations hope. Let it be theirs to conceive the delight of joy born again fresh out of great terror; the rapture of rescue from peril; the wondrous reprieve from dread; the fruition of return. Let them picture union and a happy succeeding life.

Madame Beck prospered all the days of her life; so did Père Silas. Madame Walravens fulfilled her ninetieth year before she died.

Farewell.
